PREFACE This 1941 edition of Our World Today - 

Asia, Latin America, United States brings the material up to date with the latest statistics and census figures. Especial attention is given to Latin America and our good neighbor policy, and the volume ends with a full description of the United States treated occupationally. 

This is a simple, direct story of human geography. Its first aim is to help the pupil to a clear understanding of the many geographical references which he meets through the press, the radio, the movies, and other forms of modern communication. It is a geography in the true sense, but links its story with history and the other social studies wherever such correlation vivifies for the pupil the topic under consideration. It under consideration. It seeks to preserve a sensible mean between the restricted science of the physiographer and the confusing incongruities of certain fusion courses. 

Following the trend of modern pedagogy, the text is organized into well-defined units. 

It is adapted both to the one-cycle and the two-cycle plans of presentation. Each unit is likewise organized into convenient teaching divisions which insure ordered thinking and correct interpretation on the part of the pupil.

In the selection of topics, the authors have had the benefit of the most recent educational research. They have given first importance to topics of proved utility. The social and economic influences of climate, soil, forests, mineral resources, waterways, and water power are the principal considerations in the discussion of each country. Statistical information about crops and commerce, population, manufacture, and so on, has been relegated for the most part to the Appendix, where this material is convenient for ready reference.

The text includes many teaching and study aids. Carefully prepared questions and activities are a vital part of this book. They are numerous enough and so flexibly organized as to provide for all levels of pupil ability. 

A just equilibrium is established between fact-fixing exercises and thought-provoking projects — between drill and laboratory materials. 

The challenging superiority of the pictorial illustrations, in quality and number, sets a new standard for textbooks in geography. 

No subject has greater need of visualizing aids than this. A casual turning of the pages of Our World Today will show that neither time, money, nor effort has been spared in securing the best pictures. Lively, colorful captions add to the usefulness of the illustrations. The stimulus that they give to adequate understanding of places and peoples will lighten the task of teachers and pupils. 

Our World Today has almost twice as many illustrations as are found in ordinary geographies.

The maps are in keeping with the versatile and useful character of the pictures. They are alive with color and emphasize significant information. Great care has been exercised in assuring accuracy of physical and political features. Many individual countries have full-page or double-page maps in color, and each continent has, also in color, a full-page physical-political, a population, and two seasonal climatic maps. In addition, there are numerous black and white maps. iii

iv 

PREFACE 

Other visualizing aids are the trade graphs which make clear the commercial relationships of the United States with the rest of the world.

The bibliographical material is carefully selected. The references are suited to the ages of the pupils for whom they are intended. 

The authors have based their choice of titles on the reliability of the information referred to, and on the availability of the materials presented. Every possible learning situation has been considered in the selection. 

The statistical material in the Appendix is the latest available from the United States Government and other sources. It should frequently be consulted by the pupils for areas, populations, climatic conditions, and occupations of the leading countries of the world.

Our present age, more than any other in the world's history, is intensely concerned with the problem of international relations. 

Children in school today are going to solve that problem tomorrow. Texts and teachers should serve only to show the way to truth; the pupil must find the truth himself. Let him make his own decisions about water power, and tariff walls, and war, and peace, but see to it that the decisions are made, and that the pupil has every possible facility for making them, and hope mightily that he may vision the brotherhood of man as the outstanding aim of modern civilization. To this purpose Our World Today is dedicated. 

DE F. S. 

R. W. H.

THE MOSQUE OF OMAR, JERUSALEM 

This building stands on the site of Solomon's temple.

İSTANBUL Publishers' Photo Service 

For sixteen centuries this famous old city was the head of a great empire, and the gateway between southeastern Europe and the Orient. To-day, in spite of the beautiful mosques whose minarets pierce the sky, symbolic of the 

Moslem faith, the city is striving to westernize itself. Ferries joining the railroad terminals of Europe and Asia, street cars and automobiles, and modern lighting have already modernized this picturesque city. Gone are the fezzes, the turbans, the baggy trousers, the veiled women. In their places has come the western dress we know so well. 

UNIT I.  

Empires tottered and fell as a result of the

World War. The German, Russian, Austro- 

Hungarian, and Turkish Empires are no more. 

Out of them have been made numerous countries, most of them republics, or with republican principles. In this respect, for a time at least, the greatest war of all history did help to "make the world safe for democracy."

We now come to the countries of the Near East, most of which at one time formed a part of the Turkish Empire. 

A. TURKEY 

Turkey, Old and New. - Turkey was one of those "ramshackle empires," as David

Lloyd George so aptly termed Austria-Hungary. At one time the Turkish Empire included most of southwestern Asia and northern Africa, and extended even to the very gates of Vienna. Even as late as 1913 Turkey had an area of over 700,000 square miles and a population of over 21,000,000. Included within this area were Turkey-in-Europe and Turkey-in-Asia. The latter part of her Empire consisted of Anatolia, Armenia, Kurdistan, Syria, Palestine, Egypt, Arabia, and 

Mesopotamia. To-day, modern Turkey has an estimated area of 294,492 square miles, with a population numbering approximately 16,200,000.

Under the leadership of a great man, Mustapha Kemal, Turkey was modernized.

Never in history were so many thorough reforms made in so short a time with so little opposition from the people. Before we consider this man and some of his astonishing accom- 1

POPULATION MAP OF ASIA Map Questions and Activities 

This is a map of Asia showing by colors where the people are to be found. It is a very important map and should be studied carefully. 

1. How do you account for the great density of population in China proper? 

2. Why are there so many people in India? 

3. Account for the one light spot in India. 

4. Why does so much of Asia have a population density under 16 people per square mile? 

5. Compare the population density of Java and 

Borneo. Can you give the reason for this difference?

Java is one of the most densely populated islands in the world. plishments, let us take a closer view of the country.

The Land of Modern Turkey. - Turkey is now only a little larger than the state of Texas and has about 3,000,000 more people than the state of New York. The territory of Turkey 

Courtesy International Harvester Company

HARVESTING IN THE NEAR EAST 

Moslem farmers labor in the wheat fields in a land that was old when Christ was born-still cutting the grain with sickles and binding the sheaves by hand, as was done centuries ago. Yet the machine age is creeping slowly upon them, for one of Turkey's leading imports from the United States to-day is agricultural machinery. at present consists of the former province of Anatolia or Asia Minor, the heart of old 

Turkey. Its boundaries are: on the north, Greece, Bulgaria, the Black Sea, and Russia; on the east, Persia; on the south, Iraq, Syria, and the Mediterranean Sea; and on the west, the Aegean Sea (map, pages 16-17). The old capital, Istanbul (Constantinople), is situated on the Bosporus which connects the Black Sea and the Sea of Marmara, at the west end of which the Dardanelles (Hellespont) forms an outlet into the Aegean Sea.

The Turks are mainly an agricultural 6. Does Japan seem to have many people? All people. About three-fourths of them are of it? Why the difference? 

7. Why is Siam not so densely populated as China and India? 

8. Compare this population map of Asia with the physical-political and climatic maps of the same continent. Tell whether or not one map helps to explain another. farmers. The new republican government has done much to help. Land taken from the state has been granted to the people on request, provided they take good care of it and cultivate it for three consecutive years.

The old heavy taxes have been removed and

EDUCATION IN TURKEY 

a moderate land tax established. Even military service has been reduced to eighteen months and those serving in the army are permitted to work in the fields. 

Feudalism, which prevailed in some parts of the old empire, has been abolished, and private ownership of small plots substituted. 

The government advances loans to the farmers at a reasonable interest, and distributes machines, seeds, and livestock. A great amount of attention is being given to fostering modern scientific methods in agriculture. They try to determine what the soils are best suited for, and how they may be enriched. Irrigation is being extended and crop rotation practiced. 

The farmers are taught how to fight plant diseases and pests. 

The leading crops are wheat, barley, potatoes, cotton, tobacco, and sugar beets. You have surely heard of the famous Turkish tobacco. Other agricultural products are figs, raisins, nuts, fruits, opium, and gums.

Fighting Malaria. From the standpoint of health, one of the greatest tasks of the government is the fighting of malaria. The warm marshes and swamps of these Mediterranean lands favor the growth of the mosquito that carries the malaria germ. 

Along the Turkish coast the summers are hot and dry, the winters cool and relatively wet. These, as you have probably noted, are typical Mediterranean conditions. Most of Anatolia, however, is a plateau, with high mountains in places, so that the interior has the continental type of climate, hot in summer and cold in winter, with some rain falling at all times of the year.

Mineral Resources. - Turkey has exten sive undeveloped mineral resources, including coal, petroleum, copper, manganese, gold, silver, chrome, emery, and other valuable minerals. The government is carrying out plans to increase the mining and use of these minerals. Turkey is one of the leading countries in the production of emery and chrome, which is so much used in making rustless steel. 

Transportation. - Turkey needs more railroads. Many of the old lines were destroyed in the numerous wars of past years. The government purchased all railroads built by foreign companies and is now carrying out an ambitious plan to connect all parts of the republic by these ribbons of steel. 

More highways are also being built. A law was passed requiring the villages to contribute to the construction of roads connecting the capitals of the local districts. 

Turkey now has over 25,000 miles of highways. Aviation is likewise being developed and men trained for air service. 

Communication. - Ankara, the capital of 

Turkey, has a wireless station capable of communicating with our own capital, Washington, D. C. Thousands of miles of telegraph and telephone lines give swift communication throughout the country and the mail service has been greatly improved in recent years.

Industry and Commerce. - Manufacturing of silk, rugs, flour, soap, the preserving and canning of fruit, and sugar refining have all made substantial progress during recent years. 

Trade with other countries has increased, both imports and exports showing a growth in recent years. 

Education. In the field of education radical changes have been made. For instance, under the old régime of the sultans, education was dominated by the Mohammedan religion, which meant learning the precepts of the Koran. Learning by rote prevailed as it did in China and was reserved only for the privileged few.

Now primary education is free and obligatory, requiring an attendance for five years. 

Secondary schools have been established, in which scientific studies are being stressed. 

The educational authorities are trying to make the training practical by emphasizing modern agriculture. There is a university at

Istanbul, and one is being planned for the right place at the right time. Ankara.

Modernizing Turkey. - Turkish codes of law have been borrowed from those of the leading European countries. The Penal Code comes from Italy, the Code of Civil and Criminal Procedure from France, the Code of Commerce from Germany, and a Civil Code from Switzerland.

Polygamy has been abolished. The laws of inheritance which sanctioned the inferior 

ON THE OUTSKIRTS OF ISTANBUL 

Long ago, about the fourth century, a Roman emperor built this wall as part of the fortification of the city. Today, sheep and goats quietly graze under the crumbling ruins. 

You may have read of him-Ghazi Mustapha Kemal.

Born in 1880, in Salonika, then under Turkish rule, he graduated from a military academy in 1904 with the rank of captain.

From the very beginning he was strongly opposed to the old government of the Sultan. 

He was one of the leaders in the formation of the "Union and Progress Committee" that overthrew Abdul Hamid in 1908 and proclaimed a new constitution. 

He fought with the Turks in the war with Italy in 1911 and in the two Balkan wars. 

He played a conspicuous rôle in the World

War. After the Armistice, Turkey was not only reduced to very sore straits, but was actually attacked by the Greeks. Kemal organized a new army, completely routed the Greeks, and in October, 1923, proclaimed a new republic modeled on new lines. 

Kemal's name, which he changed from the title Ghazi (general or president) to the surname Ataturk (father of Turks), will go down in history not only for his military victories,

Ewing Galloway but for his constructive work in government. 

Under his leadership, the country became much more of a unit. All the foreigners in 

Turkey were encouraged to go elsewhere, and all the Turks in foreign countries were encouraged to come home. He abolished the Sultanate, changed the alphabet, did away with men's fezzes and women's veils, established schools and colleges, built roads, railroads, and factories, and made his country respected abroad and prosperous at home. He died November 10, 1938, and was succeeded next day by his right-hand man, General Ismet Inonu. place of women have been suppressed. The seclusion of women is a thing of the past. 

Many people in Turkey now dress as they do in western Europe and the United States. 

Women no longer veil their faces. Boys and girls are no longer forced to adopt the religion of Islam (Mohammedanism) when they have reached a certain age.

There is freedom of speech, freedom of the press, freedom of education, and freedom of religious worship, at least in theory.

MOUNTED KURDISH WARRIORS OF NORTHWESTERN PERSIA 

Ewing Galloway

The Kurds live in a region of lofty mountains extending into the three countries of Persia, Iraq, and Turkey. 

Some of them live in villages, raising fruits and cereals, but many are nomadic tent dwellers, devoting their time and skill to raising horses, cattle, sheep, and goats. The Kurds have been much feared on account of their sudden raids upon the bordering villages of other races. These are some of the lawless people whom Reza Shah Pahlevi has had to subdue in order to make traveling safe in Persia.

B. PERSIA (IRAN) 

Progress in Persia. Persia has an area of about 628,000 square miles, somewhat larger than Germany, France, and Spain combined, with a population of about 15,000,000. 

You can see that the country is not even as densely populated as ours. It has been described as a "vast and miserably poor country with scanty population." 

But changes have come to Persia as well as to other countries of the Near East. Roads and railways have been built, making travel easier. Airplane service links the capital, Tehran, with Bushire, its Persian Gulf port, and with Baghdad and other foreign cities.

Motor cars and trucks are now displacing the camel caravans which formerly carried on most of the trade. The government and the Anglo-Iranian Oil Company together have constructed over 5000 miles of roads which can be used by motor cars for most of the year. 

Roads formerly infested with robbers have been made safe for travelers by the police.

Religious Beliefs and Education. - The religious beliefs of the Persian people have long stood in the way of progress. Most of the people are Moslems (Mohammedans). They have held to the belief that the Koran contained all that it was necessary for any one to know. What little education there was consisted in memorizing parts of the Koran. (What other countries that we have studied used to have this system?)

VILLAGE HOME IN THE MOUNTAINS OF PERSIA 

In the past the people who worked the land were practically serfs. While the aristocrats lived in splendor, the peasants, which means most of the population, were wretchedly poor and completely at the mercy of their masters.

Again, many of the people live in tribes, where the land is held and cultivated in common. These tribes are isolated from the people in other parts of Persia, where they live in a different manner. 

Ewing Galloway

The homes of the poorer people in Persia are mere hovels as a rule. Notice that the women in the picture follow the old custom of veiling the lower part of the face.

Only since the beginning of the present century has education of the common people been attempted. Women of all classes were generally illiterate. They were kept in seclusion and allowed to appear in public only when heavily veiled. Schools have now been established for both boys and girls. 

Conditions among the People. - Another difficulty of Persia has been the insecurity of life and property. Those who attempted to carry on agriculture, especially near the eastern borders, were in constant danger of raids from nomadic tribes.

As in many backward countries, much of the land is in the hands of a few large landowners belonging to the aristocratic classes. 

To bring the people of Persia together and establish industries and trade among them is one of the big tasks of the present Persian government.

The Land of Persia. 

Most of Persia is a mountainous plateau somewhat less than a mile above sea level.

It is not a flat tableland, as numerous mountain ranges traverse the plateau, most of them running northwest and southeast. Some of the mountain peaks are higher than those of the Alps. 

Much of eastern Persia is arid desert. All together about one-half of the country is unproductive desert land. The edges of the plateau are higher than the interior, and many of the rivers flow inland, draining into big salt marshes which form impassable quagmires. 

There are over thirty lakes in Persia, and they are mainly salt water.

The mountains are generally barren of trees or other vegetation. Between the mountains, however, are fertile level tracts which form the arable land. Very little of the coastal land can be cultivated. 

Climate. The climate of Persia differs in various parts of the country. That of the lowlands along the Caspian Sea and the Persian Gulf is moist, hot, and unhealthful. On

ORIGIN OF THE PERSIANS 

the plateau, the air is dry and generally cooler than on the lowlands, but there are wide extremes of temperature. The air on the plateau is so clear that one can see for long distances.

Rainfall is scanty, being only five or six inches in central and southeastern Persia. 

Among the mountains it increases to fifty inches in places. 

Heavy snowfall on the mountains adds to the water supply. 

If it were not for this snow, most of the country would be as arid as one-half of it is now. 

Agriculture in Persia. - Agriculture in Persia is carried on by the most primitive methods, and much of the arable land lies uncultivated. Since rainfall is light, most of the cultivation can be carried on only by irrigation. Water for irrigation is carried through canals and underground aqueducts. Mountain streams and wells furnish the supply. iron, copper, tin, platinum, lead, and sulphur, besides building stone, but few of the minerals except petroleum are being used. This has been successfully exploited for many years by the Anglo-Persian Oil Company. This company, which is controlled by the British, employs thousands of Persian workmen and pays part of its profits to the 

Persian government.

Fine forests cover the lower slopes of the mountains and some of the lower plains. These will be another source of wealth to Persia when properly used.

Fisheries. The Caspian Sea fisheries furnish a livelihood for many people in northern Persia.

Most of the fish exports go to 

Russia. Pearls, said to be the best in the world, are found in the Persian Gulf at the port of Lingeh.

Manufactures. There 

are very few manufactures in Persia Ewing Galloway apart from those carried on in

Persia. Wheat and barley are the staple grains grown all over the country. Rice is cultivated on the lowlands along the Caspian Sea. A great variety of fruits such as are grown in California are produced in Persia, and dried fruits are exported. Tobacco, silk, tea, and cotton are other products. Opium has long been a leading product of Persia, but the government now restricts its cultivation. 

Few cattle are raised, the chief animals being sheep, goats, horses, mules, and donkeys. 

Much wool is produced and used for weaving cloth, rugs, and carpets. - - 

Mineral and Forest Wealth. Persia has a large variety of mineral resources, including for which this country is famous, are among the products exported. Persia is the home of the Oriental rug, and the finest specimens have been produced here. The government is now taking steps to introduce spinning and weaving machinery.

Silk, cotton, and wool of good quality are all produced in the country. Have you ever seen a Persian shawl? The Persians achieved distinctive patterns in their woven goods by which they were easily recognized, though now imitations of these designs are carried out by weavers in other countries. 

The Origin of the Persians. - Although the Egyptians, Assyrians, Babylonians, He-

brews, and other peoples who lived in southwestern Asia all belonged to the Semitic group, the Persians belonged to the Indo- European peoples. This is the race from which most Europeans are descended, and the one to which most Americans belong. 

So far as we know, the Indo-European peoples came from the region east and north of the Caspian Sea. From there they migrated westward into Europe and southward into Persia and India. As they took 

As they took posses-

This boy lives in a village in the northern part of Persia. He is studying the Koran, which once was about the only subject taught in his school. Notice the turbanlike headdress. Semitic people, who had migrated westward along the African shores. Some of the greatest wars in history, such as the wars between 

Rome and Carthage, between the Spaniards and the Moors, have been struggles between these two groups for supremacy in the Mediterranean lands.

History of Persia. In ancient times the 

Persians ruled practically all of southwestern

Asia from India to the Aegean Sea, and for a while controlled Egypt.

Cyrus the Great captured the richest king of ancient times, King Croesus of Lydia. 

And it was Cyrus who, after conquering the Babylonian king, allowed the captive Hebrews to return to Jerusalem and rebuild their temple. 

After Cyrus came Darius and his famous son, Xerxes, both of whom were defeated by the Greeks one at Marathon, 490 B.C. and the other at Thermopylae, 480 B.C.when they attempted to extend their conquests into Europe. Darius was a great organizer, who divided the country into provinces, each with its own ruler, or satrap, controlled by the king. So after all it was a one-man government. A few good roads were built and communication between sections was carried on by messengers. 

Darius caused a record of his achievements to be inscribed on a high smooth-faced cliff known as the Behistun Rock.

Under the reign of the kings succeeding Darius and Xerxes, Persia gradually weakened, and finally yielded to the Greek conqueror, Alexander the Great (333 B.C.).

After his death, the Greeks lost control to the Parthians, but four centuries later, Persian rule was again restored. Then, in the

Middle Ages, Arab Mohammedans and later the Turks overran the country. Turkish rule was overthrown by the mighty Genghis Khan, a Mongolian conqueror, and it was not until 1500 that a new Persian ruler named Ismael arose and overcame the Mongols. 

Ismael is looked upon as the founder of modern Persia. With his reign, Persia became independent, but the autocratic and inefficient rule of the shahs, as the Persian kings are called, combined with the lack of education and the unprogressive nature of the people, did not allow much progress. 

Influence of European Powers in Persian 

Affairs. Persia lies between Russia and the Persian Gulf. If Russia could gain control of Persia, she would have a much wanted outlet on the sea (map, pages 16-17). Great 

Britain opposed Russian domination, first, because it might affect her control in India,

ARABIA AND ITS PEOPLE 

and second, because she wanted a share of 

Persia's oil. So in 1907 Russia and Great

Britain came to an agreement by which Russian interests in Persia were to be confined to the northern part of the country, and those of Great Britain to the southern. This agreement came to a close with the Russian 

Revolution. Great Britain then made an agreement with Persia by which British advisers were to have a considerable part in the government and military affairs. Soon

The Rise of a New Persian Leader. 

after the Anglo-Persian agreement was made, a new Persian leader arose who was destined to make great changes in the government of his country. This was Reza Khan, who commanded a Cossack force in the Persian army. 

In 1924, at the head of his small command, he marched into Tehran, the capital, during the night and occupied the government offices before dawn. He met little resistance and everything was quietly and quickly done.

Reza had himself appointed commander-inchief of the army. From that time on, he rose steadily in the control of the government until finally the heir to the throne was deposed and Reza Khan received the crown, taking the name of Reza Shah Pahlevi. 

Reforms of Reza Shah Pahlevi. Like 

Kemal in Turkey, Reza was determined to end the control exercised by western nations and make his country independent. He reorganized the army, and made provision for the pay and maintenance of the soldiers so that they would be loyal and efficient aids.

With this army he put down rebellions in various parts of the country and brought all sections under the control of the national government. 

American Advisers in Persia. — American advisers under the leadership of Dr. A. C. 

Millspaugh were asked to come and take charge of the financial affairs of Persia. Under their control, taxes were distributed more 11 justly. New means of raising money were devised, and certain sums were set aside for governmental expenses, for the army, for education, and for public improvements, especially much needed roads and railroads. The budget was arranged so that receipts would be slightly larger than expenses, and in this way the large public debt is being reduced. 

The Future of Persia. You may stand in 

Persia to-day and gaze skyward at the airplane - the symbol of the new order - while against the sky on the distant horizon perhaps you will see the silhouette of a camel caravan wending its way slowly across the desert the symbol of the old order. The Persia which Reza Shah Pahlevi took hold of was little changed from the Persia of the days of Darius. However, under this vigorous dictator much progress has been made, though not so much or so varied as in Turkey. A railroad between the Caspian Sea and the

Persian Gulf was completed in 1938, and other railroads are planned. The Shah has made many treaties; to strengthen his position, in 1939 he arranged the marriage of his eldest son to the sister of Egypt's king.

C. ARABIA 

Arabia and Its People. - Arabia is the name given to the large peninsula at the southwestern extremity of Asia. You may get some idea of its size when you consider that if its northern boundary were laid on the northern

MASQAT 

Ewing Galloway

This picturesque city is the capital of the independent state of Oman. Overshadowed as it is by steep and rocky surroundings, it is one of the hottest cities in the world, and few white people can stand its temperature. Most of these large buildings are palaces with courtyards and harems. As a seaport, Masqat is noted for the export of dates, motherof-pearl, Arabian horses, and wheat. boundary of the United States in Minnesota, the southern end of the peninsula would reach to the southern part of Texas, a distance of about 1500 miles. Its average width is about one-half of its length. Multiply 1500 by 750 and you will have very nearly the correct area for this huge peninsula, which supports an estimated population of only 10,000,000. 

The interior is a desert highland running almost the entire length of the country. 

Scattered oases in the valleys leading up to the central highland are inhabited by groups of Arab tribes. Some of these are quite peaceful, but in general they are noted for their fierce fanatical nature and their many feuds. 

Along the western coast, the front of this plateau comes close to the Red Sea and is bordered by high mountains. Toward the east the land slopes gently to a low marshy plain.

In the summer, winds from the southwest. bring a good rainfall to high southwestern Arabia.

The Arabian desert tribes have always kept strangers out of their land, and it is only within recent years that daring white explorers have been able to penetrate this little-known country and learn something about it. 

Contrary to former beliefs, the Arabs have some large settlements in the heart of the peninsula. Riyadh, the interior capital, has over 12,000 inhabitants. Houses of sun-dried bricks, rise several stories high in some interior cities of Arabia. What a calamity a heavy rainfall would be to such a city!

Most of the lowlands on the borders of Arabia receive some rainfall, and the inhabitants lead a settled life and raise agricultural products. When food grew scarce in the interior, some of the nomadic tribes used to

BORDERING KINGDOMS OF ARABIA 

have a habit of raiding border settlements and carrying off food supplies and anything else that they desired. The people of the settled districts lived in terror of these marauders whom nobody could catch and punish. 

Although the tribesmen have no scruples about robbing or even killing others, the individual members have a strict code of honor among themselves. 

They are Mohammedans in belief, and are most hospitable to those whom they choose to entertain, but fierce and cruel to those against whom their religious frenzy is aroused. 13 of the Persian Gulf. The boundary lines between these border kingdoms and the interior are not well established.

The largest Arab kingdom is Saudi-Arabia, which includes the Hejaz. The Hejaz is interesting to the rest of the Mohammedan world because it contains their two holy cities

WAHABI TRIBESMAN OF ARABIA 

The Wahabis, who live in the interior of Arabia, are the strongest people of the Arab 

Most of interior Arabia is ruled by Ibn Saud, Wahabi leader who is now king of Saudi Arabia.

Many of the Arabs would like to see all of the Arab states united in one large state or federation. 

This would include Iraq, Syria, and Palestine as well as all of the 

Arabian Peninsula-a total area half as large as the United States.

Bordering Kingdoms of 

Arabia. — Along the coastal lands of Arabia, several Arab kingdoms have been set up under the oversight of Great Britain.

The necessity of guarding her commerce against Arab pirates, particularly along the 

Persian Gulf, has forced Great Britain to take some part in the control of these coastal regions. tribes. Although they follow the 

Koran with almost fanatical zeal, they do not hesitate to waylay and rob pilgrims on their way to Mecca, and to make themselves feared and hated for their raids on border settlements. 

Three of these kingdoms, Hejaz, Asir, and 

Yemen, border on the Red Sea. Oman occupies the southeastern corner of the peninsula, and Koweit lies farther north along the Persian Gulf.

The part of Arabia which borders on the 

Persian Gulf between Oman and Koweit is called El Haza. This region was so infested with pirates that Great Britain had to take charge and stop their preying on the commerce of Mecca and Medina. Mecca was the birthplace of Mohammed, and Medina contains a mosque sheltering his tomb. It is the ambition of every devout 

Mohammedan to make at least one pilgrimage during his life to the tomb of Mohammed. As many as 200,000 pilgrims have been known to visit his shrine in one year. 

Pilgrims to Medina may now reach that city either by the railroad between Damascus and 

Medina, or by motor busses which run between Medina and 

Nejef on the Euphrates River. 

Mecca, farther farther south south than

Medina, may be reached by bus from the port of Jidda. Formerly this long journey all had to be made by camel. Usually it was made in caravans for the sake of protection against the nomadic Arab tribes who frequently waylaid and robbed small groups of pilgrims. 

Yemen, along the southwestern coast, contains a plateau region which is one of the most fertile parts of Arabia. Here is grown the celebrated Mocha coffee, so named after the seaport of Mocha from which most of it was formerly exported. It is now largely exported from Aden.

The rainfall here is light, but heavy mists rising from the Red Sea serve to shield the coffee plants from the hot rays of the sun and also furnish the plant with moisture. Most of the coffee is bought by Turkish merchants.

Dates are the most important product of all these countries, but some wheat and barley are grown. Masqat in Oman is the leading seaport for the export of dates. Some hides and wool are also exported. The finest camels in Arabia are bred in the interior of Oman. 

Koweit, near the head of the Persian Gulf, raises and exports horses.

Aden. Aden, a small peninsula on the southwestern coast of Arabia, is controlled by the British, together with a small surrounding area. The city of Aden is the chief seaport of 

Arabia, and the leading commercial center for the distribution of goods from Ethiopia and other African countries in this region. It is also an important coaling station for steamships using the Mediterranean route to the 

Far East.

The Tigris and the Euphrates both have their sources in high mountains to the north of Iraq, where a rather heavy rainfall together with melting snows in the springtime insure a good water supply.

Like the Nile, the Tigris and the Euphrates in their lower courses flow through an arid country where crops cannot be grown without irrigation. During the flood season these rivers overflow their banks and convert the adjoining lowlands into huge swamps, similar to those formerly found on the Nile delta. 

Modern Iraq. The land of Iraq lies mostly in a broad flat valley, extending for about five hundred miles northwest of the Persian Gulf. D. IRAQ Iraq Compared with Egypt.- Iraq is the new name for the region formerly called Mesopotamia. As Egypt is the land of one river, the Nile, so Iraq is the land of two rivers, the Tigris and the Euphrates. The former name, Mesopotamia, means "between rivers (map, pages 16-17). 99 Egypt and Iraq are alike in many respects. 

Both countries lie along river valleys and are largely dependent on the rivers for both water upland and extended the land far out into the gulf.

As one travels through Iraq to-day, he does not see as much cultivated land as existed in ancient times. After the Turks conquered it in the seventh century, the canals, which had been constructed with such great care and labor by the Babylonians, were either destroyed or allowed to fall into ruin. Consequently much of this country has become an unhealthful swamp, a breeding place for mosquitoes and other disease-carrying insects.

North of Mosul, the low plains of the valley merge into the foothills of the mountains to the north and east. This is largely a stony, arid waste, quite different from the fertile. river plains farther south. 

In the Treaty of Lausanne in 1920, Turkey surrendered her control over this country and Great Britain was given a mandate over In 1923 an independent kingdom was it.

CITIES AND TRANSPORTATION IN IRAQ 

. 19 

BAGHDAD, LAND OF ARABIAN NIGHTS 

Arab herdsmen are driving their flocks of sheep and goats along the stony road that leads to the tomb of Zebadiah. 

Once the city of Caliphs, the old town has given way to a newer city of modern buildings and streets. formed, and the British forces withdrew.

According to the Treaty of Iraq with Great Britain, the King of Iraq is to be guided by the advice of the King of England in international affairs and financial matters. 

Agriculture Possibilities of Iraq. - Engi- 

Engi- neers claim that 7,000,000 acres adjacent to the rivers, an area larger than Massachusetts, could be irrigated in winter, and about half that much in the summer time if the flood waters were controlled. The land is very fertile and could be made to produce large crops of wheat, barley, rice, millet, and cotton. 

All of these are now grown to some extent. 

Tobacco is raised in some of the hilly sections adjoining the lowlands.

As in the Arabian peninsula to the south, dates are a very important crop. On both sides of the Shatt-el-Arab for about 100 miles from its mouth is a continuous date grove.

Dates are one of the chief foods of the inhabitants and are also a leading export.

Among the foothills in the north, flocks of sheep are plentiful. Both wool and hides are exported. 

Cities and Transportation. The chief seaport of Iraq is Basra, on the Shatt-el-Arab, about seventy miles from the Persian Gulf. 

Steamers ply the Tigris between Basra and 

Baghdad, and during the flood season they go far up the Euphrates.

Baghdad is a quaint old city which in the Middle Ages became a noted center for caravan trade in this part of Asia. It is now the capital and chief city of Iraq. Like most

Oriental cities, it is much more attractive from a distance than close at hand. 

On the upper stretches of the Tigris and

Euphrates, much transportation is carried on in peculiar round boats. These are woven by the natives from willow branches, and coated inside and out with tar or pitch to make them waterproof. Rafts, supported on inflated skins of animals, are also much used for river traffic.

The Ancient Babylonian Civilization. 

Like Egypt, this land was the home of a very ancient civilization. Four great peoples, the 

Sumerians, the Babylonians, the Assyrians, and the Chaldeans in turn controlled this region from more than 3000 years B.C. to about 550 B.C.

The two most famous empires were the Babylonian and the Assyrian. The seat of the Babylonian Empire was at Babylon, southwest of the present city of Baghdad, while the Assyrians had their center of government at Nineveh, near the modern city of 

Mosul. A powerful Chaldean king, Nebuchadnezzar, also had Babylon as his capital.

The ruins of both these cities have been uncovered, and clay tablets on which were recorded different facts about the customs and laws of these ancient inhabitants have enabled us to learn considerable about them and their society.

Clay tablet invoices used in trading spread the peculiar wedge or cuneiform writing of the Babylonians through western Asia.

Messages were also written upon clay tablets and sent by messengers. Clay tablets have thus been the "books" as well as the "letters" revealing Babylonian history.

Another very interesting find was a stone shaft, upon which was inscribed a code of laws compiled by King Hammurabi about 2100 B.C. This is the oldest set of ancient laws which has been found. 

The ruins in this valley, however, have not been so well preserved as those in Egypt. 

The people here did not build lasting stone monuments for their dead as did the Egyptians. They did build some imposing palaces, and some of the remnants of these have been unearthed. But they were built of brick, most of which has crumbled into dust. One of their contributions to architecture was the arch, which was a prominent feature of the palace entrance. 

Another factor in the destruction of the ancient monuments is the slightly heavier rainfall. From ten to twenty inches fall in some parts of the valley, while it is less than ten inches in the Egyptian valley. Even stone structures are much affected by moisture in the air, and decay much more quickly in a damp climate than in a dry one. 

Why Early Civilization Developed along 

Rivers. Perhaps you are wondering why these ancient civilizations developed along rivers. First of all the rivers furnished a ready means of transportation and communication. They also furnished water for irrigation, drinking, cooking, bathing, and other purposes. 

Another reason is that the soil of flood plains is usually fertile and this was especially true of the soil in both the valley of the Tigris and Euphrates, as well as that of the Nile.

The ancient Babylonians practiced irrigation and cultivated the valley lowlands so that they were like a large garden. The Garden of Eden referred to in the Bible is thought by some scholars to have been at the ancient junction of the Tigris and Euphrates. 66 At one time, this area produced so much grain that, like Egypt, it was named the 

'Granary of the World." The stalks of wheat and other plants grew unusually large. Fruit was plentiful, and fish abounded in the rivers. 

Why Great Britain Is Interested in Iraq. — 

Great Britain is interested in Iraq for several reasons. One is that it could be made to produce crops of cereals and cotton, both of which are needed by Great Britain. Another is the known presence of enormous quantities of petroleum in the country around Mosul.

THE LAND OF PALESTINE 

21 Petroleum is very much in demand now as a source of power both on land and water. 

Naturally Great Britain, France, and our own country have become rivals for the privilege of developing and using this resource of Iraq. 

In 1935, a pipe line 1150 miles long was completed from the Iraq oil fields to the Mediterranean Sea at Haifa in Palestine and Tripoli in Syria.

Another interest of Great Britain is that the country is an overland route to India. A section of the famous Berlin-to-Baghdad railroad, running through Mosul and Baghdad to Basra in the south, lies in this country and is much used for transportation. Great Britain has a controlling interest in this railroad. Baghdad is a motor bus terminal and also has a modern airport used by transcontinental air lines flying planes between Asia and Europe. 

Naturally Great Britain does not wish Iraq to fall into the hands of any nation who might interfere with her control in India or her commerce with that country. 

The Future of Iraq. As you will now realize, the future of this little country will depend largely on how far the people can improve their irrigation systems and make their country more productive, as well as a more healthful place to live.

In area Iraq is not small, having over 143,000 square miles, but the population numbers less than 3,000,000. The people are nearly all Mohammedans. Their country is unbearably hot during the summer season, and the people have many hard problems to solve before the land can be made to bloom as it did in ancient times. Much will depend upon their leaders and the coöperation of other nations with whom they trade.

E. PALESTINE 

Blest land of Judea! thrice hallowed of song, Where the holiest of memories pilgrim-like throng; 

In the shade of thy palms, by the shores of thy sea, On the hills of thy beauty, my heart is with thee. 

- JOHN GREENLEAF WHITTIER Palestine, the Birthplace of Two Religions. 

-Palestine, often called the Holy Land, is of special interest to both Hebrews and Christians, for both religions had their origin in this little country. The idea of one god, as opposed to many gods, first reached its highest conception in the moral law laid down by the 

Hebrew prophets in Palestine. 

In the town of Bethlehem was born Jesus, the founder of Christianity, whose teachings were to spread to the furthermost parts of the world and become an everlasting influence for good among all peoples. The Bible, containing both the Hebrew and the Christian teachings, is the contribution of ancient Palestine to civilization. Do you wonder that both Hebrews and Christians take such a deep interest in this country and rejoiced when it was wrested from Turkish control? 

Have you ever read about the Crusades? If not, look them up and report what their object was. 

The Land of Palestine. - Palestine is a small country on the eastern Mediterranean coast about the size of Massachusetts. It lies between Egypt to the south and west and 1. Make a list of the ways in which Iraq resembles Syria to the north. On the east is the new Egypt. 

2. Why is Mesopotamia, now called Iraq, of particular interest to students of ancient history? country of Transjordan. 

Along the Mediterranean border of Palestine is a low coastal plain, the southern part

AN ANCIENT CALLING Publishers' Photo Service 

This is the coast of Palestine. It was in a group like this that Jesus saw Simon Peter and Andrew his brother preparing their nets. of which was the land of the Philistines of Bible times. Back of this is a hilly region leading up to a low mountainous ridge running north and south parallel to the coast. 

Jerusalem, the present capital of Palestine, is situated on this ridge at an elevation of half a mile above sea level. It is connected by rail with the seaport of Jaffa..

The eastern slope of this highland descends rather steeply to the valley of the Jordan and the Dead Sea. Throughout its whole length in Palestine, the Jordan Valley is below sea level (map, pages 16-17). Most valleys are cut by streams, but this valley is due to a section of the earth's crust having dropped. The 

Dead Sea lies 1292 feet below the Mediterranean. From the Sea of Galilee 65 miles to the north to the Dead Sea runs the Jordan 

River. The Sea of Galilee is only 682 feet below sea level, so between this sea and the 

Dead Sea there is a drop of 610 feet.

A large power plant near the Sea of Galilee uses the water power furnished by the fall of the Jordan to produce electricity. This electricity is transported to the cities, where it is used for lighting and other purposes. 

Some of the railroads have also been electrified.

The Dead Sea. - The Dead Sea is the saltiest lake in the world. Fully one-fourth of the total weight of the water is in salts of various kinds. The Dead Sea has no outlet, and for thousands of years the Jordan and other contributing streams have been pouring their waters containing small quantities of salts into this sea. The water has evaporated while the salts have remained and accumulated. If you were to go bathing in this water, floating would be easy. In fact, you could not sink. Only small forms of life can exist in this sea, hence its name. 66 A newspaper article published in November, 1927, bears this title, "The Dead Sea Guards a Colossal Fortune." How can that be?" you ask. It has been estimated that the value of the dissolved salts in the Dead Sea is

AGRICULTURAL PRODUCTS OF PALESTINE 

23 over one trillion dollars. Can you imagine such a huge sum of money?

Salt, such as we use in our food, soda, potash, and a very useful but unpleasant liquor called bromine - all of these chemicals are being extracted from these salty waters. The most valuable of all is the potash which is so much in demand for use as a fertilizer. Some one has estimated that there is enough potash in the Dead Sea to supply the world for four hundred years. 

And all of these can be sold at a lower price than such products usually bring because the hot sun and the dry air of this region do much of the work of extracting the salts. The water from the sea is simply placed in large shallow pans exposed to the sun, and the different salts are precipitated in turn to the bottoms of these pans as the water evaporates.

The big problem is the transportation of the products to market. The electric power now being made available will be of great help in this work. — - Climate and Soil. Palestine has the 

Mediterranean climate, which means that rain 

MARKET PLACE IN BETHLEHEM 

Ewing Galloway

The place on which the Christmas star shone with so radiant a light is still a little village, about five miles from Jerusalem, with but one street of low, flat-roofed houses. Up and down the street, merchants display their wares in open market, for the air is mild and the sky blue and cloudless.

Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction STREET IN TIBERIAS 

Once there were nine flourishing cities on the shores of the Sea of Galilee now only two remain as reminders of a time long past. Tiberias huddles on the west shore, as if the hills of Galilee in their sharp descent to meet the sea were shoving and jostling it into the historic waters. comes in the winter season from November to

May and the summers are dry. Irrigation is necessary for the most successful agriculture, though considerable dry farming is carried on. 

The climate varies according to the relief. 

The top of Mount Hermon, just north of the boundary of Palestine, is covered with snow in the winter time. On the other hand, in the valley of the Jordan, tropical plants can be grown.

The rocks of Palestine are largely of limestone. They weather into a rich soil which holds the water remarkably well during the dry season. This enables much dry farming to be carried on and helps the trees to survive during the period when no rain falls.

Palestine also has a good supply of underground water, which can be tapped by means of wells and used for irrigation as well as for household purposes. 

Agricultural Products. A great variety of agricultural products can be grown in Palestine. On the coastal plain, wheat, barley, oats, figs, olives, apricots, and almonds do well.

These crops can also be produced on some of the highlands where they mature a little later

24 

Ewing Galloway

WATERMELON MARKET AT JERUSALEM 

Outside of the old city walls, with their eight gates, has grown also a newer Jerusalem than the one of Biblical fame. But its sunshine is still warm and golden, and, aided by modern irrigation, it is a place where abundant fruit and vegetables grow. What a luscious crop of watermelons this appears to be! in the season. 

Excellent oranges, dates, grapes, and other fruits are grown near Jaffa,

The Jordan Valley has been described as a tropical oasis in a temperate climate. Under irrigation, vegetation here is very luxuriant. 

Ten crops of alfalfa can be grown in one season, as the growing season lasts throughout the year. Bananas, oranges, sugar cane, cotton, and semi-tropical fruits, as well as cereals, can all be grown here. The Sea of Galilee furnishes abundant water for irrigation. 

Reclamation of Swamps. One of the worst features of certain parts of Palestine has been marshlands. These places harbored swarms of mosquitoes which spread the dread disease of malaria among the people. 

One method used in draining the swamps has been to plant large numbers of eucalyptus trees. These trees grow rapidly and suck up a good deal of water, so they have been most effective aids in drying up the unhealthful lowlands. The trees also furnish a wood which can be used for a variety of purposes and a useful oil.

Manufactures in Palestine. - A number of manufacturing industries have been started in Palestine. These are on a small scale and use local products. Soap, olive and castor oil, salt, candies, cigars, perfumes, flour, leather, textiles, bricks, and other building materials are some of the manufactured products. 

Palestine lacks coal, and there is little fuel of any kind. When electric power is available from the power plant in the Jordan Valley, more manufacturing can be carried on. 

Modern Improvements. - The British have introduced many changes in Palestine. 

Schools have been established, and an effort is being made to educate all the people. 

Under Turkish rule, education as well as industry of all kinds, was neglected. Now good roads and railroads are being built and automobiles are in common use.

A department of agriculture has been established, agricultural machinery is being introduced, and loans are extended to the farmers. Goats have been barred from the hillsides so as to give the trees a chance to grow. Under Turkish rule the unreasonably heavy taxes discouraged agriculture.

Jerusalem is being transformed. New and

This is a view of Haifa, one of the principal seaports of Palestine, as seen from the Bay of Acre against the background of Mount Carmel. Mount Carmel in Palestine is a ridge from the interior highlands which here extends to the coast. A breakwater has been recently built at Haifa, so that ships are not obliged to anchor far from shore.

THE ZIONIST MOVEMENT 

25 

CHILDREN IN THE NEW PALESTINE 

Ewing Galloway

New Jerusalem has already progressed far in modern ideas and Western customs. The Zionist schools in the 

Holy City are attended by happy, well-dressed, and wellfed children. modern residences have been built. A good water supply has been provided, and electric light and power are now available.

History of the Hebrews. - The Hebrews were originally a nomadic people of the Semitic group wandering about with their flocks on the edges of the Arabian desert. As a result of a famine, they settled in Egypt, where they were enslaved by the Pharaohs.

Their great leader, Moses, delivered them from this condition by leading them out of Egypt into Palestine. The need for a common defense against their enemies caused the scattered tribes to unite. For the only time in its history, a strong, wealthy nation arose in this land under King David and his son Solomon. At this period, about 1000 B. C., both Egypt and Babylonia had weakened. 

After the death of King Solomon, the Hebrews were conquered and taken to 

Babylon as captives. Later some of them were allowed to return, but never again did these people have a land of their own. 

When the Romans conquered Palestine, the Jews were again scattered and never since then have they controlled the country. 

The Zionist Movement. The Zionist 

Movement is a plan to colonize Palestine with Jewish settlers. When the British assumed control of Palestine after the World War, they promised to favor this plan. What seems more natural than that the Jews should control the land which they held in ancient times? 

But there are many difficulties in the way of carrying out such a plan. Most of the people of Palestine are Arabs, who object to the influx of Jews. The result has been friction and bloodshed, and England has had to use troops to keep order. Many conferences have been held, but as late as 1939 no satisfactory solution had been found.

Naturally the people already in possession of the land do not wish to give it up or be ruled by some other people. So new settlements must be made largely on land not now in use, such as marshes and other waste land which can be drained or irrigated, and such land as the present owners can spare to sell to the newcomers.

It has been estimated that Palestine would probably support about 250,000 additional people. This is a very small proportion of the Jews in the world-about 16,000,000. 

The new Jewish settlements in Palestine present quite a contrast to the older Arab villages. Jaffa, with a population of about 

Publishers' Photo Service THE SHIP OF THE DRY DESERT 

Near Acre, where years ago echoed the battle cries of the Crusaders and their Saracen foes, camels still carry their burdens across the wind-swept sands. The day is not far distant, however, when automobiles and trucks will displace these faithful carriers of many centuries.

47,000 people, has narrow, dirty streets, unlighted and disorderly. Tel-a-viv, the 

Jewish suburb, has wide paved streets, with sewers, electric lights, and other modern improvements.

The future prosperity of Palestine appears to lie in the development of her mineral resources, and a more intensive use of her agricultural resources. Will the Zionist Movement succeed? Will Palestine again become a "land flowing with milk and honey" as in 

Bible times? 

Transjordan. Transjordan, as its name suggests, is across the Jordan from Palestine. 

It was included in the Palestine mandate and is ruled by a native prince and ministry under the guidance of the British. It is an Arab country, inhabited largely by nomads. 

About half of the people live in villages. The population is estimated to be about 300,000. 

The Hejaz Railroad runs through the country from north to south, while an automobile road connects the capital, Amman, with Jerusalem. The British air route from 

Jerusalem to Baghdad also crosses the country, and the British have built an aërodrome at Amman, where they keep an air force to aid in controlling the country. 

Except for a narrow strip about 30 miles wide lying between the Jordan Valley and the Hejaz Railway, the country is mostly desert. 

Where there is a sufficient water supply, the fertile areas are cultivated, two of the leading crops being grapes and wheat. Cattle raising is an important industry, the nomadic tribes especially being largely dependent on the animal industry. As in Palestine, the extraction of salt, potash, and phosphate from the waters of the Dead Sea promises to become an important industry.

F. SYRIA 

Now, upon Syria's land of roses 

Softly the light of eve reposes, And, like a glory, the broad sun 

Hangs over sainted Lebanon; 

Whose head in wintry grandeur towers, And whitens with eternal sleet,

While summer, in a vale of flowers, Is sleeping rosy at his feet. 

- THOMAS MOORE 

French Rule in Syria. - Syria, formerly a part of the Turkish Empire, was established as a separate country and placed under a French mandate at the close of the World War. France has been interested in this country on the eastern coast of the Mediterranean for several hundred years. In the time of the Phoenicians, Marseille traded with

Syria, and this trade has continued ever since. 

The French took an active part in the Crusades, and afterwards French princes ruled in Syria. French schools in Syria have already had a considerable influence in education and in introducing scientific methods in agriculture. The intention now is to educate the 

Syrians in self-government and then grant them independence.

Area and Population. This little country, with an estimated area of about 60,000 square miles, about the size of Georgia, has a little

AREA AND POPULATION OF SYRIA DAMASCUS (DAMAS) 

27 27 

Ewing Galloway

This famous old trading center, on the border of the Syrian Desert, lays claim to being the oldest city in the world and remains to-day the most oriental of all Eastern cities. It was so beautiful that Mohammed feared to go there lest he might prefer it to Heaven. Judas once lived on its "Street called Straight." The houses on the outside are gloomy, but inside the courtyards gleam with marble, and blossom with trees and shrubs and plants, while shade and cooling fountains tempt the visitor to rest. Under the roofed arcades, the little bazaars display their strange and attractive wares. less than 3,000,000 people. Most of the people are Mohammedans, the rest being largely Christians and Jews. Bedouin nomads make up about one-sixth of the total population. These inhabit the steppe regions east of the mountains and some of the drier sections of Syria, driving their flocks about from place to place according to the water supply, and coming into the towns to trade. 

Like the gypsies, they are picturesque and interesting, but their life is not particularly inviting to people used to the comforts of 

Western civilization. 

Meeting Place of the Continents. - Lo cated at the meeting place of Europe, Asia, and Africa, Syria has sometimes been called the "crossroads of the continents." In times of war, it has been a battleground between its powerful neighbors; in times of peace it is a highway, both east and west, and north and south. The railroad now follows the old caravan route across northern Syria, and the routes leading southward through Palestine into Egypt and through Transjordan into the Hejaz. The camel, long the only means of transportation across the desert, is now being displaced by the automobile. Motor busses now run between Beirut, the chief seaport of 

Syria, and Baghdad. All of the railroads in

Syria except one line were built by the French.

1. Locate four deserts in Asia. What can you say of the rainfall in these desert regions during both the summer and winter seasons? 

2. What three large islands of the East Indies are crossed by the equator? What one lies wholly south of the equator? Compare the summer and winter temperatures of these islands. Also the compare summer and winter rainfall.

Is there much difference BEIRUT The Land of Syria. - Syria lies between the sea on the west and the desert on the east. 

The western part is mountainous, the most rugged mountains being found in the southwestern part of the country. These are known as the Lebanon. Some of the higher portions reach an altitude of 10,000 feet. East of this range and separated from it by a long, narrow valley are some lower ranges called the 

Anti-Lebanon Mountains.

High mountain ridges extending to the coast, with deep gorges between, make overland travel north and south very difficult. Passes provide east and west routes across the mountains. For people on the western slopes, the sea has always been the main highway. With the desert behind, and the sea in front, nearly always in sight from Amid the terraced slopes of Mt. Lebanon flourishes the city of Beirut, capital of Great Lebanon and chief seaport of Syria. In its cultivated gardens grow olives, cereals, and tobacco, for export in the many ships sailing from its harbor. in either case? What reasons can you give for this? 

3. Southeastern Asia has the monsoon type of climate. What is meant by that? When does this region have most of its rainfall? In relation to the land and water, in what direction do the winds blow during the summer season? during the winter season? 

How can you tell this from the map? What does this have to do with the rainfall? 

4. Compare the summer and winter temperatures in the Desert of Gobi. How do you explain the great difference? 

5. Where is the hottest area in Asia during the summer season? Is this a dry region or does it have abundant rainfall? 

6. Why is there a heavy rainfall on the southern slopes of the Himalayas during the summer season but scarcely any rainfall on the northern slopes? 

7. What are the tundras? Where in Asia are they found? Do they have a heavy or light rainfall? (About ten inches of snow are considered to be equal to one inch of rain.) 

James Sawders their homes, is it any wonder that the early Phoenicians who lived here became famous as sailors and traders in the 

Mediterranean region?

Climate. Western Syria has the Mediter ranean type of climate, with mild, rainy winters and warm, dry summers. It is well watered, while eastern Syria has a light rainfall and gradually merges into the desert. 

The higher parts of the Lebanon Mountains have 45 inches of rainfall during the year, while Damascus, east of the mountains at the edge of the desert, has only about 12 inches.

As the winds are generally from the west, the western slopes of the mountains receive the most rainfall, while the eastern slopes have very little rainfall. Practically no rain falls during the summer months, so irrigation is necessary then, even in the western sections, for the cultivation of crops. 

Agriculture. Agriculture is carried on almost entirely in the valleys and on the low..

AMERICAN INTEREST IN SYRIA 

lands where the soil is fertile and it is possible to practice irrigation. Some of the western slopes have been terraced and planted with mulberry trees and grape vines. Wine and silk are two leading products of this region. 

Silk mills were established by the French and the products of these are sent largely to France. Mulberry leaves are fed not only to the silkworms but to the livestock, which are raised in considerable numbers. Sheep's butter, wool and lambskins, and leather are derived from the animal industry. Tobacco, olives, oranges, lemons, and cotton are important crops. 

Trade Centers. - Aleppo in the north and

Damascus in the south are the two greatest trade centers in Syria. Damascus, one of the oldest cities in the world, has been a famous center for the desert trade since the beginning of history. Beirut is the only seaport of importance. 

American Interest in Syria. - Americans have a particular interest in Syria because thousands of Syrians have emigrated to this country. The Syrians, as a people, are born traders, and those who live in America have built up a lively trade with their home country. Silks, laces, and embroideries are 

Syrian specialties. Americans also conduct a university at Beirut. It has over 1000 students, including people of many nationalities and religions.

THE GREAT WALL OF CHINA Publishers' Photo Service 

The Great Wall of China was built over 2000 years ago: a line of defense that winds over mountains and through valleys for 1800 miles. It has seen the sun rise and set on many dynasties and still stands as one of man's most stupendous works.

UNIT II.  A. CHINA 

Land of wonders, fair Cathay, Who long hast shunned the staring day, Hid in mists of poet's dreams 

By the blue and yellow streams, Let us thy shadowed form behold, Teach us as thou didst of old. 

OLIVER WENDELL HOLMES 

For several years past, our newspapers and magazines have been filled with news from 

China. We come upon these headlines: "China's War Spreads along the Yangtze," 

"The Leaders and the Battle Lines in 

China," "Chinese Missions Face Their 

Supreme Crisis," "The Drama of China," "China's Challenge to the West," and many others. Look at your daily newspaper to find out the latest developments in this 

Oriental country. 

What is happening in this far-away land? 

What are its resources? Do American school children have any wrong notions about China and the Chinese? Let us see if we can find answers to some of these questions. 

Wrong Notions about China. In our study of the nations of the world, we should try to understand the people and their problems from their point of view. We ought to have a sympathetic attitude toward them, rather than condemn them through ignorance because they do not happen always to have our ways. Each nation has something to give to and to learn from every other nation if we will only strive to find out what it is. 32

Ewing Galloway

46 

Ewing Galloway

PALACE OF THE DALAI LAMA, LASSA, TIBET 

On the roof of the world" lies Tibet: a country of little rain, few inhabitants, and fervent religion, for long a "forbidden land" to visitors. On a high hill is the palace of the Dalai Lama, the religious head of Tibet. 

First, let us correct a few wrong impressions.

The favorite delicacies of the Chinese are not rats and snakes. They eat other things than rice. All the people are not laundrymen.

All Chinese do not look alike. All Chinese are not cunning and crafty. They have souls even if they are not all Christians. They are not a mysterious and inscrutable race and they do not do everything backwards. We should not think of them as queer, but only different. 

Antiquity of the Chinese Civilization. - 

China has one of the oldest civilizations in the world, dating back at least 5000 years. She shares this distinction along with Egypt, Mesopotamia, and India. Other nations have risen and fallen, but China seems to go on forever. At times China has been ruled by her own people, the Chinese proper. At other times foreigners have reigned, such as the Mongolians and Manchurians. Through it all, these patient, plodding farmers have gone on tilling the soil, increasing in numbers, and absorbing their conquerors. Surely we must admire such a sturdy people, who are able to persist in spite of famine, pestilence, overcrowding, and the rule of alien peoples. 

How Large Is China? - The Chinese Re public constitutes one of the great land areas of the earth, being considerably larger than the United States. This area is divided into four provinces: China proper with an area over one-third of the whole, Tibet, Sinkiang, or Eastern Turkestan, and Mongolia (map, page 2).

The population has been estimated to be over 400,000,000, or one-fourth of the people of the entire earth. Most of these people are to be found in China proper with an area about one-half that of our own United States.

What an enormous number of people to live on such a small area! you say. Certainly exclamation is warranted. If the whole your

United States were as densely populated as 

China proper, there would be 800,000,000 people in this "land of the free and home of the brave"! Do you think it would then be a land of the free? 

RICE TERRACES IN CHINA 

Ewing Galloway

Like great stairways up the hillsides lie terraced rice gardens, the source of food of China's hundreds of millions. 

With unremitting labor, they are planted, irrigated, and made to produce a striking example of intensive farming.

LIFE ON THE WATER IN CHINA 

Swarms of Chinese are born and live and die on the waterways of China. Above, on the left, is a sampan, a kind of one-room house boat, with sail and oars. In the picture on the right, above, is a Chinese child, who probably seldom goes ashore, but lives his days within a stone's throw of the river bank. He always wears a life preserver strapped to his back so he will not drown if he falls overboard. Poor roads, few railroads, and fewer automobiles have brought about a network of canals and rivers forming the great highways of commerce. The view of Soochow Creek at Shanghai (lower) is typical of the busy waterway and the crowded shores. 35

CURIOS Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction TIBETAN CURIOS 

Amid the low, flat roofs of chimneyless houses, with their paper windows, blossom the bazaars, where famous Tibetan jewelry can be bought and silks, rugs, shawis, and tapestries have been brought by long caravan trains. 

China proper occupies an area in the southeastern part of the country which naturally must be fertile and well-watered to support such a large number of people. The three great rivers, with their numerous tributaries, cross this part of the country from the west to the east, where they have formed rich deltas and flood plains. 

The Yangtze-kiang, the "Mississippi of 

Mississippi of

China," is over 3000 miles in length, and is navigable for over 2000 miles. Sea-going vessels go as far as Hankow, 600 miles from the mouth. This river is the main artery of travel in the country and it is hard to see what the people would do without it, because they generally lack good roads and railroads. 

The Hwang-Ho, or Yellow River, in the north is not so long and so useful for navigation as the central stream. This is the river which has been so aptly termed, "China's Sorrow," because of the enormous floods it has caused.

The West is to the south and Canton what the Yangtze is to the center and Shanghai. 

These rivers and their tributaries are connected by a network of canals which indicates that the Chinese use waterways extensively. 

River Life in China. The absence of good roads forces the Chinese to use these waterways, which are alive with boats of all sizes and sorts. Millions of Chinese pass their lives in sampans and house boats. In good weather they are happy and contented, free from rent and taxes. There is always something stirring on the river, so they never need feel lonely.

Their boat life is unique; nowhere else can one see such thousands of crafts, such swarms of people, who are born, live, and die on the waterways of China. 

How So Many People Get a Living. 

Some parts of China are among the most densely populated areas in the world. The province of Kiangsu, in which Shanghai is located, is said to be one of the most densely populated political units in the world, there being 874 people to the square mile. These numerous people get a living mainly by agriculture. The Chinese are essentially an agricultural people. They farm the land carefully and although they know very little about scientific agriculture as understood in the West, they get a large production from the land. The holdings are small, and there is very little waste land. Crops are rotated, very little waste land. fertilization is practiced, and irrigation is used, with the result that grains and vegetables are produced in enormous quantities.

As in ancient times, a double line of boatmen row.

​TRANSPORTATION IN CHINA 

Means of moving people, freight, and garden produce are certainly extreme, from the street-cars, automobiles, and coolie-drawn rickshas on the Nanking Road, Shanghai (lower), to the wheelbarrows and coolie-backs (upper). Where man labor is cheap, and roads badly paved and narrow, the man with the pack or the wheelbarrow does the work so often performed by machines or animals in other countries. 37

The dense population causes untold misery so that the average life is about fifteen years less than ours. Even when they have enough to eat, the lives of some are wretched. Their clothes are coarse cotton. They live in rude mud huts, without chimneys or windows, floors or carpets. They have no books, newspapers, movies, or sports.

Their fields are all cultivated. There is no meadow or woodland, for each bit must yield its utmost. There are practically no vineyards or orchards, for the land must be used to grow sustaining foods like rice, beans, and wheat.

Though factories are being established, hand labor is still the rule, not only in the fields but also in the workshops. The Chinese are skillful workers of metal, expert carvers of ivory and amber, and good weavers of cloth, but they work for absurdly low wages. The brass cash, the most common coin in China, is worth a twentieth of a cent; but still it is too large for some transactions, so pieces of bamboo are used in some provinces for half a cash. south. The leading exports are silk and beans. 

The silk industry has flourished in China for over 4000 years. As we have seen, there is little livestock, but swine and poultry are abundant because they can live on many things which would otherwise be thrown away. Over a billion eggs are exported each year. 

Other Resources. China has other resources than her soil and climate. In coal reserves, China stands next to Canada, which follows directly after the United States.

Practically every one of the eighteen provinces of China proper has immense stores of coal.

The production of coal has increased until it is now over 20,000,000 tons each year. 

Iron ore and limestone near some of the coal fields, together with the coal, form the basis of what may sometime be a great iron and steel development. The Chinese built large ironworks at Hanyang, near Hankow, but this is now in Japanese hands.

The summer winds blow generally from off the people are to be found. Ta Manchou Ti Kuo, the ocean lying to the southeast. The summers are therefore relatively warm and moist, and the rainfall comes when it is most needed. 

These same monsoons may also be the of the terrible Chinese famines about which cause you have heard so much. As in India, some years the monsoons come too early and in others they come too late. Some years they fail to bring their accustomed amount of rainfall and droughts result. Other years they bring too much rainfall, so floods devastate the land. These droughts and floods often cause the deaths of millions in a single season.

Agricultural Products. The leading agricultural products are cotton, tea, silk, and rice. In addition to these major crops, wheat, barley, corn, millet, peas, and beans are cultivated chiefly in the north, and sugar in the
