China produces a number of valuable minerals, many of which are exported. In recent years, it has led all countries in the production of antimony and also tungsten, so essential in the making of tool steel. 

Education. The Chinese people have always regarded education of a certain type very highly. As in India, the main object in gaining an education was to hold public office. The reason for this was that for centuries the real government had been carried on by men sprung from the common 44 

YOUNG CHINA GETS SET" Pix-Errell 

It's not so hard to understand and be friendly with a boy, even from the other side of the world, when he plays the same kind of games you do. people. By means of an education, they climbed step by step to high official positions. 

These officials or mandarins were chosen in a very democratic manner by public examinations. Each candidate was shut up in a small box-like cell for three days with his writing materials. The questions he had to answer were based mostly on the works of Confucius and other ancient philosophers. Memory rather than ability to think was the quality desired. Every candidate was expected to quote long extracts word by word as they appear in the books. 

Educational Reform. As you can readily see, this type of education will not help to make a nation great. Already a reform in educational methods is under way. Some of the leading educators of the world have been brought to China to assist in the work. Under the old system, memory and literary style were highly prized by the scholar, but little attention was given to the study of science, either pure or applied. A more modern system was established in 1905, based on the study of social service and the natural sciences, rather than history and literature. 

Many Chinese students have gone to Europe and the United States for their education. The United States Government returned to China the Boxer Indemnity money to be used to send Chinese students to American colleges and universities. These students have brought Western ideas to their country, and it may truly be said that China is now trying hard to put her educational system on a modern basis. The educational institutions are giving much care to the teaching of agriculture, and notable improvements have been made. 

Manufacturing. - Manufacturing has undergone more changes even than agriculture. 

Formerly almost all Chinese manufacturing was done by hand, and the Chinese were noted for their skill in handicrafts. Now modern factories have been introduced and already number thousands. These include cotton mills, silk mills, soap, match, and cigarette factories, flour and paper mills, tanneries, and even steel mills. 

The factories have already created a laboring class with the usual problems of crowded quarters, poor sanitary conditions, low wages, and lack of recreational facilities in the cities. 

Labor unions have been organized, and strikes have occurred. The laborers here want what they rightly desire in every country-reasonable working hours, living wages, recreational and housing facilities, protection against sickness and accidents. The factory system brings labor problems everywhere.

PROGRESSIVE YOUNG CHINESE 

Ewing Galloway

Education in China has always been held in high honor. In past decades, special classes were educated to fill public offices. Now, due to the Boxer Indemnity Fund, it is possible for young Chinese men and women to study in American universities, and return for service in their own country. In young men students, such as these. lies the future hope of China. 

H. Armstrong Roberts

FINE ARTS 

H. Armstrong Roberts (Left) An Ivory carver of Canton who can carve the most intricate designs of balls within balls. (Right) Getting ready the cool green and white jade bangles and bracelets for tourist trade and foreign shipment. 41

Publishers' Photo Service THE WHEELBARROW IN CHINA 

Along the narrow foot-worn paths trundle these onewheeled vehicles, carrying immense loads; and for a quarter of a mile can often be heard the squeaking of the axle, complaining because of lack of grease.

What China Needs. The late Dr. Sun- 

Yat-Sen, called by some the "George Washington of China," outlined a program of reform. If carried out, it will give China what she needs and help to make her what she ought to be, namely, one of the greatest nations of the world. His program calls for: 

1. Development of a modern system of trans portation and communication. (a) 100,000 miles of modern railways.

At present this vast country has only 8000 miles. 

Compare this with over 250,000 miles in the United States. (b) 1,000,000 miles of modern concrete and macadam roads.

There are now 40,000 or more automobiles in China, and buses run in every province. As these cars need good roads, the Chinese have been very active in road building in recent years and now have over 60,000 miles of good roads. (c) Improvement of existing canals and the building of new ones. (d) Improvement of China's excellent rivers, so that they can be used to better advantage for navigation, irrigation, and water supply, and their floods controlled. (e) Construction of more telephone and telegraph lines all over the country. (f) Improvements in radio facilities. 

Do you realize that the whole world is becoming more alike through having similar things such as radios, moving pictures, telephones, telegraphs, automobiles, airplanes, etc.? This is sometimes referred to as the "Americanization of the world." 

2. Development of commercial harbors. (a) There should be three large ocean ports, with ample loading and unloading facilities, in North China (Tientsin), Central China (Shanghai), and South China (Canton). (b) Many smaller harbors along the coast should be greatly improved for coastwise trade and as fishing ports. (c) Commercial docks to be constructed along all navigable rivers. 

3. Modern cities with modern conveniences to be constructed at all railway centers, terminals, and alongside harbors. 

4. There should be more extensive water power development. Much of even China proper is mountainous and well watered, and furnishes excellent facilities for hydro-elec tric power. China needs power. 

5. Iron, steel, and cement works, utilizing her vast coal, iron, and limestone resources in order to supply the above mentioned needs.

6. More modern and extensive use of minerals, such as coal, iron, copper, and petroleum. 

7. More application of science to agriculture. Nine-tenths of the people are farmers, the majority of whom are very skillful, but the land could be made to yield still more by careful study. 

8. Vast irrigation works should be constructed in the dryer outlying provinces, such as Mongolia and Sinkiang. proper to the outlying provinces of the republic. Many countries refuse to allow the 

Chinese to enter in large numbers because of their lower standards of living. 

Recent History. In 1931, after a disturbance in Manchuria, Japan sent over an army to restore order and "punish bandits."

Under this pretext the Japanese overran the country and set up a puppet government, completely under their control. The Chinese appealed to the League of Nations, which

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH CHINA, HONG KONG, AND KWANTUNG 

There has been a change in our trade with China since 1929. The averages for the years 1930 up to 1941 are: imports, $54,000,000; exports, $70,000,000. 

9. There should be reforestation in central and northern China. In their great need for wood, the Chinese have robbed the hill slopes of their beautiful forests, with disastrous results to soils and streams. 

10. There should be more colonization in the provinces, Mongolia, Sinkiang, and Tibet.

The greatest problem of all in China proper is too many people. The hardiest should be encouraged and aided to take up their homes in these outlying sparsely settled parts of the country.

China can support her growing population only by the following means: (1) better methods in agriculture; (2) development of manufacturing so that she can exchange manufactured goods for foodstuffs and raw materials from other countries; (3) emigration of her people from overcrowded China sent a commission headed by the Earl of 

Lytton to report. His report recommended a separate government under the Chinese. The 

Japanese refused to recognize the report, and withdrew from the League. Next they conquered Jehol and extended their sway as far as the Great Wall. In 1934 they established Ta Manchou Ti Kuo, the Great

Manchurian Empire, commonly called Man choukuo, or Manchu Land. 

Next they found an excuse for invading 

China proper south of the Great Wall.

FUJIYAMA- MOUNT FUJI 

Ewing Galloway

Fujiyama is the famous, matchless mountain sacred to all Japanese. Once a flaming volcano, its cone-shaped peak is now crowned in white snow. During the few short weeks in summer, when the snows have melted and climbing is safe, many pilgrims climb to its summit in expiation of their sins. 

B. JAPAN 

Where fertile Kai out-stretches on one hand, And on the other hand, broad Suruga-Land,

Out of their midst beyond the ken of man, Rises the glorious peak of Fuji-san.

The clouds themselves can hardly climb its height; 

The birds but skirt its sides in soaring flight. 

Its fire is quenched with ever-falling snow, Its snows are melted by a quenchless flame;

I find no word to tell of it, no name

Page. (Printed in pamphlet issued by Japanese 

Government Railways.)

The Rise of Japan. - Of all the countries of the world, Japan is one of the most interesting, as well as one of the most important. 

She is interesting because of the characteristics and customs of the people. She is important because she stands as leader of the countries populated by the yellow races and because she is one of the strongest countries of the world.

The rise of Japan to a commanding position is one of the most fascinating stories of history. 

Such an achievement must mean that there is something remarkable about these people who have become the leaders among the peoples of the Far East. 

The Land of Japan. In many ways the land of Japan is as remarkable as its people. 

The Japanese islands are really the tops of a submerged mountain range which rises abruptly from one of the deepest known parts of the ocean basin. As Japan is one of the

THE JAPANESE ISLANDS COMPARED WITH THE BRITISH ISLES 47 

younger Asiatic nations, so the land itself is very young.

Being a young mountainous land has disadvantages, for it means volcanoes and earthquakes. Of both of these Japan seems to have more than its share. Just think of two hundred volcanoes in a land whose total area is less than our one state of California! 

Fortunately, not over fifty are any longer active. Even so, you can readily guess that a large portion of Japan is uninhabitable, owing to its mountainous character and to the danger of living near the active volcanoes. 

The most famous volcanic peak in Japan is Fujiyama. Rising directly from the waters of the Pacific Ocean to a height of over 12,000 feet, it is the most impressive natural feature in Japan and one of the most beautiful mountains of this type in the world. The 

Japanese love and even revere it, and every year during July and August white-robed pilgrims climb to its summit for adoration and prayer.

Watch for Japanese decorations on various articles for sale in this country, and note how often this snow-capped mountain appears.

Earthquakes in Japan. - Earthquakes are a serious problem in Japan, where they have often laid waste large districts. The worst in its destruction of life and property that ever occurred took place in 1923. Half the buildings in Tokyo and Yokohama were destroyed, but the Japanese at once rebuilt these cities better than before. 

The cities cannot be moved to safer locations because there are none. Steel and concrete, which resist both earthquake and fire, are now being used in construction. The 

Japanese are building low, one-story dwellings of wood and light-weight materials loosely put together. In case of an earthquake, they would sway back and forth without being thrown down. 

Hot Springs. An interesting natural feature of Japan, found in the volcanic regions, are the mineral springs of which there are at least a thousand. The waters of these springs are thought to be beneficial in curing certain diseases. Thousands of Japanese resort to them for the sake of their health, as well as simply for the pleasure of bathing in their waters. 

The Japanese Islands Compared with the 

British Isles. Like the British, the Japanese are an island people. Their islands are situated close to the mainland from which they

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH JAPAN 

Raw silk leads among our imports from Japan, its value being about five-sixths of the total value of our imports from that country. There has been a marked change in our trade with Japan since 1929. The averages for the years 1930 up to 1941 are: imports, $175,000,000; exports, $148,000,000. 

Climate. The latitude of the main islands of the Japanese Empire is the same as that of the United States. This fact suggests a favorable climate for an active people. 

Compare the latitude of Tokyo, the capital, with

Washington, the capital of the United States; with London.

But the islands of Japan have a greater range of climate than the United States. In the extreme North, the climate is sub-arctic; in the extreme South, it is semi-tropical. Like the British Isles, the climate of Japan is moderated by the surrounding waters, but Japan has greater extremes of temperature and is more affected by conditions on the near-by mainland.

In summer, the monsoon, blowing toward Asia, brings to Japan the summer rain, which is heaviest on the south and east. These moist, warm winds make the summers in Japan rather trying, but the climate of the remaining months makes up for this with its stimulating coolness, or dry, sunny weather. 

In the winter northwest winds cause heavy snowfall among the mountains in the West. 

In northern Japan the snowfall is sometimes very heavy, nearly burying the villages. But while the western slopes are suffering from the cold winds, the eastern slopes toward the 

Pacific may be bright and sunny, and snowfall seldom seen.

The rainfall, like the temperature, varies from north to south. There is a range of from less than thirty inches in the extreme North to more than eighty inches in the South (maps, pages 28-29).

With all this variety and stimulating changes in the climate of Japan, what are your conclusions as to its effects on the people?

FEW DOMESTIC ANIMALS IN JAPAN CHERRY-BLOSSOM TIME 

Ewing Galloway

In April, when Japan is all a-bloom with the pink and white sweetness of cherry blossoms, come the cherryblossom festivals. And the wind that blows through the branches showers the earth with pink and white blooms. growing in Japan. The great drawback is the high mountainous nature of the land. In fact, only about one-sixth of the 148,000 square miles in Japan proper can be cultivated. 

With such a small percentage of arable land, there must be either intensive cultivation or dependence on other resources.

Farms averaging two and a half acres and a crop production so great as to feed the people largely on home-grown foods tell the story. In their efforts the farmers are aided by a moderate and humid climate. This increases the productivity of the soil and enables two or three crops to be raised in one year on some of the land.

Most of the farms in Japan are small and are owned by the farmers themselves. All of the farmer's household help with the varied farm tasks. Practically no machinery is used, and nearly all the work is done by hand with hoe and spade. Occasionally an ox or a horse is used for some of the heavier work such as plowing.

The land is not naturally fertile. It is made to produce so much only through the use of fertilizers and careful cultivation. Hillsides are terraced, and irrigation practiced, this being necessary in growing rice. 49 The principal crops are rice, rye, barley, and wheat. Rice is the most important cereal crop. One-half of the cultivated land is devoted to it. Japanese rice leads in quality and Japan stands third in the amount of its production.

The big money crop is raw silk. In the production of this commodity Japan leads the world. Having pursued this industry for over 

1400 years, Japan has made it her largest and most important one. The experience of centuries has helped Japan to secure supremacy in the silk industry, but an important factor has been a climate favorable both to the silkworm and the mulberry tree, upon the leaves of which the worm feeds. 

Tea is the next largest money crop after silk. The Japanese have adopted strictly modern, sanitary methods in preparing their tea so that other nations have come to prefer it to that of China, which was formerly the leader in the export of tea.

In Taiwan, the Japanese government controls the camphor industry. Until the Germans succeeded in making camphor by a synthetic process, the Japanese had a world monopoly of this product. 

In the use of their land, the Japanese have developed some of their best traits, notably their great patience and perseverance, their ingenuity in overcoming difficulties, their temperate habits, and their strong physiques. 

These qualities, together with the intelligence of the people, have not only helped the 

Japanese as soldiers, but have also enabled them to forge ahead quickly in the various manufacturing industries which they are now developing. 

Few Domestic Animals in Japan. - Japan has comparatively few cattle, sheep, hogs, or horses. Several reasons are given for this.

Some of the Japanese are Buddhists and believe that animal life is sacred. Consequently they do not eat meat. Then, as in

China, all the land on the small farms is

AT SUNSET 

Ewing Galloway

When the day's catch is in, the fishermen furl their wind-weary sails, and over the bare spars drape the drying nets. needed to produce food for the people themselves.

The lack of suitable grass for forage also accounts for the lack of cattle and sheep. 

The thickness, size, and sharp edges of most of the grasses make them unsuitable for forage.

Forage grasses from other countries are being introduced. The government and some of the people are also trying to meet the growing demand for meat and dairy products by bringing in better breeds of domestic animals. 

Fisheries. Like the British, the Japanese have extensive fisheries as a result of their location. Fish mean more to the Japanese than to the British. The British have a large number of domestic animals, and they import a great deal of meat. The Japanese have few animals, and they cannot afford to import much meat.

DRYING THE CATCH 

Ewing Galloway

The Japanese are great fishermen. Here we see them drying fish in the sun on the decks of sampans on a canal near Tokyo. Why is fishing so important to the Japanese? 

Forestry. Over one-half of the total area of Japan is covered with trees. The Japanese have made a careful study of this industry, taking Saxony, in Germany, as their model for scientific forestry. They made a list of all the different kinds of trees in the country, determined which were of most use, and then regulated the planting of trees accordingly. 

Cutting trees and replacing them by striplings is carefully regulated by law.

After the trunks of the trees are cut into lumber, the branches and refuse are converted into charcoal, match-sticks, pulp for paper, or are used for firewood. Even the leaves, grass, and herbage in the forest are carefully saved and used either as fuel or fertilizer. 

Minerals. As we might expect in a mountainous country with volcanoes, Japan is fairly well supplied with minerals. Copper, sulphur, and coal are quite plentiful, and gold, silver, lead, and some petroleum are found. 

Her greatest lack is iron ore. About 

40,000,000 tons of coal are mined each year. 

Water power, furnished by their swift mountain streams, is another asset of which the Japanese are now making good use. But with her growing industries, the lack of sufficient raw materials is getting to be a big problem. Do you wonder that the Japanese look with longing eyes at the vast undeveloped resources of China just across the way, wishing that they might have the privilege of making use of some of them?

The History of Japan. -The Empire of 

Japan was founded by Jimmu Tenno, who ascended the throne as Emperor in 660 B.C.

This was about a hundred years after the traditional founding of Rome. For twelve centuries the descendants of the Emperor 

Jimmu Tenno continued to rule. 

Then the executive power fell into the hands of the leading family among the courtiers, the Fujiwara. The descendants of this family, like those of Jimmu Tenno, survive until the present time.

Several centuries later, the control of the government was wrested from the Fujiwara by the leaders of a race of soldiers who had gained strength and power fighting against the 

Ainu, powerful savage tribes living in the northern part of Japan. They were called 

Shoguns, and while they were in power the Emperor was kept in seclusion. 

The Shoguns retained one-third of the country under their direct rule, and received all the income from it. The remainder of the land was divided among a few feudal lords. 

These maintained armies of soldiers known as

Raising silk worms is one of the principal industries of Japan. The worms, fed on mulberry leaves (upper left) form cocoons from which the silken fibers are obtained. These are spun and twisted into silk thread (lower). The valuable skeins of silk are then bundled, tied, and marked for shipment (upper right). 53

Between the upper and lower classes there was a social gulf that none of the lower class could ever hope to cross. Publishers' Photo Service A JAPANESE METAL WORKER 

This expert damascene worker is hammering in fine gold and silver according to the design on the metal plate. 

Typical oriental designs adorn these plates. 

During the great period of discovery in the sixteenth century, boats from Portugal, Spain,

Holland, and England came into the harbors of Japan. Trade began. Then missionaries came in. Finally the Japanese became suspicious, thinking that the foreigners were trying to get possession of the country. 

Traders were expelled and all foreigners forbidden to land. As China had built a great stone wall around her vast domain, so Japan built a sea wall to keep out all foreigners and their strange ideas and ways of doing things. 

For over two hundred years Japan stood still while the Renaissance was transforming other countries.

The Awakening. It was the United States that then opened the door of Japan. In 1853, the United States sent Commodore Perry with a fleet of ten ships to Japan to demand a treaty assuring protection to American sailors wrecked on the Japanese coast, and permitting American ships to secure food and to trade in 

Japanese ports. Commodore Perry's ships were loaded with goods ready to trade, but they were also armed and prepared to enforce their demands. The treaty which Japan signed marked the end of isolation and the beginning of her rise to a position of world power. 

Japan Abolishes Feudalism. But the opening of their ports to trade with other nations was not the only far-reaching change that took place in Japan about this time. 

The feudal lords had become very much dissatisfied with the rule of the Shoguns, and there was a growing feeling that the Emperor should come forth from his seclusion and rule in fact as well as in name.

In 1867, there came to the throne a very enlightened Emperor, Mutsu Hito, whose reign marks the beginning of modern Japan. 

The Shoguns resigned and the Emperor assumed complete control. He held audiences with foreign representatives and encouraged the development of trade and intercourse with other nations. 

In the "Charter Oath," the Japanese "Magna Carta," he promised that a "deliberative assembly should be formed and all measures decided by public opinion, that civil and military government should no longer be separated and that all classes of the people should with one mind devote themselves to the national welfare, that the rights of all classes should be assured, that the uncivilized customs of antiquity should be abolished and impartiality and justice administered according to universally recognized principles, and that intellect and learning should be sought for throughout the world, so that the foundations of the Empire might be firmly established."

In the transition that followed, other nations helped Japan. Foreign ministers acted as advisers, and foreign experts who came to Japan at her request assisted in many ways. In this way Japan profited by the experience of the most enlightened people and speeded up her own development. She also sent students abroad to study conditions and get first-hand information. By imitation and adaptation the Japanese quickly brought their country to a high position. 

To build these up required men and money, and here Japanese loyalty stood the test. 

The Growth of Japan Brings on New Prob lems. We thus see that the rapid progress of Japan has been due to three things: (1) the character of the people, (2) a favorable climate, and (3) possession of natural resources. They have not only been able but willing to learn from others. This, together with the stimulating climate and their resources, has enabled them to introduce successfully the modern industrial system.

Now that she has attained the position of a world power, the Japanese population is growing faster than the means of support. 

Japan has a rapidly growing population, as these figures, which are of course approximate, will show : 40,000,000 45,000,000 1890 1900 1910-50,000,000 1920-55,000,000 1930

Dairen is a well-built modern city. In addition to this locomotive works, it has a number of other manufacturing industries.

Character of the People. 

the rulers and the assistance of foreign experts might not have had such wonderful results had it not been for the character of the Japanese themselves. As a people they are mentally alert, energetic, imitative, courteous, proud, and have a loyalty for country and emperor which is martyr-like in quality. The love of country has been a powerful tool in the hands of the leaders for concentrating the energies of the nation upon some enterprise which they, the leaders, have believed to be for the good of the people. 

Japan has been able to rise to her position as a world power through her army and navy. 1940 

- 60,000,000 70,000,000

This population of Japan proper is located on an area not very much larger than Italy, with only about one-sixth of it cultivated. 

This means a high density of population with certain areas packed with people. One of these areas is the fertile and level plain of 

Tokyo, the largest plain on the Japanese islands. Here the crowded villages carry on the closest cultivation of the ground known in the world. An incredible number of people live on every square mile and get their living from it. They have on an average a third of an acre apiece, an acre and a third for a family of four. (Do not these figures suggest the problem: How can Japan care for her growing population?)

How do nations provide for a growing population? By more intensive cultivation of all available land, resorting to manufacture, emigration, and by taking less densely populated lands from other nations by force. (How many of these methods is Japan using?)

THE PROBLEM OF JAPANESE EMIGRATION More Intensive Cultivation of Land. 

Japan is already a leader in intensive cultivation, and it is doubtful if very much improvement can be made in this line. If suitable grasses can be introduced to take the place of the unsatisfactory native grasses, the higher elevations could be used for stock raising and the supply of meat and milk increased. 

Hokkaido, the most northerly island, still has some cultivable land which can be made to produce such crops as potatoes, beans and peas, and fruits. It can also be used for stock raising. The production in Japan can be increased somewhat, but not enough to solve the problem of increasing population.

Manufacturing. The chief obstacles to increasing the number of manufacturing industries in Japan are the difficulties in securing raw materials and in finding markets for the products. 

Cotton, iron, and wool, for example, all have to be imported.

Naturally the money to pay for these has to come from the sale of manufactured products. 57 ever-increasing numbers, but it alone will not suffice. It must be supplemented. 

Emigration. Emigration is a means of securing relief from overcrowding. But before any country can employ this method, suitable places must be found to which the would-be emigrants can go. There are empty spaces which the Japanese could fill in

LADIES OF QUALITY 

This Japanese hostess is receiving a guest in strictly formal for a well-bred hostess to open fashion. It is always customary the door on her knees. Notice hands, which is the proper cerehow precisely they hold their monial for both hostess and guest when making their bows to each other.

The chief manufactures are cotton goods, iron and steel products, including shipbuilding, silk goods, paper, woolen goods, pottery, and many small articles, such as matches, fans, cigarettes, toys, etc. The greatest markets for her goods are the United States, China, and India. 

Labor is cheap and plentiful and willing to work long hours. But Japan must buy her raw materials and sell her products in competition with other nations who have been in the field a much longer time. This will make it difficult for her to establish trade and increase her industries.

Manufacturing, united with trade, offers the best opportunity to Japan to care for her 

Canada, the United States, and

Australia. 

But Japanese settlers cannot enter these countries, because they are not wanted. Australia has declared herself a "white man's continent." The other two countries have also put up bars against Japanese immigration.

Back of this has been the fear that wages and standards of living would be lowered. This might come as a result of competition with those who are willing to work long hours at less than the usual wage scale of white workers. Actual experience as in California has shown that it is almost impossible for the white population to compete with the 

Japanese in market gardening and fruit growing. These countries are not willing to subject themselves to a competition of this sort. In addition, mixing of the two races is not desired. 

Where are the Japanese to go? There are opportunities in the islands of the Pacific, including the East Indies. Many of the leaders look upon the continent of Asia as their chief outlet, and statistics show that the greatest number of Japanese abroad have found settlement there. There is a big advantage in this, for it enables Japan to secure a hold on the continent of which she hopes to be the leader.

While the room is extremely simple in its furnishings, it is elegant. The cushions are made of heavy silk tapestry, the table of carved teakwood. The panel on the wall at the front won a prize at the national art show. been a cause for a tribe or nation to wage war upon more fortunate neighbors or upon those controlling near-by resources. This fact has been true in the history of Japan. For by war she has gained possession of certain lands and by threats or force has obtained control of others. 

The Chino-Japanese War, 1894-95, was brought on because of the rivalry between the two countries as to which should control 

Korea (Chosen). Japan was victorious, and compelled China to recognize the independence of Korea. In addition, China turned over to Japan the southern island of Formosa. 

Japan saw that her whole interest in the nearer parts of Asia as a source of raw material and as a home for surplus population was menaced, and she began in 1896 a naval and military expansion. Russia began to strengthen her position in Manchuria, Liao-tung, and 

Port Arthur, and finally threatened to gain control of Korea. This last action was like pointing " a dagger at Japan's throat," as it has been described, and the Russo-Japanese 

War of 1904-05 resulted. 

Japan was again victorious. This time she secured all Russian rights in Port Arthur and the Liao-tung peninsula, and possession of the southern half of the island of Sakhalin. In addition she brought about the evacuation of 

Manchuria by Russia. Korea was to be granted autonomy, but was annexed in 1910, becoming officially known as Chosen. 

The World War strengthened the Japanese hold on Asia. She took Kiaochow from the 

Germans, and from China, the rich province of Shantung. Later, however, at the urgent request of the other Great Powers she yielded her sovereignty back to China.

In 1931, Japan invaded Manchuria and after acquiring complete military control set up in 1934 the puppet state of Ta Manchou 

Ti Kuo with the Emperor Kang Teh (the former Henry Pu Yi) on the throne. By a policy of force, Japan thus secured virtual control of the Asiatic mainland north of the 

Great Wall. In 1937 she invaded China proper, and by 1941 had gained control of all the leading cities of China.

C. THE INDO-CHINESE PENINSULA 

Political Divisions of the Indo-Chinese 

Peninsula. The peninsular portion of southeastern Asia between India and China is called Indo-China. Including the long Malay 

Peninsula, there are five political divisions.

The central portion comprises the independent kingdom of Siam, now called Thailand. The both controlled by the British, occupy the southern part of the Malay Peninsula. 

The Land and the People. - Both the land and the people are somewhat similar to the lands and the peoples between which they are found. The northern part is a mountainous mass, sending long spurs to the south. The rivers are swift in their upper courses and are heavily loaded with silt when they reach the lowlands. In times of high water or flood, this silt is spread over the lowlands, adding to their fertility.

The people live mainly on the lowlands between the mountain ranges and on the plains along the coast. As in India and southern China, the monsoon type of climate prevails. The heat and dampness, especially during the rainy season, make this part of Asia a most uncomfortable place in which to live. Consequently comparatively few white people who come here remain permanently. 

The homes and other buildings are strikingly similar to those of India and China. 

There is quite a mixture of races in this part of Asia. The most energetic workers, perhaps, are the Chinese, large numbers of whom have settled here. Most of the Siamese and many of the people in Indo-China are Buddhists. There are over 100,000 Buddhist priests in Siam, where they are distinguished by their yellow robes.

Agricultural Products and Lumber. -Indo- 

China is the greatest rice exporting region of the world. Rice, in addition to being the leading export of Siam and French Indo China, is the staple starch food of the people. 

Other crops are sugar cane, tobacco, spices, coffee, cacao, and cotton. Silk is also produced. Coconuts and palms grow on the lowlands, while on the highlands trees found in the northern United States, such as the

oak, pine, chestnut, apple, and peach, flourish.

But the great lumber tree is the teak. Plantations have been established in French Indo- China for growing teak, which is a leading export from both French Indo-China and Siam.

The Straits Settlements are noted for rubber plantations, a large part of the world's plantation rubber coming from there. Rubber plantations have also been established in 

French Indo-China.

Publishers' Photo Service TEMPLE OF DAWN, BANGKOK, SIAM 

In the beautiful green and gold capital of this lotus-land gleams an oriental spire. Its sides are encrusted with thousands of bits of glazed tile, until the tower looks as if it were inlaid with turquoises, emeralds, topazes, and rubies. 

Minerals. Many useful minerals, such as coal, iron ore, zinc, manganese, and tungsten (much used in the manufacture of steel), phosphates, tin, gold, and silver, are mined.

The Straits Settlements alone produce nearly half of all the tin ore mined in the world. 

Burma is important for petroleum. Coal is helping in the development of manufactures, such as cotton goods, cement, bricks, and tile. 

Animals. - Wild animals of many kinds. are plentiful. Elephants, tigers, panthers, leopards, bears, monkeys, and wild boars are among the numerous animals, some of which are much feared by the inhabitants. Herds of wild elephants have been known to destroy both villages and crops belonging to the natives. Considerable use is made of elephants as work animals. They are especially useful in moving heavy articles, such as lumber. Tame elephants are used in hunting wild elephants. White elephants, which are few in number, are much esteemed in Siam, where they are a feature of religious and political ceremonies and processions.

The common work animal in agriculture is the buffalo, much used in the rice fields. 

The zebu is also used in transportation. But the railroad and the electric street car, the automobile, and even the airplane have been introduced into these oriental countries where they are fast displacing animal transportation. 

Fish. Fish abound in the rivers and fish is largely eaten in place of meat. As in India, the Buddhists do not eat meat. The Chinese, however, have introduced pork for their own use and for sale to others who eat this kind of meat. 

Cities. Bangkok is the capital and chief seaport of Siam. Over half a million people live in this city. In its earlier days, Bangkok might have been called a floating city, for so many of the people lived on houseboats and others had their homes elevated on piles to escape the floods which spread over the lowlands. Modern Bangkok has electric lights, street cars, and other conveniences, and is a clean, well-kept city. There are attractive buildings, built of brick in many cases, and public parks.

The city is built on both sides of the Menam 

River not far from its mouth. Unfortunately a large sand bar at the mouth of the river prevents the larger ocean vessels from ascending the river to Bangkok, so goods have to be

INDO-CHINESE CITIES 

61 transferred to and from smaller boats. Over three-fourths of the country's trade passes through this city. Most of the business and commerce is carried on by the Chinese residents.

Hanoi and Saigon are the two largest cities of French Indo-China, and both are important trade centers. Oriental architecture combined with modern improvements installed by the French make these cities of the Far East very attractive. Saigon is known as one of the finest cities in the Far East. It has beautiful gardens, and double rows of trees extend along many of its streets. The city has a good harbor for ocean vessels, but only small vessels can ascend the river to Hanoi, which has a separate port. Hanoi is the capital of French Indo-China.

Rangoon, the capital and leading port of Burma, has many rice mills and ships out rice, teak, and petroleum. Singapore, the capital of the Straits Settlements, is built on an island of the same name. It has a little over half a million people, about half of whom are Chinese, and the rest mixed races from all parts of the world. Singapore is an important naval base as well as a shipping center. 

ON THE PENANG DOCKS 

H. Armstrong Roberts

The deep holds of ocean freighters are being loaded with rubber and tin for the port of New York. The turbaned stevedores add a touch of the East, but it is hard to believe that the deep jungle lies not so very far inland. 

Publishers' Photo Service A PAINTER OF BATIK, JAVA 

Batik is a figured cloth produced in Java and is one of its most ancient arts. Batik painters are always women or girls. The design is applied to the cloth free-hand by means of hot wax. Afterwards the wax is removed, leaving the pattern. This operation is repeated for every color applied to the batik. 

Singapore is the leading rubber and tin port of the Far East. It is the greatest commercial center in this part of Asia. There are many handsome buildings and the city is a British cultural center for this region. 

Surrounded by water in a tropical region, the hot damp air makes the city almost unbearable for Europeans, few of whom care to remain there permanently.

D. NETHERLAND INDIA 

volcanic origin. The mountains generally are 

A Group of Islands. — While Netherland heavily forested, and the islands have much beautiful scenery.

India is the official name of these islands, they are also called Netherlands East Indies and Dutch East Indies. They include Java, 

Sumatra, Borneo (except the northern part, which belongs to the British), Celebes, and about one-half of New Guinea, the other half being under the rule of Great Britain and Australia. 

In addition are groups of smaller islands, among them being the famous Moluccas, or Spice Islands, which were the goal of many explorers in the time of Columbus. The total area of all the islands is estimated at 735,000 square miles (map, pages 2-3). 

Mountains and Volcanoes. Nearly all of the Netherlands East Indies are very mountainous islands, and there are many volcanoes in the group. The fertile soil of Java is of 

IN BORNEO

Keystone View 

This picturesque landing place for steamboats, with its long row of floating docks, is on the Barito River, squarely on the equator. The rivers of the island play a very important part both as highways and as lines along which run the main arteries of population. Borneo has marvelously fertile soil, rains a-plenty, abundant timber, rich minerals, and could be made one of the richest and most productive countries in the world. 

Transportation. Java has a well-developed system of motor roads making it possible to reach every place of importance by automobile. Sumatra also has some good roads, but neither the roads nor the railroads of Sumatra cover the island as do those of 

Java. Celebes is generally lacking in modern means of transportation except for a tramway. 

But Celebes is made up of long peninsulas so that no part of the island is very far from the and New Guinea are comparatively undewater which is the main highway. Borneo veloped and explorers on these islands need to be well guarded against the natives of the interior.

Products. Java is one of the richest agricultural regions in the world. It is also one of the most densely populated land masses, having nearly 700 people to the square mile. Yet in addition to supporting its own people, look at the following list of things that Java produces for export: coffee, timber, tea, cacao, sugar, spices, tobacco, rubber, copra, tin, and petroleum. Over 99 per cent of the world's quinine also comes from Java.

The soil is not so fertile in the other islands, and much of their area is covered with forests, swamps, or prairies with coarse, hard grass.

Crops are cultivated to some extent in 

Sumatra and Celebes, but there is very little development of agriculture in either Borneo or 

New Guinea. Teak is the leading lumber, forests of teak in Java supplying large quantities of this valuable timber. 

Two small islands, Banka and Billiton, are noted for the production of tin, but the leading mineral industry is that of petroleum, which is produced in Sumatra, Java, and Borneo. 

Coal, iron ore, nickel, and some of the precious minerals, such as gold, silver, platinum, and diamonds, are found but not mined in large quantities.

IGUASSÚ FALLS 

Ewing Galloway

The Iguassú Falls in the Paraná River are on the border between Argentina and Brazil. They are higher than and almost twice as wide as Niagara Falls. In their luxuriant tropical setting, these falls afford one of the finest sights in the world.

UNIT III. LATIN AMERICA 

Latin America is the name given to the lands of the Western Hemisphere south of the 

United States. There are many reasons why we should know more than we do about these countries. (1) In the first place, their nearness puts them into close relations with us. (2) They furnish an outlet for the investment of American capital. It is estimated that 

American capitalists have invested over $5,000,000,000 in Latin America, and the amount is increasing annually. (3) They are very good customers in trade. They need our manufactured goods and we need their foodstuffs and raw materials for manufacturing. (4) Latin American civilization can help to enrich ours. (5) Our ownership of the

Panama Canal gives us an interest in these countries which we have never had before. (6) We must know about conditions in these countries on account of our desire to maintain the Monroe doctrine. (7) They are countries of the future and are bound to play an increasingly important part in world affairs.

They are reaching that stage of development where they think for themselves. 

Common Characteristics. The people of these countries have certain characteristics in common. For instance, the majority speak the Spanish language, the exceptions being the people of Brazil, who speak Portuguese, and several groups who speak French. The bulk of the population in most of these countries is either Indian or a mixture of 

Indian and white. In the tropical regions there are many Negroes. In Argentina, Uruguay, Chile, and southern Brazil, however, the population is mostly white. 64

THE PAN-AMERICAN UNION 

Almost all the people are members of the Roman Catholic Church. They also have a somewhat similar cultural background, derived from the Iberian peninsula of the Old World. In fact, for over three hundred years, most of the area was ruled from Spain and Portugal.

They are on the whole large countries with a sparse population. Illiteracy runs rather high in most of them. They all have somewhat similar political problems, and all need foreign capital for their development. 

Differences. In spite of these similarities and the fact that we say "Latin America" or "Spanish America," we must not think of these countries as being all alike. They differ in many ways and are very proud of their own nationalities. A man from Peru does not want to be called a Latin American. He is a Peruvian and wants the world to know it. 

Similarly, we have Chileans, Argentines, Brazilians, and so on. There are twenty distinct nations in Latin America, stretching from the Rio Grande River to Cape Horn, covering an area of over 8,000,000 square miles and having a population of about 100,000,000. 

Furthermore, these nations are not well acquainted with one another. Distances are so great and transportation so poorly developed that it has been hard for the people to travel and visit other Latin Americans in their home lands. Then, too, they differ in climate and relief and natural resources and historical background to such an extent that it becomes necessary to treat them separately. We shall attempt to do this in the following pages. 

What speedy means of traveling and spreading information are now helping Latin Americans to become better acquainted? 

The Pan-American Union. The Pan- 

American Union is an international coöperative organization, with headquarters in Washington, D. C. It consists of the twenty-one 

American Republics: Argentina, Bolivia, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Cuba, 65 

Dominican Republic, Ecuador, Guatemala, Haiti, Honduras, Mexico, Nicaragua, Panama, Paraguay, Peru, Salvador, United States,

Uruguay, and Venezuela. Its purpose is the development of commerce, friendly relations, and the preservation of peace among its members. It is supported by money contributed by each country according to its population. ​Ewing Galloway

PAN-AMERICAN UNION BUILDING 

The Pan-American Union has its headquarters in this beautiful building in Washington, D. C. Three monumental bronze and glass doors guard the entrance to the vestibule, beyond which lies a typical Latin American patio or courtyard. Plants and flowers from tropical 

America adorn this courtyard which has a glass roof constructed so that it can be opened or closed according to the season.

Its governing board is composed of the Secretary of State of the United States, and the diplomatic representatives in Washington of the other American governments.

The Union publishes monthly bulletins in English, Spanish, and Portuguese, which are careful records of Pan-American progress. 

It also issues numerous special reports and booklets on countries, cities, commodities, and so forth, of Latin America. Its library, the Columbus Memorial Library, contains 67,000 volumes and a large number of maps. 

It also has a collection of photographs, lantern slides, and negatives. The Union is housed in a beautiful building, given in part by 

Andrew Carnegie. It is one of the show places of our capital city.

Great Extent of Argentina. - This land has an area roughly the size of the United States east of the Mississippi River. It is about two thousand miles long, reaching from the tropical far into the temperate zone. If placed on North America, it would reach from 

Hudson Bay in Canada to the Yucatan

Peninsula in Mexico. In longitude it extends eighteen degrees or about the distance from 

New York to Chicago (map opposite). 

With such wide latitudinal and longitudinal ranges, would you not expect a great variety of climate and relief or topography? Such is the case, and for our purposes the whole country may be divided into four regions.

These are the northern or quebracho region, the western or wine region, the southern or pastoral region, and the central or cereal region. 

The Northern Region. This northern or quebracho region, situated, as it is, partly in the tropical or torrid zone and partly in the temperate, displays all the rich beauties common to most tropical regions. In it is the territory known as "El Gran Chaco," or "the great forest," a heavily forested area full of valuable timber, especially the famous dyewood known as quebracho. Practically every part of the trunk of this tree is valuable because from it tannin may be extracted. In some respects it is like the chestnut tree, which is also used for tanning purposes. a dining room?

Mills for the extraction of tannin are usually located in the midst of the forest lands and are, as a rule, permanent. After the removal of the bark, the logs are usually placed on large wagons drawn by four oxen. 

The oxen are superior to horses and mules for this work because they can graze in the open, and when their usefulness as working animals comes to an end they can be fattened for which carry them to the extract plants. food. The logs are hauled to short railroads 

These plants, although located many miles away from the main centers of civilization, are provided with modern machinery of the best quality. The extract is prepared in vats

LATIN AMERICA 

Ewing Galloway

THE CAPITOL AT BUENOS AIRES 

This Palace of Congress at Buenos Aires may well be described as a modern capitol building in a modern capital city. It fronts on a large plaza, the Piaza Congreso. 

The steps at the right lead up to a monument erected in commemoration of the First Congress after the Declaration of Independence in 1810. and boilers, pressed into sacks for drying, and then shipped to markets all over the world. 

Many logs are also shipped directly to foreign countries. 

We have written at length about quebracho because it is so important in the tanning world. 

In addition, this northern district has fertile plains and valleys, producing wheat, barley, rice, corn, tobacco, cotton, manioc, hemp, bananas, oranges, olives, and many kinds of vegetables. It will be a great region sometime to furnish the people farther south with tropical fruits and vegetables. Sugar refining already is well established here and gives promise of a big future expansion. Cotton, tobacco, rice, and olives could also be grown much more extensively than at present. (Find this region on the map, page 67.) 

Western Region. -The wine region in the west is a semi-arid country located at the base of the lofty Andes Mountains. The land is gently rolling and the soil is very fertile, so that with irrigating waters near at hand, abundant crops of grapes and sub-tropical fruits are raised.

This is the famous wine-making country of 

South America. Most Argentines have come from European countries where wine is a favorite drink, or are descendants of the wine-drinking peoples of Europe, the Spanish, Italians, and French. The whole country has over 5000 wine vaults and manufactures about 150,000,000 gallons of wine per annum. The city of Mendoza is in the heart of the grapegrowing and wine-making region. It is a small progressive city and is on the main line of the trans-continental railroad from Buenos Aires in Argentina to Valparaiso in Chile.

This graph shows a gradual increase in wheat acreage since the World War. All of these countries have large areas of level or gently rolling lands with a type of rainfall well suited to wheat. They are all important wheat exporters and rivals of the United States in the wheat 

The Southern Re gion. - The southern or pastoral region is the country known as Patagonia. It consists mostly of a broad, undulating plain sloping gradually eastward. In the north, the climate is temperate, but in the far south, the conditions are rather cold and severe. Undoubtedly much of this land, especially with irrigation, could be used for farming purposes. At present it is mainly used for large sheep ranches. 

The Central Region. - The central or cereal region is the real heart of Argentina.

It covers a vast, level plain, called the pampas, with loose, fertile, and easily worked soil. 

The temperature and rainfall conditions are favorable for agricultural products of the temperate zone. markets of the world.

This is one of the largest cereal and livestock regions. in the world. It might well be called the "Bread Basket of Latin America." It is the most densely populated part of the country, has the most railroads, the largest cities, the best means of communication, and is the most progressive educationally. 

Wheat, corn, barley, oats, alfalfa, and flaxseed are here cultivated on a large scale. 

Argentina is one of the seven largest wheat-producing countries in the world. 69 

This is also the main center of the cattle ranches, as well as orchards, vegetable gardens, and dairy farms. The soil is so rich and other conditions are so favorable that, with the usual farming methods and without fertilization or irrigation, wheat will yield thirty bushels to the acre, corn forty bushels, and other cereals accord- ingly. The farms are usually several hundred acres in extent, but only 30 per cent of them

Flax is grown on about the same areas as wheat and corn in Argentina. It is here grown only for seed, Argentina ranking first among the countries of the world in the production of flaxseed. What is flaxseed used for? What other name is given to flaxseed? For what else is flax used? are farmed by their owners. 

It can readily be seen that with such extensive farms and such a small population (there are only about 12,000,000 in all 

Argentina), agricultural machinery must be extensively used. Such is the case, and the per-capita production is very great.

The Livestock Industry. - 

On the pampas, where the plow has not converted the rich land into cultivated fields, the finest breeds of cattle, sheep, and horses are pastured. The grass is naturally rich. Drinking water is everywhere available and no artificial shelter necessary. 

Of course, all four regions are good for stock raising, but the pampas is the most important. The following figures will help to show the importance of livestock to the country.

South American countries lack coal, so water power is a valuable asset. Yet less than five per cent of the possible water power of any South American country has been developed and used. Nor is this stock to be thought of as representing poor varieties. The Argentine farmer takes much pride in the high breed of his cattle, sheep, horses, and swine, importing the finest stock from Europe and the United States for breeding purposes. In some respects he has even improved upon the

Manufacturing. Not only in the fields of agriculture and stock raising has progress been made. Manufacturing has made a notable start. At present it consists mainly in working over the products of farm and field. 

There are meat-packing establishments, flour mills, dairies, textile mills, and sawmills. 

Minerals. The mining industry also has made a beginning. Valuable fields of gold, silver, copper, nickel, iron, and other metals are known to exist. This wealth only awaits the coming of capital for its development. 

The Argentine government is now opening up a vast oil field in the Comodoro Rivadavia region in Patagonia. 

Commerce. The republic has ample steamship connection with Europe and the 

United States. One may get a steamer for 

Europe almost any day and for the United 

States almost any week. Around the two leading cities, Buenos Aires and Rosario, the railroad net is very thick, affording connection with almost all parts of the nation and with the neighboring countries of Chile, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia, and Brazil. In addition, the long estuary of the Rio de la 

Plata, with its navigable tributary, the Paraná River, affords excellent water com-

Exports exceed imports, a condition to be found in nearly all young agricultural countries. Of course, practically all the exports consist of agricultural, pastoral, and forest products. The imports are mainly textiles, agricultural machinery, automobiles, and iron and steel products.

Publishers' Photo Service RIVER SCENE, BUENOS AIRES 

This view shows a part of the waterfront of Buenos Aires, the capital and main port of Argentina. The many grain elevators tell us that this is an important grain exporting port. What advantages do you see in the location of this city for the export of wheat from Argentina ? 

The People of Argentina. - Immigration is encouraged to such an extent that about 150,000 annually come from foreign countries to take up land in the Argentine. These are mainly southern Europeans of hardy stock, who are helping to make the country what it is. 

There are very few native Indians in the entire country. Since foreigners now are being excluded from the United States, more will undoubtedly go to the most favorable 

South American countries. 

Education is free and compulsory, rapid strides having been made in the last few years in stamping out illiteracy. Argentina has one of the most stable governments in all Latin 

America. The recent revolution in the country should not lead us to think otherwise.

Perhaps this stability is due to her population tem, and her favorable climate. 

Leading Cities. We cannot leave this great country of the future without a few words about its two leading cities, Buenos 

Aires and Rosario. 

Aires and Rosario. Buenos Aires is truly a great capital, with about 2,100,000 people, making it the metropolis of Latin America, second only to Paris among Latin cities, and third city in size on the Western Continents. 

Buenos Aires deserves many of the good things which may be written about it. It is situated on the Rio de la Plata about 180 miles from the sea. In its well-kept parks, wide streets, beautiful buildings, transportation system, communicating facilities, and other modern conveniences, this metropolis of the Southern Hemisphere compares favorably with any city in Europe or North America. 

Rosario, second city in size, has a population of about 508,000. It is located on the 

Paraná, about 200 miles from Buenos Aires.

Railroads and river boats bring the farm and field products from the vast pampas country to the west to this city for shipment, and then distribute the machines and supplies it imports. 

Its docks have the most modern equipment for loading and unloading vessels.

Cordoba, with over 300,000 people, is a thriving city center for one of the best agricultural and stock raising areas in the interior. 

In a picturesque mountain setting, where it is easily reached by rail, it is a favorite resort for people on vacation.

HARBOR OF RIO DE JANEIRO 

This is one of the best and most beautiful harbors in the world. All the navies of the world could ride at anchor in the quiet waters of the inside harbor, and the largest merchant ships from foreign countries can moor beside its quays. 

Along the shore for miles extends a beautiful drive. At night its string of lights is like a glittering necklace. 

B. BRAZIL 

Brazil, the Largest South American 

Country. Brazil is the largest of all American countries except Canada, larger even than continental United States. It also has more people than any other Latin American country. The area is 3,286,170 square miles and the population is about 46,000,000. In fact, both the population and area of Brazil almost equal those of all other South American countries combined. She is so large that her boundaries touch every other country of 

South America except two. relations the Brazilians have had a different attitude toward the United States than is true of some of the smaller countries. Furthermore, her government was modeled very much after our own, and the people feel a kinship for us that is perhaps felt by no other 

Latin American country.

Latin American country. She is even called the United States of Brazil. 

Brazil is Portuguese, not Spanish. The people are very proud of their Portuguese ancestry, and resent the attempt of foreign business people among them to use Spanish.

POPULATION Map of SOUTH AMERICA Map Questions and Activities 

1. How do you account for the dark area around the Plata River? of the country. It stretches from near the 

Atlantic Ocean on the east to the Andes on the west, and from the temperate plains on the south to the tropical forests and jungles of the Amazon Valley. On this interior plateau, which is about half a mile above sea level, many of the progressive people of the country are to be found. The plains in the south, however, with their temperate climate, are attracting many European settlers. 

Waterways. Brazil has the most remarkable system of natural waterways of any country in the world. The Amazon alone is the world's largest river. Together with its numerous tributaries, it provides thousands of miles of navigable waterways. Its main stream is used by ocean vessels as far as Iquitos in Peru. The only way one can travel conveniently in much of Brazil is by water or air.

Climate. The Amazon Valley, like the 

Congo Valley in Africa, has the true equatorial type of climate, which is found from about 10 degrees north of the equator to about 10 degrees south. The temperature, as a rule, is uniformly high the year round; how high, of course, depends upon the altitude. For instance, if the average temperature for January is 70°, then the average temperature for July will be about the same. 

This uniformity in temperature is due to the fact that the sun does not vary much in altitude and that the days and nights are 2. Explain the large white area in the interior of nearly equal in length. The nights are much Brazil.

The rainfall, however, is variable, there being two rainy seasons and two relatively dry seasons. The rainy seasons occur when the belt of equatorial calms passes over the region. The relatively dry seasons occur when the trade winds are blowing over the 

That the dry seasons are not wholly dry is shown by the fact that this is the home of the equatorial rain forest. area.

NATIVES AND WHITE MEN IN THE EQUATORIAL RAIN FORESTS 77 

Equatorial Forests. These forests have high temperature and abundant moisture at practically all seasons, so that trees grow rapidly and the foliage is very dense. The moist, continued heat is very enervating and malaria and other tropical diseases abound. 

All kinds of insects and vermin flourish, so that life is almost unbearable to those accustomed to a temperate climate. 

A great handicap in equatorial forests is the difficulty of keeping domestic animals even in the clearings. Noxious insects plague the animals, and what little grass can grow in the forest is usually so coarse that it is not nutritious.

Another handicap is the difficulty of transportation. Explorers encounter swamps, huge projecting roots, dense thickets, and other obstructions as bad as anything our ancestors met when they first settled in America. They are also likely to be attacked by wild beasts as well as by poisonous snakes and insects. Even if a road is cleared, it is soon overgrown by new plants which spring up almost overnight and grow ten to twenty feet in a single year. If not kept open, trails are completely covered with vegetation in two or three years. A macadam road or even a railroad may suffer the same fate, though of course more slowly.

Where communication is so difficult, people naturally can profit little by intercourse with others, who bring new methods and ideas. 

Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, and Colombia as well as the interior of Brazil suffer greatly because the equatorial forests, which begin on the eastern slope of the Andes, hamper communication with the Atlantic side of the continent and, therefore, with Europe.

Difficulties of Agriculture.¹-Still another and even greater handicap of the equatorial forest is the difficulty of carrying on agriculture. The American pioneers had smaller 

1 Adapted from Principles of Human Geography, by Dr. Ellsworth Huntington. trees to deal with than those of the tropics. 

They cut mostly pine, birch, beech, and other woods easy to work, and not mahogany, teak, rosewood, and other tropical species harder than oak. Then, too, they did most of their work in the cool bracing autumn or in winter when a man wants to work fast in order to keep warm. Suppose they had had to cut oak trees on the muggiest kind of hot summer days!

After the trees have been cleared away, the difficulties of the would-be farmer in the equatorial rain forests have only begun. It is hard work to keep down the weeds on our farms, but suppose the weeds grew a foot or two a month twelve months in the year. How could any one keep them down! This is what makes agriculture so difficult in such a region. 

Natives and White Men in the Equatorial 

Rain Forests. As a result of all these obstacles the Indians of the Amazon basin have made little progress. They live by hunting with poisoned arrows and hide at the sight of a stranger. Yet we ought to pity them, for even we, with all our advantages, have not yet learned how to cultivate the lands in the equatorial forest, maintain good roads, and avoid the enervating effects upon health and character. We do these things in the Panama Zone where many people are gathered in a small space, where vast sums of money are available, and where every one is under government orders, but that is very different from the ordinary forest region. 

Vegetation grows so rapidly in these hot rainy regions that they might be the most productive parts of the world if men knew how to cultivate them. The only products we obtain from them now, however, are rubber, chicle gum, quinine, mahogany, and other forest products. The natives are employed by the white man to search for the trees which supply these products. In temperate climates trees of one kind often cover

LATIN AMERICA 

many square miles, but in the tropics many species usually grow together. The natives wander through the forests, climbing tall trees to look out over the top of the forest and pick out specimens of the trees they are seeking. 

These tropical forests, known as selvas in Brazil, contain valuable cabinet- and dyewoods, but they are not so valuable for lumber because the wood is very hard. The Brazil nuts sold in our country come from tall trees which grow in these forests. 

PICKING COFFEE IN BRAZIL 

Ewing Galloway

Boys and girls as well as older people work at this task. 

Most of our coffee comes from Brazil. When you see someone drinking coffee, think of the boys and girls in Brazil who have probably shared in the work of preparing it. About how high are these coffee trees?

Much of tropical Brazil is fairly high and so is cooler, while part of southern Brazil is within the temperate zone. The two zones, combined with the variety of relief and soils, enable the people to produce nearly all the different kinds of products of the temperate and tropical regions of the world. 

Agricultural Products. As you can well imagine, outside the forest belt, agriculture holds first place among the industries. The people not only produce enough for themselves but also a surplus for export. The leading products are coffee, rubber, sugar, cacao, manioc, cotton, rice, yerba maté (page 

93), and tobacco. The two most valuable exports are coffee and cotton. 

The coffee tree is not native to Brazil, having been named after Kaffa in Ethiopia, in which country it is supposed to have originated. It requires a deep rich soil and a plentiful amount of rainfall, from 75 to 150 inches per year. inches per year. The soil must also be well drained. The tree also requires a certain amount of shade, which is furnished by other types of trees. The coffee tree naturally grows to a height of about twenty feet, but is kept down to six or eight feet for convenience in picking.

When a coffee plantation is in bloom, usually sometime in October, it presents a wonderful sight. Harvest time does not come until May or June. Then all other work is suspended because it is important that the berries be picked at just the right time. The chief centers for coffee are the states of Rio de 

Janeiro, Minas Geraes, and São Paulo. 

Santos is the leading shipping port, more coffee leaving its docks than from any other place in the world. Brazil furnishes the world with nearly two-thirds of its coffee. 

The United States is her best customer, because we drink so much coffee. 

Brazil also makes us think of rubber, largely because it probably came from there originally, where it grew wild. Most of the world's rubber now comes from the cultivated plantations of the Far East. The rubber trees of Brazil occur singly rather than in clusters or continuous stands. The rubber gatherer cuts a path through the tropical forests, visits one tree after another, and then at some central point prepares the latex for market. Brazil has the soil and the climate for rubber. Some day she may become a leading rubber-producing country by the plantation method. Recently, Mr. Henry

Ford revealed plans to start a rubber empire of 5,000,000 acres in the Amazon Valley. 

Unless labor conditions are too unfavorable, it seems reasonable to expect that the area which once furnished the world with most of

U. S. Department of Agriculture DISTRIBUTION OF THE WORLD'S COFFEE 

Brazil is so far ahead of other coffee producers that she almost overshadows them. However, Venezuela, Colombia, Central America, the West Indies, Ethiopia, Arabia, India, and 

Java all produce coffee. its wild rubber will again come into its own with plantation rubber.

Only a small part of the rich agricultural land has been brought under cultivation. 

Cotton growing has been encouraged by the government, and Brazil ranks high among the sugar cane producing countries of the world. The annual value of agricultural products is about $1,000,000,000. 

Mineral Wealth. Brazil is a country of vast undeveloped resources of all kinds. 

There are said to be whole mountains of iron ore in the state of Minas Geraes awaiting development. In addition, gold and diamond mines are being worked. Large quantities of manganese ore are exported to the United States. Brazil does not seem to be very rich in coal, but some is mined in the state of Rio Grande do Sul in the south.

Such a large territory probably also has vast quantities of other minerals either undiscovered or unused as yet. Oil has been found in several states but has not been extensively developed. 

Transportation Facilities. The people are very unevenly distributed, most of them being concentrated near the Atlantic Ocean.

A railroad map shows this concentration well, SANTOS, BRAZIL 

James Sawders

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH BRAZIL 

Notice that the value of imports from Brazil is about twice that of the exports. There has been a marked change in our trade with that country since 1929. The averages for the years 1930 up to 1941 are: imports, $98,000,000; exports, $34,000,000. because most of the railroads are found in the east, connecting either coastal cities with the interior or interior cities with each other.

There are about 20,700 miles under operation. 

The automobile is rapidly coming into the country, and this has led to the construction of good roads. There are about 120,000 miles of first and second class roads. Speaking of transportation, let us not forget the thousands of miles of internal waterways. 

There are also good connections with the outside world, steamship lines from the leading ports of Europe and the United States calling regularly at San Salvador, Recife, Rio de

Janeiro, Santos, and other ports. The coastwise steamship service is also good. For the well-populated districts, postal, telegraph, and telephone systems are fairly well developed and are being constantly extended. 

Manufacturing. Since 1914 Brazil has made some progress in making the things she needs, due to the fact that she was cut off from her supplies during the World War. 

This is particularly true of textiles, especially cotton goods. It applies also to leather, matches, hats, clothing, furniture, cement, iron, steel, chemicals, and paper. 

Brazil has almost everything needed for manufacturing, including a population large enough for a good home market. Manufacturing suggests the problem of power. She may not have much coal or petroleum, but she does have enormous water power sites and resources. The falls of the Iguassú on the Argentine border are very beautiful and offer immense possibilities for extensive hydroelectric development. 

Education. Education, too, is making rapid progress. There are free primary schools with courses of three to five years in length, attendance at which is compulsory.

Brazil declared its independence from Portugal in 1822. 

To commemorate the event, this monument was erected - another good example of the sculptor's art which has helped to beautify the South American cities.

LEADING CITIES OF BRAZIL 

81 Secondary schools are growing in number, because she has the resources, the climate, and there are several universities. the area, and the people. And, as time goes on, she will undoubtedly receive an increasing immigration from Europe. 

. 

Leading Cities. Rio is considered to be one of the most beautiful cities in the world.

Certainly it has one of the most magnificent harbors, being ranked along with Naples, Lisbon, Sydney, and San Francisco. The name means" River of January," given to it by its discoverer, Sousa. It has about 

1,800,000 people, making it the largest city in 

Brazil, the second city in Latin America, and the largest Portuguese-speaking city in the entire world. Not only is the city naturally beautiful, but strenuous efforts have been made to develop it along artistic lines. 

The broad Avenida Rio Branco, running through the heart of the city from north to south, is lined with beautiful private and. 

public buildings. The Avenida Beira Mar skirts the shore of the bay for over four miles and is said to be one of the finest shore drives in the world. The bay itself extends in a huge semicircle lined with palms, with a background of low mountains. Sanitary and health conditions are now of the best, the death rate having been much reduced. 

The second city in size and importance is São Paulo, capital of the state of the same name, and metropolis of the biggest coffee district in the whole world. It has a considerable elevation above sea level and is a modern, hustling, progressive city in every respect. Wide streets, beautiful public buildings, spacious parks, and modern conveniences give São Paulo a high rank among 

Brazilian cities. Like many other inland cities, it is an important railway center. 

Santos, forty miles away, is its port and the greatest coffee-shipping place in the world.

Other cities of more or less importance are Manáos, the metropolis of the Amazon, Belem (Para), the big rubber port, Recife (Pernambuco), an important stop on air routes, San 

Salvador (Bahia), and Porto Alegre.
