LATIN AMERICA 

VALPARAISO, CHILE 

Courtesy Pan American Union

Valparaiso is built on the shores of a bay partly enclosed by steep-sided hills which form a most attractive setting. 

Business houses are built mainly on the narrow lowland along the water while the residences are built on the slopes in the background. Lifts, sometimes running at an angle of 45 degrees, take the people up and down. The bay is exposed to strong northerly winds which often blow during July and August, and expensive breakwaters have been built to protect vessels lying at anchor in the harbor. 

C. CHILE 

The A B C Countries. When the news papers speak of the A B C countries of South 

America, they are referring to Argentina,

Brazil, and Chile. These three countries are grouped together because they are the most progressive countries in Latin America, and take a prominent part in international affairs. 

Have you ever noticed that these three countries are either located in or have considerable areas in the 

South Temperate Zone? What significance should be attached to this fact? When do the seasons occur in the South Temperate Zone?

The Land of Chile. - Chile has been called the "California of South America,' although it is an independent country and not a part of another. (Look at the maps showing the two to see why such a comparison is made.) and has an exceptionally wide range of latitude. It is 2600 miles long from north to south. From east to west the country is very narrow, having an average width of about 70 miles. For the size of the country (about the area of Texas), the coast line is very long.

Topographically, Chile is naturally divided longitudinally (lengthwise) into the lofty 

Andes, the coastal ranges, and the valley between. Industrially and climatically, however, we might better divide it crosswise into 

Northern Chile, Central Chile, and Southern

Chile. The country has a population of about 4,600,000.

Compare the areas and populations of the three 

A B C countries. 

Climate. The climate varies very much with the latitude and altitude. North of about 26° south latitude, we find one of the Chile is on the west coast of South America world's driest deserts. The dryness is due

NITRATE OF SODA FROM CHILE 

mainly to the fact that the land is warmer than the cool Pacific winds which blow over it, and therefore the moisture does not condense. Rainfall from the Atlantic is shut off by the high Andes. This desert, called the 

Atacama Desert, is famous for its nitrate deposits.

Central Chile is very much like our own San Diego and Los Angeles districts. The summers are warm and dry, the winters mild and comparatively wet. At Valparaiso, for instance, the annual rainfall is about 20 inches. Central Chile is noted for the extraordinary amount of clear sky and sunshine which it enjoys. 

The southern part is cool and wet. Valdivia, for instance, has an annual rainfall of 

107 inches. 

The summer months are December, January, and February. The winter months are 

June, July, and August. Doesn't that sound strange? We should always remember that the seasons in the northern and southern hemispheres are reversed. (Can you think of any important commercial consequences of this fact?) The climate is so mild that there is very little snowfall in the northern two-thirds of the country except in the mountains.

Mountains, Rivers, and Lakes. - From many points in the central valley, observers may obtain a beautiful view of snow-capped peaks. Active and extinct volcanoes are both found in the southern region. The peak of 

Mt. Aconcagua, the highest mountain in South 

America, rises just across the border in Argentina, but the mountain itself spreads over into Chile and gives its name to a province as well as to a river. The rivers in Chile are short and swift, being little used for navigation but extensively employed for irrigation and water supply. The southern section has a fiorded coast like Norway, and many magnificent lakes like Switzerland. It is sometimes referred to as the "Switzerland of South America." 8883 

Courtesy Chile Publishing Company

HIGH RAILROAD BRIDGE IN CHILE 

Railroad building in a mountainous country like Chile is difficult and expensive, but the country now has over 5000 miles of track and is building more each year. 

Many bridges are required both for railroads and for auto- . mobile highways. 

Mineral Resources. As Argentina stands for cereals and livestock, and Brazil for coffee and rubber, Chile stands for minerals, notably nitrates and copper. It is one of the richest mineral countries on the globe. 

Minerals occur almost everywhere, but mainly in the northern and central divisions. Nitrates, copper, gold, silver, sulphur, borax, potash, lead, zinc, cobalt, manganese, and others occur in abundance. Some of the mines have been worked for centuries. 

Nitrate of Soda. - Plants require for their best growth three essential elements in the soil, potash, phosphates, and nitrates. Until the last few years, Germany has furnished the potash, the United States the phosphates, and Chile the nitrates for the rest of the world. 

Chile is the only country in the world where sodium nitrate or saltpeter exists in workable quantities. The ultimate reserves are said to be over 400,000,000 tons. This is enough to supply the probable needs of the agricultural world for many years to come.

The nitrates are the greatest asset of Chile because the government owns and leases the deposits. They help the country to pay for things which are imported. They furnish more than one-third of the revenue for carry-

ing on the government and they help pay the interest on foreign debts. They give work directly to 100,000 men and indirectly to 250,000, thus supporting perhaps one-fourth of the population. The nitrate region furnishes a market for the agricultural products of the central region. Many towns, both inland and coastal, owe their start and continuance to this one resource. Chilean home and foreign commerce would be much smaller without it.

The nitrate zone is about as big as the state of Nebraska. The principal deposits lie back from the coast at a considerable elevation. The region is an absolute desert. 

Not a blade of grass springs from the soil unless it is irrigated.

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH CHILE 

There has been a marked change in our trade with Chile since 1929. The averages for the years 1930 up to 1941 are: imports, $26,000,000; exports, $16,000,000. 

Thus above one of the most fertilizing elements in the world, there is no vegetation to be found! The two producing provinces are called Tarapacá and Antofagasta. 

The mineral, which occurs in patches mixed with a number of other minerals and earthy materials, is first dynamited. It is then loaded on small cars and taken to the central refining plant. Here it is put into huge vats with water, separated from the other minerals, and allowed to crystallize, after which it is put into bags for shipment. Iodine is obtained as a very valuable by-product. 

About 1,200,000 tons of nitrates are exported each year.

Our own country needs the nitrates of Chile, and Chile needs our capital and manufactured goods. Our cotton and corn crops especially need the life-giving saltpeter, and there is very little reason to suppose that we can get it all from the air by the newer electrical processes.

LATIN AMERICA 

United States capital has been attracted to the development of the Chilean copper mines. The mines have been worked for centuries, but it is only in the last few years under the stimulus of 

United States capital that modern methods and processes have been employed. 

Chile also produces iron, coal, salt, gypsum, and guano (an animal fertilizer). and sheep are pastured and fed from the products of the farms. Extensive vineyards and fruit orchards are also found in this far-famed central agricultural valley, situated between the Andes and the 

Coastal Ranges. It is estimated that Chile could support 20,000,- 

000 people with the complete utilization of her agricultural and other resources. 

The southern third of

Chile has remarkable forest reserves of both hard and soft woods which have barely been 

Courtesy Chile Publishing Company

ONIONS PACKED FOR EXPORT 

Large quantities of these fine large onions are grown in the central valley of Chile where market gardening is a big industry. The United States imports several million pounds of onions from Chile each year. The production of coal in the southern provinces is very interesting for two reasons. 

First, it is one of the few South American countries with extensive coal deposits. Second, some of the coal is actually mined beneath the waters of the ocean. 

Agricultural Resources. 

As one might expect, Chilean agricultural products are extensive and varied. They range from semi-tropical products in the north to temperate products in the south. 

The principal agricultural region is, of course, the central region around Santiago and 

Valparaiso. Here, soil and climate, irrigation and population, all combine to make one of the richest agricultural regions in South America.

The principal products are wheat, oats, barley, and other cereals, alfalfa, hay, clover, and other forage crops, vegetables, fruits, and dairy products. Horses, cattle, mules,

LIMIT 

WET LIMIT

The crop is limited on the northeast by heat and moisture, and on the west by dryness. Cattle, sheep, and corn crowd wheat out of some of the areas where it could be grown. 

What do "dry limit" and "wet limit" mean in Chile? touched for commercial pur- 

poses. Some of this wood could be used for wood pulp, fence posts, railway ties, telephone poles, and firewood. 

Manufacturing. Nor has local manufacturing been neglected. There are about 

19,000 manufacturing establishments with over 180,000 employees making textiles, shoes, paper, and many other useful articles. When Chile was cut off from foreign trade to a considerable extent by the World War, the home industries, like those of Brazil, took on a new lease of life. It is probably not the desire of the 

Chileans to make themselves independent of the outside world, but they undoubtedly could be if they so wished. 

Transportation and Communication. Having such a

THE CHILEAN PEOPLE 

long coast line, Chile is essentially a maritime country. It has extensive coastwise service from the central agricultural region to the northern mineral district and the southern pastoral and lumbering region. 

Chile has over 5000 miles of railroads, with a main railroad line running north and south from which there are short branches to the mountains in the east, and the coast in the west.

The Government is busily constructing new highways, of which there are now over 23,000 miles. Airplanes give daily service within the country and also afford quick transport to and from other countries. 

The Chilean People. - The people of Chile are energetic and ambitious, being mostly of European origin. Even the native 

Araucanian Indians are said to be the most vigorous of all Latin American natives. 

Immigration from Europe is being encouraged by the government. 89 Education, supported by the state, is free and compulsory, although there is still a relatively high percentage of illiteracy. Many of the teachers have come from Germany and the United States. These teachers have installed modern methods and modern systems. 

There are normal, secondary and commercial schools; agricultural and professional schools; schools of mines, music and art; a school for the deaf and one for the blind. The three universities are located at Concepcion and Santiago. 

Certainly Argentina, Brazil, and Chile are among the most progressive of Latin 

American countries. May we not look forward to increasing friendship and mutually beneficial financial and trade relationships with them? 

If you were to take a trip to South America, what points of interest would you look for in these three countries?

D. URUGUAY URUGUAY'S NEW CAPITOL 

Uruguayans may well be proud of this beautiful structure. the south temperate zone. The climate is invigorating and stimulating, with a mean summer temperature of about 72° F. and a mean winter one of 55° F. (Name the summer and winter months.) A fair tribute to the qualities of the climate is shown by the fact Uruguay is the smallest republic in South America, having an area slightly larger than that of the combined New England States. 

The population is about 2,000,000. 

Like the A B C countries, Uruguay is in that the country is used as a summer resort

TRANSPORTATION IN URUGUAY 

by Argentines and Brazilians. The rainfall is about 37 inches and is fairly well distributed throughout the year.

The Land and the Industries. - Broad undulating plains make up almost the entire country, with a low mountain range in the north. Uruguay has less waste land than almost any country in the world, it being estimated that 90 per cent of the country could be used for farm land.

Thus it is mainly a pastoral and agricultural country. The raising of livestock always has been and still is the chief occupation of the people. Just how important livestock is to the country is shown by the fact that almost all her exports consist of animal products. The principal ones are wool, hides, meats and extracts, grease and tallow, and live animals. The remainder of the exports are made up of oil-producing grains and flour. 91 three. Already wheat is an important agricultural crop, followed by corn, flax, oats, barley, alfalfa, linseed, and birdseed. The growing of tobacco and of grapes for wine give promise of much future extension.

Uruguay has few metal ores but many other

Uruguay? Why?

It is a wonderful sight to see thousands of heads of blooded cattle grazing over the gently rolling plains of this beautiful South American pastoral country. In the number of sheep and cattle to the square mile, Uruguay ranks among the leaders of the world.

There is some farming in Uruguay and this will increase more and more as time goes on. Grazing will give way to the production of cereals to feed the industrial people of 

Europe, the United States, or South American countries. This is a perfectly natural evolution in the development of a young country, first the pastoral, next the agricultural, and finally the industrial or a combination of all In

There are forests in the north which could be used for the development of the lumber industry. 

Transportation. The republic has about 

1900 miles of railroads, mostly British-owned, connecting places in all parts of the country. 

Highways are being rapidly extended, also air service and other modern means of communication. There are about 700 miles of navigable waterways furnished by the Rio de la Plata, the Paraná, and Uruguay rivers.

LATIN AMERICA 

Communication with the outside world is adequate. Ocean-going vessels from Europe,

North America, and other ports of Latin 

America make almost daily calls at the metropolis, Montevideo. 

The People. The people of this little country are progressive, being almost entirely of European extraction. They are very one-half miles long, is a famous street of the capital, bordered by many important buildings and handsome residences. The city is noted for its seaside resorts and modern parks. One of the most popular of the watering places is known as the Playa Ramirez.

The Solis Theater is said to be one of the most beautiful in South America, attracting worldfamous performers, for opera and drama.

Public monuments are found all over the city, commemorating important historical events. Like Rio de Janeiro in Brazil,

Valparaiso in Chile, and Buenos Aires in Argentina, it would be a city well worth visiting when you get ready to do a little traveling.

Montevideo. Montevideo, the capital and center of almost all activities in Uruguay, is one of the beautiful cities of South America.

It contains over one-fourth of the population of the country, and is the leading port with all modern shipping facilities. Three-fourths of the import and export trade passes through it. The Avenida 18 de Julio, about two and

E. PARAGUAY 

Paraguay is one of two countries of South 

America which do not touch the ocean.

It is probably the least important South American country, but it has great possibilities for future growth. This republic has an area of 176,000 square miles, and a population of about 1,000,000. It is surrounded by Brazil, Argentina, and Bolivia, but it has communication with the outside world by water and rail.

Transportation Facilities and Resources. - 

River steamers go up the Rio de la Plata to the Paraná, then up the Paraná to the 

Paraguay, up which they can go almost to the Brazilian line. Steamers make regular trips from Buenos Aires and Montevideo to 

Asunción, the capital. A railroad also runs from Buenos Aires to Asunción, partly in

YERBA MATÉ 

93 11102 

ASUNCIÓN, THE CAPITAL OF PARAGUAY 

Ewing Galloway

Asunción is an interior city situated on a small bay connected with the Paraguay River near its junction with the Pilcomayo. The Paraguay River is deep enough for large steamers and is the main artery of transportation for this part of South America. A railroad also connects Asunción with Buenos Aires and air service is maintained between the two cities. The buildings are generally low but some of them are very attractive, and patios, adorned with flowers and shrubbery, and even fountains, often make up for the plain exteriors of the houses. The climate is milder here than further south and this city is popular as a winter resort. 

Argentina and partly in Paraguay. The river trip requires about five days while the rail trip may be made in about two.

Paraguay is about two-thirds in the temperate zone, and one-third in the tropics. 

Being inland, however, it tends more to take on tropical characteristics, modified somewhat by several mountain chains and an extensive system of waterways. Most of the products of the two zones may be raised, but especially those of the tropics. The principal items of export are yerba maté, quebracho, oranges, timber, hides, dried beef, and tobacco. The principal imports are, of course, manufactured goods. 

Yerba Maté. By far the principal crop is native to the country and is called yerba maté (yer'ba mǎ'tā) or Paraguay tea. About 

30,000,000 pounds are produced annually, and maté drinkers are said to be increasing in numbers. It is almost a national drink in some of the South American countries. Its use is also said to be increasing in Europe and the United States.

It resembles both tea and coffee in containing tannin and caffein, the latter giving it stimulating qualities. It is made as we make tea and may be drunk while hot or cold. The native Indians, called Guarani, knew and used the plant long before the Spanish conquered the country. 

The tree itself grows wild, sometimes to a height of thirty feet. It has many branches covered with beautiful green leaves, which resemble those of the orange tree. The maté gatherers go into the forests with hatchets and chop off the best branches. After a day's cutting has been secured, huge bundles are made out of the branches and are carried home on the workers' heads. The leaves are

LATIN AMERICA 

Ewing Galloway

CUTTING YERBA MATE IN PARAGUAY 

On the hillsides grows an aromatic, evergreen shrub, the leaves and stems of which, when cured, produce a powder. The powder is then dissolved in hot water which makes a sort of tea, a favorite beverage of the natives of South America. Considerable quantities are now exported to Europe, where some of the people have cultivated a taste for it. cured by smoking for about twenty-four hours. Afterward they are stripped from the branches, pulverized, and placed in sacks ready for shipment.

The immense forests which furnish the maté are found also in southern Brazil and northern Argentina, but Paraguay is the main source of supply. Wild forests have in the past furnished the main supply and will continue to do so for some time. However, cultivation of maté plantations has started and, as drinkers increase in numbers, we may expect to see huge maté plantations as we now have coffee and tea plantations. 

Quebracho. Quebracho is also grown in

Paraguay, but as we have described that industry so extensively in the section on Argentina, it need not concern us here. The tree is found in the same condition and yields the same products as in Argentina, but the latter produces by far the most. 

Other Products. The soil and climate of Paraguay are well suited to the production of high-grade tobacco. Already it is an item in export trade. With the introduction of good seed, a larger population, and better methods of curing, it should become more important. The country is also adapted to the growth of cotton, and its possibilities as a producer are said to be great. The yield per acre would be high because the cotton has a long silky fiber, much desired by the weaving countries. Paraguayan hand-made lace enjoys an excellent reputation in other countries. Oranges grow in wild profusion, often in such abundance that they are fed to swine. The livestock industry is growing, cattlemen from other countries having located in the country in recent years.

Paraguay has the resources of all kinds to make her a more important country in South American economy. She lacks the people. 

Perhaps in the next few years, European immigration will make up this deficiency and this little republic will come into its own.

F. THE LAND OF THE INCAS 

Civilization of the Incas. Where one now finds the republics of Peru, Bolivia, and Ecuador, there once flourished one of the most remarkable civilizations of history - that of the Incas. Imagine the surprise of the conquering Spaniards, when they found in this 95 unknown land an Indian people who had attained a high position in the applied arts, in agriculture, and in their social relations all this among the high table-lands, valleys, and mountain slopes of the lofty Andes as well as along the coastal regions. At the height of the Inca power, there may have been 10,000,000 people living under a government in many ways superior to the Spanish government which replaced it. 

Centers of Inca Culture. The leading centers of this old and interesting culture were 

Cuzco and Quito, the latter now the capital of

Ecuador. These centers were connected by famous roads both along the coast and through the interior. There were also roads. leading from Cuzco to all parts of the empire. 

For Cuzco was the main seat of government, with its beautiful temples and palaces, and its gorgeous displays of gold and silver ornaments. It is situated in the valley of the 

Urubamba River at an altitude of 12,000 feet.

The present city has a population of about 

40,000. Here one may find many ruins of the ancient Inca culture, many of them remarkably well preserved.

The stones in their walls and buildings were very hard and were shaped like polygons. 

Many were of such huge size, so well cut, and so well placed together without the aid of mortar, that one marvels at the wonderful skill of the ancient builders. The stones were cut and placed so perfectly that it is said that a knife blade cannot be inserted between them. One is reminded of the feats performed by the ancient Egyptians in building the Pyramids.

The Laws of the Incas. The laws of the 

Incas were very just. There was little poverty, and the people were contented. 

The land was all carefully surveyed, and then divided among the people. It was not owned outright, however, but was cultivated for the common good. All natural resources were held in common.

PERU AND ITS CAPITAL 

Most business transactions were carried out in goods instead of in money. Gold and silver were used to adorn the temples and palaces, and for making dishes and ornaments. 

Copper was more highly prized than gold, because out of it they fashioned their working tools. 

Hanging Gardens of Peru. - Have you ever heard of the "Hanging Gardens of 

Babylon"? The Peruvian Indians, too, had hanging gardens. These were beautifully 97 terraced hillslopes, sometimes extending from down in the valleys up to the mountain tops among the clouds. On the level parts of these steps or terraces were raised the muchneeded crops. Soil was scarce in some parts of Inca land so that much of it had to be carried from places where it was useless to places where it could be made to produce crops. In this way they were able to support a population two or three times as large as that of to-day. 

Cuzco, PERU 

From a great Inca Throne one looks out over a city that in the eleventh century was the capital of a vast empire. 

Pizarro destroyed it in 1535, but some of its former glory is still in evidence in the palace of the Incas and a stupen dous Temple of the Sun. 

1. PERU 

Peru and Its Capital. -The largest country to be formed out of the former lands of the Incas was Peru, which has an area of approximately 524,000 square miles, and an estimated population of 6,000,000. Lima, the "City of the Kings," is the capital. It was founded by Pizarro in 1535 and has been the seat of government of this region ever since. In fact, for nearly 200 years this "City of the Kings" was the center of Spanish power in all South America.

Attracted by the gold and silver in the country, Spaniards came in thousands. From here they made settlements to the south in Chile, to the southeast in Bolivia, Paraguay, Uruguay, and Argentina, and to the north in 

Ecuador and Colombia. The Governor of 

Peru was said to have been almost as powerful as the King of Spain himself. To this center came the products of all South America

LATIN AMERICA 

to be exchanged for goods brought from foundation stones are said to have been laid Spain.

Lima is located in the valley of the River Rimac, a mountain stream which furnishes water for all purposes, especially irrigation.

The city is much nearer the Pacific coast than were the old Inca centers, being only eight miles from Callao, the leading port of the country. Callao is a city of about 60,000 people, situated about midway on the Peruvian coast. It has ample harbor accommo- 

COTTON IN PERU 

Ewing Galloway

The Peruvian cotton grows as high as your head. It is grown under irrigation along the coast. Peruvian cotton with a crinkly fiber is very much in demand for mixing with wool in the manufacture of mixed cotton and woolen goods. What other product does this Peruvian gardener produce? dations, and docking facilities for both passenger and freight vessels. It is so close to 

Lima that it is sometimes called the downtown section of Lima. It is, of course, connected with the capital by modern means of transportation. 

Like most of the capitals of the South

American republics, Lima is fashioned after the Spanish capital of Madrid, with its central plaza. In Lima this is called the 

Plaza Mayor, and from it streets radiate to all parts of the city. It is a large park around which are grouped many historical buildings, including the beautiful cathedral, whose by Pizarro himself. Pizarro's mummy reposes here and may be seen by the visitor. 

Nearly a century before Harvard University was founded in North America, the University of San Marcos, modeled after Salamanca

University in Spain, was established in Peru. 

It now has all the departments of a great school of learning. Lima also contains a national museum where relics of all the older civilizations of the country may be seen by the visitor. 

Three Regions in Peru. - Peru is divided naturally into three distinct physical regions. 

First, there is the dry zone, which extends along the coast to the foot of the Andes; second, the high plateaus between the three ranges of the Andes; third, the montana section, which extends over into the Amazon basin with its luxuriant vegetation. Let us consider each one of these natural regions in more detail. 

The Dry Zone. The dry coastal region would be unable to support any people were it not for the irrigation afforded by the many streams which reach back to the snowclad mountains. As it is, one finds along this coastal strip many beautifully irrigated valleys, which yield such valuable products as sugar, cotton, and rice.

The larger part of the cotton is exported. 

Peruvian cotton has an excellent reputation in foreign markets on account of its long crinkly fiber which may be mixed with wool in making cloth.

Most of the coastal towns have developed at the ends of irrigated river valleys where harbors make ocean shipping possible. The coastal region is dry because it is on the western side of the lofty Andes, on whose eastern slopes southeast trade winds lose their moisture. The cold Peruvian current, which comes from the Antarctic and flows northward along the coast, brings with it cool air, but this cannot give up its moisture because of

The Sierras. It is in the sierras or mountain regions that one finds the great mineral wealth of Peru. You remember that it was

WHERE THE TRAIN WAITS 

James Sawders

These highland valleys and plateaus also have farms and grazing lands. You recall that the Incas cultivated these places. 

There are estimated to be 12,000,000 sheep, 1,500,000 alpacas, and 500,000 llamas in the country, with an annual wool clip of nearly 

15,000,000 pounds. 

Have you ever seen a llama? He is to the people of the Andes what the camel is to the Bedouin of the Sahara Desert and what the reindeer is to the Lapp of the frozen tundras of Europe. He furnishes food, clothing, shelter, materials for tools, and a means of transportation. Llama meat is good to eat, and good yarn may be made from the wool. One of the common sights of the Andean lands is the long caravans of llama pack trains. The llama is a peculiar little beast of burden, refusing to carry more than about 80 pounds on his back. 

The Montana. The montana is a tropical forest region in the eastern part of the republic. 

It is a section of the Amazon Basin whose resources have been barely touched. In contrast with the coastal strip, the rainfall is Copper ingots being loaded on to flat cars on their way from Cerro de Pasco to the port of Callao and waiting ships. The country continues to produce these precious metals and in addition many others. The large output of copper comes mainly from the rich Cerro de Pasco mines, owned by United States capitalists. 

These mines, which are among the largest copper mines in the world, are situated at an altitude of over 12,000 feet above the level of the sea. They are reached by a railroad from Oroya.

Peru ranks fourth among the nations in the production of silver, and also ranks high in the production of petroleum. She is the world's leading producer of vanadium, and has an abundant supply of sub-bituminous coal. 

Many other minerals are produced in smaller amounts. wet season and one dry season. The forests furnish cabinet and dyewoods, but are most valuable for their rubber production. The whole area lacks transportation other than that furnished by the numerous streams. 

Iquitos, the rubber center, is over 2000 miles up the Amazon River, but may be reached by ocean vessels. 

Coca and Cinchona Bark. - Two very interesting products of Peru are coca, from which cocaine is made, and cinchona or Peruvian bark. From the latter, quinine, the medicine for treating malarial fever, is secured. The natives had been chewing the bark for centuries, knowing that in some mysterious way it cured them of the fever. 

Now it is grown and manufactured on a large scale, not only here but in other parts of the world, especially the Far East.

AREQUIPA AT THE FOOT OF EL MISTI 

This second largest city in Peru is the stopping place of all planes traveling commercially up and down the coast, and of the train from Mollendo to Cuzco. It is located 7500 feet above sea level, and the white-tipped volcano towers over it 13,000 feet more.

SUNDAY MARKET IN A PERUVIAN TOWN 

Ewing Galloway

Most of the people in this town are Peruvian Indians. The Indians generally work six days a week, and then do their shopping on Sunday. The producers have brought their goods and have them on display in the street. 

Any one wishing to buy a certain article must walk around until he finds it. 

Storekeepers have a poor chance in this town. 

Boundary Disputes. - Peru has had many boundary disputes with all of her neighbors, Colombia, Ecuador, Brazil, Bolivia, and Chile.

The most serious difficulty has been with 

Chile. (Have you ever heard of the Tacna- 

Arica dispute between Peru and Chile?) 

Before 1879 the southern boundary of Peru was what is now the southern boundary of the Chilean province of Tarapaca. South of that, Bolivia held what is now the Chilean province of Antofagasta. Because of their richness in nitrates, Chile claimed the two provinces and also Tacna-Arica still farther north. The conflicting claims of Peru, Bolivia, and Chile finally led to war in 1879. The superior navy and army of the Chileans gave them an overwhelming victory. Antofagasta, Tarapaca, and Tacna-Arica were added to Chilean territory, with the understanding, however, that within ten years after the treaty of peace was signed (1883), the people of Tacna-Arica should be allowed to decide which country they wanted to join. The plebiscite was never held and, as a result, great bitterness existed between the two countries, and they were on the verge of war several times. The President of the United States was asked to help settle this dispute, but his efforts met with very little success. A settlement, however, has finally been made, quite satisfactory to all concerned.

The Government of Peru. 

-The Peruvian government is modeled after our own, although revolutions have been rather frequent in the past. There are over 2800 miles of railroads, some of them of a type the most difficult in the world to construct. 

Efforts are being made to make the country modern, including the introduction of sanitation in the cities, a better educational system, improved highways, more railroads, and the building of vast irrigation systems.

The finances of the country have been put on a stable basis by the help of an American, Professor Edwin W. Kemmerer of Princeton University, an economist who has done similar work for other Latin American countries. Peru is a rich country and should have a good future.

LA PAZ, BOLIVIA 

Although it is in a valley, surrounded by mountain walls, it is nearly two and one-half miles above sea level. Steep as the climb is to this hillside seat of government, four great railroads reach the city from the other countries of South America. 

2. BOLIVIA 

The Mountain Republic. - Bolivia, the mountain republic of South America, is one of the two inland countries of that continent. 

Desire for an outlet to the sea has involved her in various boundary disputes. 

The most serious of these was with Paraguay and resulted in a long war over part of El Gran Chaco (see maps, pages 67 and 74). The war lasted three years, ending only in June, 1935, after causing nearly 

100,000 casualties.

Bolivia was also vitally interested in the dispute between Peru and Chile over Tacna- 

Arica. She would like very much to own the land through which the railroad runs from La Paz, her chief city, to Arica on the

LATIN AMERICA 

Pacific. There are other outlets to the sea by rail, one to Mollendo in Peru, another to Antofagasta in Chile, and still another to Buenos Aires in Argentina, but the Arica outlet is the shortest. You will see on the map that all of these railroad lines reach the sea through foreign territory. Like all countries, Bolivia feels that she is entitled to some frontage on the ocean. (See map, page 67.)

PLOWING IN BOLIVIA 

Ewing Galloway

With stony, poor soil to work with, and low temperatures to hinder the growth of plants and add to his discomfort, the Indian farmer on the highlands of Bolivia has a hard life. Primitive methods also help to cause low yields. This Indian is using a wooden plow, and again the slow but steady oxen are playing their part. 

Distribution of the People. - Bolivia has an area of about 420,000 square miles and a population of about 3,000,000. 

Compare her area with the areas of other Latin American countries and a selected group of mountain states in our country. Also, divide the population by the area to find out how many people there are per square mile, a very important thing to know about any nation. Why is the relation of people to area so important?

It is essential to know how these people are distributed over the country. (You can find this on the density-of-population map.) 

Strange to say, you will find that most of the people live in the high plateau section about two miles above sea level. This is one of the highest inhabited plateaus in the world. 

Agriculture in Bolivia. - The way in which the people have adapted themselves to their strange surroundings is very interesting.

Farming, herding, and mining are the chief occupations. As farmers, the Bolivians do a surprisingly good job, considering the many drawbacks. We should consider both tools and methods very crude. Oxen trudge across the fields with crude wooden plows of a type which were in use hundreds of years ago.

The two principal crops are barley and potatoes. (South America is the original home of the potato.),

The Indians do their plowing in August and 

September, their planting in November and

December, and their harvesting in what would be our spring and early summer. Does this seem strange? Not when you recall that the seasons of the Northern and Southern Hemispheres are reversed. 

The Aymaras. - The Aymara Indians are the farm laborers. They have a hard struggle against cold, hunger, and the fact that many of them are constantly kept in debt by the owners of the land. This system is known as peonage. It is a common practice in many of the South American countries, and really amounts almost to slavery. 

Many of the farmers work together in planting potatoes and harvesting grains. This helps to relieve the monotony and difficulty of hard tasks performed at such high altitudes. 

We must admire these agricultural workers, for they get good crops where many of us would probably starve.

Herding in Bolivia. - Bolivia is also a herding country where llamas, vicunas, alpacas, sheep, and goats are raised. There is plenty of grazing land at these high altitudes, but the vegetation is not very abundant. The animals just named are used either as beasts of burden, or for food, or as a source of wool for clothing and blankets. In such a cold region, wool is highly prized for making clothing and blankets.

Mining in Bolivia. - The name of Bolivia is most closely associated with mining, for billions of dollars' worth of tin, lead, copper, silver, zinc, and antimony have been taken out of this country through the centuries. 

It was the precious metals which attracted the ancient Incas and later the Spanish conquerors. 

Did you ever stop to think how much of the earth has been explored and conquered just to secure gold and silver and copper? Now men are going to the ends of the earth for oil.

GIRL SCOUTS IN BOLIVIA 

Ewing Galloway

Bolivia is training her girls as well as boys to be good citizens. These girl scouts are from La Paz.

Tin. Bolivia is probably most famous for its tin. From one-fourth to one-half of the world's tin comes from this country, most of the remainder being mined in the Singapore district of the Far East. Tin is one valuable metal which the United States does not produce. Think how different our diet would be without the common tin can! At all seasons of the year, at the poles or equator, on ocean or land, we can now enjoy fruits, vegetables, meats, and other foods because of this much-abused tin can. Tin is also used in making many other things, especially bronze, one of the valuable alloys of ancient times and still a very useful compound. 

The Montana in Bolivia. - So far, we have been considering the cold plateau of Bolivia, but there is another section. Like many other South American nations, Bolivia has a montana or tropical lowland section. It is in the eastern part, where the rainfall is abundant and the temperature high enough to produce rich, grassy plains and dense tropical forests.

From these forests come rubber, the country's second most valuable export; tropical fruits of all kinds, which are sold in the markets of the larger cities; coca, from which the drug cocaine is made; cinchona, from the bark of which quinine is made; and hardwoods, for cabinet and dye purposes. 

Other tropical crops, such as sugar, coffee, and cacao, could also be extensively produced. 

In spite of their richness, these eastern lowlands are largely unsettled, and much is still unexplored. Only one-fifth of the people live here. The natives chew large quantities of dried coca leaves, which seems to give them more strength and helps them to endure their hardships much better. Some of the tenant farmers even refuse to work unless their overlords supply them with coca leaves. 

The People of Bolivia. About half the people are pure-blooded Indians, one-fourth are of mixed blood, and one-fourth of Spanish origin. Those of Spanish blood hold the government positions, own much of the land, and manage most of the business. The pureblooded Indians and the cholos, as those of mixed blood are called, do the hard work and many of them are virtually slaves or peons.

The people dress in very picturesque and colorful costumes made by themselves from their own local materials. As in all Latin 

America, most of the people are Roman Catholics, though all religions are tolerated.

Other cities are Oruro, the center of the railway system, Cochabamba, a manufacturing and trading center, and Potosi, famous for its silver mines.

In this lake there is a small island called the Island of the Sun, supposed to have been the home of the founder of the Inca race. It contains many ruined temples and palaces, regarded as sacred by the present inhabitants.

Tiahuanaco is a city about fifty miles from La Paz, containing the ruins of one of man's most ancient civilizations. One writer says that eleven thousand years ago the buried ruins of the city of Tiahuanaca were the seat of a vast civilization.

Attractive plazas with flower gardens and fountains are a feature of South American cities. The large building in the center is the Government Palace. The architecture of these buildings resembles that in many Spanish cities. 

Quito, though it is so near to the equator, is high above sea level and surrounded by snow-capped mountains so that its climate is comparatively cool and comfortable. 

3. ECUADOR 

The Land of the Equator. Like Peru, Ecuador is divided into three parts: the low coastal region, the mountain plateaus, and the far interior or Amazon Basin section. The country derives its name from the fact that it is crossed by the equator. 

It extends north of the equator about 100 miles and south about 400 miles.

The country is shaped like a triangle, the widest part bordering the Pacific Ocean and the narrowest part ending in the upper reaches of the Amazon Valley. From sea level on the west the land rises to a height of over 20,000 feet in the beautiful snowcapped volcanoes of Chimborazo and Cotopaxi. 

The Low Coastal Region. Such a coun- 

Such a country naturally has a variety of climates. On the lowlands along the coast, where the land is typically tropical, there is a wet season from about the beginning of January to the end of March, when the heavy rains descend sometimes in torrents, causing frequent floods. The dry season lasts from May to 

December. The temperature at all seasons. of the year is uniformly high, but the nights are usually cool and enjoyable. This coastal region is the most productive part of the entire country, for here are found the fertile tropical plantations, and the important port of Guayaquil. 

The Port of Guayaquil. Only a few years ago, Guayaquil was one of the most dreaded cities in the world, because of the yellow fever and other tropical diseases found there. And so whenever a boat passenger from Guayaquil arrived at another harbor, such as Panama, he was immediately seized, rushed off to quarantine, fumigated, and subjected to other unpleasant experi-

This statue of Simon Bolivar stands in the public square of Guayaquil, Ecuador. Simon Bolivar, the George

Cacao Plantations. It is along this coastal district, also, that the large cacao plantations are to be found. Cacao plants, from which we get cocoa beans" and the chocolate and cocoa of commerce, grow well in what is known as the true equatorial type of climate, which is found from about 10° north to about 10° south of the equator. The cacao plant requires plenty of rainfall, a deep rich soil, a humid atmosphere, and still air. 

Still air is necessary because the pods containing the beans are so large and loosely attached that strong winds blow them off before they are ripe. 

Of course, other tropical regions, notably the Gold Coast of Africa, now produce cacao, but Ecuador and Brazil were among the pioneer countries. In fact, the Americas are supposed to have given cacao, the source of all our delicious chocolate products, to the world. (Can you name other plants and animals which have come from the Western 

Hemisphere?) As you well know, most of our cultivated plants and domestic animals came ences, just because he had come from that from Europe and Asia. (Why is this so?) pest-hole of a port in Ecuador. 

Now, however, thanks to knowledge gained by Colonel Gorgas at Panama and elsewhere in controlling tropical diseases and in sanitation, all has been changed and Guayaquil has been cleaned up and made prosperous. 

It has a population of about 140,000 and is one of the best ports on the west coast of South America, being situated about forty miles up the wide Guayas River. The river has many houseboats on it, where whole families reside much as they do on some of the Chinese rivers.

HOMES ON A CACAO PLANTATION

The Mountain Plateaus. In spite of the fertility of the coast, it is among the mountain valleys of middle altitude that the most tonic and temperate climate of Ecuador is to be found. This is a region of perpetual spring, neither too hot nor too cold for enjoyable activity. Although it is right on the equator, temperate-zone products may be found here on account of the altitude. 

Here also is the capital, Quito, one of the oldest cities of the Western Hemisphere and now a modern thriving government center. It is connected with the metropolis, Guayaquil, by a magnificent railroad of about 300 miles in length, climbing from the Pacific coast to Quito, which is about 9500 feet above sea level. 

The Amazon Basin. The Amazon sec tion of Ecuador, like that of Peru, Bolivia, and Brazil, is heavily forested and has great possibilities for future development. However, as yet, it is not extensively used. Rubber is produced here and also vast quantities of vegetable ivory. This comes from a stunted palm whose fruit looks like the coconut.

Inside there are from six to nine seeds, in color and composition very much like real ivory. They are used for making buttons, chess men, poker chips, and umbrella handles.

Still another valuable plant from this region is a fan-shaped palm from which the straw for making Panama hats is obtained.

Ecuador was the first country to make these hats and still produces the best grades. The "Panama hats" comes from the fact that they were formerly sent across the Isthmus of Panama. name The opening of the Panama Canal in 1914 has done a great deal for Ecuador. It has given the people much more direct contact with eastern North America and with 

Western Europe. 

Some coffee, sugar cane, and tropical fruits are also produced in the Amazon section. 

Cattle, sheep, goats, and alligators furnish hides for export. Cattle and sheep are also raised along the coast. 

Manufacturing and Mining. - Manufacturing in Ecuador is done only on a very small scale. There are some flour mills and foundries, and a few textiles are made. 

The Panama hat industry has been mentioned. Unlike most of the Andean countries, mining has been very little developed, gold being about the only metal that has

James Sawders

These girls are weaving Panama hats, which are made from a plant native to the coast of Ecuador. The plant resembles a palm, with leaves that rise fanlike from the ground. The fiber of the leaves is used to make the hats.

THE FUTURE OF ECUADOR 

113 been produced in paying quantities. There are deposits of petroleum along the coast, in the interior plateau, and in the Amazon Basin. Over 2,000,000 barrels are produced yearly. This means crude oil for fuel while refineries produce gasoline and other petroleum products for the country's industries and transportation needs. 

Education. Education centers about the 

University of Quito, which was founded in 1787. However, public education is very backward, and only a small percentage of the people can read and write. 

The Future of Ecuador. - Ecuador, with its beautiful scenery and undeveloped resources, seems to be calling to tourists and capitalists. With better means of communication and transportation, we may expect the country to advance rapidly. Good roads, automobiles, airplanes, and other modern improvements will open up the large resources of the country to international commerce.

PLAYING PELOTA IN ECUADOR 

Ewing Galloway

Pelota is the national game of Ecuador. A large ball made of stringy rubber is used. The players strike the ball with heavy, flat, wooden bats, about eight inches wide and two inches thick. The players win points by hitting the ball without knocking it across the lines. 

They lose by knocking it across them.

AVENUE IN GEORGETOWN, BRITISH GUIANA 

Royal palms, the graceful trees of the tropics, make this well-kept highway very attractive. Georgetown is built on land which is below sea level at high tide, and The country is divided into three zones. 

The first is a northern coastal belt which varies in width from ten to forty miles. This zone contains practically all of the people and most of the cultivated land. The second zone immediately south of the cultivated area is broader and slightly higher, and is made up of tropical grass-lands and some valuable timbered areas. Still farther south, there is a third and even larger area, which is mountainous and covered with tropical forests. 

Rivers. British Guiana is crossed by a great number of streams, many of which are navigable throughout much of their lower courses. The Demerara River, although not the largest, is probably the most important for commerce, because on it is located the capital and largest city, Georgetown.

The Essequibo is the largest river. 

Living Conditions. As you may well imagine, in this tropical land the temperatures are uniformly high. During the two wet seasons, a long one from April to August and a short one during December and January, the humidity also is high. There are two houses are built on piles; but the city is well governed dry periods, a long one from August to and is kept clean and healthful. 

G. THE GUIANAS 

Almost all of the countries of South America are independent republics. There are three exceptions, namely, British, Dutch, and 

Dutch, and

French Guiana. These are colonial possessions which were acquired before the Monroe Doctrine became part of the American policy. 

1. BRITISH GUIANA The Land and the People. - British 

Guiana, with an area of about 90,000 square miles, is the largest of these three possessions. 

It has a population of about 300,000, made up mostly of East Indians and Negroes with some Europeans, natives, Chinese, and various other races. 

December and a short one during February and March. The heat is sometimes oppressive, and the sanitary conditions leave much to be desired. However, the trade winds blow steadily at certain times of the year, bringing fresh and cooler air from the Caribbean. 

Tropical diseases are common, but the greatest pests of all are the mosquitoes. The houses are lightly constructed and built upon poles, both for the sake of being above the water-line and for the better circulation of air. Air must be allowed to circulate freely through the houses and at the same time they must be securely screened against insect pests of all kinds. 

Agriculture. As in all the Caribbean lands, agriculture is by far the most important industry and sugar is the principal crop. 

It makes up the bulk of the export trade, and

SURINAM, OR DUTCH GUIANA 

furnishes employment to the largest portion of the population. A very interesting feature of the sugar cane lands is that some of them are below high tide and have to be protected by dikes and canals. These numerous canals serve the threefold purpose of irrigation, transportation, and drainage. On account of the low and sometimes flooded condition of the land, machines are little used, and most of the work has to be done by hand. The great use of manual labor has led to a very unusual situation, the bringing in of contract laborers from the Far East. This accounts for the fact that East Indians make up the largest element in the population. Most of the sugar exported goes to Canada and England, owing to special rates. 

Rice is the second crop in importance and the main food of the people. The cultivation of rice was introduced and has been much encouraged by the Oriental laborers, who learned how to handle the rice crop in their homelands of India, China, and other eastern places. Numerous tropical fruits and vegetables are raised for local use, but only two, coconuts and limes, are commercially important. Coffee and cacao grown to some extent, but their cultivation could be enormously increased. 

are

Forests and Minerals. There are large forests that could yield vast quantities of tropical dye and cabinet woods, balata, latex used in the manufacture of cables and belting, and rubber. These are now produced only to a very limited extent. Many minerals are known to exist, but only diamonds, gold, and bauxite are produced on a commercial scale. Bauxite is the mineral ore from which aluminum is extracted. 

Georgetown, the Capital. - Georgetown is the principal port and city as well as the capital. It is situated on the Demerara 

River just inside the mouth, and has five wharves used by ocean vessels. Here one finds the government buildings and the com- 115

Needs of British Guiana. British Guiana needs better transportation facilities. At present, the numerous rivers and canals furnish the best and most important means of moving people and goods. There are only about 100 miles of railroads and the highways are few in number and poor in quality. 

Airplane transportation may be an important cause of future growth.

3. FRENCH GUIANA 

French Guiana is the smallest of the three Guianas. It likewise has the smallest population, about 37,000, and is the least important of the three. It duplicates many of the features of the other two Guianas. It is also famous as a place where French prisoners are sent, and as such is known as a penal colony. 

Cayenne is the capital, largest city, and the most important commercial center French is the official language of the coun try, as English and Dutch are in the British and Dutch colonies respectively.

These boys are learning how to cultivate tobacco at a Colombian Agricultural School near Bogotá. Colombia has the rich soil and good climate for growing more and larger crops of many needed products, but primitive methods and lack of transportation facilities have been a hindrance. Young men of Colombia are now being trained to use better methods, and new roads and railroads being built each year will aid in getting things to market. 

H. COLOMBIA 

Colombia Fronts on Two Oceans. Co lombia is the only South American country that has a frontage on both the Atlantic and the Pacific oceans. It is, therefore, a 

Pacific land as well as a Caribbean land.

The country has been very important historically. Bogotá, its capital, was the ruling center of a large vice-royalty of Spain called

NCO BOGOTÁ, THE CAPITAL OF COLOMBIA MARKET PLACE AT BARRANQUILLA 

117 are as yet not very well developed.

The People and Their Government. 

Like most of the 

Latin American countries, the constitution and government are modeled after that of the United States. The people of Colombia have elected many men of letters as presidents, giving preference to people of culture rather than to army men. 

Revolutions are less frequent than in many South American countries and Colombia has enjoyed long periods of peace. Spanish is the official language and those of Spanish descent are the rulers, but the majority of the people are of mixed races. 

Ewing Galloway

Barranquilla is near the mouth of the Magdalena River in Colombia. The natives bring their produce down the river in boats to this market where the townspeople come to buy their food supplies. Rivers form the chief highways for transportation in Colombia. 

"New Granada," embracing much of what is now Colombia, Venezuela, and Ecuador.

Area and Population. The Republic of 

Columbia has an area of about 448,000 square miles, and a population of around 8,000,000. 

In many Latin American lands, statistics of area and population are not wholly exact. 

Boundary lines have not yet in all cases been finally determined. Often the claims of these countries overlap. Then, too, the people are not so carefully counted as they are in the 

United States and Europe. 

Colombia a Mountainous Country. - Colombia is very mountainous, being crossed by three ranges of the Andes, known as the western, the eastern, and the central. These ranges run mainly north and south, while between them are found the rivers, such as the Magdalena and its main tributary, the 

Cauca, both of which are in part navigable.

East of the eastern range of mountains are the tropical grasslands known as llanos and the equatorial forests. The forests contain tropical hardwoods and dyewoods, while the llanos could furnish pasture lands for thousands of cattle. Both, however, Bogotá, the Capital. - Bogotá is the capi tal and most important city. Situated high on a plateau between the mountain ranges, it enjoys a temperate climate like that of Quito in Ecuador, although it is near the equator. Being so high above sea level, it is rather difficult to reach. In fact, the best way to reach it is by airplane from either coast. 

BUSINESS DISTRICT OF BOGOTÁ 

Around a large central square are grouped many fine buildings. Bogotá is a center of culture, with public libraries, a national university, several colleges, and a fine museum.

LATIN AMERICA 

FLYING THE ANDES 

It takes a heap of flying to be able to pilot a ship safely over these high mountains-yet unless one flies it is difficult to get from the east slopes to the west slopes. 

The mountain passes are narrow, steep, and hard to cross. 

The railroads are few and the wagon roads poor. 

Colombia was the first South American country to have regular air service. This service has partly solved its difficult transportation problem.

The country, however, needs more railways for carrying heavy freight and more good roads for motor travel. Bogotá has government buildings, the ever-present cathedral, a national theater, and other large buildings. 

It has its central square from which streets radiate to all parts of the city. 

Variety of Climate. In climate, Colombia enjoys a great diversity, due to the oceans and the varied relief of the land. The lowlands are uniformly hot, with wet and dry seasons. On the plateau region, Bogotá enjoys perpetual spring weather. The highest mountains are snow-capped. 

Agricultural Products. The agricultural products are as varied as the climate and the relief of the land. Rubber is the chief crop along the Pacific coast where the rainfall is heavy. Along the Caribbean coast the rainfall is not so heavy and there is a great variety of crops, including bananas, corn, sugar cane, cotton, cacao, rubber, and tropical fruits and vegetables. On the plateaus, cereals of the temperate zone, such as wheat, barley, and rye, may be grown.

Coffee is the leading crop of the country for export, the climate and soil being well suited to its production, which could be increased many fold. The United States gets about one-fifth of its coffee from Colombia each year. Bananas are also important for local consumption and for shipment to other countries. Sugar cane can be grown in many sections, and a greater production of it may be expected when there are better transportation facilities. Cattle, tropical cabinet- and dyewoods, will be produced for export as the country develops. 

Mineral Resources. Mineral resources are also abundant in Colombia. Gold, copper, platinum, and coal exist in paying quantities. And there is petroleum in both the 

Caribbean and the Pacific coastal regions.

The most extensive development of the oil resources has taken place along the Magdalena Valley. Oil is piped over 300 miles from the interior to Cartagena on the coast. 

American and other foreign capital may be expected to develop further the oil and other mineral resources.

I. VENEZUELA 

How Venezuela Received Its Name. 

Venezuela is the most northern country in

South America, and its only coast line is on the Caribbean Sea. It has an area of about 

400,000 square miles and a population of about 3,000,000. It touches three other 

South American countries, Colombia, Brazil, and British Guiana. Like so many of the

South American countries, its boundary lines are not well fixed. When the Spanish explorers first sighted this land, they saw an Indian village with the houses built on stilts. 

This reminded one of them of Venice, and he exclaimed, exclaimed, "Venezuela!" which in Spanish means "Little Venice." And so the country was named.

Variety of Surface. The land has a varied surface and consequently a varied climate. 

In the northwest is a continuation of the Andes mountains. There are also mountains in the southwest. Along the coast is a low and narrow district, while about one-half of the entire land is occupied by rolling plains known as the llanos, which are relieved here and there by small plateaus. These llanos are tropical grasslands along and south of the 

Orinoco River, which drains most of Venezuela. The Orinoco is one of the large navigable rivers of South America, and the third in size, being exceeded only by the Amazon and the La Plata systems. Many parts of the llanos are occupied by savage Indian tribes and are largely unexplored, but, with the further settlement of the country, they will be used largely for the raising of cattle for temperate-zone peoples. 

Climate. Venezuela is a tropical country, with uniformly high temperatures and wet

LATIN AMERICA 

PICTURESQUE CARACAS, THE CAPITAL OF VENEZUELA 

Ewing Galloway

Caracas, high up among the mountains, is a city that looks, from a distance, as if it had been lifted out of Spain and set down in Venezuela. It is one of the oldest cities in America, and is noted as the birthplace of Simon Bolivar. 

Surrounded by beautiful scenery, and having every convenience to be found in any up-to-date American city, Caracas is a very pleasant place in which to live. and dry seasons. The temperatures are relieved along the coast by the trade winds from the Caribbean, and in the interior, by the varied relief of the land. 

History. As we have seen, Venezuela was once a part of the Spanish viceroyalty known as New Granada. In the early part of the nineteenth century, along with the other Spanish American countries, it secured its independence with the aid of great national heroes such as Simon Bolivar.

Do you realize that all of these sister republics of the Americas have their great national heroes which they greatly admire just as we have our Washington and Lincoln? See if you can find out something about Simon Bolivar, José de San Martin, Francisco 

Miranda, Bernardo O'Higgins, Antonio José de Sucre, Dom Pedro, and Juan Manuel Rosas. some of these South American heroes. American boys and girls need to know more about the great characters and national heroes of these lands, just as the Latin Americans need to know more about our great men.

When independence was finally secured in 1830, the constitution and government were modeled after the United States. The people were not ready for self-government, however, and the country has had many revolutions. One military leader after another has ruled. With the spread of education, we may expect the rule of the people finally to prevail.

Agricultural Products. - Agriculture, of course, is the chief industry. Coffee is by far the leading crop, and makes up over half of the export trade. It grows well in all of the northern section of the country from east to west. This is the most densely populated and

This is one of the oil fields on the shores of Lake 

Maracaibo. Venezuela now holds third place among the petroleum-producing countries of the world. Most of the petroleum is sent to the Dutch island of Curaçao, where it is refined and then shipped all over the world. 

A large part of the petroleum products of Venezuela is shipped to the United States.

Other products of the soil favored by the climate are cacao, sugar cane, tobacco, cotton, corn, and tropical fruits and vegetables of many kinds.

Pastoral products are of some importance and will become increasingly more so. Hides Hides and skins from cattle, sheep, and goats are now exported. With the more complete use of the tropical grasslands or llanos, we may look forward to the time when 

Venezuela will be shipping fresh meat to the

United States and the countries of western Europe.

Mineral Wealth. The mineral wealth of 

Venezuela is very great. Asphalt is mined extensively. Gold, silver, coal, iron, copper, and salt are known to exist in paying quantities. One of the most important resources is petroleum. The industrial nations of the world are taking a new interest in Venezuela and other Caribbean lands because of oil, more important to-day than gold and silver in the days of the Spanish conquerors. One of the largest and most important oil districts in the entire world has been opened in the Lake 

Maracaibo district of Venezuela. There are promising oil fields in other parts of the country. 

Manufacturing. Although mining, agricultural, and pastoral activities are by far the most important, there is a small amount of manufacturing in Venezuela. A considerable amount of capital is invested in the textile industry. Cotton grows well in Venezuela, and there are several mills in Valencia, Caracas, and Cumana. There is an oil refinery in the Maracaibo petroleum district.

Many wild rubber trees are found, from which rubber is secured and prepared for northern markets. The people manufacture cigars, cigarettes, shoes, paper, cotton textiles, chemicals, and other items for local use. 

Forests. Over half of Venezuela is cov ered with beautiful tropical forests. From these, tropical dyewoods, cabinet woods, rubber, tannic acid, and other valuable materials are secured and prepared for market. 

The Government is now carrying out plans for reforestation and greater use of the forests. 

Caracas, the Capital. - Caracas is the cap ital and leading city, with a population of around 200,000. It is located in the mountains, only eight miles by air line from La Guaira, the leading seaport. It is one of the most picturesque and naturally beautiful

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH Venezuela 

From 1922 to 1929 our trade with Venezuela grew more rapidly than with any other South American country, but since 1929 there has been a marked change. The averages for the years 1930 up to 1941 are: imports, $22,000,000; exports, $17,000,000. are distributed over the city. Although 

Caracas is only eight miles by air line from La Guaira, the remarkably picturesque railroad line which connects the two places is twenty-three miles long. This is because it must wind back and forth to climb the mountains from the coastal city to the capital. 

Needs of Venezuela. - Venezuela needs better transportation facilities. She has over 600 miles of railroads, but could use many more. The rivers, especially the 

Orinoco, are navigable, but could be much more profitably used. Good roads will be constructed in the future and will help to open up the country. Again, as in most of these mountainous, tropical countries, air transportation will prove a great boon.

The Land of Nicaragua and Why We Are 

Interested in It. Nicaragua is a small country about the size of New York State, with a population of about 1,000,000. It touches both the Pacific Ocean and the

Caribbean Sea, and is bounded on the north by Honduras and on the south by Costa Rica.

A range of mountains, with many volcanic peaks, runs through the center. Among these ranges are two fresh-water lakes. Lake

Managua, to the north, is 30 miles long by 15 miles wide, and is connected by a short river with Lake Nicaragua to the south.

Lake Nicaragua is much the larger, being 

100 miles long and 45 miles wide. This latter lake is very important in the trans-

PEOPLE AND GOVERNMENT OF NICARAGUA 

portation system of the country. It is this lake, combined with the San Juan River which drains it into the Caribbean Sea, that would form a part of the proposed Nicaraguan Canal, which the United States, by the payment of $3,000,000, has secured the right to construct. 

Climate. The climate is tropical, but the heat is modified by ocean breezes on both coasts and by the pleasant coolness of the mountainous sections. The rainfall is rather heavy all of the year on the Atlantic coast, but on the Pacific there is a dry season from 

December to April, and a wet season from 

May to November. 

Agriculture, the Leading Occupation.

As in all the Central American countries, agriculture is by far the leading occupation of the people. Some of the products, such as corn, beans, cotton, and rice, are almost entirely for local consumption. Others, especially coffee, sugar, cacao, and bananas, form the basis of the export trade. Coffee is the chief of these, although bananas and other tropical fruits are becoming of increasing importance. Some of the big sugar and coffee estates have modern machinery for preparing their products for market, but most of the

Heavy, wooden-wheeled carts, oxen-drawn, creak and bump over the poorly developed roads of Nicaragua.

HOME IN NICARAGUA 

This is a typical country home of the poorer classes in Nicaragua. Of what does the roof consist? Why is it made so steep? plantations are so small that very simple and ancient methods are used. Corn, beans, rice, and plantains, which grow like bananas, make up the chief food of the people. 

Nicaragua and other Central American countries give great promise as sources of meat supply for the future. Cattle raising could become a very important industry, because the pasturage is good and there is plenty of fresh water. Oxen are used as work animals and for drawing loads. The ox is the most useful animal in Nicaragua. 

Forests and Minerals. There are valuable forests of tropical woods, but the extensive mineral resources of the country have been barely touched. Bluefields, however, is the center of a considerable mining industry. Gold-bearing ores are found in many parts of the country. Placer mining has been carried on along the Nicaraguan rivers for many years.

The People and Their Government. - 

Of the population, 17 per cent are white, the rest being mainly Indian with an admixture of Spanish blood. Spain has furnished the language, the ruling elements, and the customs of the people. There is little education, and illiteracy runs high. This is also another one of those countries where the people rule in theory but not in fact.

LATIN AMERICA Managua, the Capital. 

Managua, situated on the southern shore of Lake Managua, is the capital and, for many years, has been the most important city. On March 31, 1931, it was visited by a very destructive earthquake which razed practically every building in the city and killed and injured many of the inhabitants. The city now has a population of about 

115,000. Managua is near the most important coffee district of the republic, and coffee, you will remember, is the leading export crop. Managua has not always been the capital, because with the frequent revolutions the capital has shifted at times to two other important cities, Leon and Granada. Most of the streets in the cities of Nicaragua are dusty or muddy, according to the season, and sanitary conditions leave much to be desired. A small amount of manufacturing is carried on, but most of the manufactured goods are imported from the United States.

Our Future Relations with Nicaragua. 

This country, as well as other Central American lands, will mean more and more to the United States as time goes on. It will furnish an important outlet for American money. Nicaraguans need our goods and we need theirs. As ex-President Coolidge said, "Nothing could happen in Central

America without our being vitally interested in the outcome."

Reasons for Our Interest. - Perhaps no part of the outside world means more to our people than Mexico and the Caribbean lands. 

There are several reasons for this.

First, we are a Caribbean power through the ownership of land there, Porto Rico, the

Panama Canal Zone, and the Virgin Islands. 

Second, with four of the countries of the 

Caribbean area - Cuba, Panama, Santo 

Domingo, and Haiti - we have special treaty arrangements, which give us a large influence. in those countries. Cuba and Panama entered into these arrangements after they had gained their independence with our help.

Fourth, we feel a strong interest in Mexico because she is our neighbor. We wish to understand her peculiar problems so that both countries may have a kindlier feeling toward each other.

Fifth, we need the tropical products of these lands in exchange for our manufactured goods. 

Sixth, some of these countries have oil, and you already know how important oil is in the modern world. 

Seventh, billions of dollars of American money are already invested in this region, and billions more will be needed in the future.

Surely, these lands mean much to us. 

1. MEXICO 

127 

A Foreign Land a Step Away. - The 

Republic of Mexico now includes twentyeight states, two territories, and a federal district. The land is shaped somewhat like a horn, with the wide end at the north and the narrower at the south, tapering to the Isthmus of Tehuantepec. A narrow strip of low and very hot country extends along both the east and west coasts. The land, however, quickly rises in terraces to the plateau area where most of the people live and where the temperatures are moderate. Rising above all are the beautiful snow-capped Sierra Madre. 

The People and Their Mode of Living. 

The population of Mexico totals about 19,000,000, of which number about 14,000,000 are partly or wholly native Indians. The rest are whites. The whites are the best educated and the ruling class, although the Indians are gradually getting more education and gaining more power. There are a large number of foreigners in the country, including many from the United States. These people are mainly engaged in securing oil and metals and in the professions. 

Most of the Mexicans are very poor and live in villages under conditions that we should regard as very unsanitary. On the other hand, there are many wealthy people who are cultured, well educated, speak several languages, and travel extensively in Europe and the United States. They own expensive motor cars and homes with modern conveniences. Many Mexican laborers come to the United States to work in the railroad camps and sugar beet fields. We must not judge all Mexicans by these laborers, however.

The customs of the people are very different from ours. Perhaps that is one reason why we do not understand them as well as we should. For instance, most of the people
