THE PLAZA MAYOR AND CATHEDRAL, MEXICO CITY 

Ewing Galloway

The Plaza Mayor is the heart of the capital city of Mexico. Around this square of lovely trees, flowers, fountains, and statuary centers the religious, political, and commercial life of the city. The famous old cathedral, built on the site of a former Aztec temple, is one of the largest and most imposing places of worship in America. 

SCENIC CHARM 

A very young senorita perches on the wall, while grandpa, perhaps, tells her stories of Ixtaccihuatl. 

H. Armstrong Roberts

Tarascan fishermen, in dugout canoes, rig their graceful butterfly nets on shimmering Lake Patzcuaro. 131

LATIN AMERICA 

A MEXICAN IDYLL 

In the stillness of a beautiful garden in Cuernavaca. 

The wealthier Mexican wives and mothers give much of their attention to the happiness of their husbands and children. They have many servants to do the hard work about the homes. The things bought at the market are carried home in trays and baskets balanced on the servants' heads - very much like the Spanish in Europe. Many of the customs, the style of architecture, and the language have come from the Spanish settlers. 

Religion and Government. The people of Mexico are very religious and take great pride in their places of worship. Many beautiful churches are found in the cities.

The government like that in most of the countries of Latin America was modeled on the Constitution of the United States. But many of these countries have not been democracies in point of fact. Rather they have been military autocracies, that is, they were ruled by the men who have control of the strongest army.

Such was the case for a long time in Mexico. 

First one military dictator ruled the country and then another. That is why they have had so many revolutions. One faction or leader tries to overthrow another faction or leader and then the war is on. Frequently these revolting factions are financed by people who hope to receive concessions to exploit the country in case their side wins.

Mexico is striving all the time to work toward a truer form of democratic government. In this program education forms an important part. Schools are increasing in all parts of the country. As education extends to the masses, the chances for a real democracy improve and the promise for the future becomes brighter. 

Bull Fights. There are many outdoor sports in Mexico but, as in Spain itself and in so many of her children in America, the bull fights draw the largest crowds. Six to eight bulls are killed at each performance, the fight with each lasting about twenty minutes.

These animals are not like the heavy beef bulls raised in the United States, but are slender, quick, and born fighters.

Each bull is fought separately. The first to bait the bull are the picadores. They Kotaria 

A NATIONAL SPORT 

The two gentlemen waving their capes are trying to anger the bull enough to make real sport for the adept bull-fighter, waiting at the left.

A MEXICAN RANCH CITIES OF MEXICO Publishers' Photo Service 

Over the vast ranches of northern Mexico ride the fearless and skillful cowboys, managing great herds. 

Level stretches of lands between mountain ridges are too dry for farming, but are suitable for grazing. are mounted and armed only with iron-tipped pikes, but have their bodies heavily padded and their right legs well protected. The attack is then carried on by the banderilleros, who drive short, barbed sticks into the bull's shoulder, the sticks sometimes containing firecrackers. Finally the real bull fighter, or matador, comes on the scene. His duty is to drive a narrow sword between the bull's shoulders and as nearly as possible into the heart. As you can well imagine, the matador is in danger of losing his life at any moment, and the spectacle, though thrilling, is not recommended for any one who cannot stand the sight of blood. 

Cities of Mexico. The most important city of Mexico is the capital, Mexico City. 

Vera Cruz and Tampico are seaports on the 

Gulf of Mexico. Monterey is a great railroad center, and Cuernavaca, a famous pleasure resort. Guadalajara probably ranks next to the capital among the nation's cities. 

Mexico City, the capital, has a population of about 1,000,000 and is a thoroughly modern city in every respect. It was the old capital of the Aztecs, whose famous civilization was so ruthlessly destroyed by Cortes and his Spanish conquerors. The cathedral in 133 Mexico City is the largest and most imposing of all the fine religious edifices of Mexico. 

The city is growing, expanding, and fairly bristling with new, modern buildings. The

National Theater is one of the finest amusement buildings on the continent of North America, costing over $10,000,000. 

Tampico is famous for its tropical surroundings and as the center of oil production. We must not forget that Mexico has an enormous amount of petroleum, ranking high in its production. Some of the early wells were famous as "gushers" and produced oil in enormous quantities.

The city is only seven miles up the Panuco River from the Gulf of Mexico. The river is wide here with a navigable channel. Immense oil tanks extend along the southern shore for miles. Over 2000 ships register at 

Tampico in a year, carrying away oil, tropical fruits, silver, lead, copper, and zinc.

Guadalajara is considered by some to be the most beautiful city in Mexico. Its clean, wellpaved streets, numerous parks and gardens filled with tropical trees and plants, splendid public buildings, and churches of dazzling whiteness are good ground for this opinion. 

VISITORS FROM THE NORTH 

Two American planes have come to rest on the airport field at Mexico City. This city is the transportation hub of the country, and through its modern airport it is linked to Texas. Cuba, and Central and South America.

LATIN AMERICA GIANT LILY PADS OF TULE 

H. Armstrong Roberts in return send Mexico manufactured products that she needs. This is just what is taking place. Over 60 per cent of Mexico's trade is with the United States. 

In addition to petroleum and other minerals, Mexico sends us tanning materials, hides to be used in the leather industry, and henequen, or sisal, for making binder twine which is used in our wheat fields. We also get bananas, coconuts, tapioca, coffee and other tropical food products, cotton and cotton seed, mahogany, cedar, Tule is a reed which is used for weaving baskets and chairs. Here it is laid and other cabinet woods. out in great swirls to dry. 

Advantages of Trade between the United

States and Mexico. There could scarcely be a better opportunity for coöperation in trade between two countries than there is between the United States and Mexico.

Mexico is first of all a producer of raw materials. She needs manufactured goods. 

Our country can take Mexico's raw materials, use them in our manufacturing industries, and 

In return we send machinery, engines, implements, tools, and other metal goods of all kinds. We also send clothing material, such as cottons and silks, boots and shoes, and other leather manufactures. Items that give pleasure and amusement, such as automobiles, radio outfits, and phonographs, find a good market in Mexico. We also supply some food products, such as canned salmon, wheat and wheat flour, corn, and oats.

80 100 120 140 160 (4800-24) 

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH MEXICO 

In addition to petroleum, copper, and other minerals, Mexico sends us agricultural products, like sisal, bananas, and coffee, but there has been a marked change in our trade with our southern neighbor since 1929. The averages for the years 1930 up to 1941 are: imports, $47,000,000; exports, $55,000,000.

2. CUBA 

Attractiveness of Cuba. "The loveliest land that human eyes have ever seen." So said Christopher Columbus when he discovered the island of Cuba on October 28, 1492. Of course, any land looked good to the Spanish sailors after they had seen so much of the ocean. Nevertheless, perhaps the statement is not much of an exaggeration after all. Writing to Ferdinand and Isabella, Columbus declared with fervor, "This Land, O Most Serene Majesties, is so wonderfully beautiful that it surpasses all others in charm, even as the light of the day surpasses that of the night." Cuba is commonly called "The Pearl of the Antilles."

The island is strategically located very close to our southern coast, and we cannot help being vitally interested in its future development. It is only one hour by airplane from 

Key West to Habana, and a few hours by steamer.

The Land of Cuba. Cuba is an independent republic, with an area of 44,164 square miles, and a population of about 4,000,000. The island is by far the largest of the West Indian group and has a general east and west trend. Its total length, about 

760 miles, is several times its width. Several mountain chains cross the country in various directions, while between them are beautiful fertile valleys with short and often swift streams. It has a long coast line and many beautiful harbors.

Attractions of Cuba for Tourists. ——— Al though Cuba is situated in the tropics, the climate is not oppressive. During the winter months, the thermometer seldom falls below 60° F., while in the summer it seldom reaches 

90°. This warm, even climate is due to

HABANA, THE CAPITAL OF CUBA 

137 

CUBA'S NEW CAPITOL CURUT DU VILLAN 

John Kabel

In the tropical setting of a broad plaza rises Cuba's new Capitol, the finest public building in the West Indies. The dignity and beauty of the outside is echoed by the dignity and beauty of the inside. In what ways does Cuba's Capitol resemble that of the United States? the moderating influence of the ocean, the trade winds and sea breezes, the varied relief of the land, and the fact that the island extends east and west rather than north and south. Enthusiasts say that it is always springtime in Cuba.

The rainfall in the interior (60 inches) is a little more than it is along the southern and northern coasts. There is a relatively dry season during our winter and a relatively wet season during our summer.

It is the delightful winter climate which makes Cuba so attractive for tourists. In fact, Cuba is rebuilding itself for tourists. 

By modern means of transportation, it is only a short distance from our Atlantic seaboard cities, and the Cuban government as well as American capitalists is attempting with success to make an "American Riviera" out of it. A central automobile highway over 700 miles long has been constructed lengthwise through the island from Pinar del Rio through Habana to Santiago de Cuba, at an average cost of about $100,000 per mile. This bituminous concrete highway has an average width of twenty feet and is an invitation to wealthy foreign tourists to bring their automobiles with them. 

Habana, the Capital. Habana has been reconstructed during the past few years, and is now one of the most beautiful and healthful of tropical cities. It has wide and beautiful boulevards, excellent bathing beaches near by, and many attractive public buildings, such

Cuba might have remained in Spanish hands for several years longer had it not been for the fact that on February 15, 1898, the 

United States battleship Maine was blown up by a bomb in Habana harbor with the loss of 266 men. This incident brought to a head the popular indignation against the conduct of Spain in Cuba. War was declared on 

Courtesy Cuban National Tourist Commission Spain by the United States in April. The contest was of short duration, and in the peace treaty, Cuba was given her independence under the guardianship of the United States. 

A PRIVATE HOME, HABANA, CUBA 

Habana lays all the beauty and charm of its houses and gardens at the feet of the many American tourists, who all winter long sail the blue southern waters to land at its attractive waterfront. as the numerous government buildings, the national theater, and public libraries. Its presidential palace compares favorably with other executive mansions in the Americas.

There are numerous attractive parks, many golf courses and magnificent clubhouses, as well as modern hotels. Many of these real estate improvements have been made by 

New York business men and have cost millions.

Habana is one of the cleanest of cities and there is no litter in the streets. The same 

American sanitary engineers that helped to make Panama a healthful tropical city in which to live, have freed Habana from the tropical diseases which formerly raged. 

Cuban History. - Cuba has now been an independent republic for over 30 years. For nearly four centuries the land was ruled by Spain. During this period, in spite of the great agricultural, mineral, and scenic resources, the island did not prosper. Many of the governors were heartlessly cruel, but perhaps the most notorious was General 

Weyler. He was called "The Butcher because he shot so many prisoners who had tried to free themselves from Spanish misrule. 

He also herded noncombatants, farmers and 99 This guardianship, the terms of which are given in the Platt Amendment, means that the United States reserves the right to interfere in Cuban affairs to help maintain the independence of the people, and to protect their life and property. It also provides that Cuba shall make no agreements with other nations which would endanger her independence, shall contract no debt which she could not well pay, and shall lease land to the United States for coaling and naval stations. These rights have been exercised several times.

OUR INTEREST IN CUBA

HABANA, CUBA Lionel Green 

Here we see the popular promenade along the bay as it girdles the new part of the city.

Habana is the largest city in the West Indies and is an important seaport, most of the Cuban exports going out from its harbor. Great improvements have been made in Habana since Cuba became an independent country, and millions of dollars have been spent in constructing new buildings, improving the harbor, and beautifying the city. 

Cuban Agriculture. Cuba is mainly an agricultural country, with sugar and tobacco the leading crops. Sugar has been the main crop for over 150 years, and the prosperity of the island republic depends on sugar more than any other one thing. The annual crop amounts to nearly 3,000,000 tons, and 75 per cent of the output is exported to the United States. Rum, molasses, and alcohol are important by-products.

The raising of tobacco and the manufacture of cigars and cigarettes is the second industry in importance. Cuban leaf is especially fine for Habana cigars. The crop has an annual value of around $15,000,000. 

Tropical fruits and vegetables are grown abundantly. 

Minerals and Forest Resources of Cuba. 

There are also valuable mineral and forest products in Cuba. The mining of iron ore is very important, a considerable amount being exported to the United States. Cuba has much iron ore of a very high grade greatly needed by the eastern manufacturing centers of the United States. From the dense tropical forests, cabinet and dyewoods are secured for export. Both resources will be more extensively drawn upon as the country is better developed. 

Our Interest in Cuba.- 

Cuba needs our products and we need hers. She is one of our best customers, and we are her best customer. Authorities say that over $2,- 

000,000,000 of United States money is now invested in 

Cuban enterprises. These are sugar plantations, mills and refineries, railroads, hotels, tobacco lands, and other holdings. Some one has well said that "the business life of Cuba is dominated by American money.' 

Naturally we want the Cubans to have a strong and stable government. 

SUGAR MILL IN CUBA 

This load of sugar cane has just been hauled to the sugar mill where the juice will be extracted and converted into sugar and molasses. Ox-carts are much used for transporting the sugar cane. The leaves and other refuse from the stalks form the chief food for these useful assistants.

3. HAITI AND THE DOMINICAN REPUBLIC 

Two Negro Republics. The island of 

Haiti is now occupied by two Negro republics, the western third being called Haiti and the eastern two-thirds the Dominican Republic. 

It lies just east of Cuba in the long chain of islands which extend in a semicircle from the peninsula of Yucatan in Mexico to the mouth of the Orinoco River in Venezuela. These 

These islands are known as the Antilles, the four larger islands of Cuba, Haiti, Jamaica, and 

Porto Rico being called the Greater Antilles,

Courtesy Consulate General of the Dominican Republic PRESIDENT'S MANSION, SANTO DOMINGO 

46 This is the White House" in which the President of the Dominican Republic lives. Wide verandas add to the comfort of the occupants. while the smaller islands are called the Lesser Antilles. 

Haiti a Mountainous Land. The island of Haiti is very mountainous, some of the elevations being 10,000 feet high, the highest in the West Indies. As in Cuba, there are many short, swift streams running from the interior mountains and plateaus to the sea.

The warm, fairly constant temperature and the plentiful rainfall of about 50 inches annually favor an abundance of tropical vegetation.

The soils of the island of Haiti are generally very fertile and, where the rainfall is sufficient, crops repay good cultivation. Even the mountain slopes and the high interior plateaus, where some irrigation is necessary, will yield their share of agricultural products. 

a. HAITI 

Crops and Resources. Like so many of the Caribbean countries, Haiti is primarily a farming and stock-raising land. The leading export crop is coffee, which is of very high quality. 

Sugar cane production is increasing, over 30,000 tons being exported each year. Cotton, second among export crops, grows on a large shrub, sometimes developing into a good-

ATTRACTIONS OF JAMAICA FOR TOURISTS Publishers' Photo Service HAULING SUGAR CANE TO THE SUGAR MILL 

This scene is on a sugar plantation in the Dominican Republic. About 400,000 tons of sugar in addition to a great variety of other products are produced annually. 

Most of the sugar is exported in the form of brown sugar, but more than enough to supply the people is refined in the country. There are over 20 sugar mills in this small country. sized tree, with bolls containing fibers over an inch long. Other agricultural products are corn, cacao, bananas, pineapples, coconuts, and many other tropical fruits and vegetables. 

The rearing of cattle, hogs, goats, horses, mules, and poultry give promise of future development.

The forests are extensive, yielding very valuable logwood and mahogany as well as other dye and cabinet woods. The mineral resources have not been well developed. 

The People and Their Government. 

The people of Haiti are mainly Negroes and speak many dialects of the French language. 

They are mostly illiterate, and incapable of taking an intelligent part in their republican government.

The country has been torn by frequent revolutions that brought misery and poverty in their train. The various governments became so corrupt and the foreign debt so great that the United States was obliged to step in and restore conditions so that debts could be paid and corruption done away with. 

Progress was made under American super- 141 vision which lasted until 1934. Perhaps the future may see a better development of the many resources of this beautiful tropical land. 

b. THE DOMINICAN REPUBLIC 

Most of the features of the Dominican 

Republic are similar to those of Haiti. However, it is larger than Haiti, and the people speak Spanish rather than French. Otherwise, the crops, climate, forests, minerals, governmental and educational conditions are very much like those in its sister republic. 

4. JAMAICA Jamaica, the Home of the Jamaica Negro. 

-The island of Jamaica belongs to Great Britain. It is situated south of Cuba and is the largest and the most valuable of the 

British West Indies. The population totals about 1,000,000, mainly colored. These are the so-called "Jamaica Negroes" who have the reputation of being such good tropical workers that many of them are taken to other Caribbean lands to work on the various tropical plantations.

Great Britain. The harbor of Port Antonio on the northeastern shore is one of the most beautiful in the world. In the background rises Blue Mountain Peak, while below are undulating hills with palms and banana trees waving everywhere. Port Antonio is the chief banana port of the island. The banana constitutes the chief article of food in the diet of the Negroes, and the native cooks are past masters in preparing this fruit, cooked or raw, in any number of ways.

A COUNTRY ROAD IN JAMAICA 

The ox-team labors along the road in the stillness of a hot noon, while the breeze blows the white clouds and rustles the tattered leaves of the banana trees. 

Agriculture in Jamaica. -The climate of 

Jamaica allows vegetation to grow luxuriantly throughout the year. Seasons as we know them are practically absent. As one travels around the island, he sees thousands of acres covered with the heavy leaf of the banana, or the slender fronds of the coconut palm.

The homes of the working people are very poor, being only tiny huts of mud and straw. 

Yet the people are happy and sing as they work. They raise practically all of their own food and have a surplus of tropical products for export. 

Kingston, the Chief City. - Kingston is the capital and most important city. Here are modern hotels, brilliant botanical gardens, and many large public buildings. 

5. TRINIDAD 

Trinidad and Its "Acres of Asphalt." - 

Another Caribbean possession of Great Britain is the island of Trinidad, the most southerly of the West Indies. Although it is only a small island, it has a population of about 400,000. It is noted mainly for its great asphalt lake, 110 acres in extent, which is immensely valuable and appears to be inexhaustible. This Pitch Lake has been an important factor in paving many of the world's highways. It is a large level expanse, with a surface of a dull blue-gray color, with pools of water here and there after showers. The asphalt is solid enough to bear a man's weight, but if he stands long enough in one place, he will slowly sink. 

At Brighton near the edge of the lake there is a large refinery from which a narrowgauge railway runs out upon and half-way across the lake. Here and there colored workmen may be seen digging the asphalt and loading it on trains of cars. After being carried to the refineries, the water is removed and the asphalt is melted by means of hot coils of steam pipes. It is then run into barrels for shipment. Pitch Lake is the source of the material that is helping to surface so many of our roads. 

Trinidad also has petroleum, of which there is a large annual output. 

Attractions of Trinidad. - Trinidad has other resources. It is a beautiful island with an infinite variety of scenery. There are coconut plantations and fields of sugar cane, against a picturesque background of mahogany trees, groves of tall, willowy bamboos, and clusters of breadfruit, orange, and nutmeg trees.

Port-of-Spain is the chief city. It is a cosmopolitan city where one may see people of many nationalities. Its Botanical Gardens

6. BARBADOS 

Barbados, the farthest east of the British 

West Indies, has one important distinction.

It is one of the most densely populated islands in the world, having over 1000 people to the square mile. In an area of only 166 square miles it has over 166,000 people. 

BRIDGETOWN, BARBADOS 

Ewing Galloway

The tufted tops of palm trees almost meet over this street of walled-in homes.

For this reason it must be very intensively cultivated. Three-fourths of the land is carefully tilled for various food crops and for sugar and cotton.

Unlike many of the Caribbean lands, it has wide expanses of green plains instead of a volcanic surface. The white houses of the farmers look very attractive among the welltilled fields and gardens. The trade of 

Barbados is mainly with the United States and Canada. 

7. THE BAHAMAS 

The Bahamas are a group of small islands near Florida controlled by Great Britain.

They are not very important, but are developing a reputation as a resort center. Nassau, with its winter resort hotels and opportunities for golf, tennis, fishing, and bathing, is the capital and principal city. 

8. THE BERMUDAS 

The islands of Bermuda are the tiny tips of a volcanic mountain that rises out of the ocean some six hundred miles off the coast of the Carolinas. They are one of England's most popular possessions. All year round visitors from America to Bermuda (the largest island) and the smaller islands of St. George's,

Somerset, and Ireland, crowd the hotels, swim in the turquoise clear waters, bicycle over the white coral roads, picnic on the pink beaches, play golf on the scenic links, and ride in the horse-drawn carriages past banana groves, hibiscus and oleander hedges, bougainvillia vines and lily fields.

One can reach the Bermudas from the United States in less than two days by luxurious ocean liner, or in a little over five hours by giant airliner. Boats put in at 

Grassy Bay anchorage or at the dockyards at Ireland Island - freighters from across the sea and from the West Indies, England's battleships for maneuvers or repair, ships for transporting the Empire's troops garrisoned on the islands, tourist cruisers.

The Bermudas were settled in 1609 by some 

Englishmen on their way to Jamestown.

Today they are a Crown colony, headed by a Governor appointed by the Colonial Office in London and an elected Parliament. There is no woman suffrage and a man must be a landowner to vote. He has as many votes as there are parishes in which he owns land. 

There is no industry on the islands, except a small perfume-making plant. There is some income from the agricultural products - lily bulbs, onions, and potatoes. But the greatest source of revenue is the tourist trade, to which the Bermudas cater with all the charms of an earthly Paradise.

The climate and soil are suited to the production of coffee, cacao, vanilla, and bananas, small amounts of which are exported. 10. MARTINIQUE 

Martinique is another of the French West Indies. While noted for its production of rum, sugar, and cacao, it is most famous for its volcano, Mt. Pelée. The latter erupted so violently on May 8, 1902, that it entirely destroyed the city of St. Pierre, with all but two of its inhabitants. 

St. Pierre now lies at the foot of the mountain, a blackened, pathetic ruin. It has never been rebuilt.

The island is also famous as the birthplace of the Empress Josephine, wife of Napoleon Bonaparte.

MCORMICK HARVESTING METHODS CHANGE! 

Courtesy International Harvester Company

Step by step, the hard hand labor of the grain fields in America has given way to machines. In the days of the sickle and the scythe (upper left) three men would work all day to cut but six acres. If a farmer had a cradle (upper right) and worked steadily for ten hours, he could cover two acres a day. The first McCormick reaper cut the grain, but left the bundling to be done by hand. The next step was the reaper and binder (lower picture) which. in an improved form, is much used on the smaller farms where the large harvester-thresher is not practical. 148

UNIT IV.  

66 

You may have heard the United States referred to as the greatest country on earth." In many ways it is. But before we talk about it in this way, it is important for us to learn in just what ways it is great. 

The following sections will tell us not only of its size, its divisions, its rivers, lakes, and mountains, but also, more important still, of the products of its fields, forests, and fisheries, of its industries and commerce, and of its outlying possessions from Alaska in the 

Arctic to the Philippines in the tropics.

A. THE UNITED STATES AS AN AGRICULTURAL NATION 

Importance of Agriculture. - We are all dependent on the farmer. We cannot live without food, and for this we must depend upon the man who tills the soil and raises the products we use as food. We may eat meat, but the animals which furnish this meat feed upon grass, grains, and other products of the farm. If the farmers of America should cease their work even for a single season, it would bring great suffering and incalculable loss to millions of people. 

The annual value of the world's food products is three times as great as that of all other products put together. Food products, especially cereals, form a large part of the world's commerce. Besides foodstuffs, agriculture produces many of the raw materials used in industry, such as cotton, wool, silk, flax fiber and flax seed, hemp, and rubber. 149

Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction A MODERN PLOW 

This gasoline-driven tractor plows three furrows at a time. Think of the advantage of this machine over the animal-drawn plow which turns over but one furrow at a time.

The United States a Leader in Agriculture. 

- Of the six largest countries in the worldthe United States, Canada, Brazil, Russia,

China, and India- the United States leads in both the production and the export of agricultural products. Our country now produces about three-fifths of the world's corn, twofifths of its cotton, and over one-fourth of its tobacco. It raises one-fourth of the world's oats and hay, about one-fifth of its wheat, and smaller proportions of many other crops. All together the United States produces about one-fourth of the world's cereals.

These large crops in the United States are produced by the labor of about one-fourth of the nation's workers. In Russia, China, and India, on the other hand, about threefourths of the people are engaged in agriculture. In the United States, the average production of cereals for each person engaged in agriculture is 12 tons, while in the rest of the world it is only about 1.4 tons.

How can the United States produce such large crops? And why does the American farmer get better results from his labor than the Chinese or the Russian farmer who works just as hard or harder? 

Here are some of the main reasons: 

1. Large areas of fertile soil. 

2. Favorable climates. 

3. Good government. 

4. Agricultural education. 

5. Excellent transportation. 

6. Large demand for agricultural products by 

American people. 

7. Use of agricultural machinery

As you continue your studies of the United States, see if you can add other items to the above list.

Agricultural Regions of the United States. 

- The United States has not only a great variety of land surfaces and different kinds of soil, but also a wide range of climates in different parts of the country. According to the nature of the land, the climate, and the leading crops, our country is divided into a number of agricultural regions like the cotton belt, the wheat belt, and so on. But we must not think that because an area is called the corn belt, corn is the only crop raised there; it is simply the leading crop, which gives its name to the region.

The Wheat Area. The spring wheat area, which extends far into Canada, is the farthest north of the grain-growing sections. Here the wheat is planted in the spring, and the long summer days cause it to grow rapidly, Courtesy Spokane Chamber of Commerce

The size of the wheat crop depends largely on the amount of rainfall. Most American wheat is grown in areas where the rainfall is less than 30 inches annually, though from 30 to 35 inches seems to be the right amount for producing the largest yields. When the amount of rain is much below 30 inches, and especially if it does not come when the wheat plant is growing, the yield is smaller. 

In both the spring wheat and the winter wheat areas, the heaviest rainfall is generally during the early summer when it is most needed by the growing wheat plant. Later

The best part of the spring wheat area is known as the Red River Valley of the North.

This is a large, very level area which was the bed of a lake in glacial times. It is now drained by the gently flowing Red River. 

While the lake covered this area, large quantities of silt were deposited in the bed of Glacial Lake Agassiz, as it is called. This silt now forms the fertile soil on which is grown the finest wheat.

U. S. Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce VALUE OF PRINCIPAL CROPS 

The value of crops in the United States has fallen off since the beginning of the "depression." Values have varied from year to year, but the averages for each crop from 1929 up to 1941 are: corn. $1,256,000,000; wheat, $515,000,000; oats, $290,000,000; cotton, $602,000,000; hay, $643,000,000; tobacco, $223,000,000. 

The Corn Belt. - South of the spring wheat area is the corn belt, the southern part of which is also the winter wheat belt. Corn is 

America's largest and most valuable crop.

Our country grows enormous quantities annually, nearly twice the amount produced in all other countries combined. Corn is grown in every state, but the area of heaviest production is called the corn belt. Consideroats, barley, or rye. Cornstalks, moreover, furnish excellent fodder for the farm animals. Unlike other grains, corn can be left on the stalk long after it is ripe until it is convenient to harvest it. 

The United States usually exports less than one per cent of its corn crop. Canada, the 

United Kingdom, and the Netherlands are the largest purchasers. European countries buy

Both hard and soft wheat are grown in the winter wheat belt, but only hard wheat is grown in the spring wheat area. Hard wheat makes the best flour for bread, while soft wheat is used largely in making pastry flours. 

More wheat is grown in the western than in the eastern part of the winter wheat belt. 

One reason is that the western is the dryest part of the region, and wheat can be grown more profitably than any other crop. Farther east, where rainfall is more abundant, other crops thrive and less wheat is grown.

Other Cereals in the United States. 

Oats, barley, rye, rice, and buckwheat are other cereals raised in different parts of the 

United States. Oats rank third in acreage and value among cereals in the United States; barley fourth; and then follow rye, buckwheat, rice, and other cereals which are not grown enough to be a large factor in production. 

Rice. Most of the world's rice is grown in the warm, moist, monsoon lands of southeastern Asia, where it is the leading starch food of the people. But with us, rice is not eaten so much as other cereals. 

Rice requires so much water that very little is grown except where the land can be irrigated and the rice kept under water during most of the growing period.

In the United States, machinery is generally used in the cultivation and harvesting of rice. The land is very level, and the dikes constructed to hold the water on the rice areas are made gently sloping so that the machines can be driven right over them.

This enables one man to care for 80 acres, while in China or India a man could not care for more than two or three acres. Louisiana, Arkansas, Texas, and California produce nearly all of the rice grown in the United 

States each year. 

Fruits and Vegetables. There are certain elements called vitamins which are necessary in our foods if we are to be strong and well. We know the classes of food in which three of these, vitamins A, B, and C, occur. 

Vitamin A is furnished by green leaf vegetables such as lettuce and spinach, and also by milk and eggs. Vitamin B is found in nearly all foods except refined sugars and fats, so that simply a variety of foods will assure us of this substance.

On the other hand, Vitamin C is found chiefly in fruits and vegetables. Herein lies the importance of including fruits and vegetables in our diet. One of the most dreaded

The business of producing small fruits and vegetables for sale is called truck farming or market gardening. This is usually carried on near the cities where the products are to be sold, and to which they can easily be transported.

One of the largest of these areas extends from New York City southward to Norfolk, Virginia. About one-fifth of all the vegetables grown for sale are produced in this area. Long Island has become especially noted for its production of vegetables for the 

New York City market.

The sandy soil of the Atlantic coastal plain is very suitable for growing certain kinds of vegetables where other crops will not do well.

Sandy soils also heat more rapidly, making the plants grow faster. 

Florida and Georgia send a great many winter vegetables to the northern city markets during the winter season. Strawberries are shipped to New York and other northerr.

Importance of Potatoes. Potatoes are by far the most valuable vegetable crop raised in the United States. They rank second to wheat in value as a table-food plant, and are one of the most important foods in Europe and America. In fact, as a world crop, their production exceeds that of any other single table-food plant.

SWEET POTATOES 

161 Potatoes generally grow best in a rather cool, moist climate. For this reason we find most of the potato crop grown in the northern section of the United States. The six states of Maine, New York, Pennsylvania, Michigan, Wisconsin, and Minnesota produce nearly half of all of the potatoes grown in the 

United States. Aroostook County, Maine, produces most of the potatoes grown in that state.

Potatoes yield a much larger amount of food per acre than any of the cereals. Where wheat production averages about 15 bushels to the acre, potatoes average over 100 bushels in the United States. Some of the

Fruit Raising in the United States. Besides vegetable gardens, most farmers have orchards where apples, peaches, pears, plums, cherries, and other fruits are grown for home

The fruits are apples, peaches, pears, plums, grapes, oranges, lemons, grapefruit, and strawberries. California leads all other states in the production of fruits. Why?

The dots in Florida stand largely for citrus fruits. Georgia is famous for its peaches. New York and Michigan are famous for grapes. However, the apple is the most important fruit in the United States. use and frequently for sale. In many sections where fruit grows well, farmers have specialized in this branch of agriculture. 

Apples. - Being the least perishable and the most used of the fruits grown in the northern United States, apples have always been an important crop for home use as they now are for sale. In addition to storing apples in the cellar for winter use, the farmers make quantities of cider, while the women dry apples, make apple butter, apple preserves, and apple jelly. If you live in a city, you probably purchase all of these products in a store. 

New York and Washington are the leading states in the production of apples. Most of the apples in New York are grown either just south of Lake Ontario, or in the Hudson

Valley. In Washington, apples are grown in irrigated valleys. The dry sunny weather in these western irrigated regions helps to produce bright-colored fruit of a pleasing flavor which is much in demand. Washington apples are shipped to New York and sold in competition with the home-grown fruit.

Their appearance and flavor make it possible to sell them at a price high enough to pay for the long journey across the continent. 

Other regions which produce many apples for sale are found in northern Virginia, extending through Maryland into Pennsylvania, in the valleys of the Ozark Mountains in northwestern Arkansas and southwestern Missouri, along the eastern shores of Lake Michigan in western Michigan, and in irrigated areas in western Colorado, Idaho, Oregon, and in west central California. 

Grapes.-The next most valuable fruit crop in the United States is grapes. Grapevines are quite common on the farms and even on town lots all through the northeastern and east central parts of our country, but there are a few centers where grape raising has become a special industry. 

Two regions are in New York, one around the Finger Lakes, and the other in the southwest corner of the state on the lake plain south of Lake Erie. A similar region of production is found in southwestern Michigan east of Lake Michigan. Most of the grapes

LUSCIOUS GRAPES 

If all the grapes on these great, heavy bunches are dried, what a lot of raisins there will be! They were grown in Brawley, California. grown in these two states are either eaten fresh or made into grape juice. 

California is the great leader in the grape industry, producing over three-fourths of all the grapes grown in the United States. Many of the grapes grown in California are converted into raisins which can be easily shipped all over the country. But large quantities for table use are also shipped out of the state. 

Peaches. Third in value on the list of fruits are peaches. They are more sensitive to cold than apples. Peach trees, under the influence of a warm spell in the early spring, are very apt to start budding and develop their blossoms before all the killing frosts of spring are over. This makes the peach crop rather dependent upon the weather, and consequently uncertain. 

Like apples, peaches are well distributed over the farms in our country, but most of them are raised a little south of the big apple districts. Areas where peaches are produced for sale are found in New York, just south of 

Lake Ontario, in southwestern Michigan, east of Lake Michigan, in the 

Chesapeake Bay region and southern New Jersey, in central Georgia, and in central and southern California near 

Los Angeles.

The Ozark Mountain region of Arkansas and northeastern Texas also grows many peaches. Georgia makes a specialty of early peaches for the northern markets, while 

California dries and cans a large part of her peach crop. 

Pears, plums, cherries. - 

These and other small fruits, also berries of many kinds are raised abundantly on farms, especially in the East, and again in the Pacific states. 

Cranberries, without which our Thanksgiving dinner would be incomplete, are largely a New England product. 

The greatest center of production is the Cape Cod region. They are also grown in southern New Jersey and in central Wisconsin. Prunes and apricots are raised especially

Citrus Fruits. 

Oranges and Other

About one-half of all the oranges grown in the world are produced in the United States. 

California and Florida are the leaders, though

Arizona and the states bordering the Gulf of 

Mexico have good orange groves. California produces over 40,000,000 boxes of oranges each year. This nearly equals the number grown in Spain, which is next to the United 

States in the production of oranges. Florida raises about 25,000,000 boxes. This is more than the number grown in Italy, which is second among the Mediterranean countries.

Oranges were introduced into Florida by the early Spanish explorers, and have been growing there ever since. Growing oranges for sale was begun in California about 1873 when the navel orange, a seedless variety, was introduced. It proved to be a big success. The seedless variety is popular for table use though the oranges with seeds often contain more juice. California and Florida. oranges are shipped all over the United States and southern Canada, and also to Europe. 

Grapefruit is a specialty of Florida, which ships out about two-thirds as many boxes of 165 grapefruit as of oranges, and also of the Lower 

Rio Grande Valley in Texas. This fruit has a wide sale in the United States, and a few grapefruit are shipped to Europe. 

Olives. - Olives, a typical Mediterranean product, are grown mostly in California. 

A few are also produced in Arizona. Olive orchards were planted by the Spanish missionaries in California during the eighteenth century. Some of the mission orchards are still to be seen. The best California olives are canned or pickled, while the poorer ones are used for olive oil. Olive culture has been tried in some of the Gulf states, but without success. 

Figs and Dates. Figs, like olives, are a 

Mediterranean product that have been successfully introduced into the United States. 

Although California produces most of the figs for market, they are grown in all of the Gulf states and as far north as Virginia. In 

California much of the fig crop is dried, but in the southeastern states all the figs not used locally are canned. Fresh figs are soft and soon decay, so very few of them are placed on sale. 

PICKING DATES 

This date grove is in the Coachella Valley in southern California. The frames attached to the trees are for the convenience of the workers in pollinating the blossoms and, later, in taking care of the bunches of fruit and, finally, picking. Paper covers are often placed over the bunches of fruit for their protection during the ripening period.

THE COCONUT PALM 

G. W. Romer, Miam 

Do coconuts remind you of rustling palms, chattering monkeys, and jungle shadows? Yet here are coconuts growing on a small plantation in Florida. The ripened nuts, if left on the ground where they fall, will soon sprout into other trees, and so the forest is built up. 

Dates are mostly grown on oases or on irrigated areas in arid regions. Although date trees will stand considerable frost, they need a high temperature with dry air to ripen their fruit. plus fruit has always been a practical way of preserving it. Strings of apples hung up to dry in the kitchens and around the open fireplaces were a common sight in colonial times. Peaches, pears, and different kinds of berries were also dried and kept for winter use, as canning was not then practiced. 

The fruits now most generally raised to be dried and sold in the markets are peaches, pears, apricots, prunes, and grapes (raisins).

Surplus apples and other fruits are frequently dried and placed on the market, but they are not generally grown for this purpose, as apples usually can be kept throughout most of the year. 

Of the dried fruits, California produces practically all of the raisins, peaches, pears, and apricots, and the greater part of the prunes and apples, though many of these are also grown in Oregon and Washington. New

York formerly led in the production of dried fruit, but has given place to California.

The chief reason for the leadership of Cali- Dates are now being grown in two of the fornia and the other Pacific states in the dried hot irrigated valleys in California and also in Arizona. 

About 6,000,000 pounds are being produced each year, but probably less than half of these find their way to distant markets. Dates raised in the United States are of a superior quality and are dried and packed in a clean manner, so that they bring higher prices than the imported dates from the Persian 

Gulf region.

SORTING ASPARAGUS 

Before being placed in the cans, the asparagus stalks are carefully assorted into different lengths and sizes. 

This cannery is in the Sacramento Valley of California, which is noted for its large production of this vegetable. fruit industry is the climate. The summers are dry and sunny so that most of the fruit can be dried in the open. The dry air causes the fruit to dry quickly, so it is lighter in color and better flavored than fruit dried in more humid climates such as we have in the eastern states. 

Home-dried fruit on eastern farms is dark and discolored, and though it may taste good, it is not attractive in appearance and does not sell so readily as the light-colored dried fruits of the west. 

Canning of Fruits and Veg etables. That fruits and vegetables could be preserved by placing them while boiling hot in a glass vessel and then sealing the vessel so as to make it airtight, was not discovered until late in the eighteenth century. The use of tin cans did not become general until after the middle of the nineteenth century. At first tinned goods were regarded with suspicion because people were afraid they were not wholesome, and they did not become popular until late in the past century. 

Now canning factories are scattered all over the country and almost all kinds of vegetables and fruits are canned. But certain kinds are canned more abundantly than others, especially tomatoes, corn, and peas of the vegetables, and peaches, apples, and pears among the fruits. 

Besides leading in peaches, apricots, pears, and olives, California is also the leader in canned asparagus and spinach. Oregon and

Washington lead in the output of canned blackberries. New York, with its large production of fruits and vegetables, also has extensive canneries putting up a great variety of products. Wisconsin has become noted for canned peas, putting up about one-half of all that are canned in the country. Other northern states can large quantities of string

One of the largest centers of the canning industry in the United States is on the Middle 

Atlantic Coastal Plain, with eastern Maryland as the leader. Here, in Maryland, Delaware, Virginia, and North Carolina, large quantities of tomatoes, corn, peas, string beans, and other vegetables, and berries of different kinds are canned. This is not only a good region in which to raise these vegetables and fruits, but there is good transportation both by water and by land, and markets are near by.

Sirup is mostly made from sorghum on the farms. Very little enters into commerce.

Sugar. - Sugar is an energy food, and as such plays a very important part in our diet. 

The American people eat more sugar per person than the people of any other country. 

We use each year an average of over 110 pounds per person, and altogether we consume about one-fourth of all the commercial sugar produced in the world. Over half of our sugar supply comes from Cuba and most of the rest from Puerto Rico, Hawaii, and the Philippines.

We raise only about one-third of our sugar requirements. Both beet sugar and cane

Others of the Gulf states grow sugar cane, but it is used mostly for the production of molasses or sirup.

The production of beet sugar in the United States is several times as great as that of cane sugar. Sugar beets are grown in many places in the northern and western states where the 

LOUISIANA SUGAR CANE 

The alluvial soil of Louisiana yields nearly three-quarters of all the sugar cane grown in the United States.

THE ROUND-UP Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction 

The men in charge of this herd have rounded up the cattle and are branding the calves. This is done in order that animals roaming at large may be identified by their owners. How can they tell to whom the calves belong? growing season is about five months in length. 

They need a very fertile soil, a moderately warm temperature with long hours of daylight, and plenty of rain during the growing season. They can be grown successfully under irrigation. Colorado, the leading state in the production of sugar beets, grows them under irrigation. California, Washington,

Idaho, Utah, and other western states do the same. States bordering on the Great Lakes, particularly Michigan, produce large quantities of sugar beets without irrigation, as they have plenty of rainfall during the summer. 

A beet-sugar factory is expensive to build and operate. So manufacturers have to be sure of a supply of sugar beets sufficient to keep them going before they will build. Raw sugar beets are heavy, and it would not pay to haul them long distances. Therefore the factories are usually built near the fields. 

Sugar beets require two or three times as much hand labor per acre as sugar cane. The ground is prepared and the seed sown by machinery, but after the beets come up, they must be thinned out and weeded by hand.

The young plants are then cultivated with a hoe. In harvesting, the tops of the beets are cut off by hand with a knife. It takes about six times as much man labor to produce an acre of sugar beets as it does to raise an acre of corn, and twelve times as much as an acre of hay. 

Importance of Farm Animals. - Have you ever stopped to consider what a big part farm

BEEF CATTLE 

171 animals play in our lives? If you live in a large city, you may very seldom see any of them, but your comfort, and to a large extent your health and strength, depend on these domestic animals. 

Your shoes are made of leather manufactured from cowhide and perhaps goatskin (kid). Your coat is probably made of wool from the sheep. For breakfast you have milk and cream furnished by the cow. If you eat toast you spread it with butter made from milk furnished by the cow. Perhaps you have a slice of bacon, or an egg, both animal products from the farm. Beef alone is the main food produced on about three-fourths of our total land area. In addition to enormous numbers of beef cattle raised on the large ranches in the drier regions of the West, they are found on one-fourth of the farms of the United States. 

Over half of the beef cattle in the United States are found in the Great Plains Region and in the Corn Belt. In the west they graze largely on private ranches, on public land known as free range, or on grazing land in the National Forests.

Many of the cattle raised on the plains are sent to the Corn Belt to be fattened. About one-sixth of the corn crop is fed to beef cattle. In addition to a large number of dairy cows, Iowa, the largest producer of corn, has more beef cattle per square mile than any other state. Texas has over 

7,000,000 cattle, most of which are raised for beef. 

More beef cattle are now being raised in the Cotton Belt than formerly. Here it is easy to raise food for them and in the milder ​Courtesy Seattle Chamber of Commerce

Cotton Belt are not good for growing cotton or any other crop, but make good grazing lands. 

Dairying in the United States. - Dairy products, such as milk, butter, and cheese, are among the most common articles of our diet. 

Scarcely a meal is prepared without using one or more of these products. Of the money spent for food in our country, about one-fifth goes for milk, butter, and cheese. Considerably over one-half of this amount is for milk alone. The average amount of milk

obudio bia 

SHEEP Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction FILLING THE SILOS 

The machine in front of the silo is cutting the corn stalks into small pieces and forcing them up through the feed pipe to the top of the silo, where they will be dropped inside. Fodder preserved in this way makes an excellent cattle food. per person consumed in the United States yearly is about fifty gallons, or nearly one gallon per week. Wisconsin now leads in the number of dairy cows, while New York is second.

Dairies are usually located near the city where the milk is to be sold or near transportation lines leading to that city. Great care is taken of dairy cattle and of the milk in transit. Cows are regularly inspected, and milk trains are given right of way in the same manner as passenger trains. 

Butter and cheese do not need to be made near the cities, as these products are more easily transported than milk. Cheese factories especially are located in the northern states, where the nights are cool and water is abundant. They will also be found in the highlands in the western states and in North 

Carolina, Virginia, and Tennessee. Wisconsin alone makes about two-thirds of all the cheese made in the United States.

Dairying is carried on very extensively in the middle western states, where feed for the cattle is abundant and cheap. Formerly butter making was the work of the farmer's wife, who poured the milk into pans and later 175 skimmed off the cream. Now the cream separator automatically separates the cream from the milk right after milking. The farmer may then take the cream to the butter factory or creamery, and sell it. The skim milk is retained for feeding to the swine or other animals, and some of it may be used in the household. 

Sheep 

Sheep in the United States. raising is one of the oldest agricultural industries. The sheep appears to have been one of the first animals to be domesticated.

Sheep are docile animals and easily handled, but are helpless in defending themselves against the attack of other animals. Cattle are good fighters and soon rout their enemies, but sheep running at large have to be guarded.

Sheep furnish two very essential products for people living in cool countries - wool and meat. Wool is next to cotton in importance. 

Before cotton came into universal use, wool was the leading cloth fiber in the temperate zone. The preparation of wool and weaving of woolen cloth was a well-developed household industry many centuries before weaving became a factory industry. As far back as we can go in history, we read of people

Merino sheep. In recent years, however, the demand for mutton has caused many of the English mutton types of sheep, such as the Shropshire, Hampshire, and Lincoln breeds, to be raised. We consume much less mutton per capita than do the people of Great Britain and other of the great sheep-raising countries, such as Australia, New 

Zealand, and Argentina. 

Swine. Swine, or hogs as they are commonly called, are the most numerous farm animals. They are found on three-fourths of the farms in the United States. Altogether there are about 45,using sheep's wool for making clothing and 000,000 in our country. Swine are easily its flesh for food. raised, as they are not fussy about what they eat. On the farms they are useful in consuming the parings of fruits and vegetables, the "left-overs" from the table, and the skim milk and other by-products from the dairy.

Sheep were introduced into our country as soon as settlements were made in the various colonies. The flocks had to be carefully guarded against Indians and wild animals, and also had to be sheltered during the severe winters. As the population increased, the sheep industry moved into hilly regions not desirable for farming land. 

While sheep are scattered on farms all over the country, there are some places where sheep raising is especially important. In the hilly country of southeastern Ohio and in southern Michigan the farmers keep large flocks, and sheep are also plentiful on the grazing lands of southwestern Texas and semi-arid regions of many of the western states. They are also grazed on many of the range areas in connection with our national forests.

The American people eat more pork than beef. In some years the amount of pork eaten is greater than that of beef and all other meats combined. Pork is a meat much used on farms where the farmer raises a pig or two for his own use. Ham, bacon, sausage, head cheese, and scrapple are a few of the other popular forms in which the "porker" appears in the market and on our tables.

Pork and lard are among our most valuable exports. Some of the western states specialize in the production of a very fat hog, sometimes called the lard-type hog. We

Swine originally ran wild in Asia, and the Chinese were among the first people to domesticate and breed these animals. The swine in our country are descended from Chinese breeds and other breeds from Europe. Swine were among the animals introduced and raised by the first colonists. They have continued to increase and spread with the settlement and development of our country.

From 30 to 40 per cent of our annual corn crop goes to market in the form of pork. Corn is bulky and brings a comparatively low price, so the growers frequently find it more profitable to raise hogs and feed the corn to them. Goat raising in the United States. — Like Like the hog, the goat is often the butt of funny remarks by those who do not stop to think. 

Occasionally we see a goat tethered in some backyard or on a hill near its owner's home. People humorously speak of goats eating tin cans and all sorts of rubbish.

The truth is that goats require good and suitable food just the same as any animal. 

They eat coarse herbage and small underbrush that other animals do not like. So they are often turned into brush land which the farmer wishes to clear. As they are surefooted, they are frequently grazed on rocky hillsides where the grass occurs in scattered patches and the animals must clamber about among the rocks in order to reach it. 

In many cases, goat's milk is preferable to cow's milk for infants and invalids. But as most Americans do not care for goat's milk, few goats are kept for this purpose.

Most of the goats in our country are of the Angora breed. Many people do not even know that there is such a goat. And more do not realize that there are millions of them in this country. You never hear of goat chops or goat meat. Yet large amounts of goat meat are placed on the market each year as mutton. 

Angora goats are highly prized for their long, fine silky wool, known as mohair. Their original home was in Asia Minor, hence the name Angora. From this part of Asia they have been carried to South Africa, the United 

States, and other countries. 

Most of the Angora goats in our country are raised in Texas, where they are grazed on the rugged plateau lands. Brush or cutover lands belonging to the government, as well as privately owned lands, are used for this purpose. The southwestern states, Louisiana, and Oregon lead in the number of goats raised. 

Horses and Mules. "The indispensable horse!" It is a fitting name for this most useful of animals. As we could not get along without the farmer, so the farmer could not get along without the horse. For the faithful horse does most of the heavy work necessary to produce our food. 

The tractor is used on the big level farms where it is economy to use machinery. It takes the place of several horses, and covers

HORSES AND MULES U. S. Department of Agriculture 

Each dot represents 10,000 acres. The greatest state acreage and production of hay is found in New York, followed by Wisconsin, Minnesota, Iowa, and Nebraska.

Relative to acreage in crops, hay is most important in the Rocky Mountain region, where it occupies over one-half of the crop land. What is hay? What is it used for? more ground in a given time. But there is much work on the average farm that cannot be done with the tractor and for this the horse must still be used. On small farms especially the horse is preferable to the tractor and less expensive, as his feed can be grown right on the farm. In this age of machines, when we travel on trains or in automobiles and do much of our work with the aid of steam, electricity, or "gas," let us not forget our debt to the horse.

Horse racing has always been very popular in sporting circles. In Kentucky there is an area known as the "Blue-Grass Region," where many of the famous thoroughbred racing horses have been raised. The Blue 

Grass Region is a land of fertile limestone soil which produces the luxuriant grass known as blue-grass. 

Saddle horses are bred for riding purposes just as thoroughbreds are raised especially for racing. They are used for pleasure riding, and by mounted police, and in the cavalry of our army. The horse has not only helped man to get a living but also to fight his battles and give him pleasure.

The coach or driving horse, of which there are several breeds, has largely been displaced 179 in the United States by the automobile. The motor car is much quicker and needs much less care than the horse. The day is fast coming when a horse-drawn cab in the street will be as much of a curiosity as the automobile was at the close of the last century. 

The draft horse is the type generally used for drawing heavy loads in cities or towns, and for the many kinds of work on the farm. It is also used for excavating and grading in the construction of roads and buildings. Lighter weight draft horses are frequently used for drawing light delivery wagons. Heavy draft horses are used for making transfers of goods for short distances in the business sections of our large cities. 

Horses were first introduced into North America by the Spaniards, who brought them into Mexico. From there they spread into the United States, and in company with the bison roamed wild over the great western prairies. Some herds of these horses are still to be found in the western part of our country. They are called mustangs. These are the horses which the Indians captured and used. Hence the name, "Indian ponies." 

Have you ever seen the "bronco" trying to throw his rider? The "bronco" is one of the wilder

towns throughout the country. Large numbers of horses are raised in the Corn Belt where the climate is suitable and feed is easily and cheaply produced. 

Most of the mules are raised in the Mississippi Valley, Missouri being the leading state in their production. Considerable numbers of both horses and mules are exported from the United States. 

Importance of Poultry. More people keep poultry than any other class of animals.

Besides the farmers who raise poultry for U. S. Department of Agriculture sale, many a person in town keeps a few chickens in his back yard. Poultry, including ducks, geese, turkeys, guinea hens, pheasants, and swans as well as hens, are found on 90 per cent of our farms, and are much in demand for their meat as well as their eggs.

This young man from Jefferson County, Pennsylvania, belongs to a horse-raising club and he has good reason to be proud of his two-year-old Belgian gelding. For what kinds of work are these horses largely used? mustangs and resists all efforts to break him to the saddle.

The Spaniards also introduced into America the ass, familiarly known to us as the donkey. 

Descendants of these animals now found in our western states are called burros. They are very strong in proportion to their size, and being sure-footed, they are much used for riding in rough, mountainous regions.

The mule is a cross between the horse and the ass, and has some of the better qualities of both these animals. The mule is a hardier animal than the horse, smaller in size, and stronger in proportion to his size.

Mules are much used in mines, where their smaller size and their endurance make them more suitable than the horse. They are good work animals, taking the place of the horse to a large extent on the southern farms and plantations. They have also been much in demand in the army, where they are used for transporting supplies. Though not so speedy as the horse, they are frequently used to draw wagons and even carriages.

There are now about 11,000,000 horses and about 4,500,000 mules in the United States, distributed over the farms, and in cities and We produce and use each year in the United 

States over 3,000,000,000 dozen eggs. This is an average of over 200 eggs per person. 

Eggs are easily digested and are a very nutritious food. The yolk of the egg is rich in protein and fat as well as phosphorus, calcium, and iron-three much-needed mineral elements. They can be prepared in so many different ways that it is hard to find a person

181 who does not like eggs cooked in one way or another. The use of eggs in cakes, custards, puddings, and different kinds of confections accounts for a large part of our consumption.

Eggs, like meat, can be kept in cold storage, and thus made available for use during the winter season when fresh-laid eggs are scarce. 

They can also be dried and reduced to the form of a powder. Bakers and confectioners use large quantities of powdered eggs. In this form they are easily shipped, whereas eggs in the shell have to be carefully crated and handled with care.

The meat of poultry, when well cooked, is easily digested. For this reason it is often prescribed for invalids when other meats are forbidden. Chickens form much the larger part of the poultry raised and consumed in our country. Turkeys are raised especially for the winter holidays. Ducks, geese, and turkeys are found on farms in all of the states.

Our corn crop exceeds the cotton crop in value, but much of the corn is raised to be used on the farm where it is grown. Cotton is raised to be sold.

No other product has played such an important part in our history and in the agricultural development of the southern states.

U. S. Department of Agriculture COTTON ACREAGE AND PRODUCTION 

The northern boundary of the Cotton Belt is about the line of 200 days of average frost-free season and 77 degrees mean summer temperature; the southern boundary is that of 10 inches of autumn rainfall, because wet weather interferes with picking and damages the lint. 

The western boundary of cotton production without irrigation is about the line of 20 inches average annual rainfall. 

The blackest areas are those of richest soils, such as the 

Black Prairies of Texas, or heavily fertilized areas, such as those of the Piedmont. moved westward. All this region is now known as the Cotton Belt. 

Climate of the Cotton Belt. The Cotton 

Belt is limited on the west by the lack of sufficient rainfall, and on the north by the low temperature. The cotton plant requires at least 200 frost-free days in which to grow and ripen its bolls. In the Cotton Belt most of the rain comes in the spring and summer as a rule. There is little rainfall in the autumn during cotton-picking time. Too much rain at this time spoils the cotton. 

Planting and cultivating the cotton plant is usually done by machinery. But cotton picking is done almost entirely by hand as no machine has yet been invented which will do this work satisfactorily. One difficulty is that not all of the cotton bolls ripen at one time, so that several pickings are necessary. 

Differences in Soils of the Cotton Belt. - 

About two-thirds of the Cotton Belt consists of the broad coastal plain, the soil of which states has always been largely dependent is made up of alluvial deposits carried down on cotton, their chief crop. 

Effect of the Invention of the Cotton Gin. 

Cotton has been raised and used in India for over twenty-five centuries. However, until 

1793, when the cotton gin came into general use, the work of separating the fiber from the seed made cotton a very expensive fiber. 

From one to two pounds of lint was all that one person could clean in a whole day's work. 

About 12,000,000 bales or about 6,000,000,000 pounds of cotton are now raised each year in the 

United States. In 1926, over 18,000,000 bales or about 9,000,000,000 pounds of cotton were produced. 

Do you see how impossible this large output would be without the use of the cotton gin? Do you know who invented the cotton gin and how it works? 

Within ten years after the invention of the cotton gin, our exports to Great Britain increased seven thousand fold. One hundred years later we were growing three-fourths of the world's cotton supply. The area of production was gradually increased and from the Appalachian and the Ozark highlands. Cotton can be raised on some of the more sandy soils of the coastal plain, and on some soils in the Piedmont region if fertilized. 

Best of all are the lowlands of the coastal plain in Alabama, Mississippi, and Texas, U. S. Department of Agriculture COTTON BEFORE THE BLOOM 

Nearly all of the work of growing cotton is done with the use of machinery. The seed is planted in rows far enough apart so that horse-drawn cultivators can be easily run between the rows. This field is in South Carolina.

THE SPREAD OF THE BOLL WEEVIL U. S. Department of Agriculture ALL READY TO BE PICKED! 

A white tufted cotton plant showing the cotton fiber protruding from the opened bolls just as it appears before picking. This variety of cotton has very large bolls. containing a rich, black soil, and the bottom lands along the Mississippi, Tennessee, and 

Arkansas rivers.

Cotton should not be grown on the same soil year after year. If this is done, it exhausts the soil of much-needed elements, such as nitrogen, potash, and phosphorus, and the yield decreases. Fertilizers containing these plant foods are much used on the cotton plantations. In growing cotton, the cost of fertilizers is next to that of labor. "King Cotton." - Owing to the great demand for cotton and to the fact that the climate and soil of the Cotton Belt are so well suited for its growth, most farmers in the Cotton Belt came to grow cotton to the exclusion of other crops. Cotton thus became the leading crop of this part of the country. On account of this and its importance as an export, it came to be called " King Cotton." 

The Spread of the Boll Weevil. - But 

"King Cotton's" throne is undermined by a little insect known as the boll weevil. The weevil lays its eggs in the young cotton boll. 183 When the larvae are hatched, they feed upon the boll, destroying its growth and causing it to rot or dry up and fall off. This pest first entered the United States from Mexico in 1892 and has slowly spread over practically the entire Cotton Belt. Many times it has caused the loss of fully half the crop in the infected areas.

As yet it has been found impossible to destroy this pest entirely. But its numbers can be kept down by destroying as many of the weevils as possible and by planting early varieties of cotton which mature before the weevils become very plentiful. Only a few boll weevils survive the winter, and it takes them some time during the following summer to increase enough to do great damage.

Large numbers of weevils are now being destroyed by a poisonous powder which is dusted over the cotton plants by airplane. 

Another very destructive insect pest which has appeared in some parts of Texas and

Louisiana is the pink bollworm. It is being controlled by prohibiting the growing of cotton in the infected areas. This prevents it from increasing and spreading. 

Courtesy Mississippi State Board of Development PICKING COTTON 

Skillful workers pull the fluffy white fiber from the opened boll and place it in large baskets. These pickers must use good judgment and select only those bolls which are fully ripe. They must also be careful not to allow pieces of the hard outer covering of the boll to become mixed with the fiber. This scene is in Mississippi.

U. S. Department of Agriculture WAITING THEIR TURN 

Farmers with their mule-drawn wagons loaded with cotton are waiting their turn at a local cotton gin in North Carolina. Here the seeds will be removed from their cotton and the fiber pressed into bales. 

The Cotton Boll Weevil Causes Changes in Agricultural Methods. While the boll weevil has done much harm to the cottongrowing industry, it has been the cause of a beneficial change in agriculture in the South. 

The destruction due to this insect each year made cotton a very uncertain crop to southern growers. This forced them to turn to other crops in order to make a living.

Before the coming of the boll weevil, cotton had always been a very easy crop to raise. 

Plantation owners could rent their land to Negro tenants and live in town on the rents. 

Often these Negro tenants were shiftless as well as poor, and did not get much over half of the cotton they should have produced from each acre.

When the ravages of the boll weevil reduced the cotton crop so much that these tenants could no longer pay the rents, the owners were compelled to take charge, introduce scientific methods, and oversee the work of their farmers. Steps were taken to fight the boll weevil and cotton acreage was reduced. 

More land and attention were given to the cultivation of corn, cowpeas, and other forage crops, beans, peanuts, vegetables, and fruits of various kinds. These other crops are now bringing to the people of the South more money than cotton and giving them a prosperity which they never enjoyed before. 

Much of this good work has been accomplished by means of education in agriculture, both in the public schools and in colleges and universities.

Considerable attention is now being given to dairying, which was formerly neglected in the cotton-growing sections. One of the reasons for this was a disease caused by an insect called the cattle tick. With the aid of the 

United States government, this pest has been largely overcome. Cattle raising and dairying are now profitable industries. Feed for cattle and swine, formerly imported from the North Central states, is now being grown in the Cotton Belt. 99 

American-Egyptian Cotton. - Varieties of 

Egyptian cotton were introduced into the irrigated valleys of California and Arizona and experimented with until a very fine variety known as "Pima cotton was developed. This variety of cotton has a long, fine, strong fiber which was formerly in demand for use in manufacturing tires, but is now used chiefly for fine dress goods.

IMPORTANCE OF TOBACCO TO THE EARLY COLONISTS 

COTTON IN CALIFORNIA 

These cotton bales are in the Imperial Valley of California where cotton is raised under irrigation. Our southwestern-grown cotton is of very good quality. Why is it not necessary to place these bales under sheiter? 

Its cultivation is now confined to the Salt River Valley in Arizona. As it is expensive to produce and commands a high price in the market, manufacturers prefer to use a cheaper grade whenever possible. Consequently,

California growers have turned to the upland variety, for which there is a greater demand. 

Uses of Cottonseed. About two-thirds of the weight of cotton as it is picked is in seed. At first one of the big problems of cotton growers was the disposal of these cottonseeds. Finally some one discovered that the crushed cottonseed was an excellent fertilizer. Then other uses were discovered, until now there are a large number of products made from cottonseed, and they are a valuable part of the cotton crop. 

The four primary products derived from cottonseed are linters, hulls, cake, and oil. 

The linters are the very short fibers that adhere to the cottonseed after ginning. Linters are used for a variety of purposes, including wadding, stuffing material for pads, cushions, horse collars, mattresses, and upholstery, absorbent cotton, felt, lamp and candle wicks, twine, rope, writing paper, guncotton. They are also the basis for the manufacture of arti- 185 ficial leather, celluloid, collodion, varnishes, artificial silks, and photographic films. 

The cake is the compressed kernels of the seeds after the hulls are removed. When ground, it forms cottonseed meal. This is used for feeding cattle.

Refined cottonseed oil is a substitute for olive oil in this country, and it is also extensively used in the manufacture of substitutes for butter and lard. 

Importance of Tobacco to the Early Colonists. When the European colonists first came to America, they found the Indians growing tobacco from Canada to the southern part of Brazil. The colonists soon learned to use it and to raise it for themselves. They carried it over to Europe, where its use spread rapidly in spite of the opposition of both clergy and rulers. Perhaps there is no other plant so universally used to-day as is tobacco. 

Tobacco was to the early colonists in Virginia what cotton later became to the whole group of southern states a money crop. 

There were few crops that the Virginia colonists could raise for which there was a demand in the old country. At the same time they were in great need of something which they could sell or exchange for old world products which they could not produce for themselves.

Where Tobacco Is Raised. - Tobacco is raised in many places but the acreage is small. 

In the United States, tobacco fields are found in at least forty-two states, but only in certain parts of those states. North Carolina, which produces more tobacco than any other state, has over twice as much acreage in both corn and cotton as in tobacco.
