Cultivation and Preparation of Tobacco. - 

Both the culture and the curing of tobacco require a great deal of labor. The young plants have to be carefully transferred from the seed bed to the open field and cultivated. 

They must also be watched for worms which would destroy the leaves, and the suckers (shoots from the main stalk) must be broken off. off.

Curing or drying the tobacco must also be carefully done, if the tobacco is to be of high grade. Tobacco may be cured in one of three ways: (1) flue-cured, where the tobacco is placed in an air-tight barn and dried by heat carried into the barn by means of flues extending under the hanging tobacco; (2) firecured, where it is dried in the smoke of an open wood fire; (3) air-cured, where the tobacco is hung inside a building and the air allowed to circulate freely without artificial heat being used.

MANUFACTURE AND SALE OF TOBACCO Courtesy Hartford Chamber of Commerce TOBACCO IN CONNECTICUT 

Along the Connecticut River Valley are many tobacco fields, where fine plants like these are grown. It is the highest priced tobacco grown in the United States, and is used mainly for cigar wrappers. Why do you think this good grade is grown here? In what other states is tobacco grown?

How Tobacco Is Used. - Tobacco is used in a number of ways. It may be made into cigars or cigarettes; it may be shredded or cut fine to be smoked in a pipe; it may be sweetened and pressed into a solid cake known as chewing plug; or it may be powdered and used as snuff.

Different types of tobacco are used for different purposes, and these different kinds are grown in different parts of the country. For example, tobacco used especially for cigar wrappers is grown in the Connecticut Valley, in Wisconsin, and in northern Florida. Tobacco for filling cigars is a specialty in southeastern Pennsylvania and southwestern Ohio. 

Burley tobacco, used for chewing plug, is largely grown in western Kentucky. Dark, fire-cured and air-cured tobacco grown in 

Virginia and North Carolina is used for chewing plug and snuff, and a large amount is exported. Bright, flue-cured tobacco, grown especially in South Carolina and Georgia, is in demand for the manufacture of smoking tobacco and cigarettes, as well as chewing plug. Much of this tobacco is also exported. 

187 Tobacco and the Soil. The quality of tobacco is very dependent on the kind of soil and the climate. Tobacco soon exhausts most soils unless fertilizers are applied. In the early days of this industry in our country, new land was plentiful and when the tobacco growers found their crops decreasing, they simply abandoned the old fields and moved to new lands with fertile soil. Now rotation of crops is practiced and large amounts of fertilizers are applied. 

Manufacture and Sale of Tobacco. The 

United States not only grows and exports more tobacco than any other country, but also consumes more. The average yearly consumption of tobacco per person is now about ten pounds. As some of the manufactured tobacco brings a high price, our tobacco bill is a big one.

Factories for the manufacture of tobacco number over 10,000 in the United States. 

North Carolina, with six factories, makes over half of all the cigarettes made in the 

United States. Tobacco manufactures are heavily taxed; the government revenue from taxes on manufactured tobacco is larger than the amount the growers receive for raising the tobacco.

WHERE THE FISH ARE FOUND

FISH PIER, BOSTON Courtesy Boston Chamber of Commerce 

Twelve hundred feet of pier devoted exclusively to the fishing industry! It was built at a cost of $3,000,000 and includes the largest fish-freezing plant in the world. Boston is a great center for fresh fish and is the leading port for export because of the thousands of pounds unloaded daily. 

B. FISHERIES 

Fish and Early New England. - Bartholo mew Gosnold of England, who sailed along the New England coasts in 1602, recorded that his ships were "pestered with cod."

Captain John Smith was likewise impressed by the multitude of fish. He declared that they, like mines, would afford gold but "with less hazard and charge, and more certaintie and facility."

The abundance of fish thus provided food for the colonists of this region, furnished a product for export, and resulted in the start and growth of shipbuilding. Colonies to the south of New England also caught fish, but the influence of the industry upon them was much less marked. 

Uses Made of Fish. The colonists used fish for food. They learned from the Indians the value of fish as a fertilizer, and from cod livers they secured oil, which was used for 

Fish are medicinal purposes and for curing leather.

These are the principal uses even to-day. 

Where the Fish Are Found. 

found to some extent in almost all the streams and lakes of the United States. Commercially they are limited in the main to the Great Lakes, the shore lines of the country, and the continental shelf (the shallow ocean floor along the coast). Along the latter there are in various places, especially off New England, shallow areas or "banks where the fish congregate for feeding. It is here that the fishermen make their big catches. 99 Off New England such fish as cod, haddock, hake, halibut, herring, and mackerel are secured in great numbers. The region is also the greatest producer of lobsters. The 

Atlantic coast south of New England supplies bluefish, croaker, menhaden, shad, and squeteague. This area surpasses in the production of oysters, for which Chesapeake Bay and Long Island Sound are the leading cen-

Courtesy Mobile Chamber of Commerce RED SNAPPERS 

Fishing smacks have just brought these fish from the Gulf of Mexico into the port of Mobile, where they are being unloaded for distribution throughout the country. 

Now that refrigeration is possible, more attention is being given to fishing in the warmer waters. What advantages can you see in the location of Mobile as a center for this industry?

American and Canadian ports resort to these banks each year.

When a cod-fishing schooner arrives at a bank, long lines or trawls are baited. Small boats, called dories, are lowered into the water. Into each go two men along with several tubs of baited lines. Cod feed on the bottom and the line settles to their feeding ground. At regular intervals the trawl is pulled up and examined for fish. These are removed and the hooks are rebaited. 

The catch is then cleaned, iced, and placed in the hold. If the schooner is to be away from the home port for a long time the fish will be split, lightly salted, and then piled in the hold.

Upon the return to port, cod that have been salted on the boats are salted again and then dried in drying rooms of factories or out of doors on frames. Thus preserved they are ters. Chesapeake Bay is also a center for sold as salt fish, or, after the removal of the the catching of crabs.

The fisheries of the Gulf coast are developing rapidly and supply mullet, red snapper, oysters, and great quantities of shrimp. 

Along the Pacific coast the salmon are the most important fish, but herring, halibut, and cod are found extensively. The Great 

Lakes provide such fresh-water fish as pike, perch, cisco, trout, and whitefish.

Cod Fishing in the Atlantic. - Cod are taken from Delaware Bay northward, but the real cod grounds are the banks which extend off Cape Cod in a northeasterly direction to 

Newfoundland. These are irregular-shaped areas covered by comparatively shallow water. Georges Bank is the largest one near the coast of the United States. It lies some hundred miles southeast of Cape Cod and covers an area exceeding nine thousand square miles. Over it and the other banks food is more abundant than in deeper water. Because of this fact certain fish such as cod, haddock, and halibut collect here in great numbers. Hundreds of fishing vessels from bones, as shredded or boneless cod. Boston is the leading fishing port in New England, but Gloucester and Portland also are important centers. Fresh fish, cleaned, frozen, DRYING COD, GLOUCESTER

The cod are split in half, salted, and then spread out to dry. Each half is known as a flake and the arrangement shown in the picture is called a flake yard. Sometimes it is necessary to shield the drying fish from the hot sun with canvas awnings such as you see in the picture.

Mackerel Fishing. -"Fish-O!" calls the lookout and the mackerel catch is on.

Guided by the lookout the schooner is brought near the school of fish. Into the seine boat, which has been towed along, go part of the crew. Dividing the work of rowing the boat and setting the seine, these men soon have many of the fish surrounded. Then by means of lines the bottom of the net or seine is skillfully pulled together. The schooner comes up and the seine is made fast to it. 

As the seine is raised and the closely packed mackerel come into sight they are dipped out onto the vessel's deck and then placed in the hold for icing.

Instead of the purse seine just described gill nets may be used. These may be likened to great tennis nets ranging in length from 

150 to 180 feet and averaging 18 feet in depth.

Cork floats hold them up and leaden weights at the bottom hold them vertical. Mackerel swimming into the net find the openings large enough for their heads, but not their bodies, to penetrate. Attempting to pull back, the fish find themselves caught by their gill covers and held fast. Seines and nets rather than trawl lines are used because mackerel feed near the surface and travel in schools. 

Mackerel appear off the Cape Hatteras- Chesapeake Bay region anywhere from the last of March to the middle of April. Then by slow stages they move northward along the coast past New Jersey, Long Island,

Cape Cod, and finally reach the waters of Nova Scotia. Boats from various ports go out to meet them. The first catches of the season are taken immediately into the nearest port where they can be shipped by rail to the distributing points. Later when the fish are farther off shore longer trips are made.

If the catches are large and markets not easily available, the mackerel may be split 191 and salted. The season ends about the middle of October. The work of the mackerel fisherman is as strenuous as that of the cod fisherman. It is, however, not quite so dangerous, since he operates during the warmer part of the year and fogs are not so frequent. 

A GOOD CATCH! 

Ewing Galloway 

Baskets of sardines, caught off the coast of California, are being hoisted to the pier at Monterey from the fishing boat which has just brought them in. Large quantities of these small fish are caught and canned in this region as well as along the coast of Maine. 

The Pacific Coast Salmon. "Gold fish," our soldiers in the World War came to call the canned salmon upon which they were fed. 

A rather fitting name when it is realized that salmon have added millions to the wealth of our country. In 1929, an especially good year, the value of the salmon pack (canned) of the United States and Alaska exceeded $56,000,000. In addition, quantities of salmon. were frozen, smoked, salted, or sold fresh. 

Salmon are found along the Pacific coast from California to Alaska. They are caught

not only in the waters of the coast but also in the westward-flowing streams. In the 

United States proper the lower Columbia

River and the Puget Sound district are the leading salmon-catching areas.

As with other fish the habits of the salmon determine the methods of capture. Hatched in shallow streams or lakes, often several hundred miles from the ocean, the young salmon swim and feed together in schools.

When they reach a length of two or three 

OYSTER FISHING 

Ewing Galloway

This boat on the lower Delaware River is returning with the day's catch, 78,000 oysters, on board. Oysters are taken either by means of a dredge dragged along the bottom, or by a long-handled pair of tongs which can be operated by the fisherman standing near the edge of his boat. inches they gradually find their way to the ocean. Here they increase in size until they reach an average weight of four to twenty-five pounds, depending upon the species. Maturity is reached at the age of two to seven years and then they return to fresh water for the purpose of spawning. 

Fighting their way upstream against the current, struggling through rapids, and leaping small waterfalls, they finally reach shallow water. Here they deposit their eggs. During the entire journey, which may take many weeks and cover a distance as great as fifteen hundred miles, the salmon touch no food, and they die soon after spawning. 

Since the salmon travel in schools, it is possible to catch great numbers of them in nets. Where they run in definite channels of a river, traps of various kinds are sometimes used. Along the Columbia River the fish wheel is employed. This resembles a great water wheel to which are attached large scoops shaped like dip nets and made of wire netting. Placed near the shore where the fish pass in great numbers upstream, the wheel is turned by the current of the river. 

Due to overfishing, salmon are growing scarcer and the catch of this delicious fish has been steadily decreasing for many years. 

The salmon-fishing season varies somewhat with locality but extends roughly from late in the spring on into September. Those who are concerned with salmon catching or canning are thus engaged in a seasonal business. 

The time and the length of the season are determined in all cases by the run of the fish to the spawning regions. 

Oysters. The early colonists along the coast soon found the oyster. An account of the first expedition to Virginia tells of a landing party coming upon a fire where the 

Indians" had beene newly a rosting Oysters."

The Indians had fled and left many of the oysters in the fire. "We eat some of the 

Oysters," the account continues, "which were very large and delicate in taste." A letter on "Conditions of Life in Plymouth," 

1621, states: "Oysters we have none near but we can have them brought by the Indians when we will."

Natural beds of oysters were found along the coasts of the other colonies. Gradually since colonial times additional beds have been located and planted, until every coastal state except Maine and New Hampshire has an oyster industry. The oyster industry has become so important that it is the most valuable fishery of the United States. More oysters are secured in the United States than in all the rest of the world.

The salmon wheel in the Columbia River pictured above was an interesting feature of early days. Wire buckets on the revolving wheel caught the fish and threw them into a boat below the wheel. This method was so destructive of the small fish that it was abolished by law. Seining salmon (lower left) is now one of the most common ways of catching these fish as they are wending their way up the Columbia River. The men in the picture at the lower right are landing 50,000 salmon caught in a fish trap in Puget Sound. A special kind of net is used to lift the salmon from the trap into the scow. 193

G. W. Romer, Miami

NOT MOCK TURTLES, REAL TURTLES 

A fine catch of green sea turtles, from near Miami. 

These ancient, hard-shelled turtles are caught along the shores of Florida and supply the epicurean tastes of those who want green turtle soup" and "turtle steak" on their dinner menus.

Oysters are not found in the open sea but in partially inclosed waters such as bays and mouths of rivers. Moderate temperature and freedom from mud and shifting sand are the important factors in their successful growth. 

Favorable temperature conditions exist where waters do not get colder than freezing and reach sixty-eight degrees or more for part of the year. From Cape Cod to Mexico shallow waters meeting these conditions are most abundant. The greatest centers for the industry are Chesapeake Bay and Long Island Sound.

Oysters require from two to four years to reach market size. The more rapid growth occurs in the warmer southern waters. Oysters are taken with long-handled tongs or with dredges. If tongs are used they are worked by the oysterman while standing in a rowboat. Dredges are used with larger boats and are pulled slowly along over the oyster beds. When thought to be full the dredges are hauled in and the catch secured. 

After being landed oysters are disposed of in several different ways. Some are canned; others are shipped just as they are in barrels or sacks; still others are shucked and then shipped packed in ice. Shucking consists in opening the oyster and removing the meat. 

The shells are an important product, for instead of being wasted they are used for oyster beds, for making lime that is applied to the soil, for poultry grit, and for making shell roads.

Man, by excessive fishing, has been the greatest enemy of the oyster. In order to maintain the supply, oyster culture has been developed. Suitable places are strewn with empty shells. To these, tiny young oysters in the free-swimming stage of their growth attach themselves. Sometimes young oysters called seed oysters are taken from other beds and scattered over the prepared area. They are then permitted to grow until sufficiently large for market. In many states suitable oyster ground is surveyed, laid out in plots, and rented to the oystermen who are protected by law. The value of oysters from planted grounds exceeds that of oysters from natural beds.

CUTTING UP TUNA 

Great tuna fish, sometimes ten feet long and weighing nearly 1500 pounds, live in the warmer waters about the United States. These were caught along the shores of southern California.

FISH IN OUR COMMERCE 

at about $210,000,000. The annual sales by fishermen of fish and fish products are placed at over $60,000,000. The output of canned fish and by-products is valued at nearly $100,000,000. When the last two values are added the total seems rather imposing. 

Yet on several occasions the value of Iowa's corn crop has exceeded it. 

If not so large and important as some other industries, fishing nevertheless adds to the country's commerce. Refrigerating processes and transportation facilities have made it possible to distribute fresh and frozen fish over the whole United States. A few years ago the Bureau of Fisheries found that the inland city of St. Louis was supplied with fresh and frozen fish by twenty-eight states and four Canadian provinces. They came by express and by freight, and a small amount by barge on the Mississippi River.

Canned fish are in demand and fish oil, fish meal, and fertilizer all add to the number of commercial transactions.

In our export trade fish no longer occupy such a leading position as in colonial times. 

Canned fish represents another change, for it, rather than salted and dried fish, leads in the exports. Of the canned varieties sardines and salmon are the most important. About half our exports go to the United Kingdom, Canada, the Philippines, British Malaya, Australia, and Germany.

The value of the fish imported, including shellfish, is much greater than that of the fish exported. Sometimes it is almost twice as great. Canada, Norway, Japan, the

C. FORESTS 

Historical Importance of the Forests. 

The early explorers and colonists of our country along the Atlantic seaboard found themselves faced by dense forests that seemed to extend endlessly toward the west. "A vast, unpeopled wilderness," William Bradford of Plymouth described it, "filled with savage beasts and still more savage men."

The Indian had a way of living adapted to forest surroundings. He was primarily a hunter and fisherman. His food was meat from forest animals, fish from the streams, fruits, berries, nuts, and roots of the forest, and such vegetables or corn as his squaw cultivated among the dead trees. There was also sugar from the sap of the maple. His

FOREST REGIONS OF THE UNITED STATES 

tent and his clothes were made from the hides of forest animals. Sometimes his hut was of bark. His canoe was of birch bark or hollowed from a log. His weapons were of wood, leather, and stone, materials which could be secured in the forest region. 

The colonists had tools and weapons which the Indians had not, yet they, too, had to adjust themselves to the forest. It was a source of food to them, and they also turned to it for building material and fuel. Forced by the need of cleared land upon which to grow crops, they early attacked the forest with their tools and with fire.

In the building up of colonial industry and trade, the forests played an outstanding part. Lumbering, shipbuilding, and the manufacture of naval supplies were all dependent upon the forest. 

Furs and fuel were secured from it. Cattle which roamed in the forests on the outskirts of civilization provided meat, and their hides were tanned by extracts from bark. Thus gradually the colonists turned the forests of the Atlantic coast to their own use and came to dominate them. 197 settlers came to look upon the forests as an obstacle or a curse, as immense labor was necessary to clear lands, and often the woods concealed hostile Indians and animals. Now people are turning to the forests for recreation and health.

G. W. Romer, Miami

Through the deep, tangled forests of stunted cypress and saw grass runs a modern auto highway into the open, as it spans a winding river, to hide again as it blends into the vast, ever fascinating everglades of Florida. 

Uses of the Forest To-day. - A list of the uses made of our forests to-day would contain practically all those just mentioned. In addition there would be uses not thought of at that time. Wood pulp for paper, ties for railroads, telegraph and telephone poles, are some examples.

There are also indirect uses which do not require cutting of the forests. It has been found that trees on slopes retard the surface run-off of rain water, thus helping to hold the soil and to moderate flood waters. The early 

Forest Regions of the United States. - 

Nearly half the land area of the United States was originally forested. Rainfall is the determining factor in the location of forests. In the prairie country trees were found along stream courses by the early explorers and settlers, but there were no real forests. 

The forests of the United States have been divided into five regions. 

(1) The Northern Forest extends from 

Maine westward to Minnesota and southward along the Appalachian Mountains to northern Georgia. White pine has been the most valuable tree because its wood is easily worked and is very durable. So extensive has been its use for building and other purposes that it is being exhausted. Other important trees are the spruce and hemlock,

DRY FOREST HAWAIIAN ISLANDS 

The United States contains 470 million acres of forest lands of all sorts, timbered, cut over, and burned, all of which are more suitable for growing trees than for any other use. Much of this will always be forest land, and the area is ample to grow all the wood needed for our own use and for export trade, if it can be kept at work growing trees. 

Eighty-one million acres of this forest land, however, is now lying idle, a waste caused in large part by careless cutting and destructive forest fires. whose lumber, though poorer, is replacing the white pine. Hardwoods or broad-leafed 

Hardwoods or broad-leafed trees such as beech, birch, maple, and oak are scattered throughout the area. The location of this forest near centers of population and waterways has resulted in the rapid cutting and marketing of the timber. 

(2) The Central Hardwood Forest borders the Appalachian part of the Northern Forest and extends from Texas, central Arkansas, and southern Tennessee northward to the 

Great Lakes. Trees such as ash, chestnut, hickory, oak, tulip, walnut, and gum predominate. These are called hardwoods because their wood is hard in comparison with pine and spruce. They are used extensively in making farm implements, tools, furniture, and interior finishings. Much of the timber of this region has disappeared as a result of lumbering and the clearing of the land for agriculture. (3) The Southern Forest stretches along the 

Atlantic and Gulf Coastal Plains from Virginia to Texas. The warm, moist climate and the sandy soil provide conditions especially favorable for the growth of the yellow pine. 

In swampy lowlands the cypress, another valuable tree, is found. The gum, a hardwood tree, is also important. The yellow pine now supplies more of our lumber than any other tree. This section has for a number of years been the leading lumber-producing area of the United States. Small areas of tropical forest are found in Florida and Texas.

LUMBERING 

199 (4) The Rocky Mountain Forest is located on the Rocky Mountain ranges from New 

Mexico and Arizona northward to Canada.

Wherever the elevation is high enough to force the winds to give up sufficient moisture for growth trees are found. This causes much of the forest to be in scattered areas, many of which are difficult to get at. Because of this fact and the distance from the great centers of population, a large part of the forest remains. Western yellow pine, western white pine, larch, spruce, and fir are leading varieties. 

(5) The Pacific Coast Forest is probably the most magnificent coniferous (cone-bearing) forest in the world. From northern 

California through Oregon and Washington wonderful stands of huge trees are found. 

The soil, the plentiful rainfall, and the mild winters of the region favor the growth of these splendid forests. The big trees or sequoias of California, only a few groves of which are left, and the redwoods are the largest in size. Some of the sequoias are twentyfive feet in diameter at the base and three hundred feet high. They are interesting rather than important commercially

LUMBERING IN KENTUCKY 

This little sawmill is situated in the midst of a forest in Kentucky. The stream at the right furnishes the water supply. Sawmills are very likely to be found near where lumbering is being carried on. How are the logs being transported to the mill ?

Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction REDWOOD FOREST 

Through the sunlight and shadow of forests of these famous trees winds the State Highway in California. To preserve the beauty and magnificence of highways like this, the forests have been made state parks. 

The Douglas fir is the most important timber tree of the region. It, too, is a large tree. Under good average conditions it reaches a diameter of four to six feet and a height of nearly two hundred feet. Timbers for construction purposes and finished boards for interior uses are obtained from it. Hemlock, western red cedar, and varieties of pine are also common. This region is constantly increasing in importance and will supply more and more of our lumber. Washington ranks first among the lumber-producing states of our country. 

Lumbering. Turning trees into lumber requires a great deal of work, even before the sawmill can do its part. Trees must be felled, the trunks cut into logs, and the logs transported to the mills. The methods of doing all this vary somewhat in the different forest regions because of differences in the kinds of trees, in climate, in labor facilities, and in the character of the country itself.

In the northern United States where there are deep snows, the logs are pulled to the nearest roadway, loaded on sleds, and hauled to mill, railroad, or river bank. If to the last, they are piled so that they will be

Courtesy Seattle Chamber of Commerce

BIG TIMBER IN WASHINGTON 

This big timber is being taken out of the forest in western Washington. What kind of tree in Washington furnishes these huge logs?

After they are felled and sawed into logs, these are often dragged to the logging railroad in a grooved track or chute. A stationary engine from which cables are run to the logs supplies the power. Where the railroad is near by, blocks and cables are used. The logs are picked up by these and carried to the flat cars or are piled ready for them. 

The final destination of the logs in all cases is the sawmill. Here they are cut into lumber which may be used as it is or further manufactured into hundreds of other forms. 

Paper Making. Until 1859 linen and cotton rags were the principal raw materials for paper making in the United States. During the Civil War the need for paper resulted reached by the spring flood waters, which in the use of a process employing straw. will float them to the mill. 

The absence of snow in the South prevents the use of sleds. Their place is taken by logging railroads. Logs are "snaked" out (dragged out in log chains) to the railroad by mules or oxen or by small stationary steam engines. In many places the general levelness of the ground and the open lanes in the forests permit the use of high-wheeled gigs in hauling the logs to the sawmill. The swampy areas require a different method. 

The lumbermen cut their trees while standing in water. By means of engines mounted on flatboats the logs are then pulled to a central point, where they are loaded on boats and taken out of the swamp.

Many parts of the Rockies are so steep that ordinary methods of transporting logs cannot be used. The problem has sometimes been solved by sliding the logs downhill in a log or board chute. When possible the chute is flooded with water and into this flume the logs are dumped. They reach the sawmill by floating and sliding down the mountain side.

The great size of the trees in the Pacific coast forest has made their handling difficult. 

With the steady increase in population and the rise of newspapers and magazines there came an enormous demand for cheap paper. 

Out of the search for materials, pulp from wood emerged as the solution of the problem. 

From a small beginning in the sixties, the use of wood has expanded until now it is the source of 90 per cent of the paper used in our country. Spruce provides over half of

There are two kinds of wood pulp, chemical and mechanical. The former is made by cutting the wood into fine chips which are cooked in chemicals. The process reduces the wood to a pulpy mass which is the basis for the making of paper. Mechanical wood pulp is made by grinding blocks of wood against 

Publishers' Photo Service RESIN FROM THE PINE TREE 

The young man is "chipping" a southern pine tree in order to get the resinous sap. Note the troughs and the catch pails fastened to the tree under the cuts. Turpentine and rosin are two very important products derived from the pine trees of our southern forests. grindstones until the wood is reduced to fine shreds. It is used for the cheaper grades of paper. The best paper is still made from rags, but most grades are mixtures of the several different kinds of pulp. The demand for paper is so great that we are being compelled to import pulp wood, pulp, and paper.

The greater part comes from Canada, but the 

Scandinavian peninsula and Germany are important sources.

The pulp and paper mills are largely concentrated in the Eastern and Lake States.

Several factors are responsible for this. (1) The great paper markets are in the northeastern part of the country. Location near them keeps down transportation costs. (2) The wood available meets the requirements. (3) The mechanical process demands a great deal of cheap power. Water can supply this, hence many of the mills are located at water-power sites on rivers. (4) The cost of transporting logs makes it advisable for pulp mills to be near the forest. 

Naval Stores. In early days tar and pitch were used to fill the seams of boats so as to make them water-tight. Gradually the name naval stores came to be used for them and also to include turpentine and rosin. The title is still employed despite the fact that the two major commodities, turpentine and rosin, find their greatest usage outside shipping and shipbuilding.

The United States ranks first among the world's producers of naval stores, furnishing over half of the total. The long-leaf pine of the southern forest is the chief source of supply. When the tree is injured, the sap or resin oozes out and collects on the edges of the gash. Making use of this fact, there has been developed a method of tapping or scarring the tree near the base. Resin accumulates at the bottom of the incision in cups. 

At intervals the resin is collected from many trees and taken to a distillery. By a process of boiling, turpentine goes off as a vapor and is then secured in the form of a liquid through cooling. The part left is rosin, which is run out into a trough and ladled into barrels. 

Tar is secured from the distillation of pine wood.

In the past the excessive wounding of the trees caused their death in a few years. This represented a great waste unless the lumberman followed hard on the heels of the turpentine man. New methods have been devised which are expected to prolong the

350 MILLION ACRES U. S. Department of Agriculture WHO OWNS THE FORESTS? 

In the eastern forest region, which comprises about 74 per cent of the total forest area of the United States, a large percentage of the lands are privately owned. In the western forest region, about two-thirds of the lands are owned by the Federal government. What else does this graph tell you? life of the tree. As the forests have been cut over, the naval stores industry has gradually moved southward. Georgia and the Gulf 

States are now the chief producers, with 

Georgia and Florida in the lead. Savannah is the greatest market for these stores. 

Tanning Extracts. To change hides and skins into leather the process of tanning is necessary. One of the most common tanning agents has been the bark of certain trees, particularly that of oak, hemlock, and chestnut. These trees were found abundantly in the northern and central hardwood forest areas. Consequently many small tanneries were established in those sections. 

Our Decreasing Forests. - The United 

States Forest Service has estimated that probably three-fifths of our original timber is gone. How has this been brought about? (1) A great part of it was destroyed, because the early settlers had to clear the land in order to produce food. (2) The constant use of the forests for lumber and other products has made great inroads. The use has been lavish because of seemingly unlimited resources. (3) In the woods logging has often been very wasteful, utilizing only the best parts of the tree. The desire to get the timber out quickly and cheaply has frequently caused the destruction of young trees which would have restored the forest. (4) Fire has been another important factor in the loss of our forests. In 1926 the Forest 

Service estimated that on the average forest fires burned each year an area equivalent to a ten-mile-wide strip from New York City to Denver. Statistics show that most of the fires have been due to human carelessness. Insects and diseases have also taken their toll of the forests.

This graph gives you a picture of forest and cut-over lands in the various parts of the United States. Where do you find the greatest percentage of virgin timber? Why? How can a balance between consumption and production be attained? Substitution of other materials may help some. Imports, as in the case of pulp wood, may supply certain shortages, but world demand will exhaust those reserves too. Rising prices may reduce consumption. The real solution, however, seems to lie in growing more wood and in decreasing the present waste in the forest, at the sawmill, and in all wood-using industries.

By far the greater part of the 470 million acres of forest land in the United States is owned privately by individuals, lumber companies, mining companies, railroads, etc.

They must be encouraged to handle their timber as a crop and not as a mine. There are some eighty-one million acres of waste forest land, fit only to grow trees, that should be replanted. The United States Forest

In some forests, for every large tree felled, two seedlings are planted. Only in this way can our country be assured of a lumber supply in the future

National Forests. In 1891 the President was authorized to withdraw public forest lands from exploitation. This act marked the beginning of our national forest system. 

The withdrawals have continued until the national forests of the United States cover about one-fourth of the total forest area. 

Most of the national forests are in the West. 

The location is explained by the fact that the government held public land there until a later date than in the East.

At first the areas set aside were looked upon as reserves and were not used. Gradually the viewpoint developed that timber is a crop and must be used when ripe or it will be wasted. 

With this idea came the beginnings of scientific forestry. Rangers are now employed to build trails and roads, to fight fires, and to see that the forests are protected. Mature timber is marked and sold. Open land in the forests is leased to cattlemen and sheepmen for grazing purposes. Some progress is

THE UNITED STATES FOREMOST IN MANUFACTURING 

being made in replanting land denuded of trees, but this phase of work is proceeding very slowly.

The law creating national forests provided among other things that they were to be "for the purpose of securing favorable conditions of water flows." In attempting to carry out this purpose the Forest Service makes a special effort to maintain trees on steep slopes. 

By breaking the force of storms, they tend to retard the rapid run-off of water, thus reducing the loss of soil and preventing floods.

Where much soil is washed off, the streams become clogged and their flow interfered with. 

Some of the water which has been retarded seeps down through the forest litter into the soil and rocks beneath. Gradually it is released to feed the springs and streams, thus making a more even flow of water. An even supply of water is very important in streams and rivers used for irrigation, navigation, and water power.

Certain of the states, particularly New 

York and Pennsylvania, have established large state forests and are carrying on an active forestry program. This involves not only the care of the forests, and the growing of young trees for replanting, but also educational work among the people in matters of forestry. 

1. What are some of the geographic factors that determine the location of forests? 

2. Make a list of (a) the various uses of our forests, (b) the various ways in which timber is used. 

3. In which of the forest regions of the United 

States is your state? What varieties of trees are found in your community? Which state leads in the production of lumber? Which section of the country? 

4. If you are in or near a lumbering district, find out how the work is carried on. Do the methods used differ from those of other sections? If so, can you offer an explanation? 

5. "Three-fifths of all the people in the United 

States live east of the Mississippi River, while threefifths of all the standing forests that remain in the 209 country are located in the Rocky Mountains and along the Pacific coast." What bearing does this have upon the expense of lumber and lumber products? 

6. Tax laws are often a deciding factor in the work of reforestation. In many states land on which young trees are growing is taxed at a higher rate than cutover land on which there are no trees. In some states, however, taxes under the two conditions are about the same until the trees are cut. Then a tax is placed on the timber cut. In your opinion which method will probably encourage the replanting and the growing of more trees? 

7. What kinds of trees grow in the Pacific States?

D. THE UNITED STATES AS A MANUFACTURING NATION 

The United States Foremost in Manufacturing. - The United States is the greatest

CHICAGO SKYLINE Courtesy Chicago Association of Commerce 

Skyscrapers and a great wall of window panes along a waterfront! Of what other great city does this skyline remind you? Chicago is the fourth city of the world in population. It is the railroad center of the United States. Not only is it the great commercial center for the interior of our country, but it makes a high percentage of the total value of the manufactured goods in the United States. Can you name some lines for which it is noted? What reasons can you suggest for the rapid growth of this big city? manufacturing nation in the world. The value of our manufactured goods is more than twice that of the manufactures of Great Britain, which long held first place in manufacturing. Our country is sometimes called the "Young Industrial Giant of the West." 

Most of the great increase in our manufacturing has come within the past twenty-five years.

We not only produce but we consume more manufactured goods than any other nation. 

We produce over half of all the iron and steel in the world, and use the greater part ourselves. We refine over half of the world's copper, and use all but one-twentieth of it. 

We lead in the importation of rubber, from which we manufacture and consume more rubber goods than any other nation. We are first in the production of agricultural machinery, railway equipment, building material, automobiles, sewing machines, and many other articles, both large and small. 

Our national wealth is enormous $300,000,000,000and increasing each year. 

This wealth is represented by our cities and towns, our factories, our farms and farm equipment, our roads and automobiles, 

over and our railways and their equipment. In addition, we have millions of dollars invested in the industries and railroads of other countries. Foreign nations owe us many millions of dollars also.

Each year the people of the United States earn over $60,000,000,000, an average of over. $500 per person. The majority of the workers in the United States receive higher wages than the workmen doing similar work in any other country. They are able to live in comfortable homes, and enjoy everyday comforts and conveniences which even our grandparents would have considered luxuries. 

We may well ask, "What has made it possible for the United States to take the lead over all other nations in the manufacturing industry?" The following are some of the factors, to which you may want to add others: 

(1) Large area and population of the United States. 

(2) Abundance of natural resources. (3) Extensive use of machines. (4) Abundance of power. (5) Good transportation facilities. 

(6) Easy communication. 

(7) An energetic people. (8) A stimulating climate. (9) Abundance of capital.

ABUNDANCE OF NATURAL RESOURCES 

211 (10) Large home market. 

(11) Scientific education. 

(12) A stable government. 

(1) The Large Area and 

Population of the United

States. The great area of the United States enables a large number of people to live and work in comparative comfort. It also offers a variety of climates and surface features and varied re- sources.

BEAUTY AND TREASURE

(2) Abundance of Natural Resources. 

Our country is gifted with extensive forests and large areas of fertile soils and grazing lands. It has fine harbors, well located, and good inland waterways.

It also has large quantities of coal, iron, copper, lead, zinc, gold, silver, and bauxite (the clay from which aluminum is secured). 

Building stone of all kinds, clays for making bricks and pottery, limestone, sand and gravel, salt, phosphate rock, and sulphur are other much-needed mineral products found in abundance.

The Many Uses of Iron. Practically all our work is done with the aid of a tool or a machine made of some form of iron, our most useful metal. You need only to look around and notice the different articles made wholly or partly of iron or steel in order to realize the extensive use of this metal.

The many boilers and engines, locomotives and railway cars and the steel rails on which they run, steamships, automobiles and motor

Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction A RICH HILL 

Not beautiful to look at but a vital part of our modern industrial life. These are copper mines located at Butte, Montana, in a district which has yielded about one-third of all of the copper produced in the United States since 1880. These mines also produce a large quantity of silver which is found together with the copper. trucks, the steel frames of our large buildings and bridges, machinery of all kinds and numberless small tools all of these require large quantities of iron, nearly all of which is mined in the United States. 

Copper. Copper is a good conductor of electricity. It is the cheapest metal which can be used in making the wires which carry electricity. Miles and miles of copper wire are in conduits underground or strung on poles above the ground. They make it possible for us to light our rooms, to talk with our friends over the telephone, and to listen to distant music or speeches brought to us through the radio. 

Copper, in combination with zinc, forms brass. Brass is used for making many useful articles for which iron is not suitable because it rusts so easily. In combination with tin, copper forms bronze.

About 600,000 tons of copper are produced and refined each year in the United 

States. Fully a third as much copper ore as is produced in the United States is imported from Canada, Mexico, Chile, Peru, and the 

Belgian Congo, and refined here. This country has the large smelters and the fuel necessary for doing the work efficiently. 

Five states, Arizona, Montana, Michigan, Nevada, and Utah, produce nearly all the copper mined in the United States. Arizona alone produces more copper than any foreign country. 

Zinc. Zinc is a soft metal used for coating over a base of sheet iron or steel. The process is known as galvanizing, and preserves the metal from rusting. (Have you any "galvanized iron" pails or cans around your home?) The United States produces about 

30 per cent of the world's zinc. About onehalf of our supply comes from the Joplin District which extends into Missouri, Kansas, and Oklahoma. The rest comes from several other states, notably New Jersey, Montana, and Wisconsin. 

Lead. Lead is used in paints, for making solder, bullets and other small articles, pipes, and other fixtures for building purposes. It ranks next to iron in the amount mined. 

Over 20 per cent of the world's lead is produced in the United States. Much of it is mined in connection with the zinc in the 

Joplin District. The Rocky Mountain states, especially Idaho, Utah, and Colorado, also produce lead. 

Courtesy New Jersey Zinc Company

ZINC MILL 

This large mill for the refining of zinc ores is located at 

Franklin, New Jersey. What uses make zinc one of our most important industrial metals?

Courtesy Arizona Industrial Congress COPPER MINES Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction 

Above is one of the big copper mines in Arizona, which is now the leading state in the production of copper. The concentration plant, where as many of the impurities as possible are removed, is seen in the foreground, while the smelter is in the background. Below, at the left, is "The Pit" copper mine at Bisbee, Arizona. Steam shovels have been busy here for years removing a mountain of copper known as Sacramento Hill, and where the mountain once stood is now this huge hole, several hundred feet deep and covering nearly 20 acres. Copper colorings in the side of the mountain make this a most interesting sight. At the right, electric locomotives are hauling copper from the mines of the Anaconda Copper Mining Company at Butte, Montana, to the smelter at Anaconda, a few miles away. 

This is another of the large copper mining and smelting centers in the United States. 213

GOLD MINES 

Courtesy Colorado Association 

California, Colorado, Nevada, Utah, Arizona, Montana, and South Dakota now produce most of the gold in the United States. Since our country became an independent nation, we have produced nearly one-fourth of all the gold in the world. 

For several years Colorado surpassed California when the famous Cripple Creek district near Colorado Springs became the leading mining camp in the country. California now again produces more gold than any other state. Yet the value of the gold mined there each year is less than one-thirtieth of that of all the minerals produced. 

And it is not much over one-fiftieth of the value of the vegetable products of that state. 

Chimneys, smoke - bare land and huge dumps - and gold! This is a scene in the Cripple Creek district, one of the gold-producing districts in Colorado. The two mines pictured here have produced millions of dollars' worth. 

The dumps of low-grade ores thrown out are like small mountains in themselves. Do you think that you should like to live in such a region as this? 

Gold. Gold and silver are called the precious metals. Owing to their beauty, they have always been prized in making ornaments and coins. Gold has long been a standard of value and a medium of exchange among different nations.

The possession of gold means wealth, for with gold other things may be purchased.

This is why so many of the early explorers sought gold and other precious minerals. 

For many years the United States led all other countries in the production of gold. 

However, it is now surpassed by the Union of South Africa. Gold was mined in the

Appalachian Mountains in the early days. of our history. But the importance of our country as a gold producer did not begin until after the discovery of gold in California in 1848. This discovery led to the famous "gold rush" of 1849. Despite the hardships of travel, thousands found their way to this land which had recently been acquired from Mexico. Soon our country took the lead in the production of gold. 

Courtesy Colorado Association

UNDERGROUND TREASURE 

Like the gnomes of the fairy stories, these miners are at work in the interior of a gold mine in Colorado. In contrast with "placer "mining, where the gold is collected from alluvial deposits, this is called "lode" mining. The rock is dug from these underground workings and taken to the surface mills where, by means of a refining process requiring expensive machinery, the gold is extracted from the ore.

ABUNDANCE OF NATURAL RESOURCES 

This tells us that, despite its beauty and its value as a medium of exchange, it is not nearly so important or useful as the baser metals, such as iron and copper. 

Silver. Silver is not so valuable as gold.

Still it is used for the smaller denominations of coins and for making many useful and ornamental articles in common use in our homes. The quantity of silver mined each year is over fifteen times that of gold. 

The leading gold-producing states rank high, also, in the production of silver, with the one exception of South 

Dakota. California, however, which ranks highest among these states in the production of gold, ranks low in the amount of silver mined.

The Recovery of Tin. Tin is one of the metals of which our country has to import all of its supply. About one-half of the world's production is imported by the United 

States. Most of it comes from the Straits Settlements and from Bolivia. 215 utensils, and for many other purposes, including shingles and furniture.

Aluminum is made from a clay named bauxite, which is found in five states - 

Alabama, Arkansas, Georgia, Mississippi, and Tennessee. It requires large amounts 

Courtesy Indiana Limestone Company

BEDFORD LIMESTONE QUARRY 

Thirty-two men long! What a huge column! This was cut from Indiana limestone for the north office building of the State Capitol group at Harrisburg, Pennsylvania. Indiana limestone is very popular as a building stone and is shipped hundreds of miles from Indiana to other states for this purpose. 

Our extensive canning industry accounts for our large consumption of tin. Formerly tin was chiefly used in making cooking utensils. Now aluminum and enameled ware have largely taken the place of tinware for this purpose. The recovery of tin from used cans and other tin-coated vessels is an important industry in the United States.

About 33,000 tons of tin are thus saved each year. As only a little over 180,000 tons are produced in the whole world, you can see what a great saving this is. 

Aluminum. Aluminum is a light, strong metal. It is now much used in the construction of automobiles and airplanes, for cooking of electricity to manufacture aluminum, and so only those countries which have water power can afford to produce this metal. The 

United States now makes about one-third of the amount manufactured by all countries.

Building Stone. Many kinds of stone are used for building purposes, but four of the most important are limestone, sandstone, granite, and marble. Building stone is abundant, especially in the mountainous parts of our country. Some regions produce especially fine qualities and have special advantages for quarrying and shipping the stone.

Limestone is quarried in Indiana and shipped all over the country. Vermont produces the most valuable granite and the finest quality of marble for ornamental work. Both

Courtesy Kentucky Progress Commission BRICKS 

This brick plant in Kentucky has a capacity of 100,000 bricks per day. The manufacture of bricks is very widely distributed. Can you tell why? granite and marble are used for monuments and for ornamental building purposes. 

Marble is used largely indoors and granite outdoors, for granite stands the weather better than marble. Granite is displacing marble for use as tombstones. 

Brick, Cement, and Concrete. Brick is one of the oldest and one of the most common of our building materials. Bricks are easy to make, and the clay for making them is plentiful. And so brickmaking tends to be a widely scattered industry, but situated as near as possible to the place where the bricks are to be used. For bricks, like stone, are heavy and costly to transport. 

Cement is made by burning clay and lime together until a fused mass is formed. 

This mixture is then reduced to a powder, which, when wet, hardens and becomes as solid as rock. 

Mixed with sand, crushed stone, or gravel, it forms concrete. Concrete is one of the most extensively used building materials we have. Not only walls and pillars of buildings, but sidewalks and roads, piers and other parts of bridges, dams, and all sorts of construction work are now made of concrete. In liquid form it can be poured into molds and allowed to harden. Thus concrete may be used for ornamental purposes in place of the more expensive sculptured stone.

Glass and Pottery. The manufacture of glass is another large industry in the United States. We use immense quantities of plate and window glass in addition to glass bottles and other articles too numerous to mention. 

You have only to look around your home to discover the many uses of glass. 

Quartz sand for glass making is quite abundant and found in many places. However, large amounts of fuel are necessary to melt the sand, so this industry tends to be located 

THUS THE POTTER MOLDS THE CLAY 

Lenox, Inc.

The manufacture of pottery is an important industry at Trenton, New Jersey, where fine clay suitable for this purpose is found. Here is a modeler at work in one of the Trenton potteries. Form is the first thing to consider in making a piece of pottery, and much of the beauty of the finished article depends upon the skill of the worker who shapes the clay.

EXTENSIVE USE OF MACHINES 

near the fuel supply. Gas, either natural or manufactured from coal, is the chief fuel used in glassmaking. For this reason most of the glass in the United States is made in the Ohio Valley, where natural gas and coal are most plentiful. A town named Charleroi near Pittsburgh has a plate-glass industry similar to that in the Belgian city after which it is named.

Machines are now used to make all kinds of the cheaper glassware, such as window glass, kerosene lamps and lamp chimneys, bottles, tumblers and dishes, and bulbs and shades for electric lighting purposes. These machines take the place of the skilled glass blowers and lower the cost of production.

Fine qualities of glass intended for scientific purposes, such as lenses, are still made by hand. The United States imports some of the finer grades of glass, and in turn exports considerable machine-made glass. 

Pottery of the cheaper grades, such as tiles, crockery, and the heavier types of dishes, is manufactured rather extensively in the 

United States. Finer qualities of china and pottery are largely imported. Ohio, with its varied deposits of glacial clays, its coal, and good transportation systems, has taken the lead in this industry. Trenton, New Jersey, and East Liverpool, Ohio, lead in the manufacture of the finer grades of pottery.

The 

(3) Extensive Use of Machines. 

enormous output of manufactured goods in the United States with the present number of workers would not be possible without the extensive use of machines. In many cases the total number of workers in an industry is less than it was many years ago, while the output of goods is many times larger. 

A few examples will show how this is possible. In a modern clothing factory, one girl at a machine can do the work of twenty-five girls in the earlier days of the industry. One man with the aid of machinery can produce 

150 watches per year while in Switzerland a 217 man using hand methods can make only 40. 

With dough-mixing machines, one man can now do the work of twenty bakers. In the shoe industry, one man with machinery can do as much work as was formerly done by six to ten men. In glass factories, one machine does the work of fifty-four bottlemakers. The use of machinery in coal mining has nearly doubled the output per day of each miner. 

Courtesy Providence Chamber of Commerce THE MACHINE AGE 

This is an age of machinery, of swiftly turning belts and whirring wheels, especially in the United States and other industrial nations of to-day. Here is the interior of a machine shop in Providence, Rhode Island, where machines are being used to make other machines and tools.

Machines have speeded up the whole manufacturing process greatly. Formerly it took thirty hours to make a pair of knitted woolen gloves by hand; now it takes only forty minutes by machine. One hundred bushels of peas can be shelled in 1 hour by machinery where by hand 175 hours would be needed, 25,000 pounds of soap can be made in 21 hours instead of the 432 hours formerly required. Machines produce 100,000 cigarettes in 14 hours where the hand process would need 520 hours.

Courtesy Associated Knit Underwear Manufacturers of America CUTTING MATERIAL FOR READY-TO-WEAR CLOTHING 

Many layers of goods are cut through in a single operation by this cutting machine, thus speeding up the process and producing a uniform pattern. Next to the sewing machine, this is, perhaps, the greatest labor-saving device in the clothing indust. y. 

Machines tend to standardize a commodity.

A machine always makes the same article in the same way. No two articles would be quite alike if they were made by hand. 

Machine-made articles and their various parts are made according to set patterns. The machines themselves are made in the same way. 

If a certain part of a machine wears out or breaks, the purchaser may send to the firm or factory from which he bought it and get a new part. In order that this new part may fit and work satisfactorily, it must be exactly the same size and shape as the old part.

It is much cheaper to make a large number of articles according to a standard pattern than it is to make a similar number according to different patterns. In order to cut down expenses and save waste in the use of raw materials, manufacturers frequently agree among themselves to make only a certain number of different sizes and styles. 

In modern factories, each of the several operations necessary in making an article is performed by a special kind of machine. In doing the same thing over and over, the operators of these machines become skillful and speedy in their work. The unfinished product is easily transferred from one machine to another for each step in the process until it is finally complete. This method speeds up production and makes the output per workman much larger than it would be if one man performed all the operations. 

The larger production per person due to the use of machines has made it possible to pay the workers higher wages and reduce the number of working hours per day. So workmen now have more money to spend and more leisure time than they had before machines came into common use. 

Courtesy Associated Knit Underwear Manufacturers of America FINISHING SEAMS 

Finishing seams is one of the most important steps in the making of knit underwear and, in fact, any kind of garments. Here, again, the machine. in the hands of a skillful operator, does a neat and lasting job.

ABUNDANCE OF POWER 

It has been said that an American will never do a piece of work by hand if he can get a machine to do the work for him. Lacking a machine, he sets his wits to work to invent one. Anyhow, Americans are noted for their mechanical ingenuity and for the large number of labor-saving devices they have invented. Patents on American inventions recorded in the United States Patent Office now number several millions. This has been a big factor in American success in manufacturing.

Americans have also been quick to adapt and use new inventions. Manufacturers have always stood ready to "scrap" their old machinery and install the new, trusting to the greater efficiency of the new machines to make up for their losses.

Our beautiful large buildings, our comfortable homes with their many labor-saving appliances, our railways and steamships, our paved streets and hard, smooth roads - all of these have been built with the aid of machines. This is why the present is sometimes called the "machine age." 99 

These ma- (4) Abundance of Power. chines are of no use without the power to run them. Indeed only our possession of different sources of power has enabled us to make so much use of machines. The United

States makes greater use of power from natural resources than any other nation. 

Comparisons of the work done for us by machines with an equal amount done by man power, show that on an average each person in the United States has working for him every day the equivalent of fifty slaves such as did the work in ancient Greece. Do you see from this why we have been able to make such great progress?

Coal. Coal is the chief source of power used in manufacturing. Iron makes the machine but coal runs it. Both are necessary in modern industry. Coal is the main fuel used to produce steam and also elec- 219 tricity, though much water power is also being converted into electric power. 

The United States produces nearly 500,000,000 tons of coal each year, or about one-third of all the coal mined in the world.

About one-sixth of the whole United States is underlain with coal. We not only have large quantities, but much of it is of a good quality and well distributed over the country. 

Courtesy Kentucky Progress Commission A VEIN OF COAL 

This is an excellent view of the interior of a coal mine in western Kentucky. The miner is drilling a hole in the black, shiny wall of bituminous coal preparatory to blasting. 

There are three kinds of coal: anthracite or hard coal, bituminous or soft coal, and lignite or brown coal, which is still softer than the bituminous. 

Anthracite Coal. Anthracite coal is mined from a comparatively small area in northeastern Pennsylvania in the neighborhood of 

Scranton. This burns without smoke and is much used as a fuel for heating purposes, particularly in our large cities. Only about one-sixth as much anthracite as bituminous coal is mined each year. A considerable quantity is exported, mostly to our northern neighbor, Canada.

Bituminous Coal. Bituminous coal is used chiefly in manufacturing and transportation.

It burns with a long flame and usually gives off more heat while burning than hard coal. 

On the other hand, it generally makes a dense black smoke and gives off gases. For these reasons it is not so desirable except where smoke consumers are used. field of bituminous coal extending from 

Michigan to Texas. This is not so high grade as that of the eastern field. However, it supplies most of the coal used for heating and is used for fuel in the many manufacturing industries of the central states. 

In the Great Plains region, among the 

Rocky Mountains, and in the southern states are other large fields of coal, mostly lignite. Lignite is not very good either for heating purposes or for producing steam. Being so soft, it breaks up easily, which makes it hard to ship. These large fields of coal will not be used much as long as people can get a better grade of coal by easy transportation routes. 

Courtesy Norfolk-Portsmouth Advertising Board CARLOADS OF COAL 

These thousands of tons of soft coal from the Appalachian coal fields have been shipped by rail to Norfolk, Virginia, where they are awaiting export. 

Norfolk, with its fine harbor, and railroads leading back into the heart of the coal fields, is one of the world's greatest coal ports. 

The best bituminous coal is found in the 

Appalachian field extending from northern 

Pennsylvania southwestward into Alabama.

This is a high-grade coal which furnishes nearly all of the fuel for the great iron and steel industries and other manufactures in the eastern states. Steaming coal, much prized for steamships and naval boats, comes from this field also. Some of the best, which is almost smokeless, is mined in West Virginia. 

A special variety of coal good for making coke is abundant in the vicinity of Pittsburgh and Birmingham.

In the interior of our country, east and west of the Mississippi River, lies another large 

Large quantities of peat also occur, but like the soft brown lignite, it will not be used to any extent as long as good coal can be had at reasonable prices. 

Bituminous coal is not only useful as fuel, but it is the source of many other valuable products. About one thousand products made from coal are now in daily use. In addition to gas, ammonia products, and coke obtained from coal, there is tar. Following are a few coaltar products: light and heavy oils, benzol, carbolic acid, aspirin, dye-stuffs, explosives, indigo, perfumes, lamp-black, paint, and pitch. 

Unfortunately most of our coal is burned directly in its raw state, and the greater part of the gas and other by-products are lost. 

Coal now forms about one-third of the freight transported by the railroads. Perhaps sometime more of the coal will be converted at the mine into gas which can be conveyed in pipes over long distances more cheaply and efficiently than coal itself can be carried.

ABUNDANCE OF POWER 

OIL DERRICKS 

A "forest of derricks" would be a good name to apply to this oil field in southern California, near Los 

Angeles. Why do you think the oil wells are located so close together? 

Petroleum. Petroleum, more than any other fuel, has speeded up the development of American industry since the beginning of the twentieth century. Indeed it may be said that, as the use of steam brought about the old industrial revolution in Europe, the use of petroleum has brought about the new industrial revolution in America. 

Take away petroleum and gasoline, the chief fuel made from it, and we should have to give up nearly all our automobiles and motor trucks. 

Airplanes could no longer be flown. Farmers would have to abandon the use of their tractors. Gasoline engines used for pumping and many other industrial purposes would be valueless; the big machines used to excavate, 221 pave, sprinkle, and sweep our streets and roadways would lie idle.

The use of engines burning "gas" (abbreviation for "gasoline ") has enabled us to do certain types of work and travel certain distances in a fraction of the time formerly required. Thus both time and labor are saved. 66 

Step on the gas!" has become a byword for "More speed!"

Our country has been able to make and use more gasoline machines than other countries, because we have not only the coal and iron and most of the other materials for making them, but the " gas" to run them. The

United States produces and uses about twothirds of the world's petroleum.

The petroleum industry in this country began with the striking of oil in a well near 

Oil Creek in western Pennsylvania. Oil 

City, situated at the junction of Oil Creek and the Allegheny River, became the urban center for the industry. For many years western Pennsylvania was the leader in the oil industry. Then gradually other fields were 

Courtesy Norfolk-Portsmouth Advertising Board OIL REFINERY 

It is sometimes a long way from the oil fields to the oil refineries. This one is at Norfolk, Virginia. How may the oil be brought to this port? Here the crude oil (petroleum) will be refined and converted into various products, such as benzine, gasoline, kerosene, vaseline, and paraffin.

Courtesy Kentucky Progress Commission OIL SHALES 

When our supply of liquid petroleum runs out, we may have to depend upon our vast oil shale deposits. This is an oil shale cliff in Kentucky. Colorado and other Rocky

Mountain states have large deposits of this rock which in the future may prove to be one of our most valuable resources. opened to the south and west in Ohio, West 

Virginia, Kentucky, Indiana, and Illinois.

Now the chief centers of production are in California and the south central states, especially Texas, Oklahoma, and Kansas.

Some of the petroleum is transported in railway tank cars, but most of it reaches the refineries through pipe lines. These form a great network throughout the central and eastern oil-producing regions.

Uses of Petroleum. Many ships are now using crude petroleum and fuel oil as a fuel, and more and more of our homes are being heated with fuel oil. However, most of the petroleum goes to refineries where it is separated into various products. Three main products, gasoline, kerosene, and lubricating oil, and a number of other products, such as naphtha, benzine, paraffin, and vaseline, are obtained in the process of refining oil. 

Kerosene for lighting purposes was at first the most important product, but its use for this purpose is now confined largely to country districts where neither gas nor electricity is available. Considerable kerosene is used for heating and cooking purposes. Large amounts are also exported to foreign countries. 

The coming of the automobile has led to a large and increasing demand for gasoline, which now stands foremost among petroleum products. Lubricating oils, obtained from petroleum, are needed to keep machinery in running order. There is no animal or vegetable oil that is entirely satisfactory for this purpose. 

Natural Gas. Those of us who do not live in regions where natural gas is found, usually hear very little of this excellent fuel.

For natural gas cannot be stored and shipped around the country like coal or oil. It must be piped directly from the producing well to the consumer. 

Courtesy Oklahoma State Chamber of Commerce RIG IN A GAS FIELD 

This is a familiar sight in the oil and gas fields of our country. The derrick, together with the engine and belt house, is called a rig. These derricks are about 80 feet high. Tools used in drilling the wells are suspended on cables swung over pulleys in the tops of the derricks. 

Notice the large beam above the men. This is called the walking beam. It is used both in drilling a well and in pumping out oil afterwards.

ABUNDANCE OF POWER 

Natural gas is a clean and convenient fuel to use both in manufacturing industries and in homes. On account of its cleanliness and high-heating qualities, it is used in making glass, pottery, and cement. Large quantities are also used in the iron and steel industries around Pittsburgh.

Natural gas is usually present in connection with petroleum, though many times it occurs outside the oil field.

It is found in nearly half of the states. Most of it, however, is produced in 

Oklahoma, California, West 

Virginia, Texas, Pennsylvania, and Louisiana, all of which are also leading oilproducing states. A large portion of the natural gas is being used to make gasoline.

Over one-tenth of our output of gasoline now comes from natural gas. 223 waves. It turns the wheels in many of our factories, and furnishes the motive power for many of the labor-saving devices in our homes and small shops.

Electricity may be used to cool our homes. 

Courtesy Idaho State Chamber of Commerce

ARROWROCK DAM 

Beauty and utility are combined in this huge dam in a mountain valley near Boise, Idaho. It is 349 feet in height - one of the highest dams in the world. 

It stores enough water to supply 340,000 acres of land. What other use is made of water stored in these high reservoirs? 

Water Power. In the beginning of the industrial revolution, water power was the first form of mechanical energy. After the introduction of steam power, coal largely took its place. 

Water power had one disadvantage as compared with coal: factories had to be located near the source of the power. Coal, on the other hand, could be carried to the factory, so that the industry could be located wherever it was most convenient.

Water power is being developed and used more than ever. This is because it can now be transformed into electricity and in that form can be transmitted long distances and made to do all kinds of work. Electricity lights our homes, runs our street cars and some railroad trains. It carries our voice over the telephone, code messages over the telegraph, and both through the air by radio in summer or heat them in winter. It may preserve our food at ice-cold temperatures, then cook the same food for our dinner. 

It may help to wash the dishes, wash and iron our clothes and household linen, sweep and scrub the floor, scrape and paint the walls, run the furnace, and keep the clock going. 

Like a modern Aladdin, this mysterious force moves quietly but with lightning speed to perform its marvelous wonders and work its transformations in our lives. It is the strongest force now used in industry and transportation. It can work at high altitudes where steam power fails. In a tug of war between the steam locomotive and the electric locomotive, the electric locomotive is always sure to win.

Airmap Corporation of America NIAGARA FALLS 

The rushing, foaming waters of these falls are one of the most glorious gifts of nature to America. The projection of land divides the falls into two parts, the American Falls and the Canadian Falls, or, as they are sometimes called, the Horseshoe Falls. It was in 1931 that a breaking-away of the rock caused the American Falls to recede slightly in the center.

From the standpoint of power, we are now in the "Electric Age." No other event since the industrial revolution, not even the invention and use of the internal combustion engine burning gasoline, has made such great changes in our lives as the introduction and use of electricity. New uses are constantly being discovered. And yet, scientists say electricity is still in its infancy. 

Nearly two-thirds of the electricity consumed in the United States is generated by the use of coal, petroleum, or natural gas as a fuel. More water power is being developed constantly and put to use for this purpose. 

Unlike coal, petroleum, or natural gas, water power is a lasting source of energy, for it is being constantly renewed by rainfall. Coal, petroleum, or natural gas, once removed from the ground and used, can never be replaced. 

Knowing that at some time these fuels will give out or at least become much more expensive, the United States as well as other nations are developing more of their water power in order to generate electricity. Such developments are known as hydro-electric developments.

Energy, or power to do work, is measured in terms of a unit known as a horsepower. A horsepower is about equal to the energy used by a laborer in an ordinary day's work. It is estimated that our great rivers and lesser streams can be harnessed and made to furnish at least 55,000,000 horsepower. Think of a force capable of doing as much work as 

55,000,000 men daily!

The United States has now developed water power to the extent of about 18,000,000 horsepower. This is about one-fourth of all the developed water power in the world. Most of this "white coal" in the United States is found among the eastern and western highlands. A large amount is also furnished by streams in the central part of the country, especially in the northern glaciated section. 

There is now being developed in our country what is known as the super-power system.

This consists in linking together power stations, run by either coal or water power, into one great transmission system. Suppose a hydro-electric plant, for example, failed to produce enough power for its neighborhood. More electric current could be 

Airmap Corporation of America NIAGARA GORGE 

This deep gorge has been cut in the solid rock by the Niagara River, which rushes through here with great force. 

Notice the hydro-electric plant on the Canadian side of the river. The water supplying the power for this plant is drawn from the Niagara River just above the falls and is conveyed to the plant by a canal. A fall of 300 feet from the point where this canal begins to the level of the plant furnishes over half a million horsepower.
