There are certain hours in the day when a great deal of electric current is used in our cities. But the times when this "peak load" is needed varies in different cities. If all were connected, power could be switched from one city to another as wanted. 

(5) Good Transportation Facilities. Good transpor tation goes hand in hand with the development of all kinds of industries. The farmer must be able to get his wheat to market and in turn receive other products which he cannot produce on his farm. The manufacturer must have his goods transferred from his factory to the store as well as have the raw materials conveyed from their sources to his factory. 225 in having many rivers, large and small, well distributed over the country. It also has many lakes, especially in the glaciated northern section of the country. The Great Lakes of the interior are extensively used for transportation of raw materials. 

NEW ORLEANS Airmap Corporation of America 

This is an upstream view of New Orleans, the "Crescent City," a name taken from the great bend of the Mississippi at this point. Twelve railroad lines, in addition to excellent water transportation, help to make this city a great commercial center of the southern United States. A large amount of manufacturing is also carried on, including sugar refining, coffee roasting and grinding, oil refining, and the manufacture of furniture, cotton goods, and iron and steel articles.

In order to make transportation easy, the people of the 

United States have developed one of the finest railway systems in the world. It has a mileage about the same as that of all European countries combined. A network of good roads enables thousands of motor trucks to transfer goods from one place to another and serve places not reached by the railroad. 

Airplanes, too, are now being used to carry valuable articles which have to be transported quickly.

Waterways, including canals, have played their part in building up industries in our country. Before railroads and roads were built, rivers were the main highways. And even though they may not be used so much for transportation now, they are important as a source of water supply, for irrigation, and for water power. Our country is fortunate 

Many industrial cities are situated on or near the shores of these natural water highways. Notice that nearly all of our larger cities are located either on the banks of some river, or on the shores of a lake or ocean. 

(6) Easy Communication. - Quick com munication by telephone or telegraph has been a most necessary factor in building up and carrying on our great industries.

The telephone is so much a part of the modern business world that it is hard for us to see how men ever carried on business without it. It saves time and gives opportunity for a more complete message than the letter or telegram. It is also much easier to talk over the phone than to write the phone than to write a letter or send a

North America. industrial centers would not have been possible.

No other country has so great a mixture of people of different nationalities as the 

United States. All European countries are represented here. This immigration accounts for much of the increase in population in the United States during the past century. 

People who emigrate are usually ambitious to improve their lot. So our country has telegram. And think of the time and energy it would take if business men had to go and see each other every time they wanted to talk business! 

(7) An Energetic People. - Americans are noted for their energy and the vigor with which they go at their undertakings and carry them through. In slang phrase, they have 66 pep." Our salesmen are known as " gogetters" because they go after and get business. 

Americans hustle, that is, they move quickly and aim to accomplish a lot of work in a short time. In doing this, the many 

In doing this, the many machines, the rapid transportation, and quick communication are all right-hand helpers.

In all of this great industrial development of our country, we must not forget the part played by the immigrant workers. They have come here and labored in the factories, worked in the mines, helped to construct the huge buildings, and done most of the hard labor necessary in paving streets, building roads, and in other construction work. 

Without these willing laborers, our huge output of manufactured goods and our great

To these vigorous newcomers, as well as to the native 

Americans whose ancestors only a short time back were also immigrants, our country owes its quick rise to the front rank among manufacturing nations. 

(8) A Stimulating Climate. - Like the other countries which lie wholly in the temperate zones, the United States has a stimulating climate. You will notice that all of the more progressive nations are in the temperate zones. This is strong evidence that the climate in these zones favors both mental and physical activity.

ABUNDANCE OF CAPITAL 

Most of our country lies in the belt of westerly winds. Constant weather changes in addition to the regular seasonal changes give us a variety which is truly the "spice of life."

No other factor has a greater influence on our activities from day to day. We dress according to the weather. Our outdoor work and sports are largely dependent on the daily weather as well as the season of the year. These daily changes as well as the seasonal changes not only stimulate us, but they increase our needs and cause us to do more work. 

Food must be raised during the warm, growing season and stored away for use during the cold season. We must have heavy clothing for winter and light clothing for summer. Our homes must be built so that they can be heated in winter and kept cool in summer. We must have raincoats, rubbers, and umbrellas. This means more manufactured goods to supply our needs. 229 to withstand the cold than in other sections where the temperature is higher but there is much moisture in the air. 

Altogether, climate in the United States has been one of the most favorable factors in promoting manufacturing industries and those which furnish raw materials. 

Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction MOTION PICTURE STUDIO 

Making motion pictures is one of the big industries in the United States. 

Not only are large numbers of films required for use in our own country, but American films are sent all over the world as well. This is an airplane view of a studio near Burbank, California. Why does southern California make such a good "movie-land"? 

The United States is such a large country that there are considerable differences in climate. In the south the winters are mild and the weather changes, especially in regard to temperature, are not so great as in the north. 

Southern California has the Mediterranean type of climate, with mild, rainy winters and dry, warm summers. Farther north, along the coast of Oregon and Washington, there is the marine type of climate where summer heat and winter cold are both modified by winds blowing from the Pacific Ocean. 

The greatest changes between summer and winter are found in the interior. Still, the dryness of the air here makes it easier (9) Abundance of Capital. - Through their energy and the use they have made of their extensive resources, the people in the United States have been able to save large amounts of money. This money has not been hoarded, but has been used as capital to build up more industries and to operate and enlarge those already started.

The American people not only work and save money, but they invest their savings, often in corporations of various kinds. In these they buy stock which gives them an interest in the business and entitles them to share in the profits.

PLACING UNDERGROUND CABLES 

Telephone and electric wires are largely underground. 

These men are placing a cable containing telephone wires underground in a New England city. raw materials they need, and to extend credit to their customers. This is very necessary in carrying on a large manufacturing business, for in many cases the buyers have to resell the goods and get the money for them before they can pay the manufacturers. 

(10) Large Home Market. Most of the goods manufactured in the United States are sold here. American workers receive good wages, and so they spend money freely for the things they want. This large home market has been very essential in building up our manufactures. American salesmen have also gone into foreign countries to sell American goods. 

(11) Scientific Education. -Americans are great believers in universal education. Every child has the opportunity to gain an elementary and a high school education free of charge. In certain instances, this is even true of a college education. Technical schools and colleges have provided the scientific education which has played such a big part in our industrial life. Much of our success and prosperity has been due to the way in which the American people have put science into their industry. 

Manufacturers have men at work all the time studying and experimenting. We do not usually see or hear much about these chemists, electricians, and mechanical engineers as they work quietly hidden away in their laboratories. But the results of their work are seen in the marvels of our age, such as the radio, the talking pictures, rayon with its beautiful sheen and varied colors, the improved automobile, the intricate machines which save so much time and labor, the numerous safety devices which protect factory workers and railroad travelers, and the powerful explosives which rend rocks apart for building purposes. Even the simplest objects of everyday use, such as the egg-beater, have been studied in order to make them better and at the same time cheaper.

Careful studies have been made of the management of factories and business concerns. Aided by these studies and by their own experience, American business men have developed remarkable ability in handling large undertakings. 

SCIENCE AIDS INDUSTRY 

Ewing Galloway

These men in a scientific laboratory in Massachusetts are experimenting with coffee. By study and experimentation, scientists help the manufacturer and the business man to discover the best ways of carrying on their industries and preparing their products for market.

IRON ORE RESOURCES 

The United States has often been called the "Land of Opportunity." For in this land, which was less crowded than his homeland, the European immigrant has had an opportunity to get work and earn money. 

Ambitious and capable young men have had unusually good opportunities to get the education and training which would enable them to win success more easily. 

(12) A Stable Government. - Industry cannot flourish in a country where there is poor government and constant disorder. 

Property must be protected and people must 231 feel safe to go about their business. The 

United States government is one of the strongest and best governments in the world.

This is another reason why industry has been so successful. The rights of the people as well as their property have been carefully guarded. Free competition in industry has been encouraged, and taxes made as light as possible. The government has interfered only when it was necessary to protect the people against impure products, guard the health and welfare of the workers, and prevent unfair competition. 

IRON AND STEEL Courtesy Youngstown Chamber of Commerce 

These are some of the blast furnaces at Youngstown, Ohio, one of the greatest iron and steel centers outside of Pittsburgh. Into these furnaces are fed the iron ore, coke, and limestone, the raw materials for making iron, and out of them flows the fiery, liquid iron, which, when drawn off and allowed to cool in various forms, is known as cast or pig iron. Molten pig iron, mixed with other desired ingredients, is then converted into steel by different processes according to the kind of steel desired.

E. THE IRON AND STEEL INDUSTRY Importance of the Iron and Steel Industry. 

- Iron and steel are America's most important manufactures. Upon them nearly all other manufacturing industries depend for their machines.

Iron Ore Resources in the United States. 

The United States possesses large quantities of high-grade iron ore. of high-grade iron ore. It has been one of

Courtesy Michigan College of Mining and Technology

UNDERGROUND IN AN IRON MINE 

In some of the Lake Superior mines, iron ore is near the surface and so soft that it can be scooped up by big steam shovels. In others, the iron ore is taken out of underground mines such as this one in Michigan. How is the roof of the mine supported? the main factors in putting us in the front rank of manufacturing countries. Production varies greatly but usually over 50,000,000 tons are mined annually. Most of this, fully five-sixths, comes from the Lake Superior district in Minnesota, Wisconsin, and Michigan. About one-tenth comes from the 

Birmingham district in Alabama. The rest is produced in southeastern Pennsylvania, northern New Jersey, the Adirondacks in New

York, and scattered places in the Appalachians and Rockies. 

Most of the iron ore from the Lake Superior region comes from the Mesabi Range district in northern Minnesota. Here the soft earth containing the iron is scooped up by big steam shovels and loaded directly on small cars which are then run to the docks on the lake front. In nearly all other large iron mines, the iron ore is deeper in the earth and is removed through shafts or tunnels.

Large Consumption of Iron and Steel. - 

Have you ever looked around at home, at school, or on the street and taken note of the different uses of iron and steel? If you have, you found your list to be a long one indeed. The stove on which your mother cooked the dinner, the cooking utensils she used, even knives, forks, and spoons all contain iron. (Review some of the other uses of iron we have noted.) 

The Iron Industry in Western Pennsyl vania. Coke is the best fuel for manufacturing iron. Bituminous coal from which good coke could be made was abundant near 

Pittsburgh. The best coke was made near 

Connellsville, on the Youghiogheny River a few miles south of Pittsburgh. Iron ore was also found here and it was easy to smelt the ore in the furnaces near Connellsville and ship the bars down the river to Pittsburgh, where rolling mills were already established. Pittsburgh then became the great center of the iron and steel industry. It held its lead even after the better ore was brought from the Lake Superior district. 

Spread of the Iron and Steel Industry. - 

The Lake Superior district is still the main source of supply. Cars loaded at the mines bring the ore to the lake docks, where it is dumped into chutes leading to the holds of the vessels below. An iron ore steamer carrying 

Courtesy Cleveland Chamber of Commerce

ORE DOCKS AT CLEVELAND 

This is where land and water transportation meet. By means of huge grab buckets, unloaders remove the iron ore from the ore boats and either transfer it directly to waiting railroad cars or pile it up in the dock yards for subsequent shipment by rail to the iron and steel mills.

STEEL Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction MAKING STEEL 

A white-hot ingot of steel hangs over a soaking pit, held by an overhead crane. The traveling crane will carry the hot ingot directly to the rolling mill, where it will be rolled out into any shape and size desired. 

12,000 tons can be loaded in this way in 20 minutes, and then unloaded by means of grab buckets in about four hours.

The ore is shipped by water as far as the Lake Erie ports, where it is unloaded into freight cars and sent the rest of the journey by rail.

As the industry spread, lake ports, such as Cleveland, Toledo, Ashtabula, Erie, and 

Buffalo, and cities along the railroad routes, also became important iron and steel centers, getting coal for fuel from the Pittsburgh district. 

Discovery of a new method made it possible to manufacture good coke from coal in the north central states. 

This, together with the ease of transportation and the nearness to markets, has caused the steel industry to spread to many centers in the north- 233 ern Mississippi Valley, especially cities on or near the Great Lakes. Gary, Indiana, on the shores of Lake Michigan, has one of the largest steel plants in the world. Iron manufacturing is also being carried on in Duluth and other places near the iron ore fields in the Lake Superior district.

Thus the coal in some instances is now going to the iron ore instead of the ore coming to the coal as in most cases. 

Birmingham, Alabama, is the big iron and steel center in the South. Here the iron ore, coal, and limestone are all found close together, and very little transportation is required. 

Steel. A method of making steel has been known for centuries. But it formerly required a long time, several weeks at least. 

Consequently steel was made only in small quantities as it was too expensive to use except for the smaller and more valuable articles, such as cutlery.

A broken rail is a serious matter, especially if a train is going fast.

Cheap steel and plenty of it was badly needed. 

But how to get it was the unsolved problem.

Then came the great discovery which transformed the iron and steel industry. — - 

Bessemer Steel. In 1856, Sir Henry 

Bessemer, an Englishman, discovered a process by which steel could be made from molten pig iron in thirty minutes instead of several weeks. This method of making steel was named the Bessemer process. 

This process consists of pouring the molten pig iron into a large vessel called a converter and then forcing hot air through the mass. The air unites with the carbon in so the railroad builders and other construction workers turned to a new development, called open-hearth steel.

Open-Hearth Steel.- 

Open-hearth steel takes from six to eight hours to make.

It is, therefore, more costly than Bessemer steel. The open-hearth steel is made in a large "open" furnace. It can be tested in the making, and other metals added as needed to give it desired qualities. 

In this way, good strong steel can be produced to meet different requirements. Where strength and durability are necessary, as in steel rails, it is the safest to use.

Manufacturing Industries Which Use Iron and Steel. 

On account of the numerous uses of iron and steel, the manufacturing industries demanding these metals are many and varied. Buildings and bridges use about one-sixth of all the steel made each year. 

Railroad tracks, cars, and locomotives use at least one-fifth, and motor vehicles, machinery, and tools of various kinds together use up nearly one-third of the iron and steel. 

You may be surprised to learn of the large amount of steel used in making little articles. 

For example, wrenches alone require about 

60,000 tons each year. Hair clippers, scissors, needles, pins, notebook clasps, and trimmings all use considerable quantities.

Many of the large industries manufacturing iron or steel products are located in or

F. THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY Rapid Growth of the Automobile Industry. 

-The growth of the automobile industry is comparatively recent. In 1895 only 300 automobiles were produced, and these were small, awkward affairs. The industry really began in 1899 with a production of 4000 cars. 

In 1903 and 1904 a number of corporations were formed which have been the backbone of the industry. Not till about 1910 did automobiles become at all common. In

Industries. The automobile has created a tremendous demand for the products of many other industries. Influence of the Automobile on Road Building. automobile has stimulated the building of good roads.

Formerly many parts of the country were notorious for their bad roads. Now they are equally well known for their excellent highways. 

Millions of dollars have been spent and many millions more will be spent for good roads.

INDUSTRIES The Importance of Food-Manufacturing Industries. Our manufactured food prod ucts are the most valuable group of manufactures in the United States. Most of the industries preparing foods are scattered over the country near their source of supply, but two of them, milling and meat packing, have tended to develop on a large scale in convenient centers. Minneapolis, near the great wheat fields of the West, is one of the greatest flour-manufacturing cities in the world. The Falls of St. Anthony in the Mississippi River furnish power for the mills. Cattle, sheep, and swine are shipped to these stockyards by carloads and confined in pens until they can be slaughtered. 

Chicago is the meat-packing center of the world. Some of the big packing houses appear in the background. where most of the animals which supply the meat are raised. Good railway transportation makes it possible to have large, central establishments where the animals can be brought and handled in large numbers. 

In these large plants, all the work is done under strict governmental inspection, and the meat is handled in a sanitary manner. 

Meat Packing a Large Industry. - The 

American people are large meat eaters. Our annual consumption of meat averages about 

140 pounds per person. Millions of animals are killed each year for meat and their preparation for home consumption is necessarily a big industry. 

Transportation in the Meat-Packing Indus try. At first cattle and swine were raised commercially only in the Atlantic States near cities with slaughterhouse facilities. The animals used to be driven to market. This was true even after the settlers crossed the Appalachian barrier and settled in the Ohio Valley beyond. But it was a long drive to the eastern markets, and many of the animals died on the way. Those that survived were thin and in poor condition so that it was necessary to fatten them before they were killed.

Whenever possible, waterways were used to get the animals to market. The opening of the Erie Canal in 1825 provided an allwater route from the Great Lakes region to the Atlantic seaboard. The people in Ohio found it easy to raise corn and produce a surplus of pork which could be cured and then sent to market. Cincinnati developed such a thriving pork-curing and packing industry that it was nicknamed "Porkopolis."

ADVANTAGES OF LARGE PACKING PLANTS 

Then the railroad made it possible to ship live animals by rail so that they arrived in good condition. With the railroad came the swift development of the central Mississippi Valley. The prairie plains were transformed into farms. The dryer plains to the west became a vast grazing region where millions of cattle roamed. The Corn Belt came into existence, and great numbers of swine were raised. 

The packing houses, like the population, had a "westward movement." 

Chicago, with its splendid railroad facilities and its good water supply, became the great center which it is to-day. The stockyards of

Chicago became a famous sight for visitors.

There are now 67 officially recognized public livestock markets in the United States. 241 rooms or freezers where meats are kept for some time, temperatures as low as 30° and 40° below zero may be maintained.

The Influence of Refrigeration in the Meat- Packing Industry. Before refrigeration was introduced about 1870, methods of packing meat were very crude compared with those of to-day. Slaughtering had to be done during the cold winter months. Fresh meat could 

Fresh meat could then be kept long enough to market it near by or to cure it by pickling, smoking, or salting. Now refrigeration has changed all that: the temperature in the refrigerator cars is kept about the freezing point, while in cold-storage in canned soups. 

Then there are other products, such as beef extracts, bouillon cubes, gelatin, and lard. The hogs raised in the Corn Belt are very fat and produce large quantities of lard, which is one of our important exports. All these products are prepared in the large meat-packing centers. 

Advantages of Large Packing Plants. - 

One of the advantages of large meat-packing plants is that the owners can afford to install the best machinery for doing the work efficiently and cheaply. The process of converting an animal into meat might be compared to the process of putting together an

automobile in the Ford factory. But in the automobile factory, each worker adds something to the machine as it is conveyed slowly along. In the packing industry, each man bones are used to fashion various useful articles such as buttons, crochet needles, handles for knives, pipestems, and chessmen.

The hair from the hides is used to put in plaster, to stuff horse collars, and for other purposes. Hair from the tails is used to stuff mattresses. Fine hair cut from the inside of the cows' ears is used for artists' brushes. 

Wool from sheepskins is carefully saved. The hides are tanned for leather and the hoofs furnish glue, while the intestines are carefully cleaned and dried to be used as sausage casings. Certain inedible glands are saved, from which a large number of medicinal products are manu- 

 factured. Fat is used for 

MEAT AT WHOLESALE 

This is an inside view of a big meat-packing plant in Los Angeles where meat is being cut up for the retail meat market. Meat-packing plants are to be found in many of our large cities, which are naturally large consumers of meat and of many of the by-products from the meat industry. cuts away something. This continues until the whole carcass has been made ready for the refrigerator and the inedible parts disposed of. 

This is another example of the "speeding-up" process in which American industry excels. One of the big packing firms can kill and dress 6000 hogs an hour. Another company can handle in its numerous plants over 57,000 animals every working day. 

Another advantage of the large packing house is that it can make effective use of the by-products. Formerly in small establishments most of the inedible parts of the animal and even some of the edible portions, went to waste. Now nothing goes to waste in a big plant. The blood is caught, allowed to coagulate, and then cooked, dried, and finally pulverized to form fertilizer. Horns and making soap. And finally all the scraps and left-overs are converted into fertilizers.

These by-products yield a large return to the packers, who for this reason can sell the meat cheaper.

H. THE TEXTILE INDUSTRY Rank of the Textile Industries. - Next to food in its importance among our needs, comes clothing. The materials for clothing are largely furnished by the four main textile industries- cotton, wool, silk, and linen. 

Of these linen is least important in the United States as we import all but a small portion. 

The preparation of the flax fiber from which linen is made requires hand labor which is too expensive in our country. 

1. COTTON MANUFACTURING IN THE UNITED STATES The Lead of Cotton in American Textile 

Industries. Cotton manufacturing is our

AN EARLY COTTON-MANUFACTURING CENTER

Lowell literally grew up around its cotton mills. Located in the textile section of Massachusetts on the Merrimack 

River, this city is well known for producing textiles, carpets, toilet preparations, chemicals, mohair plush, and silks. 

Approximately 30,000 workers are employed by its various industries. leading textile industry. With our immense production of cotton and our large consumption of cotton goods, this is very natural. 

Our climate not only favors the production of cotton and the wearing of cotton clothing, but also the manufacture of cotton goods. 

Then there are many uses besides clothing for cotton goods. Curtains, draperies, certain kinds of carpets, sheets and pillow cases, tablecloths, napkins, and towels - all are all are made of cotton.

For the origin of cotton we must go to India. Centuries before commerce began between western European countries and the Far East, the people of India were weaving cotton cloth so fine and sheer that it received the name woven wind." 66 Englishmen began to purchase it in India during the seventeenth century and afterwards introduced it into the American colonies. Its use was better suited to our climate than to that of England, so it became popular in America and the demand for it later led to its manufacture in this country. 

Effect of Inventions on Cotton Manufacture. 

Before the invention of textile machinery, cotton goods were more expensive than wool and decidedly a novelty. The invention of spinning and weaving machines which could be run by water power or steam revolutionized the textile industry and in a few years changed it from a household to a factory industry. Textile machinery was first adapted to the weaving of cotton. The invention of the cotton gin followed, greatly lowering the cost of the raw cotton, with the result that cotton cloth became much cheaper than woolen cloth, and even the poorer classes could afford to use it. 

The Start of the American Cotton-Manufacturing Industry. After the Revolutionary War, Americans wished to manufacture their own cotton goods. But England, naturally, wished to keep this industry for herself, and refused to allow any textile machines to leave the country. Finally, Samuel Slater, an English mechanic, came to 

America, bringing "a machine in his head."

He built a crude machine, and started a cotton mill in Pawtucket, Rhode Island, in 1790. 

This marked the beginning of the cottonmanufacturing industry in the United States.

Slater trained other men who established held first place in the cotton-manufacturing industry, but now her supremacy is challenged by the cotton-growing states in the 

South. Massachusetts still holds first place in the number of spindles, but North Carolina is second, and the southern states

MANCHESTER 

Manchester, on the Merrimack River in New Hampshire, is one of the greatest cotton-manufacturing cities in the United States. A number of the cotton mills appear in the center of this picture. Soon there was a group of cotton mills with Providence, Rhode Island, as the center. Others were built in Massachusetts before many years, Lowell, Lawrence, Springfield, and

Holyoke being the largest centers for the industry. Manchester, New Hampshire, and

Woonsocket, Rhode Island, also erected large textile plants. Twenty years after Slater constructed his first mill in Rhode Island, there were over 60 cotton mills in New 

England, with more being built. 

Water power was used to run the machines.

As the number and size of the mills increased, and the water power became insufficient, coal and steam were substituted. 

With her early start, New England long as a whole have more active spindles than the New England states. New England, however, ranks first in the production of the finer grades of cotton goods.

Advantages of New England for Cotton Manufacturing. 

New England was a favorable region in which to start the cotton-manufacturing indus- try for several reasons. First was the water power furnished by the numerous streams.

Then the moist climate favored the industry, especially in the cities near the coast. 

Labor also was plentiful, as farming was difficult and many were glad to give up their small farms and go to work in the factories.

New England was also the most active part of the country in trade and consequently merchants and traders there had the capital to put into this new industry. 

Cotton Manufacturing in the South. 

But the southern states, too, have many advantages for the manufacture of cotton goods. One is the abundance of water power; coal, and oil. Hydro-electric plants make it possible for cotton mills to locate in more favorable places than just at the fall line where most of the water power is found. Labor is abundant and cheap. The people from the mountain districts are glad to move into the mill towns. The climate is milder than it is in the North, so that workers can live comfortably on lower wages than in the northern states.

You probably would add as another reason that the mills are near the raw cotton. 

Strange to say, this advantage counts for little, as better results are obtained by mixing cottons from different regions. 

Often the kind of cotton needed in one of the mills in a South Atlantic state has to be brought from Texas, for example. The railroad transportation required may cost more than water transportation from the same producing state to a mill in a coastal city of Rhode Island. 

Good harbors, rivers, and railway transportation are other advantages which the

A SOUTHERN COTTON MILL 

The Olympia Mill, located in Columbia, South Carolina, is one of the largest cotton mills in the South. There is now more manufacturing of cotton south of the Mason and Dixon Line than north of it. Why? and flannelettes. All of these require less skill in the making than the fine lawns, dimities, voiles, or cambrics, that are made in the New England mills. Another lack of the South has been that of finishing plants. 

After weaving, cotton goods have to be bleached, dyed, or otherwise finished before they are ready for sale. Most southern goods have to be sent north to be finished. 

Moreover, most of the textile machinery factories are in the North, so this machinery and parts for replacement have to be shipped long distances.

Furthermore, the mills are farther away from their best customers, which are largely in the northeastern states. They have to ship their goods longer distances than the New England mills. This takes more time and increases the cost. 

2. THE WOOL-MANUFACTURING INDUSTRY 

Wool Manufacturing a Much Older Industry than Cotton Manufacturing. - Wool

manufacturing is much older than cotton manufacturing. As we have seen, the latter did not begin on a large scale till the Industrial Revolution. Wool, on the other hand, had long been known and used; its weaving was one of the main household industries. 

TEXTILE INSPECTION 

States census (1790) wool manufacturing was still essentially a household industry. There were three or four exceptions, especially Germantown, Pennsylvania, where German spinners and weavers started several factories. 

This early start may be the cause of Philadelphia's prominent position in wool manufacturing to-day.

England Opposed Wool 

Manufacturing in America. 

England, naturally, did not want to see our woolen industry develop, so she did everything to hamper its growth. The English prized their woolen-cloth industry, and did not want us to share in its profits. So they prohibited the export of English machinery to this country. 

They dumped goods on our market so as to reduce the price of the home product.

They bought our sheep, only to send them to the butcher.

They even purchased some of our machinery and then sent it to England. 

In spite of all these handicaps, the industry gradually grew, although we were much behind England at the time of the Civil War. This is the interior of a large woolen mill. Experts are carefully examining the finished goods to make sure that there are no imperfections of any kind before the material leaves the mill. Goods having any defects must be sold at a greatly reduced price.

There were several reasons for this situation. First, sheep were widely distributed, especially among the farmers of the northern colonies. They furnished both food and clothing materials. 

Another reason for wool weaving was that, in every town, there were people who knew how to spin woolen yarn. Also, there was a great demand for warm clothing in the northern states. Thus we find that woolen yarn and cloth were made in nearly all colonial homes, especially in the northern and middle colonies. In New England there was a law compelling each household to make certain quantities of yarn, depending on the number of "spinsters" in the home.

This home method continued for many years, with the gradual establishment of hand-work factories. At the time of the first United 

The Introduction of Worsteds. There was a change in the wool industry at about the time of the Civil War. First, the war stimulated the demand for good wool cloth to make uniforms for the soldiers. Then, shortly after, worsted cloth was introduced. 

This revolutionized the industry, because methods similar to those used in the cotton industry could be employed. The wool industry, thereafter, depended more on machinery, more women could be employed, and its products could be called staples. 

After the Civil War, we began to draw upon all parts of the world for our wool supply. To-day, worsted cloth is made much

GROWTH OF THE SILK-MANUFACTURING INDUSTRY 

more extensively in the United States than woolen cloth by the earlier process. Some of the largest woolen mills in the world have been built in this country, notably those at 

Lawrence, Massachusetts.

Sources of Our Wool.-Raw wool has 

Raw wool has long been one of our most important imports from foreign countries. Of course, we raise large quantities at home, even more than we import. Our leading states in the production of raw wool are Texas, Wyoming, Montana, California, Utah, Oregon, Ohio, Idaho, and

New Mexico. The chief foreign sources of raw wool are the United Kingdom, China, Australia, Argentina, Uruguay, British India, Canada, Palestine and Syria, New Zealand, and British South Africa. 

The Future of the Wool-Manufacturing 

Industry. As the population of our country increases, we may expect the relative production of worsted and woolen cloth to decrease. 

The reason for this is that it will be increasingly harder to get wool. Already we are using substitutes, such as natural and artificial silks, and the finer grades of cotton. 

3. THE SILK-MANUFACTURING INDUSTRY 

Popularity of Silk in the United States. 

The people of the United States buy more silk goods than those of any other nation. 

This is due to several factors. 

First, we have a large population with a high standard of living. In other words, many of our people can afford to buy silk goods. 

Second, style is a very important factor.

Here again we come in contact with that very important force, "keeping up with the 

Joneses." If our neighbors wear silks, then we must wear silks, whether we can afford it or not. In this connection, there is much deliberate advertising by the manufacturer to increase the use of silk goods.

Third, and this may be the most important reason many of our people, especially the 

247 women, consider silk superior in many respects to cotton, wool, linen, or any other form of clothing material. 

Growth of the Silk-Manufacturing Industry. Though dependent on foreign sources for its raw material, the silk-manufacturing 

A SILK LOOM 

Wide World Photo 

Does this complicated machine suggest to you one reason for our big-scale production of silk and other textiles? Weaving is no longer a laborious hand operation but a machine process where dozens of yards of uniformly woven material are turned out in the time it would take to make one yard by a hand loom. industry of the United States is by far the largest in the world. The mills are scattered through many states, although there is a large concentration in Pennsylvania, New Jersey, and New York. The annual output of all of our silk factories has a value of nearly $1,000,000,000.

So many mills are in the above-named states because silk manufacturing is a parasite industry. It moves to the labor supply,

which is principally women. The women are the wives, sisters, or children of the men who work in the iron, coal, and cement industries in the same areas. The production in Paterson, New Jersey, alone, which has the largest in the industry, amounts to about 10 per cent of the total. 

Sources of Raw Silk. To supply these mills, many millions of pounds of raw silk are imported annually. Japan alone supplies nearly 85 per cent. The rest comes mainly from China, Italy, and France. Japan leads because she has deliberately set out to do so. 

She has the climate, the labor, and the determination to lead. Schools of sericulture (growing silkworms) have been established all over the empire. They teach the people the best methods of cultivating and caring for the mulberry trees and the silkworms. 

Why don't we produce our own silk? Mulberry trees, upon which the silkworms feed, will grow in nearly all parts of the country. 

The answer is to be found in the labor situation. Silk culture needs cheap, patient, careful, and deft labor, something not yet to be found in the United States. Japan and 

China have a plentiful supply of such labor. 

History of Silk. The history of silk starts with Hoang-Ti, third Emperor of China. He charged his Queen, Si-Ling-Chi, to examine the silkworms and test the practicability of using the thread from the cocoons. In her zeal, she collected large numbers of the worms, fed them herself, and discovered how to reel the silk and make it into cloth. This was about 1700 B. C. For her discovery she was deified, so the Chinese records say, and ever since has been known as the "Goddess of Silkworms."

The Chinese are said to have kept their method a secret for over 2000 years. However, it gradually spread to Persia and other countries of southwestern Asia, and finally reached southern and western Europe and the Americas.

Silk formed the basis of a lucrative trade between the Mediterranean lands and the 

Monsoon lands of the Old World. Only people of high rank or great wealth could afford it.

Rayon or Artificial Silk, the New Fabric. - 

The making of rayon or artificial silk is one of the most important developments of the twentieth century. It is based upon the observation of silk-spinning in nature. The silkworm eats mulberry leaves which it converts into a semi-fluid condition in two long glands within its body. As these glands approach the head, they grow smaller, and at a double opening beneath the mouth, apparently in a single thread, the glutinous material is finally forced out. This gummy liquid hardens upon exposure to the air into fine threads. Out of them the silkworms make their cocoons, the unwinding of which gives us the raw silk of commerce. 

A Frenchman, Count Hilare de Char donnet, first produced an artificial fiber in 1884. But it was not until 1903 that an 

Englishman named Stearns discovered the so-called "viscose method" now most largely used.

In making rayon by this method, a good grade of wood pulp is used as raw material. 

By various chemical treatments the wood pulp is converted into a jelly-like cellulose. 

This is dissolved in water to make the spinning solution. In other words, by chemical means a substance is made similar to that which the silkworm makes in its body. The solution is then sent to the spinning machines, where it is forced through the spinning nozzles. 

These nozzles have very fine holes, the number varying according to the size of the thread it is desired to produce. These threads are then put into a solution which hardens them. After that they are washed and dried, twisted and reeled, just as is natural silk. Finally the finished rayon thread is put up in skeins, each containing

IMPORTANCE OF THE LEATHER INDUSTRIES 

249 3000 yards and weighing ten pounds. In this form it is ready for the manufacturers.

The Expansion of the Rayon Industry. - Rayon began to be used in quantity in the United States about 

1910. Within twenty years, we were importing millions of pounds and manufacturing several times as much more. We are the largest producers as well as consumers of artificial silk in the world.

RAYON PLANT 

Courtesy Chamber of Commerce, Asheville

Cheap electric power derived from abundant water power is aiding the development of the textile industries in North Carolina. This rayon plant at 

Asheville is one of the largest of its kind in the world. What is rayon and of what is it made? What easily obtained raw materials could this city use in the manufacture of rayon?

Qualities of Rayon.- 

Rayon has good dyeing qualities, and its thread is of uniform size. It loses part of its strength when wet but regains it when dry. It should be washed by hand. It can even be boiled without harm and is no more inflammable than other fibers. Its power of absorbing moisture is an asset. Rayon is good for making underwear, whereas natural silk turns yellow and rots from perspiration. It has no injurious effects upon the skin, and may be made of almost any desired thickness and strength. 

Possibilities of Rayon. - Rayon is now extensively used for underwear, hosiery, ribbons, wire insulation, bed spreads, draperies, trimmings, satins, laces, embroideries, and many other things.

Rayon really is a new fiber. It is not merely a substitute for cotton, wool, silk, and linen, but a new product with unique qualities of its own. It will never replace the other fibers, but it is a valuable addition to them. 

By various mixtures and combinations, new fabrics may be produced which otherwise would not be possible. Our country has a new and growing industry in the making of artificial silk. You will be interested to watch the growth of this thriving industry. 

I. THE LEATHER INDUSTRIES 

Importance of the Leather Industries. 

The tanning of leather, together with the manufacture of boots and shoes, and other articles of leather, is another big industry which grew with the development of the United States. This country now makes nearly $1,500,000,000 worth of leather each year. Some of this is exported. But most of it, together with large quantities of imported leather, goes into the manufacture of boots, shoes, and slippers, suit cases and bags, saddles and harness, belts for machinery, and numerous other articles. Leather products in the United States each year are worth over $1,500,000,000. American shoes especially are noted all over the world, and are sold in nearly every civilized country. 

The production of leather and of leather products in our country is so much greater than that in other countries that we are led to ask the reasons. Two things are absolutely necessary for making leather: hides or skins, and tanning materials. The possession of large quantities of both was the

ONE SOURCE OF LEATHER Courtesy First National Bank of El Paso 

When the first settlers came to America, they found the Indians skilled in making leather. In the cattle roaming over our western plains, we now have our most important source of leather. Leather is to-day, as it was in ancient times, one of the most important articles used for clothing. main factor in building up our large leather industry. There is no longer enough of these items to supply our present big industry, so hides and tanning materials are imported. 

Where Leather Comes From. Cattle, Cattle, sheep, and goats furnish most of the hides and skins from which leather is made, but the skins of many other animals are also used. 

Horses also furnish hides, and the skins of pigs, deer, kangaroos, dogs, seals, alligators, frogs, lizards and even snakes, ostriches, and sharks add to our supply.

The cattle-raising industry in the United States furnishes the larger part of our supply of cattle hides, but large numbers of hides are also imported from Argentina, Uruguay, Canada, and many other countries.

The heavy cattle and horse hides are necessary in making sole leather for footwear. 

Large numbers of goat skins are imported from India, China, Brazil, northern Africa,

Spain, and other Mediterranean countries where goats are plentiful. These are used especially for the "uppers" of shoes. 

The Tanning Process. The skins of animals soon decay unless something is done to preserve them. Usually they are salted and dried, or placed in a solution of salt. 

This preserves them until they can be taken to the tannery. When so treated they can be shipped anywhere. When it is necessary to carry them to a seaport or railroad station for shipment, they are dried, as they are most easily handled in this form. Our imports are classed as "dry" hides or "wet" hides.

Tanning is the process of changing skins or hides into leather. The tanning process varies. One method consists of placing the hides or skins in a solution of vegetable tannin obtained largely from the bark or wood of trees. Chestnut, oak, and hemlock trees have furnished much of the bark used in 

American tanneries.

IMPORTANCE OF FOOTWEAR 

The older process of tanning leather consisted of placing alternating layers of skins and bark in a vat and then pouring in water until the whole was covered. The tannin in the bark soaked out by the water changed the texture of the flesh in the skins so that it became tough and resistant to decay. This is why the industry was scattered through the Appalachians where the chestnut and oak trees were abundant, and across the northern United States from Wisconsin eastward where the hemlocks were found. 

Hides were shipped to the places where the bark was found. Now it is so easy to ship the bark by railroad that the tanneries may be located where hides are produced or in places to which they may easily be shipped. 

Furthermore, it is now customary to extract the tannin from the bark or other tanning materials. The solution is then evaporated until it is rather strong, or perhaps reduced to a powder. It is then easily shipped anywhere.

The United States now produces only about half of its tanning materials. Other sources of tannin than those already named are quebracho wood found in South America, sumac, of which the leaves are used, valonia 

CLEVERAG

Chrome tanning, fat liquoring, and dyeing of upper leather usually successive operations are carried out in revolving drums like these. The one at the right is discharging fully tanned skins. 

obtained from the acorns of the valonia oak found in the Balkans and Asia Minor, and several shrubs and trees of the Far East. 

Mineral tanning. Another way of tanning leather is to place it in a mineral solution called chrome liquor. This tans the leather in six hours while the vegetable tanning takes six months. Quite a difference, is it not? Nearly all of our shoe leather (except sole leather) and also glove leather is tanned by this newer process. In fact about

TANNING LEATHER OUTDOORS 

This is the older way of tanning leather. Alternate layers of hides and bark are placed in vats and water then poured in until the whole is covered. This is called vegetable tanning. This process takes as many months as mineral tanning now takes hours to transform the skins into leather. one-half of all our leather is now tanned by chrome liquor, which is so much quicker and cheaper than the older tanbark. 

THE BOOT AND SHOE INDUSTRY 

Importance of Footwear.

Perhaps no phase of the leather industry is more important than that of the manufacture of boots and shoes. Some one has said that civilization has advanced on foot. Well-shod races are the ones who have advanced

99 the civilizations of the world. Not only are bare feet easily injured, but they give ready entrance to disease germs. Much of the trouble from "hookworms in tropical countries, and even in our own country, could be largely avoided by protection and care of the feet.

Courtesy United Shoe Machinery Corporation A COLONIAL SHOE SHOP 

This is the old Freeman Winslow Shoe Shop in Salem,

Massachusetts, one of the earliest shoe shops in America. 

The skilled shoemaker of Colonial times, with comparatively simple tools, made each shoe complete. In a modern shoe factory, each worker performs but a small part of the work on each shoe, and that is done with a machine. The local shoemaker in a Colonial town depended on farmers in his locality to supply him with leather; a modern shoe factory may have leather brought from the most distant countries of the world. 

Through long experience, leather has proved to be the most satisfactory material for covering the feet. Leather resources, shoe machinery, and large demand have all helped to put our country far in the lead in the making of shoes and other footwear. 

Every year we make an average of three pairs of shoes for every person in the country. 

The total production is rather evenly divided among women's, men's, and children's shoes. 

The rest, including slippers, are classified as miscellaneous footwear. 

Massachusetts and New York make almost half our footwear. Missouri also has become an important shoe-manufacturing state. 

Massachusetts produces the largest quantity of men's shoes, women's shoes, and shoes for misses and small children. New York has the largest output of shoes for boys and youths and miscellaneous footwear, including slippers.. The greatest demand for this enormous quantity of footwear exists at home. 

Still we export annually several million pairs. 

American-made shoes have a good reputation in the markets of the world.

Shoemakers in Colonial Times. -For merly, every little community had its local cobbler who made shoes to order by hand.

Now, nearly all boots and shoes are made in factories. This factory system had a small start as far back as 1750. In that year John Adam Dagys applied the principle of the division of labor to shoemaking in Lynn, Massachusetts. So successful was this start that by 1795, Lynn alone was producing 300,000 pairs per year.

Boston was the principal importing point for raw materials, such as hides and skins, rosin, and thread. It was also an exporting point for the finished product. From Lynn, the industry spread to other centers such as 

Brockton, Massachusetts, which established a reputation for the making of men's shoes. 

All of this early manufacturing around Boston was on a relatively small scale. Hand labor was employed, and there were almost no machines.

Introduction of Machinery. - The first machine was one for rolling sole leather. It was introduced about 1845. Elias Howe's sewing machine was adapted to the sewing of uppers in 1851. Have you ever stopped to consider how much the sewing machine has contributed to the development of the factory system in the United States? 

Gordon McKay's machine for sewing soles to uppers was set up in 1861. Other machine processes have been added, until up to date over 5000 patents have been issued in the boot and shoe industry alone! Shoes may now be completely manufactured by machin-

SPREAD OF THE SHOE INDUSTRY 

ery.

The division of labor with the use of machinery has been carried so far that each worker, on an average, produces enough footwear every year to supply a thousand men. 

Spread of the Shoe Industry. - Massa chusetts got an early start and is still the leading state in shoe manufacturing. But the industry has gradually moved westward with the westward expansion of the population. St. Louis, Missouri, is now a very important center, as are other interior cities.

They are near the raw materials, and also have the labor, capital, and market. Boots and shoes, because of our machine methods, are made in larger quantities in the United 

States than anywhere else in the world.

PEGGING MACHINE 

The first shoe-pegging machine was used in New York in 1852. This machine would peg around the sole of a man's shoe in one minute and do the work better than it could be done by hand. The adaptation of machines to the making of shoes has been our country's outstanding contribution to the shoe-manufacturing industry and is one of the main reasons for our lead in this industry.

J. THE UNITED STATES AS A COMMERCIAL NATION 1. DOMESTIC COMMERCE Our Large Domestic Commerce. - The people of our country carry on a vast amount of domestic commerce. Domestic commerce is that carried on within the country itself, as distinguished from foreign commerce which is carried on with other nations. This home commerce is sometimes estimated by some to amount to billions of dollars per year, and a very conservative estimate would be about $100,000,000,000. It is far greater than that to be found within any other country. Why should there be such a vast amount of trade or exchange of goods among our people?

There are many reasons which you could undoubtedly give. Try to do so before you read any more of this chapter. 

Division of Labor as a Cause of Commerce. 

Perhaps the most important reason is the division of labor. This term means that certain groups of people, such as farmers, engage in one occupation, while certain other groups, such as manufacturers, spend their time doing another type of work. Within each one of these groups there are many subdivisions, so that one man or woman may do just one task for a living. For this one task, he receives money which he uses to purchase all the necessities and some of the luxuries of life. Thus the division of labor leads to the exchange of goods or services.

MOTORITE OUR MANY WANTS AS A CAUSE OF TRADE 

255 sit to he

LOS ANGELES 

Ewing Galloway

Here is the inner harbor at San Pedro Bay, with its long landing docks, its freight sheds, its oil storage drums, its thread-like railroad tracks, all signs of a busy and important port. 

At this point, the members of the class may wish to make a list of the occupations engaged in by their parents. Place this list on the blackboard. It will lead to a very interesting discussion. 

Our Many Wants as a Cause of Trade. 

Another reason for trade is our ever-increasing wants. Such wants as food, clothing, shelter, tools, fuel, means of transportation, and means of communication are universal even among primitive people. The number of our wants increases with civilization. 

The more we go about to well-furnished homes, the more we mingle with well-dressed people, and the more we travel, the more numerous our wants become. "Keeping up with the Joneses" is a powerful factor in creating more commerce.

The extension of credit also helps. Haven't you noticed the appeal, "Ten dollars down and ten dollars a month for ten months!"?

You can now afford to own an automobile, a radio, an electric refrigerator, and what not, because these enterprising dealers will furnish you credit.

This is all tied up with our "standard of living," which is determined by the kinds and amounts of things we use. amounts of things we use. The wish to better this standard is one of the strongest driving forces in the world, because every one wants to eat and dress and enjoy life as well as he can, and especially as well as his neighbors. 

The standard of living among our people is higher than among any other large group in the world. In nearly all respects, the average 

American family now lives better than kings of a few centuries ago.

The luxuries of past generations have now become necessities. We owe this to high wages and the lower cost of the things we use. This has been brought about by ma-

chines, improved means of transportation, and other forces. As the laborer's wages have increased, his wants have increased. This raising of the standard of living we call progress. 

Differences within the Country Result in 

Trade. - Of course, such a great amount of commerce would not be possible in a small country. The United States is a large coun- Q1214 170 country are vast but unequally distributed. 

Some states have coal, others do not. Some states have petroleum, while others have copper. In fact, a leading occupation in some of the states is the mining of coal, gold, silver, copper, iron, or some other valuable mineral, while they depend on other states for food, clothing, and the other necessities and luxuries of life. Thus a difference in mineral resources helps to create domestic com- merce. 

Differences in Industrial Development. A difference in the stage of industrial development makes for trade. 

For various reasons, the New 

England states are primarily manufacturing states, depending on the rest of the country for many of their raw materials. They depend on the 

South for raw cotton to supply their numerous cotton mills. Minnesota and other north central states supply the New England states with wheat and flour. Montana and the western states furnish the woolen mills with raw wool. Steel for their many small metal-ware manufactures comes from Pennsylvania or some other steel-manufacturing

Unequal Distribution of Population. - 

Although our country is of large size and has a great population, the people are very un-

STEAMBOATS GIVE WAY TO RAILROADS 

equally distributed. Some states and even larger sections are very densely populated while the reverse is true of other regions. 

The sections with only a few people produce usually raw materials and food, while the sections with many people are mainly engaged in manufacturing. These conditions bring about an exchange of products, and this means, of course, more trade and commerce.

The people of the plains trade with the people of the mountains; the seashore dwellers exchange their goods for products of the interior; the people of the city carry on commerce with the people of the country; forested sections trade with the grassland areas; and regions with rich soil trade with regions of poor soil. 

Free Trade between 

States. Our tariff applies only to foreign countries.

Between our states we have 257 that the whole eastern half of our country was at one time heavily forested. There were no roads or railroads, so the early settlers used the natural waterways for moving themselves and their goods. In Europe trade between different countries is greatly hindered by the different tariffs and the different standards of money (pounds in England, francs in France, marks in Germany, etc.). But here our goods move freely across dozens of state lines and all bills are paid in dollars and cents.

Water Transportation in the Development of Our Commerce. - Perhaps no two factors have been so important in helping to build up this vast trade as the development of means of transportation and communication. Waterways were the first transportation lines to be used systematically. Water travel was easier because the way was furnished by nature. It should be remembered 

Steamboats Give Way to Railroads. - It must be remembered that we have about 

25,000 miles of navigable waterways in the 

United States. Why did they not keep on developing? Some of them did, like the Great 

Lakes, for instance. But many others were allowed to fall into disuse regardless of the fact that water routes afforded the cheapest and best known means of transport for heavy, bulky, and cheap commodities. 

Most of them did not keep on developing because of the coming of the railroad. Railroads have done more to develop our country and build up our vast commerce than any other single factor. How could the railroads

Courtesy Great Northern Railways MILLING DISTRICT OF MINNEAPOLIS 

Falls in the Mississippi provided the power which led Minneapolis to be at one time the leading flour-milling center in this country. How has its locapassenger train on 

Without the railtion in respect to the wheat fields helped? Notice the long the stone bridge which crosses the Mississippi at this point. roads, this great agricultural and industrial development of the central part of our country could not have taken place. put most of the waterways out of business? 

There are several reasons: (1) A railroad can lay its course to suit the location of the resources to be tapped and the industrial centers to be served. Numerous branch lines can be built as feeders, making a network of transportation between all points where the volume of traffic justifies it. 

Waterways, however, must follow courses determined by nature. (2) Through shipments can be made by railroads with an ease and economy unattainable by the waterways. (3) Trains run considerably faster than boats, particularly boats on narrow and winding waterways. A freight train makes twenty miles an hour, or more, while the speed of canal boats is less than four miles per hour. River and lake boats, however, make better time. (4) Waterways freeze over during the winter months in northern climates. The New

York canals have about 215 navigable days throughout the year. Railways, on the other hand, offer a regular service to shippers throughout the year. There are winter inconveniences, it is true, but never enough to stop trains entirely. (5) The risk involved in shipping by rail is less than that of shipping by water. 

Railroads are willing to assume practically all of the risks, while special insurance is required by the waterways. (6) Wherever railroads had to meet the competition of waterways, they cut the rates. 

They made up the difference by increasing the rates where they did not have to meet this competition. (7) The railways refused to coöperate with the waterways by making rate agreements with them for through traffic. (8) Railways control shipping points. The control of terminals and transfer facilities

Importance of the Great Lakes. - The 

Great Lakes have furnished a notable exception to this general decline of inland waterways as commerce carriers. This is because they furnish the finest body of interior waterways in the entire world.

The vessels used are of a special type made for the cheap transportation of bulky goods, such as iron ore and grain from the Upper 

Lakes, and coal from the Lower Lakes. 

These boats are among the most efficient carriers in the world. Most of the freight is through cargo which travels a considerable ages are, as a rule, very meager at ports on the Great 

Lakes, as package freight is not an important part of their commerce.

The enormous traffic upon these five great bodies of water is shown by the fact that they carry annually almost one-fourth as much tonnage as all the railroads of the United States taken together. As many cargo tons of goods pass through the Sault Ste. Marie Canals connecting Lake Superior with the other Great Lakes as pass through all the other great canals of the world, including the Suez and Panama canals. A still larger volume of traffic passes through the Detroit River, connecting the Upper Lakes (Superior, Michigan, and Huron) with the Lower Lakes (Erie and Ontario).

Railroads and Domestic Commerce. -- 

The railroad mileage of the United States is around 240,000, a total which stands for about one-third the mileage of the entire world. Railroads carry people and goods to all parts of the country throughout the year. 

The first lines were, of course, very crude, but many improvements have been made with the passing years. The first rails were made of wood. Later they were covered with strap iron. Still later cast-iron rails

Use of Automobiles Stimulates Road 

Building. The enormous increase in the number of automobiles has greatly stimulated road building in this country. It is largely helping to solve our transportation problems. A generation ago, it was a very unusual sight to see an automobile. Now it is our commonest form of transportation. 

To-day, there are over 31,000,000 automobiles, motor trucks, and motor buses in the United States. This is about two-thirds

Wilmington is located on the Delaware River where rail and water transportation meet. In the early days, there was no standard width or gauge. Now the width between the rails has been so well standardized that cars may travel from one railroad to another in any part of the country. 

Watch a passing freight train sometime and note the names of the different railroads as they appear on the cars. A car is often loaded at the terminal of the railroad company which owns it and then sent over the lines of some other railroad in order to reach its destination. In this way, the cars belonging to any one railroad company may be scattered all over the country.

The Part of Roads in Commerce. - Roads have always played an important part, and with the coming of the automobile and motor truck will do so more and more. The first roads were mere Indian trails which followed the river valleys and the general contours of the land. These early Indian trails became traders' routes over which the explorers and pioneers carried their supplies. Later these motor vehicles must have good roads-hence the great development of roads in the United States. Highways now reach every community of any size and are considered prime necessities.

Furthermore, motor transportation has with railroad lines which formerly did not placed many communities in direct touch enjoy that advantage. Motor transportation has thus completed the linking together of all

The United States leads the world in the manufacture and use of airplanes. Young folks today think of the airplane as a commonplace means of transportation; yet many of their elders can remember the time when there were no airplanes. Thus in less than a lifetime, aviation has become one of our most important industries as this great plant at Santa Monica, California, shows. Speedy, comfortable planes go out to all parts of the world from this factory.

Means of Communication an Aid to Commerce. - Mod ern means of communication have also helped to enlarge our home commerce. Business and industry have always relied on rapid and efficient systems of communication. In the past few years their increasing demands have led to still further improvements in this field.

The Airplane Arrives. Now come the airplane and airship! Just as the past generation was born to accept railway transportation as a matter of course, and just as the present generation has accepted the automobile as an everyday vehicle, so the rising generation looks to the skies. It may be that the airplane and airship will have as great an effect on the next generation as the automobile has had upon the present. Many of the readers of this book will be pilots of aircrafts, helping to carry people and goods to all parts of the country.

Can you imagine what the business man would do without the telephone, telegraph, radio, and mail service? There are about 

20,000,000 telephones in use in the United

States, far more than in any other country. 

A letter may be sent from any part of our country to any other part for three cents, though the local rate is two cents. The service is unexcelled and this may be said of all our other methods of communication. 

2. FOREIGN COMMERCE Importance of Foreign Commerce. - - By foreign commerce is meant the trade between merchants in our country and those in other countries as distinguished from trade within this country itself. Our foreign commerce

THE UNITED STATES SEEKS FOREIGN TRADE

LOADING COTTON 

Bales of cotton are being loaded onto this steamer at the State Docks, Mobile, Alabama. Cotton is not only a leading export from Mobile to foreign countries, but is also shipped to the cotton mills in our northern states by way of the sea, as water transportation is cheaper than railroad transportation. is only about one-tenth the size of our domestic trade, but it is immensely important and will become more and more so. Without an export market for our surplus goods, many of our occupational groups would suffer materially. Then, too, our imports contribute in numerous ways to the convenience and comfort of us all. 

Our Exports. The leading item in our export trade is machinery, with an annual value of around $500,000,000. Formerly, cotton was the most valuable export, but as foreign demand for cotton lessened, our southern cotton growers started to grow other products instead of so much cotton. 

Other important exports are wheat and other grains, wheat flour, automobiles and trucks, gasoline and other petroleum products, tobacco, copper, meat products, coal, agricultural machinery, and lumber. There are also many manufactured products not included in these. 

The United States Seeks Foreign Trade. 

Our people aim to increase this export trade. 

We have commercial attachés in almost every country. We have schools of foreign commerce where one may study the languages, customs, needs, and desires of other peoples.

Our manufactured products may now be found in all parts of the world, to such an extent that some one has exclaimed, "The whole world is becoming Americanized!"

This may be an exaggeration, but it certainly contains some truth.

We profit not only from our exports, but from imports also. Our foreign trade con-

As soon as you get up in the morning, foreign trade begins to supply your needs. The 

East Indies contribute vegetable oils to your bath soap; your sponge grew in the tropical waters of the Caribbean Sea; your toothbrush has bristles from the Far East; your toothpaste tube is imported tin; your comb may be made of imported rubber. 

Proceeding to dress, you call upon all parts of the world for your personal adornment.

Silkworms raised in Japan and China furnish the material for your hosiery and tie; your outer clothing contains wool imported from 

Scotland or New Zealand. Your garters owe their elasticity to the rubber plantations of Sumatra, while your linen handkerchief is made of flax from Ireland, Canada, Belgium, or the Netherlands. The bristles of the brushes with which you brush your hair may come from some parts of distant China. 

Hurrying down to breakfast, you will find coffee which has been imported from Brazil, tea which has been sent to you from the Far East, or cocoa which is a product of various tropical countries. To sweeten these beverages you use sugar from Cuba. Your breakfast china may contain English or French clay; the knives, forks, and spoons may contain imported aluminum and tin. You may start the meal with a banana which has been imported from Honduras or with slices of pineapple which have come from Hawaii. 

Leaving your house, you may walk on heels of rubber which has come from Brazil or the Far East over asphalt from Trinidad, and, if the day is wet, wear rubber overshoes

Perhaps you ride in an automobile which depends on rubber for its tires. 

As you look over the morning news, you are holding paper made perhaps in Canada.

On your desk at school are to be found pencils whose lead is made of graphite from 

Mexico, Ceylon, Korea (Chosen), Canada, or Madagascar. The finger grip of your pen is of cork from Spain. The telephone and the dictaphone in the office use imported asphalt, carbon, flax, mica, platinum, nickel, rubber, shellac, silk, and tin in their construction. 

It must be obvious that only a small fraction of the everyday articles, which depend on our import trade, have been touched upon in this sketch. As you walk about your house, travel around by one conveyance or another, as you enjoy your sports, look at the things that surround you, note how many there are which, either wholly or in part, are of foreign origin, and think over what you would do if these essential imports were to be denied you. 

Ocean Shipping. The subject of foreign trade brings us to another form of transportation ocean shipping. To carry on for- 

eign commerce there are two kinds of ocean service. One comprises "chartered" or tramp" vessels which are usually owned by individuals and will go wherever freight is to be carried or picked up. They do not have definite routes or schedules, but are willing to go to all parts of the world which can be reached by water. 

The other consists of "line" carriers, which run on a regular schedule between definite places. (Have you ever heard of the "White 

Star Line,' Cunard Line," "French Line,' "North German Lloyd," and others?) The line traffic is best developed on the most traveled route, the North Atlantic. The main advantages of "line" carriers are regularity and speed. The main advantage of the " tramp" carriers is cheapness. One disadvantage of the "line" vessels is that they must travel on schedule whether or not there is a full load. there is a full load. Much of the freight of the world is carried by tramp ships, but as time goes on, steamship lines are being established more and more. 

The North Atlantic Route. There is an overwhelming concentration of tramp and liner shipping in the North Atlantic. Upon this route a major portion of the entire world's

This route is sometimes called the main artery of the British Empire, but much commerce of the United States also passes through it. 

The Suez Canal was opened in 1869. 

Before that, ships from Europe or Eastern 

North America bound for the Far East had to go around Africa or South America, either of which was a very long route. The Suez route is very much shorter and also much 

SEATTLE 

It is a lock canal, with three step-ups near each end of the route. It has a total length of about 50 miles from deep water in the Atlantic to deep water in the Pacific. The lock chambers have a clear width of 110 feet and a length of 1000 feet, giving handling room for the largest ships. The canal was opened in 

August, 1914.

The Panama Canal now handles about as much traffic as the Suez. Four main shipping routes pass through the canal, connecting the following regions:

103 miles long, 35 feet deep, with a bottom width of 108 feet. Its original cost was $127,000,000. It has become of great importance to trade between Europe and the East.

The Panama Canal. To many people, the most interesting of the world's artificial waterways is the Panama Canal, which crosses the Isthmus of Panama at its narrowest point. It was built by the United States 

Government at a cost of about $400,000,000. 

For shipping between these regions the canal often saves much time and money. It has stimulated the trade of the Gulf seaports of the United States. It is helping to bring North and South America closer together commercially. Furthermore, it enables the United States Atlantic and Pacific fleets to unite quickly on either coast. 

Foreign Communication. This foreign commerce also brings into play another type of service, overseas communication. Submarine telegraphic cables extend to all parts of the world. Long-distance radio telegraphy and telephony make possible quicker business

Some of the boys and girls who read this book will become foreign consuls or commercial agents in other countries. They will have to learn very much more about geography, for there is no other subject so closely related to our vast domestic and foreign commerce.

K. TERRITORIES AND POSSESSIONS OF THE UNITED STATES 

In considering the great size and wealth of our United States, we must not forget our many outlying connections. The United 

States, with her abundance of land and varied resources, has never felt the need of acquiring colonies as have some of the European nations. And yet, for one reason or another, we have taken into our national family numerous islands and other pieces of territory which have a total area of over 700,000 square miles. These are listed in
