1. ALASKA 

Eternal snows and glacial bars the human tide held back 

Till lure of yellow dust revealed the precious veins of black.

Now, barriers melt and thousands see Alaska's smiling face 

And find in this white wilderness a pleasant dwellingplace.

The Purchase of Alaska. Alaska was the first outlying possession obtained by the United States. It is a huge country with gold, copper, and other mineral deposits and .vast forests. Reindeer thrive there, and in some parts it is warm enough in summer for garden vegetables and even wheat to grow. 

Cattle can also be raised in some parts of 

Alaska. 

66 When Alaska was purchased in 1867, very little was known about the interior of this large country. It was looked upon as land of snow and ice," good only for its furbearing animals, and its fisheries. 

William H. Seward, Secretary of State, who arranged the purchase of Alaska from Russia for $7,200,000, was regarded as a very foolish man. The purchase was called "Seward's Folly." But time has proved that 

Seward's purchase was a good investment, for

Alaska has turned out to be very valuable indeed. We do not know even yet the extent of its mineral wealth and its possibilities as a homeland where crops can be grown and farm animals kept.

ALASKA'S BEAUTIFUL SCENERY DISCOVERED

A FINE TEAM OF HUSKIES 

Ewing Galloway

Over the cold, desolate, snow-covered wastes of winter, mush the faithful Eskimo dogs of the Alaskan fur trapper, bred and trained to do their share of work in this country of the far north. They trot in tireless file over the frozen, often unmarked, trails, drawing the dog sled with valuable furs to the nearest trading post- or race over the snows in competition with other dog teams for the sport of their Alaskan owners. 

There are now less than 60,000 people living on this large area, about the population of a small American city. If the land were divided equally among all the people now living there, each one would have about ten square miles of land. Contrast this with Belgium where we should have to place 600 people or more on each square mile. Or contrast it even with the United States, where, if the people were evenly distributed, there would be about 

40 people on each square mile of land.

Why Russia Sold Alaska. - One of the reasons why Russia sold Alaska you can easily determine from the map. It was so far away from the home government in 

Leningrad (formerly St. Petersburg) that it was difficult to govern and protect the colonists.

Consult the globe or a world map and note the longitude of Leningrad and that of central Alaska. 

How many degrees apart are they? Keeping in mind that there was no Trans-Siberian railroad at that time, trace the routes by which government officials would probably go from Leningrad to Alaska.

Another reason was that Russia had so much land in Siberia that she was not particularly interested in keeping Alaska. The country appeared to the Russians to be very much like northern Siberia - a cold, inhospitable waste land, valuable only for its furs. Alaska belonged to Russia for eighty years, during which time fur was all the country yielded. Everything else the Russians tried proved a failure.

Alaska's Beautiful Scenery Discovered. - 

After it was purchased by the United States, Russian America was given the old Indian name Alaska, meaning the "Great Country." 

The flurry of talk over the purchase of Alaska, "Uncle Sam's Ice-Box " as it was nicknamed, died down, and for a number of years Alaska was almost forgotten. 

Finally some adventurous tourists discovered that Alaska had some beautiful scenery, and returned to the United States very enthusiastic over the wonderful sights they had seen high, snow-capped mountains, fiords, and massive glaciers creeping from the mountains down to the very coast. 

---

Newspaper writers visited Alaska and then wrote articles for their papers, telling about the forests, the flowers, and the grasses. 

People began to realize that Alaska was not a land of perpetual snow and ice, and more went there each year, until now fully 10,000 tourists visit it annually.

National Parks in Alaska. - The United 

States government sent explorers and surveyors to Alaska to map the country and determine its resources. Three areas containing some of the finest scenery have been 

Ewing Galloway

RAILROAD ACROSS THE NORTH AMERICAN DIVIDE 

From the southeastern seaport on the Pacific to the city of White Horse on the upper Yukon, one narrow track of gleaming rails winds up through the mountains and crosses the White Pass divide, 2600 feet above the sea. set aside as national parks. One of these is Mt. McKinley National Park. Mt. 

McKinley, which is 20,300 feet high, is the highest peak in North America. Its summit was first reached in 1912. On clear days it may be seen from a distance of 200 miles.

The upper portion of this mountain is covered all the time with snow and ice, from which large glaciers extend in all directions. 

Another national park is known as Glacier 

Bay National Monument, containing seven enormous glaciers, among them Alaska's best known glacier, the Muir. Several of these glaciers extend to the waters of the bay, where huge blocks of ice break off and float away as icebergs.

There are numerous others at the heads of the fiords among the high mountains near the coast. The largest, the Malaspina glacier, which lies on the side of Mt. St. Elias, covers an area of 1500 square miles, far larger than the famous glaciers of Switzerland.

The third national park is called the Katmai 

National Monument. Mt. Katmai is a vol cano which, after lying quiet for many years, suddenly blew its top off in 1912. This was one of the greatest explosions in history. 

Five cubic miles of rock material were blown into the air, leaving a large open space now filled by a lake. Near by is a valley called the "Valley of Ten Thousand Smokes."

This is also included in the park. Before the eruption, it was a green, grassy valley through which led a well-known trail. Now it is covered with volcanic dust, and thousands of openings allow jets of hot steam to escape from the earth. It is possible to hang a kettle over one of these openings and allow the hot vapor to cook your dinner. 

Alaska might be called the "Land of Volcanoes and Glaciers," as there are so many of them in the country. There are over twenty active volcanoes. 

Variety of Land Features and Climates in Alaska. When thinking of Alaska, we must not forget the large size of the country which gives room for a variety of land features as well as climates. The northernmost part is only 18° from the North Pole, while the southern tip is in the same latitude as northern England.

Alaska has its polar region, a land of the midnight sun, where the only permanent inhabitants are Eskimos. But it also has a region tempered by the ocean, where the average winter temperature is no lower than that of New York City or St. Louis, and is several degrees higher than that of Chicago. 

The summer temperature in this region, the

THE CENTRAL REGION OF ALASKA 

southeastern part of Alaska, is much lower than that in the above-named cities in the United States. 

Alaska has still other regions where the temperature may be over 90° in the shade in the summer while it runs as low as 70° below zero in the winter. Green, grass-covered areas, studded with brightly colored wild. flowers in summer, become barren, cold, snow-covered wastes in the winter. 

Alaska has high, rugged mountains in some parts, and low, even swampy, plains in others. On some land farm animals may be grazed and crops produced, but on other land nothing can be grown. There are areas where only grass or moss will grow, while others are heavily forested. 

Ewing Galloway

ALASKAN MARBLE QUARRY 

Down the steps into a quarry cavern of 

Deautiful marble a resource of Alaska just beginning to be used! After the blocks are cut, here at Tokeen, they are shipped to Seattle to be polished.

So when we think of Alaska we must not think of it as being all alike. Different parts of Alaska vary from each other just as different parts of the United States. 

Regions of Alaska. — Alaska may be divided into three regions: (1) a Pacific region, (2) a central region, (3) a polar region. 

(1) The Pacific Region. This is largely a mountainous region with deep valleys and many fiords along the coast. Formerly Alaska was higher above sea level than now. When the sea flowed in over the lower lands, only the higher parts of the mountains near the coast were left above water. This is the cause of the very irregular coast line. Alaska has over 2000 miles of mountainous coast line with fiords. The Alaskan Peninsula and the

Aleutian Islands are simply the higher parts of the Alaska Range, which was partially 277 covered by the ocean. The 

Alaska Range is the northern end of the great Cordilleran system which runs through North and South America.

The Pacific region has a heavy rainfall, as much as 

150 inches or more falling in some parts. On the Alaskan 

Peninsula and the Aleutian 

Islands, the rainfall is not quite so heavy. Fogs are frequent in the coastal regions, and there are fewer clear days than in the interior.

The lower slopes of the mountains in southeastern 

Alaska are heavily forested with evergreen trees, such as spruce, hemlock, and cedar. These dense forests add much to the scenic beauty of the Pacific region. 

Naturally the chief industries are lumbering and fishing. Although there is some good farm land, generally the land is more valuable for growing trees than farm crops. On the 

Aleutian Islands there are no trees, but there is a heavy growth of grass which can be used for grazing farm animals. 

(2) The Central Region. The central region of Alaska is largely the valley of the 

Yukon River and its tributaries. Considerable low land is found here, though the higher plateau areas reach a height of 2000 feet or more. 

The interior of Alaska is a land of extremes in temperature - very cold in winter and very warm in summer. The cold, however, is a "dry" cold like that of the northern interior of the United States. A person does not feel the cold as he would if there were more moisture in the air.

Ordway, Juneau, Alaska

MENDENHALL GLACIER, ALASKA 

Alaska, the land of amazing contrasts. Here we see gay flowers blooming at the foot of a glacier. That is because the summer days are long and warm with sunshine.

There are no glaciers in central Alaska and the rainfall and snowfall are lighter. From about 25 inches near the coast, the rainfall decreases to about 10 inches in the interior. 

Snowfall ranges from 3 to 8 feet, with more snow on the higher than on the lower lands. 

Thick moss and grass cover much of the land, and forests are found along the streams. 

The trees are smaller than those of southern Alaska. They do not grow at all above a height of 3500 feet or so above sea level. 

Wild flowers in abundance beautify the landscape in summer. 

Midsummer days, with the sun shining nearly all of the 24 hours, become uncomfortably warm. This long period of sunshine each day makes plants grow very rapidly. 

So they mature in fewer days than they do farther south. Potatoes, oats and barley, root crops, and garden vegetables are grown in sufficient quantities to supply the people living there. Even wheat can be ripened near 

Fairbanks. Garden vegetables may be grown on the southern slopes as far north as the 

Arctic Circle. 

The Yukon River and its tributaries are the main highways of travel. The Yukon

River is navigable for small river boats as far as Whitehorse, Canada, a distance of 2200 miles. But it is open for navigation only three months. The rest of the year it is frozen. The breaking up of the ice in the 

Yukon River in the late spring is a thrilling sight. (Have you ever seen it shown on the screen?) Some of the smaller streams in 

Alaska freeze solid during the winter season.

The lack of good roads and the hordes of mosquitoes make summer travel in this part of Alaska rather unpleasant. In the winter time, when snow is on the ground and the streams are frozen, reindeer and dog teams are used for transportation. 

(3) The Polar Region. The polar region of Alaska is separated from the central region by the Brooks Mountain Range. This part of the country is mainly a low plateau sloping toward the coast, where it is bordered by a narrow coastal plain. 

A POLAR BEAR 

Ewing Galloway

This polar bear broke a rope with which he had been lassoed and climbed upon an iceberg in the Bering Sea just off the coast of Alaska. Here he was caught unawares by the photographer.

REINDEER IN ALASKA 

The polar region has a very scanty population, mostly Eskimos who live by hunting and fishing.

The polar region is not a good grazing country except perhaps for reindeer, and caribou, which are similar to the reindeer, but smaller in size. Caribou furnish the Eskimos with meat, hides, and sinews, and their antlers are used for making many useful articles. 

Birds, especially wild ducks and geese, are plentiful during the summer. Other birds found here then are robins, eagles, hawks, owls, snipe, loons, gulls, and an occasional swan.

The Discovery of Gold. - 

In 1880 gold was discovered at Juneau, the present capital of Alaska. Americans then had another reason for getting interested in their northern territory. 

279 

Other Minerals in Alaska. Gold is not the only valuable mineral found in Alaska. 

Silver, copper, lead, and tin have all been uncovered and mined. For several years, the value of the copper mined was greater than that of the gold. Other useful metals 

HERD OF ALASKAN REINDEER 

Ewing Galloway

On the large areas of Alaskan land unsuitable for agriculture, herds of reindeer are turned out to graze. They have proved of fourfold value to the 

Eskimo, furnishing him not only meat and milk for food, but also skins for clothing and a means of transportation. 

Later, in 1896, gold was discovered in the valley of the 

Klondike, a tributary of the 

Yukon River in Canada, near the border of

Alaska. This discovery was followed by the famous Klondike gold rush, which resulted in many hardships as well as thrilling experiences on the part of the gold seekers. Three years later rich deposits were found near 

Nome in western Alaska.

Gold is now known to exist in paying quantities in many places in Alaska, but machinery is needed to get it out. Most of the gold that has been taken out has come from alluvial deposits carried down by streams from the lodes or bedrock where the original deposits occur. The value of the gold alone produced up to the present is about fifty times the purchase price of Alaska. are known to exist. Over 100,000 tons of coal are now being mined each year. Petroleum has also been discovered in several places.

Reindeer in Alaska. - The United States government first introduced reindeer from 

Siberia into Alaska in order to give the

Eskimos a means of making a living, as the animals on which they were dependent were getting scarce. A small herd of 171 reindeer in 1892 has now increased to nearly 600,000, most of which belong to the natives. The reindeer furnish not only meat and milk, but also skins for clothing and a means of transportation. In the summer the reindeer feed on mosses, lichens, mushrooms, and grass.

In the winter they live on the moss which they dig out from under the snow.

SEAL ROOKERY 

Every year the bleak, rocky shores of the Pribilof Islands are crowded with the sleek, black seals, who have come there to breed. It is while they are there that it is so easy to catch and kill them. Where are these islands located in respect to Alaska? To whom do they belong?

The reindeer is the chief domestic animal of the Lapps, a people of far-northern Europe.

When the reindeer were imported into Alaska, some Lapp herdsmen were persuaded to come there and teach the Eskimos how to take care of them. 

Reindeer meat, frozen and canned, is now being shipped to the United States. There is room for many more reindeer in Alaska, and perhaps the far north may sometime become an important source of meat supply. 

Furs and Fur Farming. At the time of its purchase, Alaska was thought to be valuable chiefly for its furs. Furs are still one of 

Alaska's most valuable products. Yet their total value is only a fraction of that of the fisheries, and is only about half that of the copper now being mined. 

On the mainland of Alaska, foxes - red, blue, silver, and black, beavers, minks, wolves, and muskrats are some of the more important fur-bearing animals. Now that these animals are getting scarcer, fur-farming is being started. The government has encouraged this industry by leasing large tracts of land to those who wish to raise animals for their furs. 

Fur Seals. One of the most highly prized furs obtained from Alaskan territory is that of the fur seal. So great has been the demand that the animals were in danger of being exterminated when the four countries most interested-Great Britain, Russia, Japan, and the United

States signed a treaty regulating the matter. Each forbade its citizens to kill seals in the sea, and restricted the number that might be killed each year on the land.

The fur seals come mostly 

to the Pribilof Islands, a group of islands in the Pacific Ocean southwest of the mainland of Alaska. The

United States owns these islands, but she agreed to share the profits with the other 

ALASKAN FISH CANNERY 

Ewing Galloway

These fish have been brought into a cannery on the coast of Alaska near Juneau. Fish are abundant in the many inlets along the coast of Alaska, and fishing is naturally one of the leading industries. Large quantities of fish, especially salmon, are canned and shipped both to the United States and to other countries.

Millions of seals visited these islands yearly about the time Alaska was purchased. In 1911, only a remnant were left. Now considerable numbers again visit the islands each year, of which about 50,000 are killed. 

Fisheries. - Fish are 

Alaska's most valuable export. When the Russians owned Alaska, canning fish was not practiced. Although the Russian colonists had all the fish they wanted to eat, they exported only a few salted salmon. 281 The trees in the interior are too small and grow too slowly to be very valuable as a lumber supply. They furnish small timber and also provide fuel for the people who live there. 

KETCHIKAN 

Ewing Galloway

Ketchikan is a modern town of electric lights, banks, churches, schools, and newspapers, in the most southerly part of Alaska. It is Alaska's largest seaport and industrial town and a great fishing center, particularly for salmon and halibut. More salmon is canned in Ketchikan than in any other city of the world. 

Since salmon are by far the most valuable of Alaskan fishery products, catching and canning salmon is the most important part of the fishing industry. Halibut, herring, and cod are also caught and exported. 

Most of the salmon are caught near the mouths of the rivers in the summer. Consequently most of the canneries are situated on the river banks near the coast, and are idle during the winter period. Vessels carrying the necessary workmen and empty cans leave our Pacific ports just before the salmon season, and then return after the catch has been canned.

Timber Resources of Alaska. - Only a small portion of Alaska's timber is being cut each year, and a large part of this is used for making salmon cases. The best timber is found in the mountains of the Pacific region. It is estimated that governmentowned forests could, with proper reforestation, be made to supply us with one-third of the wood pulp needed for our paper industries.

Water Power, Another Alaskan Resource. 

The Pacific Mountains of Alaska possess much water power in their many streams, which are well fed by the heavy rainfall. 

Some of the mining plants and a few of the canneries are making use of this source of power. 

Needs of Alaska. First of all, Alaska needs more good roads and railroads. There are now about 800 miles of railroad lines and about 450 miles of hard roads, but these reach only a small part of the country. 

Also, Alaska needs more men with capital to come in and develop her industries and use more of her resources. Alaska could be made to produce more gold, copper, and coal, and some petroleum.

More fur farms could be established and more reindeer could be raised. It is estimated that the pastures in Alaska would support

between three and four million reindeer.

More farm crops and garden vegetables could be grown if the workers in new industries caused a demand for them. All of these would help to support more people in Alaska. 

A BOW AND ARROW EXPERT 

Ewing Galloway 

Natives of Alaska. When the Russians first took possession of Alaska, they found two native peoples living there, Eskimos and Indians. The Indians in the southern regions were the most civilized. They lived in log houses, and had a well-organized tribal form of government. The Aleuts, who lived on the Aleutian Islands, were also well advanced.

The Eskimos are a docile, peaceful people, skilled in carving and in providing for their needs from the animal life around them. 

The Indians of the interior were the least advanced, and were given to cannibalism.

All of the tribes, Eskimos and Indians, depended on hunting and fishing for a living. 

Through the influence of missionaries and teachers, most of them have been converted to Christianity. 

Education. There are two distinct and separate school systems in Alaska, as follows: (1) Schools for Alaska Indians which are under the direction of the U. S. Office of 

Indian Affairs, through its Alaska Division.

These schools number approximately one hundred and fifty. (2) All other public schools in the Territory are under the direction of the Territorial

This young Eskimo woman is proudly displaying the Department of Education, with the Commisducks she has shot with her bow and arrow. Her home is in northern Alaska, near the Arctic Ocean. Some

Eskimo women are often very expert with the bow and arrow.

How Aviation Is Helping Alaska. — Many companies are now operating airplanes in 

Alaska, carrying both passengers and freight.

The planes are equipped with skis for landing on the snow in cold weather, changing to wheels when the ground is bare. Hydroplanes are used in southeastern Alaska. 

Traveling by airplane costs about the same as traveling by dog sled. However, a journey that would take a week by dog sled can be made in an hour by airplane.

Aviators are also helping to locate the minerals and other resources of Alaska, flying over places which have never been explored. sioner of Education, Juneau, Alaska, at its head. 2. THE PANAMA CANAL 

The Panama Canal, like the Suez Canal, cuts across a narrow isthmus between two continents and saves vessels the long trip around the southern one. As early as 1550, different European nations saw the advantage of such a canal. Spain, Portugal, Holland, and France all considered building it, but the difficulties were too great. Count Ferdinand de Lesseps, who built the Suez Canal, undertook to build the Panama Canal for the 

French. But mismanagement, and the loss of his workers due to yellow fever, forced him to give up. 

The United States Takes Up the Task of 

Building the Canal. In 1902, the United

States paid the French $40,000,000 for their rights to build the canal and the equipment which they had used. Colombia, to whom 

Panama then belonged, could not agree on terms satisfactory to both Panama and the 

United States. So Panama seceded from

Colombia, and its independence was recognized by us. A treaty was then made with 

Panama. It gave the United States control over a strip of territory ten miles wide-five miles on each side of the central line of the canal — and, in addition, the land up to an elevation of 100 feet surrounding Lake

Gatun, which lies along the route of the canal. 

For the use of this territory, the United States paid Panama $10,000,000 and also makes a yearly payment of $250,000. Colombia has been paid $25,000,000 to recompense her for the loss of Panama. Before considering the canal itself, let us learn something about the country through which it passes. 

---

Being less than 10° from the equator, Panama is a true tropical country. The climate is generally hot and moist, with a heavy annual rainfall. The vegetation is luxuriant and there are swarms of insects. 

The temperature averages 80° on both the Atlantic and Pacific coasts, but it is somewhat lower on the higher elevations. A dry 

Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction STEAMSHIP IN MIRAFLORES LOCK 

the soil is fertile and produces abundant crops. 

Forests covering the mountain slopes are one of Panama's most valuable resources. Pearls from Panama have been known since the days of Balboa, and pearl fishing is still a profitable industry.

The government of Panama is modeled after that of the United States, and education is compulsory for all children. Panama wants more people and is offering land at a low price to encourage immigrants to come there and farm.

The Panama Railroad. - The Panama 

Railroad was completed over fifty years before the canal, and, like the canal, was built by Americans. But the Panama Railroad was a private enterprise, while the canal was built by the United States government. 

The discovery of gold has twice influenced the building of a highway across the Isthmus of Panama. In the sixteenth century, after the discovery of gold in Peru, much of the gold and silver was brought to the western coast of Panama by ship and then transported overland to the eastern shore, where it was again placed on board ships and carried to Spain. 

The precious metals moved from the Pacific to the Atlantic, while supplies for the colonists went in the opposite direction.

In 1849, after the discovery of gold in Two locks are used in raising ships to and lowering California, many gold seekers chose the them from Miraflores Lake, which is over fifty feet above the level of the Pacific end of the Panama Canal. Electric locomotives running on tracks on the walls of the locks tow the ships through. season lasts from January to April, while the heaviest rains come in October and November.

The low, swampy coastal lands are most unhealthful, for here are bred the diseasecarrying mosquitoes.

Banana cultivation is the principal agricultural industry. Coconuts are also grown and exported. Other products are cacao, sugar cane, tobacco, rice, corn, and many different kinds of vegetables and fruits. Only a small part of the country is farmed, although Panama route. This led American capitalists to see the necessity for a railroad, which was begun in 1850 and completed five years later. 

Commercially, the railroad was a great success, bringing much commerce to Panama and making millions for the men who built it. 

It was repaired, double-tracked for most of the way, and used for transporting supplies and equipment during the building of the canal. It is now controlled and operated by the United States government. 

Cleaning up the Canal Zone. One of the first things that had to be done before building the canal was to make the Canal Zone a

DIGGING THE PANAMA CANAL GAILLARD CUT IN THE MAKING Panama Canal 

Men and machines are here shown digging the Gaillard Cut, which was the most difficult task in the construction of the Panama Canal. The cut is nine miles long. Numerous slides in the soft volcanic rock gave the engineers much trouble, and made it necessary for them to remove twice as much rock as was originally estimated. healthful place for the workers to live.

Colonel Gorgas, who did such good work in cleaning up Havana after Cuba came into our possession, was selected to oversee this work. 

Three diseases were most troublesome: (1) a plague carried by rats which came to shore from infested ships; (2) malaria, carried by mosquitoes; (3) yellow fever, also carried by mosquitoes.

To get rid of the rats, ships were carefully inspected and the rats killed. Tons of poison were placed where rats could get at it, and men were hired to kill rats in all ways possible.

To get rid of the mosquitoes was a much bigger task. Ditches were dug and swamps drained; jungles were cut down and burned; liberal quantities of oil were used on pools of stagnant water; houses and even rain barrels were carefully screened; the sick in the hospitals were isolated, and screens placed so that no mosquitoes could reach them.

A general clean-up was carried on in the cities. Rubbish and refuse were carted 285 away, sewers were installed and streets paved, and a plentiful supply of pure water was provided. 

Millions of dollars were spent in this health work, but 

Colonel Gorgas conquered yellow fever and malaria, and made the Canal Zone a safe place for workmen to live. 

The healthfulness of the

Canal Zone to-day as compared with some of the other

Caribbean lands is a tribute to the good work of Colonel 

Gorgas and his efficient helpers. It proves that tropical regions can be made healthful places for white men to live.

Digging the Canal.-About two years were spent in putting the railroad in good running order, and in making other preparations, before actual digging began. Two features of the canal added to the difficulties.

Cities in the Canal Zone. 

-The two older cities in the Canal Zone, Panama and

Colon, are under the rule of 

Panama, except that the 

United States has charge of sanitary and health affairs. 

Panama, on the Pacific side of the Isthmus, very early became one of the best built and wealthiest cities in the New World. It was sacked and destroyed in 1671 by the English buccaneer Morgan, who carried off much treasure. Afterwards, the city was rebuilt about six miles away on a location where it could be defended more easily. From a city which was formerly dreaded as a pest hole, it has become a busy commercial and tourist center. It contains many attractive buildings.

Ewing Galloway

HARBOR OF ST. THOMAS 

St. Thomas is the principal seaport of the Virgin Islands. The mountains make a beautiful setting for this tropical city built along the curving shore of one of the finest harbors in the West Indies. paved streets, parks, and well-constructed St. John, are large enough to be of much importance and to be generally known by their names. buildings.

The United States has built two ports of entry for the canal - Cristobal on the 

Atlantic end, and Balboa on the Pacific.

Both of these ports are well provided with piers and facilities for loading and unloading vessels, docks for repairing ships, repair and machine shops, and coaling and oil stations for supplying fuel. 

3. THE VIRGIN ISLANDS Why the United States Purchased the Virgin Islands. - The Virgin Islands are a group of some fifty small islands lying about forty miles east of Puerto Rico. They were discovered by Columbus, who was amazed at the great number of the islands. Only three of them, St. Croix, St. Thomas, and

The Virgin Islands, formerly known as the 

Danish West Indies, were purchased by the 

United States from Denmark in 1917 for $25,000,000, because they were on the direct line between European ports and the Panama Canal, and between the Atlantic ports of North and South America. They are also near Puerto 

Rico, another possession of the United States.

One of the islands, St. Thomas, has on its south side an unusually fine harbor where the United States maintains a naval station, which is also an important coaling and oil station. The Navy has radio stations here and on St. Croix. 

The Islands and the People. 

On a narrow lowland at the head of the bay which forms

the fine harbor of St. Thomas, is the town of 

St. Thomas, the capital (formerly Charlotte 

Amalie). Fully seven-eighths of the people of St. Thomas live in this town. The rest are scattered on estates all over the island. 

The houses are mostly one- or two-story with red-tiled roofs, from which rain water is collected.

ST. CROIX, VIRGIN ISLANDS 

A bull team is used to distribute cane seed for planting. driver guides his team. Yoked by their horns, the animals obedient.

St. Thomas appears to be the top of a former mountain range now almost entirely covered by the sea. A high range of hills forms the backbone of the island. From them, others of the Virgin Islands and also Puerto Rico can be plainly seen. The island is about 13 miles long and two miles wide. 

Formerly it was covered with forests, but they have been cut down. The soil is fertile, and most of the foodstuffs except cereals could be raised, but the people have neglected agriculture for shipping. The island has long been famous for the production of bay rum.

St. John, a small island with less than a thousand people, lies about four miles east of St. Thomas. It also has a good harbor, but there is no settlement of any size. 

St. Croix, which lies about 40 miles southeast of St. Thomas, is the largest and most thickly populated of the three main islands.

Over half of the people of the Virgin Islands live here.

The northern part of the island is mountainous, but the southern part consists of fertile plains and low rolling country where agriculture can be carried on very successfully. Good roads 

Triangle Photo Service 

Notice the way the seem tractable and make communication easy.

There are large sugar estates, and a little sea island cotton is produced. There are two towns, one of which was formerly the seat of the 

Danish colonial government. 

Though both of these towns are on the seacoast, neither has a good harbor. Both cargoes and passengers have to be taken off the ocean steamers and conveyed to land in lighters and small boats.

The United States govern ment maintains an Agricultural Experiment Station on St. Croix and is showing the people of this and the other islands how to farm more successfully. The growing of garden vegetables, such as Bermuda onions, peppers, eggplants, and tomatoes, is being especially encouraged. 

Each school has its garden club, and the boys and girls are given seed of the best varieties of vegetables to be planted in the home gardens.

The people of the Virgin Islands are mostly Negroes or mulattos, less than ten per cent being white. Under the rule of the United

States, education is compulsory. They are citizens of the United States, and many of them have taken advantage of this fact by coming to our country, where normally they have better opportunities to earn a living.

This new-old city, on a tiny coral island, is Puerto Rico's capital and chief seaport. The old city is surrounded by a picturesque wall, and is famous for Morro Castle; the new city is very modern, with attractive buildings and wellpaved roads. 

4. PUERTO RICO 

Puerto Rico as a Spanish Possession. 

Puerto Rico was another of the Spanish colonies which became United States possessions at the close of the Spanish-American War in 1898. It is the smallest and the farthest east of the four large West Indian islands known as the Greater Antilles. 

What are the other three islands of the Greater 

Antilles? Notice the chain of islands curving generally southward from a point east of Puerto Rico toward the mainland of South America. These islands are called the Lesser Antilles. The northerly group of the Lesser Antilles are called the Leeward Islands, and the southerly group are known as the 

Windward Islands. The latter group extend so near the coast of South America that it is very easy to pass from one to the other.

Columbus was the first to visit Puerto Rico. 

He found the island inhabited by a peaceful agricultural tribe of Indians. This tribe was given the name of Borinquen. It was quite different from the warlike tribes who inhabited the Windward Islands, and were known as Caribs.

The Puerto Rican tribes and also those who inhabited the Lesser Antilles all originally came from the Orinoco Basin of South America. Those who went to the Lesser Antilles were subject to frequent attacks from the mainland, and the tribes from one island went out to prey upon the tribes of other islands. 

They had no chance to develop any culture of their own. 

Puerto Rico, however, was farther away from South America. It had fertile soil and a healthful climate. So the early invaders

settled down in their new homeland, raised crops, and acquired considerable skill in making different kinds of tools from stone and wood.

They traveled about in canoes generally made of logs hollowed out by fire. They were equipped with oars and sometimes sails, 111 

NEW CAPITOL OF PUERTO RICO 

Caribs from the other islands then came to 

Puerto Rico. Though they were good fighters, they were no match for the trained Spanish soldiers, and were quickly subdued. Negro slaves from Africa were introduced as early as 1510. The Puerto Ricans of to-day are largely descendants of the Spanish settlers, San Juan, the capital, is situated on a coral islet off the northern coast of Puerto Rico and is connected with the main island by bridges and a causeway Here, at the seat of the old Spanish government, are located the federal buildings. 

The governor of Puerto Rico is appointed by the President of the United States, but representatives elected by the people make their laws. Sessions of the legislature are held in this beautiful new building which enabled them to travel about very swiftly.

The houses of these early people were made of fibers tied together and covered with bark, while the roofs were thatched with straw. 

The houses were raised on posts to avoid floods and also insects such as ants, which have been a real plague in this country at times. Coconut shells provided dishes for household use, though some of the Caribs along the coast made rude vessels of clay. 

The Spaniards took possession of Puerto Rico shortly after 1500. They forced the 

Borinquen to work so hard and treated them so harshly that in less than a hundred years none of them were left on the island. the original Indian inhabitants, and the Negroes. 

You will notice that most of the Lesser Antilles belong either to the English or to the 

French. The English made several attempts to get possession of Puerto Rico, but, together with Cuba, it remained in the possession of 

Spain until 1898.

Labor Conditions in Puerto 

Rico.-Puerto Rico was never very prosperous under Spanish rule, and though the United States has done much to better conditions since 

1898, many of the people are still very poor. A single crop failure means much suffering, unless outside help is given. 

With a small area, Puerto Rico has a population of about 1,869,245, over 525 people to the square mile. It is over ten times as densely populated as the United States. As most of the people are dependent on agriculture, it is hard for them to find work and make a living. In most densely populated countries, many of the people earn their living in the manufacturing industries.

The manufacture of sugar and cigars, fine needlework, and the making of clothing for men and women are the chief industries. 

Wages are low and there is not enough work for everybody. Several large American companies are considering locating their industries in Puerto Rico.

THE CLIMATE OF PUERTO RICO 

Education. In 1899, there was one school building in Puerto Rico. Twelve years later, over a thousand had been built throughout the country. This shows the great advance that has been made in popular education under United States government. The majority of boys and girls now attend the public schools. There is also a large enrollment in the University of Puerto Rico. Twenty-five years ago, only 17 per cent of the people. could read and write; now more than 60 per cent can do so. 

Government of Puerto Rico. The Gov ernor of Puerto Rico is appointed by the President of the United States. The legislative body, consisting of a Senate and House of 

Representatives, is elected by the direct vote of the people. The people of Puerto Rico are citizens of the United States. The island itself is a United States possession.

The Land of Puerto Rico. - Puerto Rico, and also the Lesser Antilles, are part of a great mountain chain that has been built up from the bottom of the sea by volcanic action. 

North of Puerto Rico is Brownson Deep, where the sea reaches a depth of 24,000 feet, one of the deepest spots known. South is Tanner 

Deep. We may think of Puerto Rico as the top part of a high mountain submerged in the ocean.

A mountainous ridge running generally east and west divides the island into a northern and a southern section. Along the shore in many places are very flat areas, called playas, which have been built up of alluvial material. Much of the soil is of a sticky, clayey nature, not easily penetrated by water. 

This hinders the rainfall from sinking into the ground. Yet it also helps to keep the soil from being washed off the cultivated slopes. 

There are many short rivers in Puerto Rico.

In the northern part is a limestone rock region. 

The rivers here sometimes disappear, after coming down from the highlands, and then reappear several miles nearer the sea. A

The Climate of Puerto Rico. - Puerto Rico has a tropical climate. This means that it is warm most of the time, with no great differences between summer and winter temperatures. The heat is tempered by the constant

northeast trade winds, and sunstroke is tobacco, but the industry did not flourish until almost unknown.

The northeast trade winds bring abundance of rain to the northern and eastern slopes.

TOBACCO IN PUERTO RICO 

American cigar manufacturers took charge. 

They established factories where the cigars and other forms of tobacco could be made in a sanitary manner, and they secured a market through advertising.

Coffee is third in importance among Puerto 

Rican crops. It was at one time the leading crop, and large quantities were sold in 

Spain and other European countries. The central mountainous district of Puerto Rico is the coffee district. Fertile soil and almost daily rainfall here furnish nearly ideal conditions for growing coffee. 

Puerto Rican coffee has a rather strong flavor which many Americans do not like, so when it is sold in the United States, it is usually blended with other coffees. 

Some Puerto Rican coffee goes to Cuba, but most of that exported is sent to Europe.

The people of Puerto Rico themselves use large quantities. They consume as much per person as the people in the United States. Among the low hills of Puerto Rico are spread out the irrigated, well-caredfor tobacco fields. The houses are the little cottages of the native farm workers. Why do you think part of the field is covered with cheesecloth ? 

The tobacco industry does not stop with growing the tobacco. Many millions of cigars and cigarettes are manufactured each year in the island tobacco houses. been known to have no rainfall for a whole year.

Agricultural Crops. -Sugar is Puerto Rico's most valuable crop. The sugar cane is grown on the flood plains of the lower valleys and the flat lands near the coast. More land is devoted to growing sugar cane and more people are employed in producing sugar than any other product. The value of the sugar exported is about two-thirds of all the exports. 

You can see from these facts how dependent the people are on this one crop. Much of the sugar cane is grown on irrigated land, which gives a large yield. 

Considerable fruit is also grown in Puerto 

Rico. Grapefruit and pineapples are the chief fruits exported, but oranges and bananas are grown too. 

Rice, codfish, and beans, all of which are imported, form the chief food of the Puerto 

Ricans. Formerly sweet potatoes, yams, bananas, corn, and other vegetables were produced in large quantities for home use. 

But since the foreign demand for the other crops has increased so much, the farmers find it more profitable to grow those, and then buy the food products they need for their own use. (Do you think that it would be

Problems of Puerto Rico. Lack of suffi cient education and the poverty of the greater part of the people are the two big problems of Puerto Rico. As we have seen, schools are being established, and as much money is being spent on educating the young people as the government can afford. Perhaps, after more of the people are educated, they will know better how to overcome their poverty. There is land not now being used which could be cultivated and made to produce food crops. ARMISTICE DAY IN PUERTO RICO 

A parade of Camp Fire girls through the narrow, Old World streets of San Juan. Thus the new order takes its place in the life of to-day. 

Puerto Ricans need to learn how to use their land to the best advantage to get larger crops.

Many of the people lack ambition. Consequently they will not do any more work than they must do in order to get a bare living. 

Much of this lack of ambition is due to disease and the effects of the hookworm. Physicians are now working among the people and doing what they can to overcome the hookworm disease. Much of it could be avoided if the people were more sanitary and wore shoes. But most of them are too poor to buy shoes, so they go barefoot. This gives the hookworms a chance to enter their feet. (Do you know what is meant by the hookworm disease? Some member of your class should give a special report on this scourge of warm climates.) 

5. HAWAII A SONG TO HAWAII

-J. D. REDDING 

Uncle Sam's Pacific Paradise. Away out in the Pacific Ocean, about 2000 miles from San Francisco, is a group of hundreds of small islands. They are known as the 

Hawaiian Islands, or simply Hawaii, which is the name of the largest island. Only eight of the islands are inhabited. 

Owing to its delightful climate and beautiful scenery, Hawaii is becoming very popular with tourists. Being situated within the Tropical Zone and surrounded by the ocean, the islands have a mild climate with little change from month to month.

So Hawaii is an all-year-round playground, where visitors may go at any time and enjoy the outdoor recreations. Some enthusiasts have found Hawaii so delightful that they have called it a paradise. 

Origin of the Hawaiian Islands. Like 

Puerto Rico, the Hawaiian Islands are the tops of volcanic mountains which have been built up in the midst of the sea. Hawaii, the largest, is made up of four volcanic mountains. Two of these, Mauna Loa and Kilauea, are among the world's most famous active volcanoes.

The lava lake in the crater of Kilauea is one of the most wonderful sights in the world.

Here the molten rock keeps boiling and bubbling constantly, sometimes splashing high into the air. The main crater of Kilauea is about two and one-half miles in diameter, but the open pit containing the fiery lava is only about 3000 feet across. Sometimes the

ROYAL HAWAIIAN HOTEL Publishers' Photo Service 

The pale, coral walls, warm sands on a sunlit beach, blue waters, the promise of moonlight and the dreamy harmony of Hawaiian singers-all these to lure the holiday visitor into staying forever. lava sinks far down into the pit, leaving a steaming, smoking hole perhaps 1500 or 2000 feet deep. When this occurs, portions of the sides break off and fall with a roar into the hot mass below, sending up clouds of reddish dust.

Mauna Loa is active only once in about ten years, yet its crater is constantly steaming as if it were ready to break forth at any time. Strange as it may seem, snow lies throughout the year on the top of this mountain, and also on Mauna Kea, which has not been active for 200 years. Both of these mountains are over 13,000 feet high. Their height impresses one all the more because they rise directly from the sea. 

Haleakala, called by the Hawaiians the "House of the Sun," is one of the largest inactive volcanoes in the world. Its summit is over 10,000 feet high. The crater is 20 miles in circumference while the steep inside walls descend a mile or more to the lava floor. 

Numerous cinder cones rise from the bottom of the crater. These, together with the many peculiar shapes taken by the cooling lava, make it a scene one never forgets.

Kilauea and Mauna Loa, together with Haleakala, have been set aside as national parks. It is possible to drive in an automobile almost to the brink of the large crater of Kilauea, and then walk across the hardened lava to the fire pit. 

Discovery of the Hawaiian Islands. The 

Hawaiian Islands were rediscovered in 1778 by Captain Cook, an Englishman, who lost his life there in a fight with the natives the following year. We say rediscovered, because the islands had been seen by an English explorer many years before. Captain Cook called them the Sandwich Islands in honor of his friend, the Earl of Sandwich, in England, and they were known by this name until late in the nineteenth century. 

Annexation of Hawaii by the United States. 

When first discovered, each of the larger islands of Hawaii had a separate king, but in 1790 they were all united under one king. 

In 1894, the people became so dissatisfied

THE PEOPLE OF HAWAII 

295 that a revolution, led by white immigrants, took place.

The queen was deposed, and a republic proclaimed. They then asked to become a part of the United States, but arrangements for annexation were not completed until 

1898. In 1900 a territorial form of government was established, and the official name is now the "Territory of Hawaii."

Courtesy Hawaiian Tourist Bureau RIDING THE SURF 

What fun! Surfriding at Waikiki Beach, Honolulu, is a thrilling sport.

When the surf is high, the experts skim over the water with the speed of an automobile, but the amateur is very likely to receive a sudden ducking. 

The People of Hawaii. - 

The present population of 

Hawaii consists largely of Asiatic peoples who have emigrated to the islands in recent times. Japanese and 

Chinese make up a little over half of the population. Other nations represented are the Portuguese, Spanish, Filipinos, and Koreans. Out of a population of a little over 400,000, only about one-sixth are native Hawaiians. been a tall, strong, and handsome people. 

But intermarriage with other races inhabiting islands in the Pacific has changed their physical appearance. Most of the present native

Hawaiians are rather short in stature, with The original Hawaiians are said to have dark skins and large, expressive eyes. 

Publishers' Photo Service THREE HAWAIIAN BELLES 

These Hawaiian girls, dressed in their native costumes, are preparing some of their national dishes. Notice the wreaths of flowers adorning their heads. Hawaiians love the flowers which grow so abundantly on their islands. 

They are a very capable people, and had developed an orderly government recognized by other nations before they sought union with the United States. Boys and girls were compelled to go to school, and English as well as the native Hawaiian languages were taught in all the public schools. The people paid taxes for the support of the government, schools, public roads and buildings, and other improvements. Their agricultural industries were well developed, and they carried on a thriving commerce with other countries. 

With the mild climate and many good beaches favoring water sports, the Hawaiians are naturally at home in the water. Many of them have become skilled in diving, swimming, and in riding the waves on surfboards.

FLOWER GIRLS 

James Sawders

To the tourists in the Islands, the line of Hawaiian girls is a familiar sight. 

For from them one buys the leis - those flower wreaths of multi-colored hibiscus, frangipani, and ginger blossoms that, cast into the seas as the boat steams out of the harbor, float back to Hawaiian shores as an earnest of one's own return.

Modernized Hawaii. - Honolulu, the cap ital, is very much like a modern American city. It has paved streets, electric lights, street cars and automobiles to take you about, while public parks and hotels offer you every comfort and convenience that you would find at home. Roof gardens, theaters, golf links, bathing beaches, and dancing in the openall of these amusements are available to the visitor.

Railroad lines, supplemented by automobile roads, enable one to travel in ease and comfort around all the larger islands to see the volcanoes and other sights. Airplane trips between the four main islands are being planned. 

Hawaii at the Crossroads of the Pacific. 

Notice the position of the Hawaiian Islands in relation to the continents of Asia and Australia, and North and South America. They are so situated that they are a convenient stopping place for many of the vessels crossing between these eastern and western continents. Hence they are said to be at the crossroads of the Pacific, where they are a most convenient coaling station. 

The Honolulu port has been dredged and enlarged so that vessels of four transpacific steamship lines now stop regularly at Honolulu.

In addition to this service, vessels run regularly between our Pacific coast and 

Hawaii, and also between our 

Atlantic coast and Hawaii, by way of the Panama Canal.

Round-the-world tourist ships also stop there.

Hawaii also has regular air service. Airplanes flying between San Francisco and 

Hong Kong stop at Honolulu on their weekly trips across the Pacific.

Climate and Soil. - The Hawaiian Islands are situated in the northeast trade wind belt, so these mountainous islands have ample rainfall throughout the year, especially on their northeastern slopes. Where rainfall is

INDUSTRIES AND TRADE OF HAWAII 

297 lacking on the southwestern slopes, it is often Philippines, Puerto Rico, Portugal, and other possible to irrigate.

The lava thrown out from volcanoes weathers into very rich soil that contains many of the elements needed by growing plants. This is why Hawaii has such fertile soil.

The tropical vegetation grows luxuriantly. Forests of tree ferns with their beautiful foliage and many odd plants and flowers add to the attractions of Hawaii. Some one has likened the fiery volcano of Kilauea looming among the tropical vegetation to a ruby set in an emerald bower of tropical grandeur. countries. 

Pineapples are the second largest crop in Hawaii. Most of them are canned, but some of the green fruit is shipped to the 

A PINEAPPLE CANNERY 

H. Armstrong Roberts 

A twirl, a twist, and the juicy pineapples, skinless and eyeless, are moved along, one more step toward the canned product. 

Have you ever seen the night-blooming cereus? These plants open their blossoms only at night. They grow outdoors in Hawaii, and their creamy white blossoms may be seen at night several times during the year.

Industries and Trade of Hawaii. - Each year Hawaii sends out many millions of dollars' worth of products. Sugar heads the list, its value being more than that of all the other products combined. The tropical climate, steady rainfall, and remarkably fertile soil together give Hawaiian sugar plantations the largest yield per acre of any in the world.

Practically the entire crop in the form of raw sugar is shipped to the western coast of the United States, where it is refined and put on the market. The work of raising the sugar cane and extracting the raw sugar is done in the most scientific manner. At first Chinese and then Japanese came to the islands in large numbers to work on the sugar plantations, but later their emigration to Hawaii was forbidden, and laborers now come from the 

United States. Juice and peelings are used to make alcohol and as feed for the farm animals. Several million cases of canned pineapples are shipped to the United States each year. 

Some bananas are also sent to the United 

States from Hawaii. However, it is easier to get bananas from Central America, so most of our bananas come from there. Hawaii also grows good coffee, but as we are accustomed to the coffee we get from Brazil and Colombia, there is little demand for Kona coffee, as the 

Hawaiian coffee is called. Both coffee and bananas could be produced in much larger quantities in Hawaii, if there were a market for them in this country. 

A variety of fruits and vegetables are grown for home use. American visitors do not always like the Hawaiian vegetables, so some vegetables are imported from the United 

States. Considerable rice is raised, but more has to be imported to supply the people.

There is very little manufacturing carried on in Hawaii, though an effort is now being made to start small factories to give work to people who can find none in the fields. 

The native Hawaiians are as a rule musical, and musical instruments are one of their exports.

The government is also encouraging the tourist industry. Hawaii has many attractions for tourists. If more people would visit the country, the business of caring for them would add to the income of the Hawaiian people. 

The Midway Islands. The Midway 

Islands, which are really a part of the Hawaiian group, are about 1200 miles to the northwest. Their name suggests their location, which is midway between Asia and America.

Note that they lie just east of the Inter national Date Line. 

This group consists of two larger islands, each less than two miles long, and two islets, all surrounded by an atoll. There are no large native animals, the animal life consisting of sea birds, which come here to breed, fish, turtles, and crabs.

The only people who live on the Midway 

Islands are the employés of the cable stations. 

6. AMERICAN SAMOA Location of Samoa. American Samoa 

American Samoa consists of seven small islands which came into the possession of the United States in 1899 by virtue of a treaty with Great Britain and Germany. The latter took possession of the remainder of the group.

These islands lie about midway between 

San Francisco and Manila. They are also about midway between Sydney, Australia, and Hawaii. The nearest port of any size is 

Auckland, New Zealand, about 1500 miles distant. The total area of the seven islands included in American Samoa is only 58 square miles.

Pago Pago, the Capital. Pago Pago on the island of Tutuila is the capital. Pago 

Pago has a beautiful harbor, the finest in the 

South Pacific. Tutuila is a volcanic island, and mountains rise on each side of the harbor. 

The water in the harbor is so deep that it is sometimes difficult to pull up the anchors.

Steamers can come close to the shore with no danger of going aground.

Pago Pago was first ceded to the United States by the native chief in 1872 for a naval and coaling station. You can see from its location that it is a good stopping place on the long route across the Pacific. It is valuable not only as a naval and coaling station, but as a trade center for the United States in this part of the Pacific. 

The naval authorities maintain a hospital at 

Pago Pago and look after the health of the people. Earthquakes and hurricanes are two unpleasant features of Samoa and other tropical volcanic islands in the Pacific. Hurricanes are sometimes very destructive and even destroy the roads. In times of trouble like this, the United States has to help the people, but generally they are self-supporting and carry on their government without assistance.

The Natives of Samoa. - The natives of 

Samoa are among the most intelligent of the Polynesian groups which inhabit the Pacific islands. All of them can read and write, and all are Christians. No foreigners are allowed to hold land in American Samoa, so this keeps the land for the native Samoans. 

Government. There are three political divisions in American Samoa, each ruled by a native governor appointed by the American Commandant-Governor. The native governors appoint county chiefs who in turn appoint village chiefs. Thus the government is placed almost entirely in the hands of the natives.

Agricultural Products. - Tropical fruits of many kinds are grown in abundance.

AIRWAYS OF THE PACIFIC 

Courtesy Pan American Airways

This map is a story of adventure and conquest. It is a story of man's conquest of the air. 

Those pathways across the Pacific are highways of the sky. Swift clippers, riding the wings of the wind, speed over that wide blue ocean to the Land of the Rising Sun, some to the Orient, some to the ports down under. 

Day after day they wing their tireless way with their cargoes of friendship and good will. Perhaps, if you watch the map closely, you can see them flying along and may dream of the day when you, too, shall ride the wings of the wind over those shining waters.

7. GUAM Guam a Volcanic Island. - Guam is the largest and most southerly of a group of islands known as the Marianas Islands.

These are a group of volcanic islands lying north and south along the 145th meridian east of Greenwich. The only reminder of former volcanoes in Guam are the hot springs. 

Coral reefs border the island in places, the largest being along the southwest coast.

Guam under United States Control. 

The United States took possession of Guam during the war with Spain. It is about 30 miles long with an estimated area of 225 square miles. It is over 5000 miles from 

San Francisco and about 1500 miles from Manila.

Guam is under the control of the United States Navy, and a naval station and hospital are maintained at the capital, Agana. The island is important to the United States as a cable station and as a naval and coaling station. A powerful radio station has also been built there. The chief port is Piti, about five miles from Agana. No foreign vessel is allowed to enter this port except by special permission from the United States government. 

Agana, the Capital of Guam. — Agana, the capital, is an attractive town with about 9500 people. It is built on a low level area at the foot of a steep bluff. There are no sidewalks, but the streets are kept very clean.

It has a good water supply, sewers, electric lights, and other modern improvements.

Limestone and coral rock furnish building material. 

Education. The native people of Guam are descendants of the former Malays and 

Spaniards and others who came later. They call themselves Chamorros. They use their own language, but English is taught to all children in the schools. Education is free and compulsory. When the Americans first took charge, the little girls wore long dresses with trains. These are now short, and girls take part in games just as the boys do. 

Guam had no ani 

Domestic Animals. 

mals when it was first settled by the white man. Now water buffaloes (carabaos), cattle, horses, mules, pigs, goats, cats, and dogs have all been introduced.

The water buffalo, or carabao, is used for carrying burdens, drawing carts, and plowing for rice as in the Philippines. Cows are also trained to carry and draw loads. It is a common sight to see a sleek cow, very much like a Jersey, trotting along as swiftly as a horse with her calf galloping at her side. 

Agricultural Products. Rice and corr (maize) are both grown, but additional rice has to be imported. Sweet potatoes, yams. plantains, breadfruit, and manioc (tapioca) are important substitutes for cereals. 

Oranges, lemons, bananas, coffee, cacao, and tobacco are other products. Copra (dried coconut) is the chief export. Coconut oil is used by the natives in place of butter and lard in cooking. 

8. THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS Distance of the Philippines. The Philip pines are the most distant of the United

States possessions. Manila, the capital of the 

Philippines, is over 6000 miles from San 

Francisco, the nearest port on our western coast. From the eastern coast of the United 

States, the Philippines are just about half way around the world, the route from New York to Manila by way of the Suez Canal being less than 200 miles longer than the route by way of the Panama Canal. 

Similarity between the Philippines and the 

Hawaiian Islands. Like the Hawaiian 

Islands, the Philippine Islands are of volcanic origin. There are about fifty volcanoes in

MANILA HARBOR Courtesy Holley Institute of Visual Instruction 

From the battlements of old Fort Santiago one looks over the harbor of Manila, the chief commercial center for the Philippines, where the Pasig River meets Manila Bay. these islands, twenty of which are more or less well adapted to the growth of all kinds of active. Earthquakes are frequent. tropical products. There are but two seasons, the wet and the dry. These are most noticeable in the western parts of the northern islands where most of the rain falls during the northern summer. The eastern sides of the southern islands have a heavy rainfall, with more rainfall during the northern winter.

Over 7000 islands are included in the Philippines, but less than 500 of them have an area of one square mile or more. The two largest islands are Luzon in the northern part of the group, and Mindanao in the south. 

All of the larger islands are mountainous and there is much beautiful scenery. The irregular coast line affords many good harbors. 

The islands have a combined coast line of over 11,000 miles. This is longer than that of the United States.

Fertile, well-watered plains, lying between the mountains and the coast, provide excellent land for farming or for growing plantation crops such as sugar cane, tobacco, and rubber. 

Climate. As the Philippines lie wholly within the tropics, they have a warm climate 

The People of the Philippines. - There are a great many different tribes and many native languages in the Philippines. The differences in race, language, and religion have been great hindrances in bringing these people together under one government. 

In possession of the large island of Mindanao and the Sulu Islands are the Moros, a

Malayan people who were at one time converted to Mohammedanism. The greater part of the population consists of a number of tribes and people of mixed blood known col-

EDUCATION IN THE PHILIPPINES 

lectively as the Filipinos. Most of these are 

Christians. Political leaders generally belong to this group, which comprises some of the most advanced native people in these islands.

The United States and the Philippines. - 

The Philippines were discovered in 1521 by the Spanish explorer, Magellan, and later became Spanish colonies. At the close of the 

Spanish-American War, the Philippines passed into the possession of the United States.

American statesmen knew that the people of the Philippines were not ready for selfgovernment and they felt that it was the duty of the United States to keep control over the country until the people could be educated and the leaders taught through experience how to carry on the government. 301 

H. Armstrong Roberts

FILIPINO HOMES 

These flimsy houses on stilts would not survive in the windstorms which occur in most parts of the United tion against the weather. Of what do these homes appear to be built? Why are they built so high above the ground and left open underneath?

In 1935, Congress passed an act granting States, but the Filipino does not need very much protecconditional independence to the Philippines, their independence to be recognized after ten years of successful self-government under the guidance and protection of the United

States Government. A constitution was drawn up and, according to its provisions, the Filipinos now elect their president and other officers and also the members of a National Assembly to make laws for the islands. 

Education. One of the foremost tasks of the United States in the Philippines has been the education of the people. One thousand 

American school teachers were imported.

Public schools have been established as fast as possible and since there was no common language, the pupils are being instructed in English. Most of the boys and girls of school age are now in the public schools, where they are taught not only to read and write but, also, to work with their hands and make useful articles. They are being trained in taking care of gardens and in doing farm work.

A SCHOOL Drill 

Ewing Galloway

Here is a physical education drill by school children in Manila. Wonderful strides in education have been made in the Philippines under the control of the United States. 

Bodies as well as minds are being trained in the public schools.

Physical education is also an important part of their training.

There are many well-educated and highly cultured women as well as men in the Philip pines. When a Filipino Commissioner comes to the United States to represent his country, his children who have been attending the public schools in Manila may go right into their grades in the public schools of Washington, D. C.

The United States has also done much to educate the Filipinos in sanitary ways of living and to prevent the spread of disease. 

Streets in the cities have been paved, sewers installed, and good, pure water supplied to the people. 

Money Crops. Sugar is the most important crop grown for export from the Philippines. These islands send to the United

States each year over a billion pounds of sugar, or just about enough to give ten pounds to each person.

Coconut products are next in value among 

Philippine exports. Copra (dried coconut), copra meal or cake, shredded coconut and coconut oil all derived from the coconut are exported. Coconut oil is much in de- 

mand in America and Europe for the manufacture of margarine, soap, and candles.

The natives use it largely for cooking purposes. Coconut palms grow well in the

Philippines, and many plantations have been started. The trees start to bear when they are six or seven years old, and, if carefully cultivated, they will average 200 coconuts to a tree for many years. The fiber of the husk surrounding the coconut is used in weaving mats, and in making a strong twine. 

Another much-prized product is abaca or Manila hemp. This is not a true hemp but is the strong fiber of a plant which is quite similar to the banana plant. The fibers are long, hard, and very strong, and are much used for manufacturing marine rope, towing rope, oil well cables, lariats, and fine grades of binder twine. The Filipinos use this fiber for making cloth, hats, baskets, and many other useful articles. Waste fiber or fiber of a poor grade is used for making paper. Manila paper is noted for its strength.

Forests. Lumber is also a valuable resource of the Philippines. Nearly half of the surface is in forests, some of which contain very fine lumber. Nearly all of the forest land belongs to the government. Forest areas are leased to firms who wish to cut the timber. In this way the government controls the cutting of timber and prevents wasteful methods of lumbering. 

Lumber from the Philippines is now being shipped to the 

United States and even to Europe. 

Rice Culture. The largest and most valuable crop in the 

Philippines is rice, which is the chief food of the greater part of the people. More ground is devoted to the cultivation of rice than to all the crops which are raised for export. Though their crop is large, not enough rice is raised to supply the people and some rice has to be imported. 303 are supported by stone walls which if placed end to end would extend half way around the equator.

In cultivating their rice fields, the Filipinos use a long-horned buffalo, or carabao. This animal is very slow but he is hardy and can work even where the ground is covered with 

Courtesy Philippine Tourist Association RICE TERRACES IN THE PHILIPPINES 

The rice terraces of the Ifugaos at Banaue, Mountain Province, rank as one of the outstanding examples of engineer skill and manpower. The mountains have been chiseled down into a series of gigantic steps, with retaining walls of cleverly matched stone. These terraces were built over 1300 years ago. 

A failure in the rice crop brings much misery to many Filipinos who are dependent on the annual crop of rice and cannot afford to buy other food if this crop fails. They do not save and store up either money or food to be used in case of need. 

Not all of the people in the Philippines, however, eat rice. In fact, to some of them, rice is a luxury. Between fifteen and twenty million bushels of corn are raised. This is made into porridge or roasted on the cob.

Some of it is fed to the animals. 

One of the most remarkable features in Luzon are terraces for growing rice on the mountain sides. The terraces extend for thousands of feet up the mountain sides and water. The carabaos are very gentle and even the little children can handle them.

Manufactures. - Many small manufacturing industries to supply everyday needs of the people are being developed in the 

Philippines. Machinery, hats, umbrellas, shoes, hemp, starch, rattan, and cotton goods are some of the articles made. Embroideries are a specialty of the women who are expert in originating and carrying out their own designs. 

Present Needs of the Philippines. - One of the greatest needs of the Philippines is more and better means of transportation more good roads for automobiles, more railroads, and better steamship service between the islands. 

Another great need is better methods in agriculture, and the development of planta-

tions to grow more products for export. 

Large areas of land not now being used to any extent could be cultivated. The Philippines could produce enough rice to supply their own needs and, in addition, greatly increase their production of sugar, hemp, copra, camphor,

L. UNITED STATES IN HER WORLD RELATIONSHIPS 

The interests of all nations are ours also. We are partners with the rest. What affects mankind is inevitably our affair as well as the affair of the nations of 

Europe and Asia. 

WOODROW WILSON 

The Swift Shuttles of the Seven Seas. 

Suppose the world were flat and you could rise so far above it in an airplane that you could see it all in one sweep. The first thing you would notice would be that the oceans cover much more of the surface of the world than the continents. 

And as you looked more carefully, you would realize that these vast water areas do not separate the land areas so much as they bind them together. For going and coming over these oceanways are ships carrying passengers and goods. Like swift shuttles they are constantly weaving back and forth. over the seven seas. Across the land areas long freight trains speed, bringing to the ports products destined for other lands. Transfers are quickly made at the wharves. Freight becomes cargo now, and cargo, freight. Back over the land go the long lines of freight cars, while out to sea steam the ocean carriers. 

Although you cannot see them, messages are flashed by telephone, telegraph, cable, and radio, across the continents, under the seas, through the air. And high overhead in your airplane, you could understand as never before the true significance of all this ceaseless activity. The world is exchanging goods. 

East and West, North and South, are offering

THE NORMANDIE IN NEW YORK HARBOR 

P.L. Sperr

This great ship weaves, like a shuttle, back and forth across the Atlantic, endanger the mines and farms, factories and railroads, which constitute our investments.

We want to understand them better also in order to have friendly business relations. We want to know more about their history, their geography, and scenic beauty, for some day we might visit them.

This old world of ours is growing smaller. Oceans are bridges to-day and not barriers. Nations are neighbors.

The Interdependent World. 

- The old Biblical expression bringing strangers from foreign lands to visit us, and carrying us to visit for- might have said, "No nation eign lands weaving a pattern that shall make nations friends. each other the products natural to their own soils and climes. 

Yes, the world is exchanging goods. And the United States, so favorably located between land and water areas, has more commerce constantly coming and going than any other nation of the world.

Our Overseas Neighbors.-The Depart ment of Commerce estimates that our investments abroad total about $15,000,000,000, not counting the war debts. Although we cannot comprehend such a huge sum of money, we can grasp what it means to us as a nation.

In the first place, it means that we are interested in the countries where we have investments. Our magazines to-day carry many articles about foreign countries. Some of them are published especially to interpret international events and to make us know and understand better the people of other lands.

And if we have interests in these countries, we want them to succeed. For their success means our success too. We want them to have firm governments, for frequent uprisings liveth unto itself alone." With the remarkable advance in transportation and invention during the past century, the whole world has grown smaller. We can now appreciate the full significance of President 

Wilson's words: "The interests of all nations are ours also. We are partners with the rest. 

What affects mankind is inevitably our affair."

THE HAGUE COURT AND THE LEAGUE OF NATIONS 

Only a few years ago a great drought affected a large section in northern China. 

Crops withered and failed. Thousands starved. All industrial activity was halted.

This particular section had developed trade relations with the near-by part of Russia. 

That trade was seriously affected. Eastern

Russia, not able to exchange goods as heretofore, lost a very valuable income. So, in its turn, it was unable to purchase as formerly from certain markets in Japan and western 

Europe. Ordinarily, with the profits of the trade with China, eastern Russia had purchased cotton goods in large part. Now the sale of cotton goods was severely hampered. 

The effect of this reached across the Atlantic and made itself felt in our country. It directly affected the price of baled cotton on the wharves in the South. The mills in New 

England were compelled to slow down, and eventually to release many workers. One of the results was a long period of hard times in such cities as Fall River, Lowell, and Lawrence, coming to a head in a serious strike. 

This is only one illustration of the fact that what affects one part of the world is directly felt in some other part, though thousands of miles away. It is well for us now and then to study and trace out cases like this - and there are many of them. Thus we may realize very definitely how interdependent the nations of the world are to-day.

The vast interchange of the goods of the world brings about many problems that must be solved by the countries concerned. Disputes arise which, if not settled, lead to bitterness and even war. 307 disputes by arbitration. A permanent court of arbitration, the Court of International Justice, was set up at The Hague in 1921. 

The World Court and the League of Nations. Some agency of this kind is what the poet, Tennyson, had in mind when he wrote of "The Parliament of Man." In
