Distinguished judges from all over the world honor its bench. They pass upon questions of difference between two or more nations who are willing to submit them to the court for decision.

Although distinguished citizens of the United States have served as judges of this famous court, the government of the United States has never become officially a member. 

At the Paris Peace Conference which met to settle the issues of the World War, it was proposed by President Wilson that the nations of the world should establish a league for the peace, safety, and good-will" of all its members. A League of Nations was formed, although the United States did not then become and is not now a member. The 

League meets regularly at Geneva, Switzer- 66

HEADQUARTERS OF THE LEAGUE OF NATIONS 

This imposing Palace of the Nations, just outside the city of Geneva, overlooks beautiful Lake Leman. land. About fifty nations of the world send delegates to attend its sessions (1939). 

The constitution of the League provides for the three branches of government: executive (the Secretariat), judicial (the World Court), and legislative (the Council and Assembly).

When the League was first established, it exerted wide influence for peace, and succeeded in preventing numerous threatened outbreaks. More recently, however, especially since the withdrawal of Germany, Italy, and Japan, it has not been particularly effective. However, conditions in the world today indicate that there is more need than ever of some body or organization that can help coöperation among all nations.

ROCKY MOUNTAIN NATIONAL PARK

M. SCENERY IN THE UNITED STATES 

The Nation's Playgrounds. For many years the United States government and most of the states have been setting apart areas with the most beautiful scenery or unusual natural features as public parks. 

The aim is to preserve these areas in their natural state for the enjoyment of all the people now as well as in the future. No trees are cut unwisely, no plants are disturbed. Birds and other animals, excepting those which prey upon the deer and other grazing types, are protected. Under these conditions, the animals grow very tame, lose their fear of people, and become one of the most interesting features of the parks. 

National Parks and Monuments. -There are now twenty-six national parks, one of which is in Alaska and another in Hawaii. 

In addition to these there are over seventy national monuments. The exact difference between parks and monuments is hard to explain. A national park is established by act of Congress whereas a presidential proclamation creates the national monument. 

Generally the national monuments are smaller and are set aside to preserve special features of historic or pre-historic interest, or of biologic as well as geologic interest. 

They comprise ruins of the homes of the early Indian dwellers in our country, and the later Spanish Missions, rocks eroded in striking forms, limestone caves, fossils and petrified trees and plants, and glaciers. The national parks are usually larger and have been set apart on account of their scenic beauty. 

Hot Springs National Park. - The national parks contain not only the most magnificent scenery in our country but also a great

This valley was produced by glaciation. A tongue of ice forced its way through, scouring off these hardest of rocks and letting them stand like steep walls to hem in the valley. This is one of the finest examples of a glaciated valley in the world.

Grand Canyon Park. - The 

Grand Canyon of the Colorado in Arizona is another great scenic feature included in our national parks. Here the stream has cut its way down through a high plateau, forming a marvelous canyon. 

One may stand on the rim and view the Colorado River at the bottom of the gorge a mile below. Theodore Roosevelt declared this canyon to be "absolutely unparalleled throughout the wide world." 

Courtesy Colorado Springs Chamber of Commerce

GARDEN OF THE GODS 

Nature, in a freakish mood, produced these grotesque shapes and masses - the remains of mountains worn and washed away by winds and waters. variety of natural wonders. The first area to be reserved was the hot springs region in Arkansas. These hot springs have notable curative effects on certain diseases, and Congress desired to give as many people as possible the benefit of these springs at a low price. The springs are situated at the base of the Ozark Mountains in a region of much beauty.

Yellowstone National Park. - Yellowstone 

Park, with more geysers than all the rest of the world combined, mud volcanoes, petrified forest, Grand Canyon of the Yellowstone, lakes, streams, waterfalls, and other attractive features, is the most celebrated of our national parks. This park is also interesting for its many wild animals, especially deer, elk, bears, and a herd of bison.

Yosemite National Park. - Yosemite Park contains the celebrated Yosemite Valley. 

Here the steep granite sides rise almost perpendicularly from over 2000 to nearly 6000 feet above the floor of the valley. Numerous waterfalls, one with a sheer drop of over 1600 feet, add to the attractions.

Mount Rainier National 

Park. Mount Rainier in 

Washington is one of a number of extinct volcanoes belonging to the Cascade Mountain system. It is famous especially for its glacier system, the largest in the United States.

Crater Lake National Park. 

- Crater Lake, also in the Cascade Mountains, is noted for its unusual origin, for its great depth and the deep blue of its water. Mount Rainier once had its top blown off. The volcano where 

Crater Lake now is had its top sink back into the earth, leaving a deep steep-sided depression in which this famous lake now lies. 

Later eruptions produced small cones in the bottom of the basin. One of these now forms an island in the lake.

Courtesy Atchison, Topeka and Santa Fe Railway

INDIAN CLIFF DWELLINGS 

Cut into these rocks of the ages are the homes of an ancient people the Indian cliff dwellers of New Mexico. There they stand, with their stone carvings, just as they were built-high up the steep sides where only hand and foot holes gave access to the rooms. The ruins of these homes and the articles found in them tell us much about the lives of these early people who had attained a high degree of civilization.

One of the striking facts about our national parks is that each is quite different from all the others. Each has some distinguishing feature unexcelled in beauty if not in size

Children especially need some place where they may play free from the dangers of the street. Boys and girls on farms and in small towns or villages have open spaces or vacant lots. But in the city, where land is so valuable, there is likely to be no place for play except the street. Many of the city children cannot afford carfare to take them out of the city. So for these and others who must stay in the city, public playgrounds have been established. Playgrounds connected with the public schools are also opened to the boys and girls during vacation. 

Baseball diamonds, tennis courts, and other facilities for play are frequently provided for adults as well as children. By these various means, the people of the United States are trying to make it possible for all to enjoy some form of outdoor recreation.

PUBLIC PARK IN HOUSTON, TEXAS 

This is a good example of the beautiful recreation centers provided in cities of the United States in all parts of the country. anywhere in the world. Our national parks are not only playgrounds and pleasant places to visit, but also museums in whose exhibits we can read the history of the past. 

City Parks and Playgrounds. City parks and public playgrounds are commonly found in all of our larger cities. As people are crowded together in cities they realize the need for open spaces where they may go to enjoy fresh air and sunshine. Cities try to excel in having beautiful parks with ornamental shade trees, shrubs, and flowers. In many cases a zoo and a greenhouse containing tropical plants and rare flowers add to the attractions of the park. Artificial lakes and swimming or wading ponds are often found in the larger parks or playgrounds.

APPENDIX A THE EARTH 

earth and passing through the poles. Parallels of latitude are imaginary circles round the How do we know that the earth is round? earth parallel to the equator, which is an 

Here are two of the best proofs: 

1. Eclipse Proof. Sometimes the earth gets between the sun and the moon in such a way that it casts a shadow on the moon.

This is called the eclipse of the moon. The shape of this shadow is always circular and the only object that always casts a circular shadow is a sphere. This is probably the best proof that the earth is round. 

2. Ship Proof. When a ship goes out to sea the lowest part above the water line disappears first and the highest part disappears last. When a ship is approaching land the highest part appears first and the lowest part above the water line appears last. Over all the oceans, larger lakes, and seas, the rate of appearance and disappearance is the same in all directions for the same rate of travel of the ship. This proves that the earth is equally curved in all directions. A sphere is the only object which has equal curvature in all directions. 

Rotation of the Earth. This sphere on which we live rotates from west to east, turning on its axis once in every twenty-four hours. The ends of the axis are the North and South Poles. From this rotation, day and night result, and these alternate periods of daylight and darkness help to determine the periods of work and rest.

A TOTAL ECLIPSE OF THE SUN 

A total eclipse of the sun, with its awe-inspiring darkness, occurs only at rare intervals in any one locality, and to witness such an eclipse is a long-remembered experience. 

Because we know the cause of eclipses, they do not strike terror to our hearts as they did for people in ancient times. 

Notice the circular shape of the shadow of the moon on the sun. The shadow of the earth during an eclipse of the moon is likewise circular. What does this prove about the shape of both the earth and the moon? degrees on the surface of the earth north or south of the equator. FRONT A LOCATION SCHEME Second Avenue East Third Avenue East STREET Directions and Location. Places are lo- 

cated in many cities by means of a scheme something like the one shown in the above diagram: 

A street running east and west (Main

Street in this case) is selected from which to reckon distances north and south. First 

Street North means one block north of Main Street, while First Street South means one block south of Main Street.

Similarly, a street running north and south (Front Avenue in this case) is selected from which to reckon distances east and west. For instance, First Avenue East means one block east of Front Avenue, while First Avenue 

West means one block west of Front Avenue. 

Thus, if some one should desire to know where a house in this city is located, he could be directed with reference to the two base lines, Front Avenue and Main Street. 

The earth may be likened to a huge city, with the equator as Main Street and the 

Prime Meridian as Front Avenue. The dis tance from the equator to either pole is divided into 90 equal parts, or degrees, marked off by parallels of latitude, which correspond to the streets in our diagram. For example, 1° North Latitude corresponds to First Street 

North in the diagram, while 1° South Latitude corresponds to First Street South.

In like manner, the distance east and west from the Prime Meridian to the meridian directly opposite, one-half of the way around the earth, is divided into 180° marked off by meridians of longitude. Thus, 1° East Longitude means 1° east of the Prime Meridian and corresponds to First Avenue East in the diagram. On the earth, each of these "blocks" or degrees is further subdivided into 60 divisions called minutes and each minute, in turn, into 60 seconds. Symbols for these terms are: (degrees); (minutes); " (seconds).

These schemes of measurement are useful both on land and sea. The mariner on the ocean, responding to a wireless from his brother sailor in distress, must know not only his own latitude and longitude but also that of the one who is calling him and from this must determine the direction in which to steer

The explorer in the heart of a vast continent, if he has the proper instruments with him, can determine his latitude and longitude, and can indicate on a map the proper location of any feature of interest he may have discovered.

THE SEASONS 

Climate. The rotation of the earth and its revolution about the sun, through their effects on temperature, air pressure, rainfall, and winds have much to do with climate. 

Climate, in turn, determines the plant life which is necessary for animal and human life. It helps to determine the amount and kinds of clothing worn, the type of shelter, and even the amount of energy and ambition which a people possess. A simple grass hut and very little clothing suffice for the Negro in Central Africa, while the Eskimo wears heavy furs, builds his igloo of snow, and fishes through a hole in the ice.

Effects of the Seasons. Climate in the temperate zones is closely related to the seasons. There changing conditions must be met. At one time, people are concerned with keeping warm, at another, with keeping cool. They must adapt their food, clothing, and shelter to meet these changing conditions. Food must be produced during the growing season and stored away in one form or another for use during the nongrowing season.

The coming of winter gives rise to various transportation problems. Inland waterways and harbors may be blocked with ice; heavy snowfall frequently holds up traffic on the railroads; and the removal of snow from the streets of modern cities is one of the heaviest items of seasonal expenses. 

The most active and progressive peoples of the world live in the temperate zones, and these areas are not only marked by seasonal extremes in temperature, but by daily changes in weather conditions as well. Such changes 

319 are good for people and are stimulating to industrial and commercial activity.

Causes of the Seasons. What causes the seasons? The following are some of the facts which may help to answer this question. (a) For all practical purposes the earth may be considered a perfect sphere. (b) Owing to the great distance of the sun, its rays are practically parallel as they reach 

MODERN SNOW REMOVAL Brown Brothers 

Removing snow from city streets is one of the big expenses in northern latitudes. Formerly men were employed to shovel the snow into carts which hauled it away. Now much of this work is done by huge machines which pick up the snow and convey it into big trucks which are then driven to some suitable place and the snow dumped. the earth. Consequently, all of the rays but one strike the curved surface of the earth obliquely. This one ray strikes the earth vertically. The farther away a point on the earth's surface is from the vertical ray the more slanting will be the rays which strike it. (c) Looking down on the North Pole. the earth rotates counterclockwise. It also revolves around the sun counterclockwise. (d) The axis of the earth is inclined 23.5 degrees from the perpendicular to the plane of the earth's orbit, or path around the sun. (e) During its journey around the sun, the earth's axis constantly points in the same

APPENDIX 

direction; that is, in each new position, it is parallel to its former positions. 

The results of all these conditions are that at one time of the year, during the northern summer, the North Pole is inclined toward the sun while the South Pole is inclined away from the sun. Again, during the northern winter season, the North Pole is inclined away from the sun while the South Pole is inclined toward the sun. Twice during the year, on March 21 and again on September 22, neither the North Pole nor the South Pole is inclined toward the sun and the sunlight reaches both poles.

In the United States when the North Pole is inclined toward the sun, during the whole period from March 21 to September 22, the days are longer than the nights. In other words, we have more hours of sunlight than of darkness. Also, the noon sun is higher in the sky and the rays of sunlight strike the earth more directly, so that a given area receives more heat than it does during a similar period when the North Pole is inclined away from the sun. On March 21 the days and nights are equal. Then the days gradually increase in length and the nights decrease until June 21, when we have our longest day. 

Then the days decrease and the nights increase until on September 22 they are again equal. 

The warmest season and consequently the growing season for most of the United States is the period which centers at June 21. Then the hours of sunlight are longest and areas are receiving the greatest amount of heat from the sun, but our hottest weather usually occurs in July and August, rather than in June, owing to the gradual storing up of heat on the earth's surface. 

From September 22 to March 21 in the northern hemisphere, the days are shorter than the nights, the shortest day occurring 

December 21. On that date, the noon sun is lowest in the sky, the rays of sunlight are most oblique, and our country receives the least heat from the sun. Naturally, this results in lower temperatures, but the coldest weather usually comes after, rather than before, December 21. (Why?) 

Spring and fall (or autumn) are neither very warm nor very cold. In relation to crops in nearly all parts of the United States, we may consider spring the planting season, summer the growing and ripening season, and fall the harvesting season.

Causes of Winds. The air moves from one place to another largely as a result of differences in air pressure on the surface of the land or water. Air always moves from a high pressure to a low pressure area, as illustrated in the above diagram. 

The circulation of the air through the chimney of a kerosene lamp also illustrates this principle. When the wick is lighted, the air within the chimney is heated. As the air becomes warmer, it expands and floats up and out, thus causing the first motion (1) in a circuit of air called a convection current. 

This makes the air lighter within the chimney (a low pressure) than the air around

APPENDIX 

the outside (a high pressure). The latter air moves into the holes at the base of the chimney (3) pushing the warmer, lighter air (4) upward and out of the chimney. As this air is cooled, it, in turn, becomes heavier (2) and settles down. The motion will continue as long as the wick remains lighted.

This principle applies to the air movements on the earth. The earth receives more heat near the equator than anywhere else. (Why is this so?) This results in what is known as the heat equator, or equatorial low pressure area where the air rises or is pushed upward. 321 the rotation of the earth. Because of the rotation of the earth, the winds are directed to the right in the northern hemisphere and to the left in the southern hemisphere. 4 The winds just north of the heat equator are called northeast trade winds, and those just south of the heat equator are called southeast trade winds. Winds are → always named according to the direction from which they come, not according to the direction in which they are blowing. (What is a north wind? What is a south wind?) The winds between the high pressure areas and the polar lows are called the prevail-

Importance of Winds to All 

Life. Winds and calms are very important to plant, animal, and human life because of the relation which they bear to temperature, rainfall, and other factors of weather and climate. There are low pressure areas at the poles due to the rotation of the earth. According to the principle, the air moves from the high pressure areas to the low pressure areas. This means that the air will move in both north- origin, and they are of great benefit as erly and southerly directions from the high pressure areas of the earth. APPENDIX THE MIGHTY OCEAN 

The eternal tides ebb and flow-storms lash the waves into huge breakers which dash relentlessly against the rocky shores or surge on to the sands in long, muttering rolls the shape of the shore lines is forever changing. 

That is the mighty force of the ocean. Not only is it a highway which leads to all lands, but it also means life itself to the people on the earth. Without it, the land would become drier than the driest of deserts, and neither animal nor vegetable life as we know it could exist. 

THE OCEAN The Influence of the Ocean. - The ocean plays a very important part in human lives, whether one lives along the shores or not.

Besides supplying food and carrying commerce it is a very important factor in weather and climate. It is the earth's great moderator of temperature, preventing the extremes of heat and cold. It is also the source of most of the water vapor of the air. If the oceans were to disappear, the whole earth would be drier than the driest deserts of to-day. 

Ocean Currents. - Ocean currents are really rivers of water in the ocean caused by the pushing and pulling of the most steadily blowing winds on the earth, the trades.

The trades give a westerly trend to the waters near the equator. These waters would go entirely around the earth if it were not for the continents. The continents turn the waters to the north and to the south, after which the rotation of the earth further deflects them to the right in the northern hemisphere and to the left in the southern hemisphere. 

Ocean currents are a powerful factor in climate, carrying warm waters into high latitudes and cold waters into low latitudes, thus helping to distribute temperatures. The most famous of the world's ocean currents is the Gulf 

Stream, which emerges from the warm waters of the 

Caribbean Sea and passes between Florida and the Bahama 

Islands. It then skirts the eastern shore of the United

States about as far north as Cape Cod, and continues on to the Newfoundland Banks, after which it crosses the Atlantic as the West Wind Drift.

Another famous ocean current is the Japanese Current, which is to the Pacific what the Gulf Stream is to the Atlantic.

EXPLORING THE POLAR REGIONS BY AIR

For there the airships, light as eagle feather, Will draw the comrade continents together.

Lightly as foam upon a flowing tide! 1

Discovery of the North Pole. - Admiral 

Peary was the first man to reach the North 

Pole. He traveled by dog sled. In his last 

1 New York Times, June 26, 1926.

APPENDIX PEARY'S SLEDGE Keystone View Company 

This sledge was used by Admiral Peary on his trip to the North Pole. Think of the difference in trekking over the rough ice of the far north with this frail vehicle, making but a few miles of progress each day, and riding in airship or airplane, covering hundreds of miles each day. public address shortly before his death in 

1920, he predicted that future polar explorers would travel by air. How true his prophecy!

Several times men have flown over the North 

Pole, and the South Pole has also been reached by an aërial expedition. 

The First Polar Air Expedition. - The first attempt to reach the North Pole by air was made by the Swedish scientist, Andree. 

He conceived the idea of sailing over the polar regions in a balloon. With two companions, he set out from one of the Spitsbergen islands in July, 1897.

For 33 years, the fate of Andree and his companions remained a mystery. Then in 

August, 1930, a Norwegian scientific expedition in charge of Dr. Gunnar Horn accidentally discovered the remains of Andree and his two companions on a little-visited island near the Spitsbergen group. A boat, stove, camera, rifles, and other articles carried by the expedition were also found. 

On Andree's body was his diary and ship's log revealing the story of his adventure. 

The Conquest of the North Pole by Air. 

On May 9, 1926, Rear-Admiral Richard

E. Byrd and his assistant, Floyd Bennett, flew from Spitsbergen to the North Pole and back again in less than 16 hours. In less than two-thirds of a day they did what it took Admiral Peary two-thirds of a year to accomplish. Within a few hours of his return, the radio made Byrd's triumph known all over the world, whereas five months passed before news of Peary's success came through. 

Here is Byrd's description of the North Pole: "It did not look different from other miles of ice over which we had just passed. 

There was the same stretch of brilliant white marked by ridges, hummocks, and a few leads (open channels in the ice) recently frozen, showing green against white. This was the desolate top of the earth and we were over it. I shook hands with Bennett, who smiled and nodded satisfaction, then made my way aft and saluted the gallant Peary. 66 From pressure ridges I knew that the ice there was in constant motion. The ice was composed of large cakes, of fields whose edges, except at the leads, jammed up against each 

Keystone View Company THE PLANE THAT FLEW OVER THE NORTH POLE 

Up in the air, with wings outspread, this great man-made bird flew over the North Pole and back. It is the giant 

Fokker used by Rear-Admiral Byrd when he flew from 

Spitsbergen in 1926 and completed the trip over the Pole and then radioed the news to the world.

other and were pressed so tightly by wind and ocean currents that ice and snow was forced up to varying heights, depending upon the pressure exerted. Cakes were glistening white with hummocks. "Here and there on most of them and on some drifts caused by strong winds I knew the surface was more uneven than it looked from the plane. Floes were of many shapes and sizes, and pressure ridges ran willy-nilly, apparently without rhyme or reason. 66 Then

After we reached the Pole, we went a little further, changing from north to south in an instant- a curious sensation. we circled so as to go around the Pole again. 

We had the strange experience of changing time so rapidly that we went all around the clock in a few minutes and changed from one day to another. "In order to keep my head clear there and not get confused, I had to forget peculiar time and direction and concentrate on the position of the blessed sun. For the magnetic compass swung in disconcerting fashion as we turned back. But when the revolution was completed we looked down for the last time on the top of the world, took our course by the sun compass and headed back toward Spitsbergen." 

Two days later, May 11, 1926, the now famous dirigible, the Norge, left Spitsbergen on her way to Alaska via the North Pole.

Fifteen hours later a wireless from the airship announced that she was over the North Pole.

Within a few hours we were reading about it in the newspapers. This was the first direct message that was ever received from the "top of the world." In less than three days the Norge landed at Teller, on the west coast of Alaska. Again the welcome news was flashed to a world anxious for fear that this unknown land of the north had claimed more victims. Science had won, and the navigators had scored one more triumph. 

Again in May, 1928, the North Pole was 325 reached by an airship, the Italia, in command of General Nobile. Unfortunately, this expedition met with disaster on the return trip, and eight of the fifteen men lost their lives.

Discovery of the South Pole. - Roald 

Amundsen, a Norwegian, with wide experience in Arctic explorations, was the first to reach the South Pole, which he discovered 

December 14, 1911. A little over a month later, January 18, 1912, Captain Robert F.

Scott, an Englishman, also reached the South 

Pole. On the return trip, Scott and his party perished in one of the fierce Antarctic blizzards within one hundred and fifty miles of the home station.

Conquest of the South Pole by Air. - As 

Rear-Admiral Byrd was the first to reach the North Pole by air, he was likewise the first to fly over the South Pole. This feat he accomplished November 29, 1929. 

On August 25, 1928, Rear-Admiral Byrd's ice ship, the City of New York, left Hoboken on the long trip to the Antarctic. 

On Christmas Day, 1928, Byrd sent the following message to the Secretary of the Navy:

APPENDIX 

66 We have reached to-day the great mysterious ice barrier. It presents to us an ice cliff higher than the masts of the ship. "On this Christmas Day we are thankful and proud to report that we have been able to carry the American flag several miles farther south than it has ever been, and it seems 

DOG TEAM IN ANTARCTICA 

Chinook dogs, especially trained in New Hampshire during the winter of 1927-1928, were taken to act as leaders of the dog teams.

Several teams of dogs were included to help in overland transportation and exploration. 

A powerful short-wave sending and receiving set kept the expedition in touch with the folks back home. Think of being in Antarctica and listening to a program of songs and entertainment broadcast from 

Pittsburgh and other stations! 

On Christmas Day, as the 

City of New York approached the great ice barrier, the crew listened to broadcasting from home. 

Later Byrd made another trip on which he stayed longer in the Antarctic, mapping and surveying. He returned in 1935. On these expeditions he chose as his base the Ross Ice Shelf just behind the Bay of Whales. This shelf extends inland about 

400 miles to a mountain which marks the beginning of the edge of the plateau on which the South Pole is situated. Over the rough snows of Little America, under the cheerless sun of an Antarctic summer, dog teams trotted out with supplies to depots along the route to the South Pole, or pushed off on some important geological exploration. 

Two members of the Byrd Expedition are helping this team over one of the bad places along the route, where cracks in the ice gape wide. fitting that an airplane, that instrument of good will, should reach its furthest south on 

Christmas Day. "We are 2400 statute miles from the nearest human dwelling, in the only area in the world where a ship can get so far from civilization. That we are here safely is due to 

Providence and my loyal and stout-hearted shipmates, who have worked together unselfishly as a unit. It will probably be some days before we get ashore, on account of ice."

Byrd's expedition was the best-equipped of any that ever went into the Arctic or 

Antarctic regions. It cost over $500,000. 

The list of supplies included over 1200 items among which were tractors, sleds, dogs, machines for repair work, tents, skis, food, clothing, footgear, furs, books, and radio. 

Reasons for Polar Explorations. - Why do men risk their lives in exploring these cold and inhospitable regions? They have wanted to know what these polar regions are like, and what they have to tell about the magnetic variations, ocean currents, and air conditions. Peary's expedition to the North

Pole proved that it is surrounded by water. 

Scott's and Amundsen's expeditions revealed a land continent around the South Pole.

The large area north of Siberia and Alaska, between 75° North Latitude and the North 

Pole, had never been seen by any man.

Peary and others thought there might be land there, but the Norge's passage across this area revealed nothing but ice fields and

RC 

GETTING READY FOR A FLIGHT APPENDIX 

Wrapped in warm, fur-lined coats, hooded and booted, Rear-Admiral Byrd and some of his men are making final preparations for a flight from their base at Little America. 327 a plateau. The Arctic Ocean is just as deep as the Antarctic Plateau is high-about 10,000 feet. It would seem that the earth had been pushed in around the North Pole to form a deep ocean basin, and pushed out for a corresponding distance in the vicinity of the South Pole to form a plateau.

The northern polar region is flat, with fields of floating ice; the southern polar area is rimmed by glistening high mountains, in many places rising abruptly to a height of from 10,000 to 15,000 feet. It is possible to fly at a low elevation over the North Pole; the height of the mountains in Antarctica makes it doubly difficult for the aviator to reach the South Pole with his heavily laden stretches of water. If there is any land, it plane. can be of no great extent.

To explore and map as much as possible of the unknown land of Antarctica was one of the main objects of the Byrd Antarctic 

Expedition, though the thrilling flight to the 

South Pole and back is the event that captured people's imagination. Byrd explored and mapped 280,000 square miles of the 

Antarctic Continent, and found that the 

South Pole is about 10,000 feet above sea level.

Geologists went along to study the rock formations and the fossils in order to learn something about the geological history of the region. They also wanted to find out whether the Antarctic mountain ranges were part of the Andean system, and whether the continent itself was perhaps a connecting link between South America and Africa.

Contrasts of the Polar Regions. In some respects, the polar regions are alike. Both are vast areas of ice, snow, and extreme cold. 

Fog and clouds hinder the work of the explorer during the summer months. Each pole has a year consisting of one long day and an equally long night. 

In other ways, these "ends of the earth" are just as opposite in character as they are in position. One is an ocean; the other,

The Arctic Ocean is surrounded by land over which it is possible to reach its waters; the Antarctic Plateau is surrounded by a deep ocean. A rim of ice makes it difficult, if not impossible, to approach the land of Antarctica in most places. In the few places where this barrier opens during the summer season, ships are in danger of being caught and crushed by the shifting pack ice.

The land area of the Arctic polar region is much less than that of the Antarctic and it 

PENGUINS ON DRESS PARADE 

Pompously strutting along the snows, in their stylish black and white, these summer visitors to Antarctica were not in the least afraid of the strange visitors from the north. They gathered around in friendly fashion, and furnished amusement as well as food for the members of the Byrd Antarctic Expedition.

APPENDIX 

3. Using a map of the northern polar region, or a globe, explain how it was possible for Byrd to go all around the clock in a few minutes. is nearer to the main centers of civilization. 

It has, therefore, been easier to establish bases and carry on Arctic explorations from the United States and the European countries which have furnished leaders and equipment Almanac, consult reference books, and prepare special for these expeditions.

There is much life in the Arctic. Musk oxen, bears, and foxes inhabit the land areas, while seals, walruses, and fish abound in the waters. Birds, also, are quite plentiful in the summer. Even in the northernmost villages, the Eskimo has no great difficulty in getting his food. In the Antarctic, there is no life except along the shores, and then only in the summer time. Even the birds seldom go far inland. Seals, fish, and whales, however, are quite common in the surrounding waters during the short summer season. 

An aviator forced to land somewhere in the Arctic might be able to find something to eat, but in Antarctica he would starve unless he carried his own food supplies. Think of a vast area, as large as the United States and Mexico together, in which no one, so far as we know, has ever lived!
